# When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process? Correspondences Between Game Theory and Reinforcement Learning

Eduardo C. Garrido-Merchán Affiliation: DIGNUM Centre, Universidad
Pontificia Comillas Affiliation: Madrid, Spain
Email: [ecgarrido@comillas.edu](mailto:)

October 30, 2026

###### Abstract

Reinforcement learning and game theory describe the same object from two
sides. Game theory specifies what a rational outcome of a strategic
interaction is; reinforcement learning specifies a procedure by which an
agent could arrive at one through experience. The two literatures
nevertheless maintain separate vocabularies for identical mathematical
structures, and the textbook treatments that bridge them tend to present
the correspondence as a sequence of analogies rather than as a set of
theorems. This monograph supplies the dictionary. We take every standard
class of game, from the normal-form game through repeated games,
stochastic games, partially observable stochastic games, extensive-form
games with imperfect information, Bayesian games, graphical and
congestion games, games with communication, Stackelberg games, potential
and supermodular games, coalitional games, mean-field games and
evolutionary games, and we identify for each one the decision-theoretic
model that reinforcement learning already studies under a different
name. For every pairing we state precisely whether the correspondence is
a definitional identity, an exact reduction supported by a theorem, or a
structural analogy that fails under identifiable conditions, and we
exhibit the degeneracy that recovers the single-agent model when the
number of agents is one. The same treatment is applied to solution
concepts: best response and policy improvement, Nash equilibrium and the
fixed point of the Bellman optimality operator, subgame perfection and
the principle of optimality, maximin value and robust control,
correlated equilibrium and shared-randomness policies, quantal response
equilibrium and the soft Bellman operator of maximum-entropy
reinforcement learning, dominance and action masking, the Shapley value
and multi-agent credit assignment, the core and the stability of a
credit scheme, the Vickrey-Clarke-Groves transfer and the counterfactual
baseline, Blackwell approachability and no-regret learning. We then turn
the dictionary into practice, mapping each model class onto the training
procedures that are sound for it, cataloguing the classical learning
dynamics together with what each of them converges to, and recording the
computational complexity of the associated solution problems so that the
reader can see which reductions buy tractability and which merely
relabel the difficulty. A final part is devoted to the correspondences
that do not hold: the non-stationarity that invalidates the
independent-learner reduction, the impossibility results that forbid
uncoupled convergence to Nash equilibrium, the inadequacy of Nash
equilibrium as a learning target in general-sum problems, the gap
between the coarse correlated equilibria that no-regret self-play
reaches and the correlated equilibria it is often credited with, the
selection of the risk-dominant rather than the payoff-dominant
equilibrium by noisy independent learning, and the equilibrium-selection
gap that separates centralised planning from decentralised execution in
common-payoff games. The intended use is as a reference. Every section
opens with a two-column glossary that places the game-theoretic and the
reinforcement learning vocabulary side by side, every definition is
followed by a worked example with explicit numbers, and every number
printed in an example is recomputed from the model definition by an
accompanying program. Summary tables collect the correspondences, the
solution concepts, the complexity landscape, the learning dynamics, the
evaluation measures and the algorithmic recommendations in a form that
can be consulted without reading the surrounding text.

Keywords: multi-agent reinforcement learning, stochastic games,
partially observable stochastic games, decentralised POMDPs,
extensive-form games, mechanism design, equilibrium computation,
learning in games.

## 1 Introduction

A reinforcement learning researcher who opens a game theory textbook,
and a game theorist who opens a reinforcement learning textbook, each
encounter a large amount of material that they already know, written in
a notation they do not. The partially observable stochastic game of the
game theory literature and the decentralised partially observable Markov
decision process of the planning literature are, up to the restriction
of the reward structure, the same object. The information set of an
extensive-form game with perfect recall and the action-observation
history of a partially observable Markov decision process pick out the
same equivalence classes of play. Best response and greedy policy
improvement are the same operation. Subgame perfection and Bellman’s
principle of optimality are the same requirement. The Harsanyi
transformation of a Bayesian game into an extensive-form game with an
initial chance move, and the augmentation of a latent-parameter Markov
decision process into a partially observable one, are the same
construction applied twice. None of this is secret, and parts of it are
folklore in both communities, but the folklore is scattered, the
statements are usually informal, and the conditions under which a
correspondence is exact rather than suggestive are rarely written down.

This monograph is an attempt to write them down. A game is a Markov
decision process in three distinct senses, and the whole of what follows
depends on keeping them apart. The organising criterion throughout is
whether there is a map between two models that preserves the quantity
one actually cares about, namely the value of a strategy profile and
therefore the set of solutions. Resemblance of form is never sufficient.
We will distinguish three strengths of correspondence and we will label
every entry of every table with one of them. A correspondence is
definitional when the two models are the same tuple with different
names, as with a common-payoff partially observable stochastic game and
a decentralised partially observable Markov decision process. It is an
exact reduction when a theorem supplies a value-preserving and
solution-preserving map between models that are not syntactically
identical, as with the equivalence between a decentralised partially
observable Markov decision process and a continuous-state Markov
decision process over occupancy states ([Dibangoye et al.,
2016](#bib.bib45)), or between a decentralised control problem with
partial history sharing and a single-agent partially observable Markov
decision process played by a fictitious coordinator ([Nayyar et al.,
2013](#bib.bib128)). It is a structural analogy when the two models
share a form but the map either fails to preserve solutions or requires
assumptions that the general case violates, as with the claim that an
independent learner in a multi-agent environment faces a Markov decision
process, which holds exactly when the other agents are stationary and
fails precisely when they are learning. These three strengths are the
three answers to the question in the title. A game is a Markov decision
process by definition in the first case, by theorem in the second, and
by appearance alone in the third.

The distinction matters because the literature is full of statements of
the third kind presented in the voice of the first. Treating the other
agents as part of the environment is the standard justification for
applying single-agent algorithms to multi-agent problems, and it is
correct as a statement about a frozen opponent profile and incorrect as
a statement about a training run in which every agent is simultaneously
changing. Likewise, the observation that a common-payoff game is just a
Markov decision process over joint actions is correct for a centralised
planner and silently false for independent learners, because the
reduction preserves the optimal value but destroys the information about
which of several optimal joint actions the agents will each individually
select. The gap between those two readings is the entire subject of
coordination and equilibrium selection, and a dictionary that elides it
is worse than no dictionary at all.

The treatment is intended to be exhaustive over the standard taxonomy of
games and succinct within each entry. We begin in
[Section 2](#S2 "2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
by fixing the single-agent baseline, the Markov decision process and the
partially observable Markov decision process together with the belief
and history constructions that will be reused throughout.
[Section 3](#S3 "3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
introduces the normal-form game as the stateless limit and identifies
the one-agent degeneracy with the multi-armed bandit.
[Section 4](#S4 "4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
develops the solution concepts as fixed points of operators and shows,
for each, the single-agent object it collapses to, and
[Section 5](#S5 "5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
weakens the rationality assumption in three directions, obtaining
dominance and rationalisability, the bounded-depth models of iterated
reasoning, and the quantal response equilibrium, which is the
multi-agent soft Bellman equation.
[Section 6](#S6 "6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
treats repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process
that makes the folk theorem a statement about equilibrium multiplicity
in a large policy class.
[Section 7](#S7 "7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is devoted to stochastic games, the central object of the
correspondence, and to the three reward structures, zero-sum,
common-payoff and general-sum, that determine which single-agent
reduction survives.
[Section 8](#S8 "8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
introduces partial observability and the lattice of models that it
generates, and
[Section 9](#S9 "9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
collects the exact reductions that convert these multi-agent models into
single-agent ones over enlarged state spaces, which is where most of the
theorem content of this work is located.
[Section 10](#S10 "10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
treats the structured classes, graphical, polymatrix and congestion
games, whose reinforcement learning counterpart is the coordination
graph.
[Section 11](#S11 "11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
handles extensive-form games, perfect recall and Kuhn’s theorem, and the
correspondence between information sets and action-observation
histories.
[Section 12](#S12 "12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
treats incomplete information, the Harsanyi transformation and its
reinforcement learning counterpart, the Bayes-adaptive Markov decision
process, together with common knowledge and the hierarchy of interactive
beliefs.
[Section 13](#S13 "13 Communication: cheap talk, signalling, persuasion and emergent protocols ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
covers the operation of letting agents choose what others observe,
through cheap talk, costly signalling and information design, and
connects it to emergent communication.
[Section 14](#S14 "14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
covers commitment, bilevel structure and opponent shaping, and
[Section 15](#S15 "15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
carries the same asymmetry to its limit, in which the designer chooses
the game rather than a move, which is reward design read from the other
side and which brings with it the price of anarchy and two impossibility
theorems about aggregation.
[Section 16](#S16 "16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
connects potential and supermodular games to reward shaping, value
factorisation and the Individual-Global-Max condition, and
[Section 17](#S17 "17 Coalitions, stability of credit, and bargaining ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
connects coalitional game theory to credit assignment through the
Shapley value, the core, the nucleolus and the bargaining solutions.
[Section 18](#S18 "18 The population limit: mean-field games and mean-field control ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and
[Section 20](#S20 "20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
treat the two continuum limits, in the population of agents and in the
learning rate respectively, with
[Section 19](#S19 "19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
between them collecting the classical learning rules, fictitious play,
no-regret dynamics, Blackwell approachability, stochastic stability and
the variational-inequality view that underlies extragradient and
optimistic methods, together with what each converges to.
[Section 21](#S21 "21 The complexity landscape ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
assembles the complexity landscape.
[Section 22](#S22 "22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the practical core, mapping each model class to the training
procedures that are sound for it.
[Section 23](#S23 "23 Correspondences that fail, and the theorems that forbid them ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
collects the negative results, and
[Section 24](#S24 "24 Open problems at the interface ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
the open problems.

Figure 1: The lattice of models. Arrows denote inclusion of model
classes: the tail is a special case of the head. Moving right relaxes
the reward structure from a single agent to a shared payoff to arbitrary
payoffs; moving down relaxes the observability of the state. The
single-agent column is the subject of ordinary reinforcement learning;
the whole diagram is the subject of this monograph. The two shaded
entries in gold are the classes for which an exact reduction to a
single-agent model over an enlarged state space is available and is
discussed in
[Section 9](#S9 "9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").

Four conventions are used throughout and are worth stating at the
outset. The first is that we always report the single-agent degeneracy.
Every model class we introduce has a one-agent special case, and in
every table we name it, because the fastest way to check that a proposed
correspondence is coherent is to set $`n=1`$ and verify that both sides
collapse to the same familiar object. The second is that we distinguish
the planning problem, in which the model is known and a solution is to
be computed, from the learning problem, in which the model is unknown
and a solution is to be approximated from sampled interaction. A
reduction that is valid for planning need not be usable for learning,
and this is the most common source of confusion in the area. The
occupancy-state reduction of a decentralised partially observable Markov
decision process is exact, and it requires knowledge of the transition
and observation kernels in order to propagate the occupancy state, so it
is a planning tool that becomes a learning tool only under additional
modelling assumptions.

The third convention is the one that gives this work its shape as a
reference, and it is applied without exception, so it is worth stating
as a contract. Every section opens with two paragraphs naming the two
readings of its subject, the one a game theorist brings and the one a
reinforcement learning researcher brings, followed by a three-column
table that places the two vocabularies side by side and glosses each
pair in plain words. The tables are numbered and cross-referenced, so a
reader who meets an unfamiliar term can find it without reading the
surrounding argument, and a reader who already knows one column can read
the other as a translation. Within a section the unit of exposition is a
triple. Each definition is followed immediately by a reinforcement
learning reading, set off by a marked run-in heading, which names the
corresponding reinforcement learning object, pairs the symbols one by
one, and says what the definition changes about an implementation; and
that is followed by a worked example with explicit numbers. The triple
appears thirty-eight times, once per definition, and the reader who
wants only the dictionary can read the tables and the readings and skip
everything else.

The fourth convention concerns the numbers. Nothing in this subject is
difficult once the objects are small enough to compute by hand, and
almost everything in it is impenetrable when stated only in general
form, so the examples carry as much of the content as the propositions
do. Every numerical value printed in an example, from the equilibrium of
a two-by-two matrix to the value of Kuhn poker and the stationary
distribution of a log-linear learning chain, is produced by a program
that recomputes it from the model definition. The program accompanies
this work and checks each printed figure against its recomputation, so
the examples can be audited rather than trusted.

A word is owed about the symbols themselves, because this work
translates concepts between two fields and a reader arriving from either
one will meet notation that is not the notation of its own textbooks.
The choice made here is that the reinforcement learning side is primary.
States are $`\mathcal{S}`$, actions are $`\mathcal{A}`$, kernels are
$`\mathcal{T}`$ and $`\mathcal{Z}`$, rewards are $`\mathcal{R}`$,
policies are $`\pi`$, values are $`V`$ and $`Q`$, and the discount is
$`\gamma`$, as in [Sutton and Barto (2018)](#bib.bib174). Game-theoretic
objects are grafted onto that skeleton rather than the reverse, so
payoffs are $`u_{i}`$, best responses are $`\mathrm{BR}_{i}`$ and
correlated profiles are $`\sigma`$, and the agent set is the
calligraphic $`\mathcal{N}`$. The choice follows from the purpose: the
intended reader is one who already thinks in the first vocabulary and
wants the second, and forcing that reader to relearn $`\mathcal{S}`$ as
a set of histories would defeat the object.

The cost is borne by the reader coming the other way, and
[Table 1](#S1.T1 "In 1 Introduction ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the compensation. It places the notation of [Osborne and Rubinstein
(1994)](#bib.bib133), the standard graduate reference, beside the
notation used here, object by object. Three differences are structural
rather than cosmetic and are worth naming. First, Osborne and Rubinstein
take the preference relation $`\succsim_{i}`$ as primitive and the
payoff function as a representation of it, whereas we write $`u_{i}`$
throughout, which is the special case in which the preferences are
representable and the representation has been fixed. Second, they build
the extensive form on a set of histories $`H`$, so that a node is a
sequence of actions, whereas we build it on a set of nodes
$`\mathcal{V}`$ and recover histories by reading paths; the two are
isomorphic and the first is the cleaner foundation, while the second is
nearer to what an implementation stores. Third, their discount factor is
$`\delta`$ and ours is $`\gamma`$, which is the single most frequent
source of confusion when the two literatures are read side by side.

| Osborne and Rubinstein                                                         | This work                                                         | How to read the pair                                     |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------- |
| Player set $`N`$                                                               | Agent set $`\mathcal{N}`$                                         | Finite; $`n=\left\lvert\mathcal{N}\right\rvert`$ in both |
| Action set $`A_{i}`$                                                           | $`\mathcal{A}_{i}`$                                               | Identical object, different font                         |
| Action profile $`a\in A`$                                                      | $`a\in\mathcal{A}`$                                               | Identical; both write $`a_{-i}`$                         |
| Preference relation $`\succsim_{i}`$                                           | Payoff $`u_{i}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}`$                         | We assume a representation and fix it                    |
| Strategic game $`\langle N,(A_{i}),(\succsim_{i})\rangle`$                     | $`G=(\mathcal{N},\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\},\{u_{i}\})`$                 | The same tuple                                           |
| Mixed strategy $`\alpha_{i}\in\Delta(A_{i})`$                                  | $`\pi_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})`$                             | $`\Delta(\cdot)`$ is common to both                      |
| Mixed extension $`\langle N,(\Delta(A_{i})),(U_{i})\rangle`$                   | $`u_{i}(\pi)`$                                                    | Their $`U_{i}`$ is our multilinear $`u_{i}`$             |
| Extensive game $`\langle N,H,P,f_{c},(\mathcal{I}_{i}),(\succsim_{i})\rangle`$ | Tree on nodes $`\mathcal{V}`$ with partitions $`\mathcal{I}_{i}`$ | They index by histories, we by nodes                     |
| History $`h\in H`$                                                             | A node of $`\mathcal{V}`$; trajectory prefix                      | A path from the root                                     |
| Player function $`P`$                                                          | Which agent acts at a node                                        | Same role                                                |
| Chance moves $`f_{c}`$                                                         | Kernels $`\mathcal{T}`$, $`\mathcal{Z}`$                          | Nature’s randomisation                                   |
| Information partition $`\mathcal{I}_{i}`$                                      | $`\mathcal{I}_{i}`$                                               | Identical; $`I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}`$ is one set            |
| Behavioural strategy $`\beta_{i}`$                                             | Policy $`\pi_{i}`$                                                | One distribution per information set                     |
| Discount factor $`\delta`$                                                     | $`\gamma`$                                                        | The commonest collision between the two                  |
| Coalitional game $`\langle N,v\rangle`$                                        | $`(\mathcal{N},v)`$                                               | They write $`v(S)`$, we write $`v(C)`$                   |
| Bargaining $`\langle X,D,\succsim_{1},\succsim_{2}\rangle`$                    | $`(\mathcal{U},d)`$                                               | We work in utility space directly                        |

Table 1: Concordance with [Osborne and Rubinstein (1994)](#bib.bib133),
the standard graduate reference, whose notation differs from the one
used here. The right-hand column glosses the pair.

We assume familiarity with the basic vocabulary of both fields at the
level of [Sutton and Barto (2018)](#bib.bib174) for reinforcement
learning and [Osborne and Rubinstein (1994)](#bib.bib133) or [Fudenberg
and Tirole (1991)](#bib.bib63) for game theory. The reader who wants a
broad and gentle entry point to the intersection will find it in
[Albrecht et al. (2024)](#bib.bib4), whose taxonomy we follow in outline
and sharpen in detail, and in the surveys of [Buşoniu et al.
(2010)](#bib.bib28), [Hernandez-Leal et al. (2019)](#bib.bib79) and
[Yang et al. (2020)](#bib.bib195). The present work is complementary to
those: where a survey enumerates algorithms, we enumerate identities.

## 2 The single-agent baseline

We fix notation for the two single-agent models that every
correspondence in this work degenerates to. Throughout, $`\Delta(X)`$
denotes the set of probability distributions over a finite set $`X`$,
and for a tuple $`x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})`$ indexed by agents we write
$`x_{-i}`$ for the subtuple obtained by deleting the $`i`$-th component.

Before any definitions, a word about the two readings, because this is
the habit the whole monograph asks the reader to acquire. A game
theorist looks at a sequential decision problem and sees a one-player
extensive-form game with chance moves: a tree whose nodes are positions,
whose edges are moves, whose leaves carry payoffs, and whose solution is
obtained by backward induction. A reinforcement learning researcher
looks at the same object and sees a Markov decision process: a state
variable that summarises the position, a kernel that moves it, a reward
that accrues along the way, and a solution obtained by iterating an
operator until it stops moving. The two pictures differ in what they
take as primitive. The game theorist takes the tree as primitive and
derives the state as an equivalence class of histories; the
reinforcement learner takes the state as primitive and derives the tree
by unrolling. Every disagreement about notation in this field traces
back to that choice, and knowing which direction one is reading in
resolves most of them.

| Game theory                        | Reinforcement learning                                                | How to read the pair                          |
| ---------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------- |
| Decision node of a one-player tree | State $`s\in\mathcal{S}`$, or history $`h_{t}`$                       | Where you are and what you may do next        |
| Move at a node                     | Action $`a\in\mathcal{A}`$                                            | The choice under your control                 |
| Chance node                        | Transition kernel $`\mathcal{T}`$, observation kernel $`\mathcal{Z}`$ | The choice not under your control             |
| Payoff at a leaf                   | Discounted return $`\sum_{t}\gamma^{t}\mathcal{R}_{t}`$               | What is being maximised                       |
| Pure strategy                      | Deterministic policy $`\pi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{A}`$                | A complete contingent plan                    |
| Behavioural strategy               | Stochastic policy $`\pi:\mathcal{S}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A})`$           | A plan that may randomise locally             |
| Backward induction                 | Value iteration, dynamic programming                                  | Solve the end first, then work backwards      |
| Principle of optimality            | Bellman equation                                                      | Optimal plans are optimal in every subproblem |
| Value of the game at a node        | $`V^{\star}(s)`$                                                      | What the problem is worth from here on        |
| Information set                    | Action-observation history $`h_{t}`$, belief $`b_{t}`$                | What you know when you must choose            |
| Nature’s move                      | Latent state, unobserved parameter                                    | Uncertainty with a known distribution         |
| Security level against nature      | Robust value $`\max_{\pi}\min_{p}V^{\pi}_{p}`$                        | What you can guarantee with no distribution   |

Table 2: The single-agent vocabulary read from both sides. This table is
the key to
[Section 2](#S2 "2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and the one on which all later tables depend.

###### Definition 2.1 (Markov decision process).

A Markov decision process is a tuple
$`M=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{R},\gamma,b_{0})`$ in
which $`\mathcal{S}`$ is a finite set of states, $`\mathcal{A}`$ a
finite set of actions,
$`\mathcal{T}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\Delta(\mathcal{S})`$ a
transition kernel,
$`\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}`$
a bounded reward function, $`\gamma\in[0,1)`$ a discount factor and
$`b_{0}\in\Delta(\mathcal{S})`$ an initial state distribution.

 

The game-theoretic
reading. [Definition 2.1](#S2.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 2.1 (Markov decision process). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is a one-player extensive-form game with chance moves, in the sense of
[Definition 11.1](#S11.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 11.1 (Extensive-form game). ‣ 11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
presented in compressed form. Unrolling it gives a tree whose
non-terminal nodes alternate between the single agent and chance: the
agent’s nodes are the states $`s`$, its edges are the actions $`a`$, the
chance nodes implement $`\mathcal{T}(\cdot\,|\,s,a)`$, and the payoff at
a leaf is the discounted sum of the rewards collected along the path.
The state is therefore not primitive from the game-theoretic side; it is
the label of an equivalence class of histories that a game theorist
would write as an information set, and
[Proposition 11.6](#S11.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 11.6 (Information sets are action-observation histories). ‣ 11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
makes that identification exact. The discount $`\gamma`$ plays the role
that patience plays in
[Section 6](#S6 "6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
and $`b_{0}`$ is the initial chance move. Everything in this monograph
is obtained from this tuple by adding agents, removing observability, or
both.

A stationary policy is a map $`\pi:\mathcal{S}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A})`$,
and its value function $`V^{\pi}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}`$ is the
expected discounted return
$`V^{\pi}(s)=\mathbb{E}\big[\sum_{t\geq 0}\gamma^{t}\mathcal{R}(s_{t},a_{t},s_{t+1})\,|\,s_{0}=s\big]`$
under the process generated by $`\pi`$ and $`\mathcal{T}`$. The
action-value function is
$`Q^{\pi}(s,a)=\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{T}(\cdot\,|\,s,a)}[\mathcal{R}(s,a,s^{\prime})+\gamma V^{\pi}(s^{\prime})]`$.
The entire theory of the single-agent case rests on the observation that
the optimality operator

|     |                                                                                                           |                                                                     |     |
| --- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle(\mathbb{T}V)(s)\;=\;\max*{a\in\mathcal{A}}\;\mathbb{E}*{s^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{T}(\cdot\, | \,s,a)}\Big[\mathcal{R}(s,a,s^{\prime})+\gamma V(s^{\prime})\Big]`$ |     | (1) |

is a $`\gamma`$-contraction in the supremum norm on
$`\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{S}}`$, so that by the Banach fixed point theorem
it has a unique fixed point $`V^{\star}`$, and that a policy greedy with
respect to $`V^{\star}`$ attains it. Two structural facts that we will
invoke repeatedly follow. The first is that the maximisation in
[Equation 1](#S2.E1 "In 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
decouples across states, which is why dynamic programming works at all.
The second is that the maximum of a linear function over
$`\Delta(\mathcal{A})`$ is attained at a vertex, which is why
deterministic policies suffice in the single-agent case. Both facts fail
in the multi-agent setting, the first because the reward of agent $`i`$
depends on the actions of others and therefore the per-state problems
are coupled through the other agents’ policies, and the second because
equilibria of general games require mixing. Nearly every difficulty
catalogued in this monograph is a consequence of one of these two
failures.

The smallest example that exhibits every ingredient is worth carrying
through the rest of the work, so we fix it now and return to it whenever
a multi-agent construction needs a one-agent shadow.

###### Example 2.2 (The slippery corridor).

Let $`\mathcal{S}=\{L,M,R\}`$ be three cells of a corridor, let
$`\mathcal{A}=\{\mathrm{left},\mathrm{right}\}`$, and let the agent’s
step succeed with probability $`0.8`$ and leave it where it is with
probability $`0.2`$. The cell $`R`$ is absorbing. A reward of $`1`$ is
collected on the transition that enters $`R`$ and nothing is collected
anywhere else, and $`\gamma=0.9`$. Writing the Bellman equation at $`M`$
under the action $`\mathrm{right}`$,

|     |                                                                                                                                              |     |
| --- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle V^{\star}(M)\;=\;0.8\,\big(1+\gamma V^{\star}(R)\big)\;+\;0.2\,\big(0+\gamma V^{\star}(M)\big)\;=\;0.8+0.18\,V^{\star}(M),`$ |     |

because $`V^{\star}(R)=0`$, which gives
$`V^{\star}(M)=0.8/0.82=0.9756`$. The same computation at $`L`$ gives
$`V^{\star}(L)=0.8\cdot 0.9\cdot 0.9756+0.18\,V^{\star}(L)=0.8566`$, and
the greedy policy moves right everywhere. Three features of this
calculation recur throughout the monograph in disguised form. The
equation at $`M`$ refers to $`V^{\star}(M)`$ on both sides, which is the
self-reference that a contraction resolves; the equation at $`L`$ refers
only to $`V^{\star}(M)`$ and $`V^{\star}(L)`$, which is the locality
that makes dynamic programming cheap; and the maximisation over the two
actions at $`L`$ can be performed without knowing what happens at $`M`$
beyond its value, which is the decoupling across states that
[Equation 4](#S3.E4 "In Definition 3.6 (Induced stage game). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
will destroy. $`\lozenge`$

###### Definition 2.3 (Partially observable Markov decision process).

A partially observable Markov decision process is a tuple
$`P=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{R},\gamma,b_{0})`$
in which
$`(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{R},\gamma,b_{0})`$ is as
in
[Definition 2.1](#S2.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 2.1 (Markov decision process). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
$`\mathcal{O}`$ is a finite set of observations and
$`\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\Delta(\mathcal{O})`$ is an
observation kernel, with $`\mathcal{Z}(o\,|\,s^{\prime},a)`$ the
probability of observing $`o`$ after taking $`a`$ and arriving at
$`s^{\prime}`$.

 

The game-theoretic reading. Adding $`\mathcal{Z}`$ to
[Definition 2.1](#S2.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 2.1 (Markov decision process). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is what a game theorist calls coarsening the information partition. In
the unrolled tree, two nodes that the agent could previously tell apart
now lie in the same information set whenever they share an
action-observation history, so the policy must assign one distribution
to the whole class. The action-observation history $`h_{t}`$ is
therefore the information set, the belief $`b_{t}`$ is the distribution
over nodes within it that a sequential equilibrium requires in
[Section 11](#S11 "11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
and the requirement that $`b_{t}`$ be the Bayes posterior under the
policy being evaluated is the consistency condition of that solution
concept. The one-agent case is the easy case: because nobody else is
conditioning on anything, the belief is a sufficient statistic and
[Theorem 2.5](#S2.Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 2.5 (Belief-MDP reduction; belief sufficiency and the functional equation, , ; piecewise linearity and convexity, , ). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
applies.
[Proposition 8.4](#S8.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition 8.4 (Inclusions and degeneracies). ‣ 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
records that with two agents it is not.

Because the state is hidden, a policy must be defined on what the agent
has seen. The action-observation history at time $`t`$ is
$`h_{t}=(o_{0},a_{0},o_{1},a_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},o_{t})\in\mathcal{H}_{t}`$,
and a general policy is a map $`\pi:\mathcal{H}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A})`$
where $`\mathcal{H}=\bigcup_{t}\mathcal{H}_{t}`$. Histories grow without
bound, and the standard remedy is to replace the history by a sufficient
statistic. The belief state is the posterior over the hidden state given
the history,

|     |                                                   |                                                                      |                                                                   |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------- | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle b*{t}(s)\;=\;\mathbb{P}(s*{t}=s\, | \,h*{t}),\qquad b*{t+1}(s^{\prime})\;\propto\;\mathcal{Z}(o\_{t+1}\, | \,s^{\prime},a*{t})\sum*{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{T}(s^{\prime}\, | \,s,a*{t})\,b*{t}(s),`$ |     | (2) |

the second expression being the Bayes filter that propagates it. The
belief state is a sufficient statistic for the history in the sense that
$`\mathbb{P}(s_{t}\,|\,h_{t})=\mathbb{P}(s_{t}\,|\,b_{t})`$ and the
optimal value is a function of $`b_{t}`$ alone, which yields the first
of the reductions we shall be cataloguing.

###### Example 2.4 (Two ticks of a Bayes filter).

A sensor reports which of two rooms contains an object. The prior is
uniform, $`b_{0}=(0.5,0.5)`$, the object does not move, and the sensor
is correct with probability $`0.85`$. After one reading of “left”,
[Equation 2](#S2.E2 "In 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
gives

|     |                                                                                                           |     |
| --- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle b_{1}(\mathrm{left})\;=\;\frac{0.85\times 0.5}{0.85\times 0.5+0.15\times 0.5}\;=\;0.85.`$ |     |

After a second reading of “left” the same formula applied to $`b_{1}`$
gives $`b_{2}(\mathrm{left})=0.85^{2}/(0.85^{2}+0.15^{2})=0.9698`$. The
arithmetic is elementary and the structural point is not: the pair
$`(0.85,0.15)`$ is all the agent needs to carry forward, so a recurrent
network that learns to maintain two numbers instead of the whole
observation history is not a heuristic compression but an exact one. The
multi-agent reductions of
[Section 9](#S9 "9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
are attempts to find the analogous two numbers when there is more than
one agent, and
[Proposition 8.4](#S8.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition 8.4 (Inclusions and degeneracies). ‣ 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
will explain why they are much larger objects. $`\lozenge`$

###### Theorem 2.5 (Belief-MDP reduction; belief sufficiency and the functional equation, [Åström, 1965](#bib.bib9); piecewise linearity and convexity, [Smallwood and Sondik, 1973](#bib.bib165)).

Let $`P`$ be a partially observable Markov decision process with
$`\mathcal{S}`$, $`\mathcal{A}`$ and $`\mathcal{O}`$ finite and
$`\mathcal{R}`$ bounded. Define the belief Markov decision process
$`\tilde{M}=(\Delta(\mathcal{S}),\mathcal{A},\tilde{\mathcal{T}},\tilde{\mathcal{R}},\gamma,\delta_{b_{0}})`$
whose states are beliefs, whose transition kernel is induced by
[Equation 2](#S2.E2 "In 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
together with the observation likelihood
$`\mathbb{P}(o\,|\,b,a)=\sum_{s^{\prime}}\mathcal{Z}(o\,|\,s^{\prime},a)\sum_{s}\mathcal{T}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a)b(s)`$,
and whose reward is
$`\tilde{\mathcal{R}}(b,a)=\sum_{s,s^{\prime}}b(s)\mathcal{T}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a)\mathcal{R}(s,a,s^{\prime})`$.
Then $`\tilde{M}`$ is a Markov decision process with a continuous state
space, its optimal value function $`\tilde{V}^{\star}`$ satisfies
$`\tilde{V}^{\star}(b_{t})=V^{\star}(h_{t})`$ for every history
$`h_{t}`$ with belief $`b_{t}`$, and an optimal stationary policy of
$`\tilde{M}`$ induces an optimal history-dependent policy of $`P`$.
Moreover, at every finite horizon $`\tilde{V}^{\star}`$ is the maximum
of a finite set of linear functions of $`b`$, hence piecewise linear and
convex.

[Theorem 2.5](#S2.Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 2.5 (Belief-MDP reduction; belief sufficiency and the functional equation, , ; piecewise linearity and convexity, , ). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the template for most of this monograph. A model that appears to lack
the Markov property with respect to its natural state variable is shown
to possess it with respect to a statistic of the history, at the cost of
enlarging the state space from a finite set to a simplex. The
multi-agent reductions in
[Section 9](#S9 "9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
have exactly this shape, with the belief replaced by an occupancy state
or a public belief state and the action replaced by a decision rule or a
prescription. The price is also the same: the enlarged problem is a
Markov decision process, so dynamic programming applies in principle,
and the state space is a continuum, so dynamic programming is not
immediately executable.

Two further single-agent objects are needed. The first is the robust
Markov decision process, in which the transition kernel is only known to
lie in an uncertainty set
$`\mathcal{P}(s,a)\subseteq\Delta(\mathcal{S})`$ and the agent maximises
the worst-case return,

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                                    |     |     |
| --- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle(\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{rob}}V)(s)\;=\;\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\;\min_{p\in\mathcal{P}(s,a)}\;\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim p}\big[\mathcal{R}(s,a,s^{\prime})+\gamma V(s^{\prime})\big].`$ |     | (3) |

Under rectangularity of $`\mathcal{P}`$, meaning that the uncertainty
sets at distinct state-action pairs are independent, the operator in
[Equation 3](#S2.E3 "In 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is again a $`\gamma`$-contraction and the theory goes through ([Iyengar,
2005](#bib.bib84); [Nilim and El Ghaoui, 2005](#bib.bib130)). We will
see in
[Section 7](#S7 "7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
that
[Equation 3](#S2.E3 "In 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is literally the Bellman operator of a two-player zero-sum stochastic
game in which the adversary chooses the transition distribution, which
is the cleanest example in this work of two fields deriving the same
operator for unrelated reasons.

###### Example 2.6 (The robust and the nominal choice disagree).

An agent chooses between two actions at a state whose successors are
worth $`10`$ and $`0`$. Action $`a`$ reaches the good successor with a
probability that is only known to lie in $`[0.6,0.9]`$; action $`b`$
reaches it with probability exactly $`0.7`$. Under the midpoint estimate
$`0.75`$ the expected continuation of $`a`$ is $`7.5`$ and that of $`b`$
is $`7.0`$, so the nominal optimiser picks $`a`$; discounted at
$`\gamma=0.9`$ these are $`6.75`$ and $`6.3`$. Under
[Equation 3](#S2.E3 "In 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
the adversary sets the probability of $`a`$ to $`0.6`$, giving $`6.0`$
before discounting and $`5.4`$ after, against $`6.3`$ for $`b`$, so the
robust optimiser picks $`b`$. The two orderings are opposite, and no
amount of data about the average outcome of $`a`$ resolves the
disagreement, because the two criteria are answering different
questions. The reinforcement learning reading is that a conservative
agent and a risk-neutral agent trained on the same transitions will
disagree about the policy whenever the uncertainty is asymmetric, and
[Proposition 7.8](#S7.Thmtheorem8 "Proposition 7.8 (Robust control is a zero-sum stochastic game; , ). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
will identify the conservative agent as the maximiser in a two-player
zero-sum game. $`\lozenge`$

The second is the Bayes-adaptive Markov decision process, in which the
model parameters are themselves unknown and are given a prior. Deferring
the details to
[Section 12](#S12 "12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
we note here only that the construction is an instance of
[Theorem 2.5](#S2.Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 2.5 (Belief-MDP reduction; belief sufficiency and the functional equation, , ; piecewise linearity and convexity, , ). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
applied to a partially observable Markov decision process whose hidden
state is the pair consisting of the physical state and the unknown
parameter, and that this is the exact counterpart of the Harsanyi
transformation of a game of incomplete information.

| Single-agent object           | Multi-agent generalisation                                        | Developed in                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| ----------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Action $`a\in\mathcal{A}`$    | Joint action $`a=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in\prod_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}`$ | [Section 3](#S3 "3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")                                    |
| Policy $`\pi`$                | Policy profile $`\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{n})`$                    | [Section 3](#S3 "3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")                                    |
| Reward $`\mathcal{R}`$        | Reward profile $`(\mathcal{R}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{R}_{n})`$        | [Section 7](#S7 "7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")                                |
| Greedy improvement            | Best-response correspondence $`\mathrm{BR}_{i}`$                  | [Section 4](#S4 "4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")                |
| Fixed point of $`\mathbb{T}`$ | Nash equilibrium of the induced game                              | [Section 4](#S4 "4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")                |
| Dominated action              | Strictly dominated strategy; rationalisability                    | [Section 5](#S5 "5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")                      |
| Softmax policy                | Quantal response equilibrium                                      | [Section 5](#S5 "5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")                      |
| Principle of optimality       | Subgame perfection, Markov perfection                             | [Section 4](#S4 "4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")                |
| Deterministic optimal policy  | Mixed and behavioural strategies                                  | [Section 11](#S11 "11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")  |
| Belief state $`b_{t}`$        | Occupancy state, public belief state                              | [Section 9](#S9 "9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")               |
| Robust Bellman operator       | Minimax operator of a zero-sum game                               | [Section 7](#S7 "7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")                                |
| Bayes-adaptive MDP            | Harsanyi transformation, Bayes-Nash equilibrium                   | [Section 12](#S12 "12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")       |
| Factored MDP                  | Graphical game, coordination graph                                | [Section 10](#S10 "10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning") |
| Reward shaping                | Potential function, difference rewards                            | [Section 16](#S16 "16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")    |
| Reward design                 | Mechanism design; incentive compatibility                         | [Section 15](#S15 "15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")                     |
| Credit over time (TD)         | Credit over agents (Shapley value)                                | [Section 16](#S16 "16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")    |
| Return decomposition          | Core, nucleolus, bargaining solution                              | [Section 17](#S17 "17 Coalitions, stability of credit, and bargaining ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")                          |
| Gradient ascent               | Learning dynamics; variational inequality                         | [Section 19](#S19 "19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")            |

Table 3: The single-agent vocabulary and the multi-agent terms that
generalise it. The third column names the section in which the
generalisation is developed.

## 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit

The normal-form game is what remains of a stochastic game when the state
space is a singleton and the horizon is one. Working out this limit
carefully is worthwhile because every solution concept in the subject is
defined first here and then lifted, and because the lifting is not
always the obvious one.

The two readings are easy to state here and are the ones most often
confused in practice. A game theorist sees a payoff tensor: a function
from joint actions to a vector of numbers, one per agent, together with
a question about which joint actions are consistent with rationality. A
reinforcement learning researcher sees a one-step environment: an
environment with a single state, a factored action space, and a vector
reward, together with a question about what a learning rule converges to
when every component of the action is chosen by a separate learner. The
payoff tensor and the one-step environment are the same data structure,
and the two questions are different. Keeping them apart is the
discipline this section asks for: the game-theoretic question is about
fixed points of a correspondence, and the reinforcement learning
question is about trajectories of a dynamical system, and they have
different answers even when the underlying tensor is identical.

| Game theory                       | Reinforcement learning                                  | How to read the pair                            |
| --------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------- |
| Agent, player $`i\in\mathcal{N}`$ | Agent, learner, policy head                             | One decision maker, one reward signal           |
| Action set $`\mathcal{A}_{i}`$    | Action space of agent $`i`$                             | The columns of this agent’s slice of the tensor |
| Joint action $`a\in\mathcal{A}`$  | Joint action; one environment step                      | One cell of the payoff tensor                   |
| Payoff $`u_{i}(a)`$               | Reward $`\mathcal{R}_{i}`$ for a one-step episode       | The number agent $`i`$ wants to be large        |
| Mixed strategy $`\pi_{i}`$        | Stochastic policy over $`\mathcal{A}_{i}`$              | A row of logits after a softmax                 |
| Strategy profile $`\pi`$          | Joint policy, product policy                            | What every agent does at once                   |
| Multilinearity of $`u_{i}`$       | Independence of the action samplers                     | Each agent draws its own action privately       |
| Payoff tensor                     | Reward model of the one-step environment                | The same array, read by the same indices        |
| Common payoff                     | Shared reward; team reward                              | One scalar for everybody                        |
| Zero-sum                          | Adversarial reward $`\mathcal{R}_{2}=-\mathcal{R}_{1}`$ | One agent’s gain is the other’s loss            |
| General sum                       | Mixed-motive reward                                     | Neither aligned nor opposed                     |
| Stage game induced at $`s`$       | One-step game with bootstrapped targets                 | Immediate reward plus discounted value          |

Table 4: Normal-form vocabulary read from both sides.

###### Definition 3.1 (Normal-form game).

A normal-form game is a tuple
$`G=(\mathcal{N},\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}},\{u_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}})`$
in which $`\mathcal{N}=\{1,\dots,n\}`$ is a finite set of agents,
$`\mathcal{A}_{i}`$ is the finite action set of agent $`i`$, and
$`u_{i}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}`$ is the payoff function of agent $`i`$
defined on the joint action set
$`\mathcal{A}=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{A}_{j}`$. A mixed strategy
of agent $`i`$ is $`\pi_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})`$, a profile is
$`\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{n})`$, and payoffs extend multilinearly,
$`u_{i}(\pi)=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\big(\prod_{j}\pi_{j}(a_{j})\big)\,u_{i}(a)`$.

 

The reinforcement learning
reading. [Definition 3.1](#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 3.1 (Normal-form game). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is an environment with one state, one step and a factored action space.
The agent set $`\mathcal{N}`$ is the set of policy heads,
$`\mathcal{A}_{i}`$ is the action space of head $`i`$, the joint action
$`a`$ is what the environment receives in one call to its step function,
and $`u_{i}`$ is the $`i`$-th component of a vector reward. The mixed
strategy $`\pi_{i}`$ is the stochastic policy of head $`i`$, and the
profile $`\pi`$ is what a practitioner calls the joint policy. The
multilinear extension $`u_{i}(\pi)`$ is the expected reward of that
joint policy, and it is multilinear for one reason only: each head
samples its own action from its own distribution without looking at the
others’ samples. That independence is a property of the sampling code,
not of the game, and relaxing it by drawing the whole joint action from
a single distribution over $`\mathcal{A}`$ is what
[Definition 4.5](#S4.Thmtheorem5 "Definition 4.5 (Correlated and coarse correlated equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
does. The whole of the difference between a Nash equilibrium and a
correlated equilibrium is the difference between $`n`$ calls to a random
number generator and one.

###### Example 3.2 (Evaluating a joint policy, term by term).

Take the two-agent game with action sets $`\{C,D\}`$ and payoffs

|     |                                  |     |
| --- | -------------------------------- | --- | --- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r | c   | c   | }\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil C\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil D\hfil\lx@intercol\\ |

\cline{2-3}\cr C&3,3&0,5\\
\cline{2-3}\cr D&5,0&1,1\\
\cline{2-3}\cr\end{array},`$ | |

which is the prisoner’s dilemma and which
[Example 3.4](#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Example 3.4 (The four games that this monograph keeps returning to). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
meets again alongside its three companions. Consider the joint policy in
which the row head cooperates with probability $`0.5`$ and the column
head with probability $`0.25`$. The four joint actions have
probabilities $`\pi_{1}(C)\pi_{2}(C)=0.125`$,
$`\pi_{1}(C)\pi_{2}(D)=0.375`$, $`\pi_{1}(D)\pi_{2}(C)=0.125`$ and
$`\pi_{1}(D)\pi_{2}(D)=0.375`$, summing to one. Weighting the row
agent’s payoffs by these gives

|     |                                                                                                 |     |
| --- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle u_{1}(\pi)\;=\;0.125\cdot 3+0.375\cdot 0+0.125\cdot 5+0.375\cdot 1\;=\;1.375,`$ |     |

and the same weights against the column agent’s payoffs give
$`u_{2}(\pi)=0.125\cdot 3+0.375\cdot 5+0.125\cdot 0+0.375\cdot 1=2.625`$.
Two observations are worth making. The first is that
$`u_{1}(\pi)=1.375`$ is a number neither agent can read off any single
cell of the matrix; it is an average over an outer product, which is
exactly what a critic trained on sampled joint actions estimates. The
second is that the computation is linear in $`\pi_{1}`$ for fixed
$`\pi_{2}`$, so the best response of
[Equation 5](#S4.E5 "In 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
maximises a linear function over a simplex and is attained at a vertex,
which is why best responses may always be taken pure even when
equilibria may not. $`\lozenge`$

The multilinearity of $`u_{i}`$ in the profile is the structural fact
that makes the theory work, and it is exactly the independence
assumption that each agent randomises privately. Relaxing it, by
allowing the joint distribution over $`\mathcal{A}`$ to be an arbitrary
element of $`\Delta(\mathcal{A})`$ rather than a product measure,
produces the correlated equilibrium of
[Section 4](#S4 "4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
and the relaxation is what converts an intractable fixed-point problem
into a linear program.

Three reward structures recur and we name them once.

###### Definition 3.3 (Reward structures).

A normal-form game is common-payoff, also called identical-interest or a
team game, when $`u_{i}=u_{j}`$ for all $`i,j`$. It is constant-sum when
$`\sum_{i}u_{i}(a)=c`$ for all $`a\in\mathcal{A}`$ and some constant
$`c`$, and zero-sum when $`c=0`$; with $`n=2`$ this means
$`u_{2}=-u_{1}`$. It is general-sum when no restriction is imposed.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. These are the three shapes the
reward signal can take, and the choice among them is the single most
consequential line in an environment specification. Common payoff means
one scalar reward broadcast to every agent, which is the setting of
cooperative benchmarks and the only one in which the word “optimal” is
unambiguous. Zero-sum with two agents means
$`\mathcal{R}_{2}=-\mathcal{R}_{1}`$, so the environment returns one
number and negates it for the opponent, which is the setting of
self-play board and card games. General sum means a reward vector with
no relation among its components, which is the setting of every social
dilemma, every market and every system whose agents have separate
owners. Nothing else about a multi-agent problem determines as much:
[Table 12](#S7.T12 "In 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is organised entirely by this trichotomy, and the first of the five
questions in
[Section 22](#S22 "22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
asks for it.

The three structures are best met at once, in their smallest instances,
because almost every claim in the rest of this work is first checked
against one of them. We give them here and refer back to them by name
throughout.

###### Example 3.4 (The four games that this monograph keeps returning to).

Each matrix lists the row agent’s payoff first. The prisoner’s dilemma,
in which defection strictly dominates cooperation for both agents and
the unique equilibrium $`(D,D)`$ is worse for both than $`(C,C)`$,

|     |                                  |     |
| --- | -------------------------------- | --- | --- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r | c   | c   | }\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil C\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil D\hfil\lx@intercol\\ |

\cline{2-3}\cr C&3,3&0,5\\
\cline{2-3}\cr D&5,0&1,1\\
\cline{2-3}\cr\end{array}`$ | |

is the canonical general-sum game and the canonical social dilemma.
Matching pennies, in which the agents want to match and to mismatch
respectively,

|     |                                  |     |
| --- | -------------------------------- | --- | --- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r | c   | c   | }\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil H\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil T\hfil\lx@intercol\\ |

\cline{2-3}\cr H&1,-1&-1,1\\
\cline{2-3}\cr T&-1,1&1,-1\\
\cline{2-3}\cr\end{array}`$ | |

is the canonical zero-sum game and the smallest game with no pure
equilibrium. The stag hunt, in which both agents prefer to hunt the stag
together but hunting it alone is the worst outcome,

|     |                                  |     |
| --- | -------------------------------- | --- | --- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r | c   | c   | }\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil\mathrm{Stag}\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil\mathrm{Hare}\hfil\lx@intercol\\ |

\cline{2-3}\cr\mathrm{Stag}&4,4&1,3\\
\cline{2-3}\cr\mathrm{Hare}&3,1&3,3\\
\cline{2-3}\cr\end{array}`$ | |

is the canonical coordination game with a conflict between payoff
dominance and risk dominance, and we return to it in
[Example 19.6](#S19.Thmtheorem6 "Example 19.6 (Which equilibrium of the stag hunt survives rare mistakes). ‣ 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The game of chicken, in which each agent prefers to be the one who does
not yield,

|     |                                  |     |
| --- | -------------------------------- | --- | --- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r | c   | c   | }\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil\mathrm{Swerve}\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil\mathrm{Straight}\hfil\lx@intercol\\ |

\cline{2-3}\cr\mathrm{Swerve}&6,6&2,7\\
\cline{2-3}\cr\mathrm{Straight}&7,2&0,0\\
\cline{2-3}\cr\end{array}`$ | |

is the canonical anti-coordination game and the one on which correlation
pays, as
[Example 4.6](#S4.Thmtheorem6 "Example 4.6 (Correlation that beats every equilibrium of chicken). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
computes. Read as reinforcement learning environments all four are
one-state, one-step, two-agent problems with a two-dimensional factored
action space and a two-component reward; read as games they are the four
qualitatively distinct two-by-two strategic situations. Nothing in the
multi-agent difficulty requires more than this. $`\lozenge`$

The single-agent degeneracy of
[Definition 3.1](#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 3.1 (Normal-form game). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is immediate and instructive. With $`n=1`$ a normal-form game is a
function $`u_{1}:\mathcal{A}_{1}\to\mathbb{R}`$ and the problem of
playing it well is the problem of computing $`\argmax_{a}u_{1}(a)`$. If
$`u_{1}`$ is accessible only through sampling, by playing an action and
observing a noisy payoff, the problem is a multi-armed bandit, and if
the sampling is preceded by an observation drawn from a distribution the
problem is a contextual bandit. The three objects, one-shot
optimisation, bandit and contextual bandit, are therefore the one-agent
shadows of the normal-form game, the repeated normal-form game under
bandit feedback, and the repeated normal-form game with a public state.
We record this in
[Table 5](#S3.T5 "In 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
together with the corresponding statement for each reward structure,
where the point of interest is that the single-agent degeneracy is the
same object in all three rows: with one agent there is no distinction
between cooperation and competition.

###### Example 3.5 (What the prisoner’s dilemma becomes when one agent is deleted).

Delete the column agent from
[Example 3.4](#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Example 3.4 (The four games that this monograph keeps returning to). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
by freezing it at $`C`$. The row agent then faces the vector
$`(u_{1}(C,C),u_{1}(D,C))=(3,5)`$, which is a two-armed bandit whose
better arm is $`D`$. Freeze it at $`D`$ instead and the vector is
$`(0,1)`$, whose better arm is again $`D`$. The two bandits have the
same argmax, which is precisely what strict dominance means, and this is
why the prisoner’s dilemma is solvable without any theory of
equilibrium: it is a game in which both one-agent shadows point the same
way for each agent. The stag hunt is not: freezing the column agent at
Stag gives the row agent $`(4,3)`$ with argmax Stag, and freezing it at
Hare gives $`(1,3)`$ with argmax Hare. There the shadow depends on what
the other agent does, and that dependence is the whole of the
multi-agent problem. $`\lozenge`$

| Model                      | Full payoff feedback                           | Bandit feedback                                   | With public state                       |
| -------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------- |
| $`n=1`$                    | Known objective; one-shot maximisation         | Multi-armed bandit                                | Contextual bandit                       |
| $`n\geq 2`$, common payoff | Team coordination problem                      | Cooperative bandit; distributed optimisation      | Team Markov game with one state         |
| $`n=2`$, zero-sum          | Matrix game; value by linear programming       | Adversarial bandit; Exp3 and regret matching      | Zero-sum game with public state         |
| $`n\geq 2`$, general sum   | Normal-form game; equilibrium is PPAD-complete | No-regret learning converging to $`\mathrm{CCE}`$ | Stateless general-sum game with context |

Table 5: The normal-form game and its single-agent degeneracies.
Feedback columns indicate what the agent observes after a round.

The lifting of a normal-form game into a sequential setting admits two
distinct constructions that are frequently conflated, and distinguishing
them is the main point of this section. The first is the repeated game,
treated in
[Section 6](#S6 "6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
in which the same normal-form game is played at every step and the
agents condition on the history of play. The second is the stage game
induced by a stochastic game at a state, treated in
[Section 7](#S7 "7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
in which the payoff entries are the immediate rewards plus the
discounted continuation values. The second construction is the one that
makes dynamic programming work in the multi-agent case, and the
continuation values are what distinguishes it from the first.

###### Definition 3.6 (Induced stage game).

Let $`\{V_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}}`$ be a profile of value functions
$`V_{i}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}`$. The stage game induced at state
$`s`$ is the normal-form game $`G_{s}[V]`$ with agent set
$`\mathcal{N}`$, action sets $`\mathcal{A}_{i}`$, and payoffs

|     |                                                                                  |                                                                                                     |     |
| --- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle u^{\,s}_{i}(a)\;=\;\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{T}(\cdot\, | \,s,a)}\Big[\mathcal{R}_{i}(s,a,s^{\prime})+\gamma V_{i}(s^{\prime})\Big],\qquad a\in\mathcal{A}.`$ |     | (4) |

 

The reinforcement learning
reading. [Equation 4](#S3.E4 "In Definition 3.6 (Induced stage game). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the bootstrapped target of a centralised critic, restricted to a
single state. A practitioner who trains $`Q_{i}(s,a_{1},\dots,a_{n})`$
and freezes $`s`$ is holding a payoff tensor in memory, one entry per
joint action, one tensor per agent, and $`u^{\,s}_{i}(a)`$ is exactly
that entry. The entries are not environment rewards: each is an
immediate reward plus a discounted continuation, so the tensor changes
at every sweep of value iteration and the game it defines is a different
game at every iteration. This is the object on which a multi-agent
Bellman backup must act, and the question the rest of this monograph
keeps returning to is what operator should replace the $`\max`$ of
[Equation 1](#S2.E1 "In 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
when the thing being backed up is a tensor rather than a vector.

Comparing
[Equation 4](#S3.E4 "In Definition 3.6 (Induced stage game). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
with
[Equation 1](#S2.E1 "In 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
isolates the whole difficulty of the multi-agent extension of dynamic
programming. In the single-agent case the right-hand side of
[Equation 4](#S3.E4 "In Definition 3.6 (Induced stage game). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is a vector indexed by $`a`$ and the Bellman update takes its maximum.
In the multi-agent case it is a payoff tensor and the Bellman update
must take some solution of the game it defines, a value operator that we
write $`\mathrm{sol}_{i}(G_{s}[V])`$. Which operator is appropriate,
whether it is single-valued, whether it is non-expansive and whether its
fixed point is the equilibrium one wants are the questions that
[Sections 7](#S7 "7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and [23](#S23 "23 Correspondences that fail, and the theorems that forbid them ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
answer, and the answers are affirmative for zero-sum games, affirmative
for common-payoff games under a coordination assumption, and negative in
general.

###### Example 3.7 (A stage game built from bootstrapped values).

Take two agents at a state $`s`$ with
$`\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathcal{A}_{2}=\{0,1\}`$, deterministic transitions
that send the joint action $`(0,0)`$ to a state worth
$`(V_{1},V_{2})=(10,0)`$ and every other joint action to a state worth
$`(0,10)`$, immediate rewards all zero, and $`\gamma=0.9`$. Then
