[Equation 4](#S3.E4 "In Definition 3.6 (Induced stage game). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
gives the stage game

|     |                                  |     |
| --- | -------------------------------- | --- | --- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r | c   | c   | }\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil 0\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil 1\hfil\lx@intercol\\ |

\cline{2-3}\cr 0&9,0&0,9\\
\cline{2-3}\cr 1&0,9&0,9\\
\cline{2-3}\cr\end{array},`$ | |

in which agent $`1`$ wants the joint action $`(0,0)`$ and agent $`2`$
wants anything else. The entries are not rewards of the environment;
they are rewards plus bootstrapped continuations, and they change at
every sweep of value iteration. A reinforcement learning practitioner
meets this object without naming it every time a critic conditioned on
the joint action is trained, because the cross-section of that critic at
a fixed state is exactly a payoff tensor. The operator that the Bellman
update must apply to this tensor is the choice that
[Table 12](#S7.T12 "In 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
tabulates. $`\lozenge`$

A last remark concerns the status of randomisation. In a single-agent
Markov decision process the optimal policy may be taken deterministic
because the Bellman optimality operator maximises a linear function over
a simplex. In a normal-form game the analogous statement is false: the
matching-pennies game has no pure Nash equilibrium, and [Nash
(1950b)](#bib.bib127) guarantees existence only in mixed strategies.
This is the first place at which the single-agent intuition breaks, and
it propagates. Stochastic games require stationary mixed policies,
decentralised partially observable Markov decision processes admit
deterministic optimal joint policies for the finite-horizon planning
problem but not for the independent-learning problem, and extensive-form
games require behavioural strategies whose equivalence to mixed
strategies holds only under perfect recall, which is the content of
[Theorem 11.4](#S11.Thmtheorem4 "Theorem 11.4 ( , ). ‣ 11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
below.

###### Example 3.8 (Why matching pennies has no deterministic solution).

Enumerate the four joint actions of matching pennies. At $`(H,H)`$ the
column agent gains by switching to $`T`$; at $`(H,T)`$ the row agent
gains by switching to $`T`$; at $`(T,T)`$ the column agent gains by
switching to $`H`$; at $`(T,H)`$ the row agent gains by switching to
$`H`$. The better-reply graph is a four-cycle with no sink, so no pure
profile is a fixed point of the best-response correspondence. The unique
equilibrium is $`\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}=(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})`$ with
value $`0`$, which is what the linear program of
[Theorem 7.6](#S7.Thmtheorem6 "Theorem 7.6 ( , ). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
returns. The practical consequence for a reinforcement learning engineer
is specific and is usually discovered the hard way: an algorithm whose
policy class is deterministic, such as a greedy $`Q`$-learner or a
deterministic policy gradient method, cannot represent the solution of
this game, and what it will do instead is cycle through the four-cycle
above for as long as it is trained. A flat learning curve in such a run
is evidence of a limit cycle rather than of convergence, which is the
diagnostic point made again in
[Section 20](#S20 "20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
$`\lozenge`$

## 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to

Reinforcement learning has one notion of a solution, the optimal policy,
and it is characterised in two equivalent ways, as a maximiser of the
value and as a fixed point of greedy improvement. Game theory has many
notions of a solution, and almost all of them are obtained by taking one
of those two characterisations and weakening it so that it can
accommodate several agents with different objectives. Organising the
solution concepts this way makes the correspondence with reinforcement
learning mechanical rather than mnemonic.

The two readings diverge most sharply here, and the divergence is worth
naming before the definitions. A game theorist regards a solution
concept as a prediction: a set of profiles at which a story about
rational agents is internally consistent, with no claim that anyone will
arrive there. A reinforcement learning researcher regards a solution
concept as a target: the thing an algorithm is supposed to converge to,
with the implicit claim that convergence is the measure of success. The
gap between a consistency condition and a convergence target is the
source of most of the confusion in the area, and it is why
[Section 23](#S23 "23 Correspondences that fail, and the theorems that forbid them ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is necessary. A Nash equilibrium is a perfectly good consistency
condition and a bad convergence target; a coarse correlated equilibrium
is a weaker consistency condition and a good convergence target. Neither
statement contradicts the other.

| Game theory                                         | Reinforcement learning                             | How to read the pair                            |
| --------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------- |
| Best response $`\mathrm{BR}_{i}(\pi_{-i})`$         | Greedy policy improvement; $`\argmax_{a}Q_{i}`$    | Do the best you can, others held fixed          |
| Nash equilibrium                                    | Fixed point of simultaneous improvement            | Nobody gains by moving alone                    |
| $`\varepsilon`$-equilibrium                         | $`\varepsilon`$-greedy improvement; approximate PI | Nobody gains more than $`\varepsilon`$ alone    |
| Mixed strategy                                      | Stochastic policy                                  | Randomisation as part of the solution           |
| Correlated profile $`\sigma\in\Delta(\mathcal{A})`$ | Policy conditioned on a shared random seed         | A joint distribution, not a product             |
| Mediator, recommendation                            | Shared signal, broadcast latent variable           | A common random bit that both read              |
| External regret                                     | Regret of a bandit algorithm                       | Loss against the best fixed action              |
| Swap regret                                         | Regret against action-dependent rewrites           | Loss against the best rewiring of your own play |
| Minmax value                                        | Robust return; worst-case value                    | What you can guarantee unilaterally             |
| Pareto frontier                                     | Non-dominated set in multi-objective RL            | Nobody improves without hurting somebody        |
| Utilitarian welfare                                 | Team reward $`\sum_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i}`$            | Add the rewards and maximise                    |
| Egalitarian welfare                                 | $`\min_{i}`$ return; fairness objective            | Maximise the worst-off agent                    |
| Price of anarchy                                    | Gap from independent learners to a planner         | What decentralisation costs                     |

Table 6: Solution-concept vocabulary read from both sides.

The elementary object is the best-response correspondence. For a
normal-form game and an agent $`i`$,

|     |                                                                                                                                                          |     |     |
| --- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\mathrm{BR}_{i}(\pi_{-i})\;=\;\argmax_{\pi_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}\;u_{i}(\pi_{i},\pi_{-i})\;\subseteq\;\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i}),`$ |     | (5) |

and $`\mathrm{BR}(\pi)=\prod_{i}\mathrm{BR}_{i}(\pi_{-i})`$ is the joint
correspondence. With $`n=1`$ the right-hand side of
[Equation 5](#S4.E5 "In 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the set of distributions supported on $`\argmax_{a}u_{1}(a)`$, so
best response is greedy action selection, and in the sequential case it
is the policy improvement step of policy iteration. Every solution
concept below is a statement about $`\mathrm{BR}`$ or about a relaxation
of it.

###### Definition 4.1 (Nash equilibrium).

A profile $`\pi^{\star}`$ is a Nash equilibrium when
$`\pi^{\star}_{i}\in\mathrm{BR}_{i}(\pi^{\star}_{-i})`$ for every
$`i\in\mathcal{N}`$, equivalently when
$`\pi^{\star}\in\mathrm{BR}(\pi^{\star})`$. It is an
$`\varepsilon`$-Nash equilibrium when
$`u_{i}(\pi^{\star})\geq u_{i}(\pi_{i},\pi^{\star}_{-i})-\varepsilon`$
for every $`i`$ and every $`\pi_{i}`$.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. A Nash equilibrium is a joint policy
at which running one more step of policy improvement on any single agent
changes nothing. Spell this out, because it is the identity on which the
rest of the dictionary rests. Freeze the policies $`\pi^{\star}_{-i}`$
of every agent but $`i`$. By
[Proposition 7.11](#S7.Thmtheorem11 "Proposition 7.11 (Fixed others induce a Markov decision process). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
agent $`i`$ then faces an ordinary Markov decision process, so it has an
ordinary action-value function
$`Q_{i}(a_{i})=u_{i}(a_{i},\pi^{\star}_{-i})`$ and an ordinary greedy
policy. The condition
$`\pi^{\star}_{i}\in\mathrm{BR}_{i}(\pi^{\star}_{-i})`$ says that
$`\pi^{\star}_{i}`$ is already greedy with respect to that $`Q_{i}`$. A
Nash equilibrium is therefore a profile at which $`n`$ simultaneous
policy-iteration loops have all stopped at once, and the symbol
dictionary is $`\pi^{\star}_{i}\leftrightarrow`$ the policy of head
$`i`$, $`\mathrm{BR}_{i}\leftrightarrow\argmax_{a_{i}}Q_{i}`$, and
$`\pi^{\star}\in\mathrm{BR}(\pi^{\star})\leftrightarrow`$ no head’s
greedy step moves. The $`\varepsilon`$ version is the stopping criterion
of approximate policy iteration applied per agent: $`\varepsilon`$ is
the largest improvement any single head could still make, which is the
quantity that
[Table 39](#S22.T39 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
calls NashConv when summed over agents and exploitability when there are
two agents and the game is zero-sum. What does not carry over from the
single-agent case is uniqueness: a policy-iteration loop on one agent
has one stopping point, and $`n`$ coupled loops may have many, with
different returns.

###### Example 4.2 (Finding the equilibria by checking the fixed-point condition).

The definition is a condition to be checked, so check it. In the
prisoner’s dilemma, fix the column agent at $`C`$; the row agent’s two
values are $`u_{1}(C,C)=3`$ and $`u_{1}(D,C)=5`$, so
$`\mathrm{BR}_{1}(C)=\{D\}`$. Fix the column agent at $`D`$; the values
are $`0`$ and $`1`$, so $`\mathrm{BR}_{1}(D)=\{D\}`$ again. The row
agent’s best response is $`D`$ whatever the column agent does, and by
symmetry the same holds for the column agent, so the joint
correspondence $`\mathrm{BR}`$ is the constant map sending every profile
to $`(D,D)`$. A constant map has exactly one fixed point, so $`(D,D)`$
is the unique Nash equilibrium, with payoffs $`(1,1)`$. Notice what the
check consisted of: two $`\argmax`$ computations over a two-element
$`Q`$-vector, per agent. That is all a Nash equilibrium ever is.

How far is the good outcome from being one? At $`(C,C)`$ each agent
earns $`3`$ and the best unilateral deviation earns $`5`$, so the
largest improvement available to any single agent is $`2`$. By
[Definition 4.1](#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 4.1 (Nash equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
$`(C,C)`$ is an $`\varepsilon`$-Nash equilibrium exactly for
$`\varepsilon\geq 2`$, and the number $`2`$ is the exploitability that a
diagnostic would report for a pair of cooperating policies.

Now matching pennies. At $`(H,H)`$ the column agent’s values are $`-1`$
for $`H`$ and $`+1`$ for $`T`$, so it deviates; the same argument at the
other three cells shows that no pure profile is a fixed point, so the
game has no pure equilibrium. Solving the indifference conditions gives
the unique equilibrium $`\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}=(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})`$
with value $`0`$, which a linear program confirms. The contrast with the
prisoner’s dilemma is the one to keep: in the first game the fixed-point
condition is satisfied by a deterministic policy that greedy
$`Q`$-learning can represent, and in the second it is not, so the same
algorithm run on the two games succeeds in one and cycles forever in the
other for reasons that have nothing to do with its hyperparameters.
$`\lozenge`$

A Nash equilibrium is therefore a fixed point of the joint best-response
correspondence, and [Nash (1950b)](#bib.bib127) establishes existence in
every finite game by applying Kakutani’s theorem to $`\mathrm{BR}`$,
which is non-empty, convex-valued and upper hemicontinuous. The
single-agent degeneracy is exact and is worth stating as a proposition
because it is the hinge of the whole dictionary.

###### Proposition 4.3 (Nash equilibrium degenerates to Bellman optimality).

Let $`G`$ be a normal-form game with $`n=1`$. Then $`\pi^{\star}`$ is a
Nash equilibrium of $`G`$ if and only if
$`\supp(\pi^{\star})\subseteq\argmax_{a}u_{1}(a)`$. Let $`G`$ be a
stochastic game with $`n=1`$, which by
[Proposition 7.3](#S7.Thmtheorem3 "Proposition 7.3 (Degeneracies of the stochastic game). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is a Markov decision process $`M`$. Then a stationary profile
$`\pi^{\star}`$ is a Markov perfect equilibrium of $`G`$ if and only if
$`\pi^{\star}`$ is an optimal policy of $`M`$, equivalently if and only
if $`V^{\pi^{\star}}`$ is the fixed point of the operator $`\mathbb{T}`$
in
[Equation 1](#S2.E1 "In 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").

###### Proof.

For $`n=1`$ the correspondence $`\mathrm{BR}_{1}`$ in
[Equation 5](#S4.E5 "In 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
does not depend on its argument, since there is no $`\pi_{-1}`$, so the
fixed point condition $`\pi^{\star}_{1}\in\mathrm{BR}_{1}(\cdot)`$
reduces to $`\pi^{\star}_{1}\in\argmax_{\pi_{1}}u_{1}(\pi_{1})`$, and
since $`u_{1}`$ is linear on the simplex its maximisers are exactly the
distributions supported on the maximisers of $`u_{1}`$ over
$`\mathcal{A}_{1}`$. For the sequential statement, Markov perfection
requires $`\pi^{\star}_{1}(\cdot\,|\,s)`$ to be a best response at every
state $`s`$ against the continuation value induced by $`\pi^{\star}`$
itself, which by
[Definition 3.6](#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Definition 3.6 (Induced stage game). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the condition
$`\supp\pi^{\star}_{1}(\cdot\,|\,s)\subseteq\argmax_{a}\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}}[\mathcal{R}(s,a,s^{\prime})+\gamma V^{\pi^{\star}}(s^{\prime})]`$
for every $`s`$. That is simultaneously the greedy condition and the
statement $`V^{\pi^{\star}}=\mathbb{T}V^{\pi^{\star}}`$, and by
uniqueness of the fixed point of the $`\gamma`$-contraction
$`\mathbb{T}`$ it holds if and only if $`V^{\pi^{\star}}=V^{\star}`$. ∎

[Proposition 4.3](#S4.Thmtheorem3 "Proposition 4.3 (Nash equilibrium degenerates to Bellman optimality). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
explains both why Nash equilibrium looks like the right generalisation
of optimality and why it is a poor learning target. It is the right
generalisation in the sense that it is the unique weakening of “greedy
with respect to one’s own continuation value” that is well posed when
the continuation value depends on others. It is a poor learning target
because the properties that make the single-agent fixed point usable,
uniqueness and attainability by a contraction, are both lost: a finite
game may have exponentially many equilibria, they may have different
payoffs, and no best-response-like operator is a contraction in general.
We return to this in
[Section 23](#S23 "23 Correspondences that fail, and the theorems that forbid them ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").

###### Example 4.4 (Three equilibria, three payoff vectors, one game).

The battle of the sexes,

|     |                                  |     |
| --- | -------------------------------- | --- | --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r | c   | c   | }\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil\mathrm{Opera}\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil\mathrm{Football}\hfil\lx@intercol\\ |

\cline{2-3}\cr\mathrm{Opera}&2,1&0,0\\
\cline{2-3}\cr\mathrm{Football}&0,0&1,2\\
\cline{2-3}\cr\end{array},`$ | |

has two pure equilibria, $`(\mathrm{Opera},\mathrm{Opera})`$ with
payoffs $`(2,1)`$ and $`(\mathrm{Football},\mathrm{Football})`$ with
payoffs $`(1,2)`$, and one mixed equilibrium. To find the mixed one,
make each agent indifferent. If the row agent plays Opera with
probability $`p`$, the column agent’s payoffs are $`p`$ from Opera and
$`2(1-p)`$ from Football, equal when $`p=2/3`$. If the column agent
plays Opera with probability $`q`$, the row agent’s payoffs are $`2q`$
from Opera and $`1-q`$ from Football, equal when $`q=1/3`$. At that
profile each agent earns $`2/3`$, which is strictly worse for both than
either pure equilibrium, because with probability
$`2/3\cdot 2/3+1/3\cdot 1/3=5/9`$ the two miscoordinate and earn
nothing. The reinforcement learning lesson is that “the algorithm
converged to a Nash equilibrium” is compatible with a payoff of $`0.67`$
and with a payoff of $`2.0`$ in the same game, so it is not a
performance claim. The lesson is sharpened by
[Theorem 6.2](#S6.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 6.2 (Folk theorem, discounted case; , ). ‣ 6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
where the equilibrium set becomes a continuum. $`\lozenge`$

The relaxations of
[Definition 4.1](#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 4.1 (Nash equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
that reinforcement learning actually attains are obtained by replacing
the product measure $`\prod_{i}\pi_{i}`$ by a joint distribution. Let
$`\sigma\in\Delta(\mathcal{A})`$ be a correlated profile, to be read as
the output of a mediator who samples $`a\sim\sigma`$ and privately
recommends $`a_{i}`$ to agent $`i`$; the construction and the solution
concept it supports are due to [Aumann (1974)](#bib.bib10).

###### Definition 4.5 (Correlated and coarse correlated equilibrium).

A correlated profile $`\sigma\in\Delta(\mathcal{A})`$ is a correlated
equilibrium when for every agent $`i`$ and every deviation map
$`\phi_{i}:\mathcal{A}_{i}\to\mathcal{A}_{i}`$,

|     |                                                                                                                                     |     |     |
| --- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\sigma}\big[u_{i}(\phi_{i}(a_{i}),a_{-i})\big]\;\leq\;\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\sigma}\big[u_{i}(a)\big].`$ |     | (6) |

It is a coarse correlated equilibrium when the weaker condition holds in
which $`\phi_{i}`$ is restricted to constant maps, that is, when for
every $`i`$ and every $`a_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}`$,
$`\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\sigma}[u_{i}(a_{i}^{\prime},a_{-i})]\leq\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\sigma}[u_{i}(a)]`$.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. Implement $`\sigma`$ as follows.
Before the episode, draw one integer from a shared random seed that
every policy can read; let a lookup table map that integer to a joint
action; give each head only its own component. The head now conditions
on a signal that is correlated with what the others will do, which no
product policy can represent. The correlated-equilibrium condition
[Equation 6](#S4.E6 "In Definition 4.5 (Correlated and coarse correlated equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
says that each head, after reading its own component and updating its
posterior over the others’ components, still prefers to play what it was
told, so obedience is greedy with respect to the conditional
$`Q`$-values. The coarse condition says only that obeying beats
committing in advance to one fixed action, without reading the signal at
all.

The deviation map $`\phi_{i}`$ is the object with the least familiar
name and the most familiar content: it is a rewiring of the head’s own
output, a function that takes the action the head was about to emit and
returns a different one. The corresponding regret notion is swap regret,
and the restriction of $`\phi_{i}`$ to constant maps gives external
regret, which is the regret that every bandit and policy-gradient method
already measures. So the two solution concepts are exactly the two
regret notions a practitioner can log, and
[Example 4.7](#S4.Thmtheorem7 "Example 4.7 (A coarse correlated equilibrium that is not correlated). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
shows that logging the wrong one hides a real failure.

The difference between the two conditions is the difference between
committing to a deviation before seeing the recommendation and deciding
after seeing it, and it is exactly the difference between a learner that
must choose a fixed alternative action and one that may condition its
alternative on what it was about to do. We make both the inclusion and
its strictness concrete, because in two-by-two games the two concepts
coincide and the textbook examples therefore hide the distinction.

###### Example 4.6 (Correlation that beats every equilibrium of chicken).

In the game of chicken of
[Example 3.4](#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Example 3.4 (The four games that this monograph keeps returning to). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
the two pure equilibria give welfare $`7+2=9`$, and the mixed
equilibrium, in which each agent swerves with probability $`2/3`$, gives
each agent $`14/3=4.667`$ and welfare $`28/3=9.333`$. Now let a mediator
draw a joint action from

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                                |     |
| --- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\sigma(\mathrm{Swerve},\mathrm{Swerve})=\tfrac{1}{2},\quad\sigma(\mathrm{Swerve},\mathrm{Straight})=\tfrac{1}{4},\quad\sigma(\mathrm{Straight},\mathrm{Swerve})=\tfrac{1}{4},`$ |     |

and privately tell each agent its own component. Check
[Equation 6](#S4.E6 "In Definition 4.5 (Correlated and coarse correlated equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
for the row agent. Told to go straight, which happens with probability
$`1/4`$, it knows the other swerves and earns $`7`$, while swerving
would earn $`6`$, so it obeys. Told to swerve, which happens with
probability $`3/4`$, its posterior over the other’s action is
$`(2/3,1/3)`$ on swerve and straight, so obeying earns
$`\tfrac{2}{3}\cdot 6+\tfrac{1}{3}\cdot 2=14/3`$ while going straight
earns $`\tfrac{2}{3}\cdot 7+\tfrac{1}{3}\cdot 0=14/3`$, so it is
indifferent and obeys. The same holds by symmetry for the column agent,
so $`\sigma`$ is a correlated equilibrium, and its welfare is
$`\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot 12+\tfrac{1}{4}\cdot 9+\tfrac{1}{4}\cdot 9=10.5`$,
strictly greater than the welfare of every Nash equilibrium of the game.
A linear program over
[Equation 6](#S4.E6 "In Definition 4.5 (Correlated and coarse correlated equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
confirms that $`10.5`$ is the maximum achievable welfare over the whole
correlated-equilibrium polytope. In reinforcement learning terms the
mediator is a shared random seed broadcast to both policies before they
act, and the example is the precise statement of what that seed buys: a
welfare of $`10.5`$ instead of $`9.333`$, obtained with no change to the
reward function, no communication during play, and no departure from
unilateral rationality. It is also the precise statement of what it
costs, namely that the policies are no longer independently executable.
$`\lozenge`$

The inclusion
$`\mathrm{NE}(G)\subseteq\mathrm{CE}(G)\subseteq\mathrm{CCE}(G)`$ holds
in every game, and the first inclusion is an equality when restricted to
product measures: a product distribution satisfies
[Equation 6](#S4.E6 "In Definition 4.5 (Correlated and coarse correlated equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
if and only if it is a Nash equilibrium. Both $`\mathrm{CE}`$ and
$`\mathrm{CCE}`$ are defined by finitely many linear inequalities in
$`\sigma`$, hence are non-empty convex polytopes computable by linear
programming, which is the formal reason why the relaxation buys
tractability. The reinforcement learning significance is that these are
the objects that learning dynamics actually converge to. If every agent
runs an algorithm whose external regret

|     |                                                                                                                                                  |     |     |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\Regret_{i}(T)\;=\;\max_{a_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\big[u_{i}(a_{i}^{\prime},a^{t}_{-i})-u_{i}(a^{t})\big]`$ |     | (7) |

is sublinear in $`T`$, then the empirical distribution of joint play
$`\bar{\sigma}_{T}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t}\delta_{a^{t}}`$ converges to the
set of coarse correlated equilibria, which is immediate from
[Definition 4.5](#S4.Thmtheorem5 "Definition 4.5 (Correlated and coarse correlated equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
once the average regret vanishes. If the stronger swap regret is
sublinear the limit set is the set of correlated equilibria; regret
matching achieves this directly ([Hart and Mas-Colell,
2000](#bib.bib75)), and [Blum and Mansour (2007)](#bib.bib19) give a
generic reduction turning any no-external-regret algorithm into a
no-swap-regret one. No such statement holds for Nash equilibrium outside
special classes. This is the single most useful transfer from game
theory into multi-agent reinforcement learning: it tells the
practitioner what a self-play run is actually converging to, and the
answer is usually a coarse correlated equilibrium of the empirical game
rather than a Nash equilibrium of anything.

Since the difference between the two relaxations determines what a
no-regret run is entitled to claim, we exhibit it in the smallest game
where it can appear.

###### Example 4.7 (A coarse correlated equilibrium that is not correlated).

In a two-by-two game the two concepts coincide, and the reason is a
one-line computation worth performing. The coarse constraint for the row
agent deviating to its first action is
$`\sum_{i,j}\sigma(i,j)[u_{1}(1,j)-u_{1}(i,j)]\leq 0`$; the term with
$`i=1`$ vanishes identically, so the constraint reduces to
$`\sum_{j}\sigma(2,j)[u_{1}(1,j)-u_{1}(2,j)]\leq 0`$, which is precisely
the conditional constraint
[Equation 6](#S4.E6 "In Definition 4.5 (Correlated and coarse correlated equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
at the row $`i=2`$. With only two actions each conditional constraint is
recovered this way, so $`\mathrm{CE}=\mathrm{CCE}`$ for every two-by-two
game, and a search over four hundred random two-by-two games finds no
separation, as it must not.

Three actions suffice. Take rock-paper-scissors, with the row agent’s
payoffs $`u_{1}(a_{1},a_{2})\in\{-1,0,1\}`$ in the usual pattern, and
let $`\sigma`$ be uniform on the diagonal, that is, probability $`1/3`$
on each of $`(R,R)`$, $`(P,P)`$ and $`(S,S)`$. Both agents earn $`0`$
under $`\sigma`$. A constant deviation to Rock faces the marginal
$`(\tfrac{1}{3},\tfrac{1}{3},\tfrac{1}{3})`$ over the opponent’s actions
and earns $`\tfrac{1}{3}(0-1+1)=0`$, and the same holds for Paper and
Scissors, so no constant deviation gains and $`\sigma`$ is a coarse
correlated equilibrium. It is not a correlated equilibrium: conditional
on the recommendation Rock the agent knows the opponent plays Rock, so
switching to Paper earns $`1`$ instead of $`0`$, a conditional gain of
$`1`$ worth $`\tfrac{1}{3}\cdot 1=\tfrac{1}{3}`$ in expectation. The
unique correlated equilibrium of rock-paper-scissors is the uniform
distribution over all nine cells.

The practical reading is exact. Two independent no-external-regret
learners playing rock-paper-scissors may settle into a pattern in which
their actions are perfectly correlated and both earn the equilibrium
payoff, and neither will detect a problem by monitoring its own external
regret, because there is none. A third party who could rewire each
agent’s action as a function of what it was about to play would extract
a gain of $`1/3`$ per round. This is why the evaluation table in
[Table 39](#S22.T39 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
asks for the maximum constant deviation gain for a coarse correlated
target and for the maximum swap gain for a correlated one: they are
different numbers and only the second detects this failure. $`\lozenge`$

Two further concepts complete the stateless picture. The maximin or
security value of agent $`i`$ is
$`\underline{u}_{i}=\max_{\pi_{i}}\min_{\pi_{-i}}u_{i}(\pi_{i},\pi_{-i})`$,
the payoff guaranteed against an arbitrary opponent. In a two-player
zero-sum game the minimax theorem of [von Neumann (1928)](#bib.bib183)
gives
$`\max_{\pi_{1}}\min_{\pi_{2}}u_{1}=\min_{\pi_{2}}\max_{\pi_{1}}u_{1}`$,
the common value is the value of the game, and the set of Nash
equilibria coincides with the product of the sets of maximin strategies,
so that equilibrium is interchangeable and payoff-unique. Nothing in
that sentence survives outside the zero-sum case, which is why zero-sum
multi-agent reinforcement learning has clean guarantees and general-sum
multi-agent reinforcement learning does not. The reinforcement learning
counterpart of the maximin value is the robust objective of
[Equation 3](#S2.E3 "In 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
and the correspondence is literal rather than analogical, as
[Proposition 7.8](#S7.Thmtheorem8 "Proposition 7.8 (Robust control is a zero-sum stochastic game; , ). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
will record.

Pareto optimality and social welfare are the remaining concepts, and
they are the ones that most often express what an engineer actually
wants. A profile $`\pi`$ is Pareto dominated by $`\pi^{\prime}`$ when
$`u_{i}(\pi^{\prime})\geq u_{i}(\pi)`$ for all $`i`$ with strict
inequality for some $`i`$, and Pareto optimal when it is dominated by
nothing. The utilitarian social welfare is $`\sum_{i}u_{i}(\pi)`$ and
the egalitarian welfare is $`\min_{i}u_{i}(\pi)`$. With $`n=1`$ all of
these collapse to maximisation of $`u_{1}`$, which is the degeneracy
that makes single-agent reinforcement learning free of the normative
questions that dominate the multi-agent case. In a common-payoff game
Pareto optimality and welfare maximisation coincide with each other and
with the maximisation of the shared reward, which is why cooperative
multi-agent reinforcement learning can state an unambiguous objective,
and the residual difficulty there is not what to optimise but how to
make decentralised agents select the same optimum, which is equilibrium
selection rather than equilibrium computation.

###### Example 4.8 (Four criteria disagree on the prisoner’s dilemma).

In the prisoner’s dilemma of
[Example 3.4](#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Example 3.4 (The four games that this monograph keeps returning to). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
the unique Nash equilibrium $`(D,D)`$ has welfare $`2`$. The welfare
maximum is $`(C,C)`$ with welfare $`6`$. The Pareto frontier consists of
$`(C,C)`$, $`(C,D)`$ and $`(D,C)`$, with payoff vectors $`(3,3)`$,
$`(0,5)`$ and $`(5,0)`$; the equilibrium is not on it. The maximin value
of each agent is $`1`$, attained by defecting, because the opponent can
always hold the agent to $`1`$. The egalitarian optimum is $`(C,C)`$. So
the equilibrium criterion, the utilitarian criterion, the Pareto
criterion and the egalitarian criterion select different profiles, and
only the security criterion agrees with the equilibrium. A designer who
says “we want the agents to reach equilibrium” has therefore chosen the
welfare $`2`$ outcome over the welfare $`6`$ one without saying so. The
same arithmetic recurs in every sequential social dilemma benchmark, and
[Section 6](#S6 "6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
explains the one device, namely history dependence, that lets the
welfare-$`6`$ outcome become an equilibrium. $`\lozenge`$

###### Example 4.9 (Credit for a shared return, exactly).

Three agents clear a warehouse. Alone, agents $`1`$ and $`2`$ each shift
$`10`$ pallets and agent $`3`$ shifts $`4`$. The pairs $`\{1,2\}`$,
$`\{1,3\}`$ and $`\{2,3\}`$ shift $`26`$, $`16`$ and $`16`$, and all
three together shift $`36`$. Writing $`v`$ for this characteristic
function,
[Equation 16](#S16.E16 "In Definition 16.4 (Shapley value; , ). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
gives $`\phi=(14.67,\,14.67,\,6.67)`$, which sums to $`36`$ as
efficiency requires. The computation for agent $`3`$ averages its
marginal contribution over the six orderings of the three agents: it
adds $`4`$ when it arrives first, $`6`$ when it arrives after one of the
others, and $`10`$ when it arrives last, and the weighted average is
$`\tfrac{1}{3}\cdot 4+\tfrac{1}{3}\cdot 6+\tfrac{1}{3}\cdot 10=6.67`$.
The core of this game is non-empty and contains $`\phi`$, so the
allocation is also stable in the sense of
[Definition 17.1](#S17.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 17.1 (Transferable-utility game, core and nucleolus; , ). ‣ 17 Coalitions, stability of credit, and bargaining ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning");
the nucleolus is $`(14.5,14.5,7)`$, which differs. In a cooperative
multi-agent reinforcement learning system $`v(C)`$ is the return
obtained when the agents outside $`C`$ are replaced by a default policy,
$`\phi_{i}`$ is the credit assigned to agent $`i`$, and the difference
between $`\phi_{3}=6.67`$ and the difference reward
$`v(\{1,2,3\})-v(\{1,2\})=10`$ is exactly the difference between
averaging over all orderings and evaluating one of them, which
[Proposition 16.5](#S16.Thmtheorem5 "Proposition 16.5 (Difference rewards are a single Shapley term). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
makes precise. $`\lozenge`$

| Game-theoretic concept            | Reinforcement learning counterpart                               | Degeneracy at $`n=1`$          |     |
| --------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------ | --- |
| Best response $`\mathrm{BR}_{i}`$ | Greedy policy improvement; argmax over $`Q`$                     | Greedy policy                  | D   |
| Nash equilibrium                  | Fixed point of simultaneous policy improvement                   | Optimal policy                 | D   |
| $`\varepsilon`$-Nash              | Approximate policy iteration; $`\varepsilon`$-greedy improvement | $`\varepsilon`$-optimal policy | D   |
| Markov perfect equilibrium        | Stationary optimal policy of each agent given the others         | Optimal stationary policy      | D   |
| Subgame perfect equilibrium       | Bellman principle of optimality; backward induction              | Optimal policy                 | D   |
| Maximin / security value          | Robust objective, worst-case return                              | $`\max_{\pi}V^{\pi}`$          | E   |
| Minimax theorem value             | Unique value of the robust Bellman fixed point                   | $`V^{\star}`$                  | E   |
| Correlated equilibrium            | Policy with a shared signal; swap-regret limit                   | Optimal policy                 | E   |
| Coarse correlated equilibrium     | Limit of no-external-regret self-play                            | Optimal policy                 | E   |
| Strictly dominated strategy       | Action provably never greedy; action masking                     | Suboptimal action              | D   |
| Rationalisable strategy           | Survivor of iterated best-response reasoning                     | Optimal action                 | D   |
| Quantal response equilibrium      | Fixed point of softmax policies; soft Bellman                    | Softmax policy                 | E   |
| Level-$`k`$, cognitive hierarchy  | Depth-limited opponent modelling                                 | Greedy policy                  | S   |
| Pareto optimality                 | Non-dominated multi-objective policy                             | Optimal policy                 | D   |
| Social welfare optimum            | Team reward $`\sum_{i}\mathcal{R}_{i}`$; centralised objective   | Optimal policy                 | D   |
| Nash bargaining solution          | Product-of-advantages objective                                  | Optimal policy                 | E   |
| Stackelberg equilibrium           | Bilevel optimum; leader with best-responding follower            | Optimal policy                 | E   |
| Evolutionarily stable strategy    | Asymptotically stable rest point of learning dynamics            | Global optimum                 | S   |
| Core of a coalitional game        | Stability of a credit-assignment scheme                          | Trivial                        | S   |
| Nucleolus                         | Lexicographic min-max credit allocation                          | Total return                   | E   |
| Shapley value                     | Per-agent credit; marginal contribution baseline                 | Total return                   | E   |
| Folk-theorem equilibrium          | Non-Markovian policy conditioning on history                     | Optimal policy                 | D   |
| Trembling-hand perfection         | Robustness of the policy to execution noise                      | Optimal policy                 | S   |
| Sequential equilibrium            | On-policy belief filter plus greedy action                       | Optimal policy                 | E   |

Table 7: Solution concepts and their reinforcement learning
counterparts. The column $`n=1`$ gives the single-agent degeneracy,
which is in every case the optimal policy or one of its
characterisations. The last column records the strength of the
correspondence: D for definitional identity, E for an exact reduction
supported by a theorem, S for a structural analogy whose failure
conditions are stated in the text.

[Table 7](#S4.T7 "In 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
collects these correspondences together with the ones developed later in
the monograph. Three entries deserve comment because they are the ones
most often stated loosely. Subgame perfection and the Bellman principle
are the same requirement, namely that a strategy be optimal in every
subproblem and not merely along the realised path, and the equivalence
is definitional once one observes that a subgame of a single-agent
sequential problem is the problem restarted at a state. Markov perfect
equilibrium is the restriction of subgame perfection to stationary
state-dependent strategies and is therefore the exact multi-agent
analogue of restricting attention to stationary policies in a discounted
Markov decision process; the restriction is without loss in the
single-agent case and with loss in the multi-agent case, because
non-Markovian strategies support equilibria that stationary ones do not,
which is the content of the folk theorem in
[Section 6](#S6 "6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The Shapley value entry is marked as an exact reduction because the
axioms that characterise it uniquely are exactly the properties one
demands of a credit-assignment scheme, and because the difference reward
used in cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning is a single term
of the Shapley sum, as
[Proposition 16.5](#S16.Thmtheorem5 "Proposition 16.5 (Difference rewards are a single Shapley term). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
makes precise.

## 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality

Nash equilibrium presumes that every agent’s belief about the others is
exactly right. Three families of concepts weaken that presumption in
different directions, and all three have reinforcement learning
counterparts that are in daily use and are rarely recognised as such.
Dominance and rationalisability weaken it by asking only what survives
common knowledge of rationality, which is the game-theoretic content of
action pruning and action masking. Level-$`k`$ and cognitive hierarchy
models weaken it by bounding the depth of recursion, which is the
content of depth-limited opponent modelling. Quantal response weakens it
by replacing exact maximisation with a softmax, which is literally the
content of entropy-regularised reinforcement learning. The refinements
at the end of the section go the other way and strengthen Nash
equilibrium by requiring robustness to small mistakes, which is the
content of training a policy that must survive execution noise.

The two readings here are almost the same sentence spoken with different
emphasis. A game theorist asks what a perfectly rational agent could
believe, and treats departures from maximisation as a modelling device
for experimental data. A reinforcement learning researcher asks what a
finite computation can produce, and treats departures from maximisation
as either an exploration mechanism or a regulariser that makes an
optimisation problem well behaved. The mathematics is identical and the
motivation is different, which is why the softmax appears in one
literature as a model of human error and in the other as a device for
making a Bellman operator smooth.

| Game theory                     | Reinforcement learning                              | How to read the pair                         |
| ------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------- |
| Strictly dominated strategy     | Action that is never greedy for any opponent policy | Safe to delete from the action space         |
| Weakly dominated strategy       | Action never strictly better, sometimes worse       | Unsafe to delete; order of deletion matters  |
| Iterated elimination (IESDS)    | Iterative action masking                            | Prune, then prune again given the pruning    |
| Rationalisable strategy         | Survivor of unbounded best-response recursion       | What a rational agent could justify playing  |
| Common knowledge of rationality | Unbounded recursion depth in modelling              | I know that you know that I know             |
| Level-$`k`$ agent               | Opponent model unrolled $`k`$ times                 | Best response to a level-$`(k{-}1)`$ model   |
| Cognitive hierarchy             | Mixture over modelling depths                       | Best response to a distribution of depths    |
| Quantal response, logit choice  | Boltzmann or softmax policy                         | Better actions more likely, not certain      |
| Precision $`\lambda`$           | Inverse temperature $`1/\tau`$                      | How sharply the agent maximises              |
| Quantal response equilibrium    | Fixed point of softmax best responses               | Soft Bellman fixed point with several agents |
| $`\varepsilon`$-equilibrium     | $`\varepsilon`$-greedy improvement stopping rule    | Nobody gains more than $`\varepsilon`$       |
| Trembling-hand perfection       | Robustness to action-execution noise                | Survives a small probability of any action   |
| Proper equilibrium              | Noise weighted by how costly the mistake is         | Expensive mistakes are rarer                 |

Table 8: Bounded-rationality vocabulary read from both sides.

###### Definition 5.1 (Dominance).

In a normal-form game, a strategy $`\pi_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})`$
strictly dominates $`\pi_{i}^{\prime}`$ when
$`u_{i}(\pi_{i},a_{-i})>u_{i}(\pi_{i}^{\prime},a_{-i})`$ for every
$`a_{-i}\in\mathcal{A}_{-i}`$, and weakly dominates it when the
inequality holds weakly everywhere and strictly somewhere. A pure action
is strictly dominated when some mixed strategy strictly dominates it.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. A strictly dominated action is one
that is not greedy with respect to any $`Q`$-function the game can
produce, whatever the other agents do, so deleting it from the action
space cannot change the optimal policy of any agent. This is the formal
licence behind action masking, and it is the only licence there is. It
is specific to strict dominance: a weakly dominated action is greedy for
some opponent behaviour, so masking it changes the set of equilibria,
and the order in which weakly dominated actions are deleted changes the
answer. One detail matters in practice and is easy to miss. The
dominating object in
[Definition 5.1](#S5.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 5.1 (Dominance). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is a mixed strategy, not an action, so an action can be safely maskable
even though no single other action beats it; a mask computed by
comparing $`Q`$-values pairwise will therefore miss deletions that a
linear program would find, and
[Example 5.2](#S5.Thmtheorem2 "Example 5.2 (An action worth deleting that no other action beats). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
exhibits the smallest case.

###### Example 5.2 (An action worth deleting that no other action beats).

Give the row agent three actions against the column agent’s two, with
row payoffs

|     |                                  |     |
| --- | -------------------------------- | --- | --- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r | c   | c   | }\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil\text{left}\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil\text{right}\hfil\lx@intercol\\ |

\cline{2-3}\cr a*{1}&4&0\\
\cline{2-3}\cr a*{2}&0&4\\
\cline{2-3}\cr a\_{3}&1&1\\
\cline{2-3}\cr\end{array}.`$ | |

Compare $`a_{3}`$ with $`a_{1}`$: it loses in the left column, $`1<4`$,
and wins in the right, $`1>0`$, so $`a_{1}`$ does not dominate it.
Compare $`a_{3}`$ with $`a_{2}`$: the same in reverse. No pure action
dominates $`a_{3}`$. Now compare it with the mixed strategy that plays
$`a_{1}`$ and $`a_{2}`$ with probability one half each, whose payoff
vector is
$`(\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot 4+\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot 0,\;\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot 0+\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot 4)=(2,2)`$,
which beats $`(1,1)`$ in both columns. So $`a_{3}`$ is strictly
dominated by a mixture and by nothing else, and may be masked.

The practical reading is that a safe action mask is the output of a
linear program, one per action, asking whether some distribution over
the remaining actions beats it in every column; it is not the output of
a pairwise comparison of $`Q`$-values. The prisoner’s dilemma is the
easy case where the pairwise test suffices, since $`D`$ beats $`C`$ by
$`2`$ against $`C`$ and by $`1`$ against $`D`$, so defection dominates
in the pure sense. Most games are not the easy case. $`\lozenge`$

###### Definition 5.3 (Iterated elimination and rationalisability; [Bernheim, 1984](#bib.bib15); [Pearce, 1984](#bib.bib138)).

Let $`\mathcal{A}_{i}^{0}=\mathcal{A}_{i}`$ and let
$`\mathcal{A}_{i}^{k+1}`$ be the set of actions in
$`\mathcal{A}_{i}^{k}`$ that are not strictly dominated when the
opponents are restricted to $`\mathcal{A}_{-i}^{k}`$. The set
$`\mathcal{A}_{i}^{\infty}=\bigcap_{k}\mathcal{A}_{i}^{k}`$ is the set
of actions surviving iterated elimination of strictly dominated
strategies. An action is rationalisable when it is a best response to
some belief over the opponents’ rationalisable strategies, the belief
being allowed to be correlated across opponents. For two agents the two
sets coincide; for more than two they coincide when correlated beliefs
are permitted.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. Iterated elimination is action
masking applied repeatedly, in which each round masks the actions that
are never greedy given that the previous round’s masks are common
knowledge. The first round is computable from an agent’s own experience,
since an action whose $`Q`$-value has been lower under every opponent
policy ever observed is a candidate for deletion. No later round is,
because round $`k`$ needs the fact that the other agents performed round
$`k-1`$, which is information about their reasoning rather than about
their play. The rationalisable set is the limit of the recursion, so it
is what an agent could justify playing under unbounded mutual reasoning,
and the gap between it and the Nash equilibrium set measures exactly how
much equilibrium assumes beyond rationality.
[Example 5.4](#S5.Thmtheorem4 "Example 5.4 (Iterated elimination solves a game that dominance alone does not). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
runs the recursion to a unique answer and
[Example 5.5](#S5.Thmtheorem5 "Example 5.5 (Rationalisability is strictly weaker than equilibrium). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
exhibits a game in which it deletes nothing at all.

###### Example 5.4 (Iterated elimination solves a game that dominance alone does not).

Consider the three-by-three game in which the row agent’s payoff is
$`u_{1}(i,j)=4-i-j`$ and the column agent’s is $`u_{2}(i,j)=2+j-i`$,
with $`i,j\in\{0,1,2\}`$ indexing the rows and columns,

|     |                                  |     |
| --- | -------------------------------- | --- | --- | --- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r | c   | c   | c   | }\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil 0\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil 1\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil 2\hfil\lx@intercol\\ |

\cline{2-4}\cr 0&4,2&3,3&2,4\\
\cline{2-4}\cr 1&3,1&2,2&1,3\\
\cline{2-4}\cr 2&2,0&1,1&0,2\\
\cline{2-4}\cr\end{array}.`$ | |

Row $`0`$ strictly dominates rows $`1`$ and $`2`$ for the row agent,
since $`u_{1}`$ decreases in $`i`$ for every $`j`$, and column $`2`$
strictly dominates columns $`0`$ and $`1`$ for the column agent, since
$`u_{2}`$ increases in $`j`$ for every $`i`$. Four eliminations leave
the single profile $`(0,2)`$ with payoffs $`(2,4)`$, which is therefore
the unique Nash equilibrium and is reached without ever writing down a
fixed-point condition. The reinforcement learning reading is that an
agent which observes only its own reward can discover the first round of
elimination from data, because a dominated action has a lower
$`Q`$-value under every opponent policy it has ever seen, but cannot
discover the second round without reasoning about the other agent’s
incentives, because the second round depends on the opponent having
performed the first. Independent learners therefore recover the first
level of this hierarchy and no more, which is the precise sense in which
they are level-$`1`$ reasoners. $`\lozenge`$

###### Example 5.5 (Rationalisability is strictly weaker than equilibrium).

In matching pennies every action of every agent is rationalisable: Heads
is a best response to the belief that the opponent plays Heads, Tails to
the belief that it plays Tails, and both beliefs are themselves
supported on rationalisable actions. So iterated elimination removes
nothing and the rationalisable set is the whole game, while the Nash
equilibrium set is the single profile
$`(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})\times(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})`$. The
gap is informative. An algorithm that only ever deletes provably bad
actions will never solve matching pennies, because there are no provably
bad actions; solving it requires the additional assumption that the
opponent’s belief about you is correct, which is exactly what
equilibrium adds to rationality. This is the cleanest statement of what
a self-play assumption buys and of what it presupposes. $`\lozenge`$

Bounding the depth of the recursion in
[Definition 5.3](#S5.Thmtheorem3 "Definition 5.3 (Iterated elimination and rationalisability; , ). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
gives the second family. A level-$`0`$ agent plays a fixed non-strategic
distribution, usually uniform; a level-$`k`$ agent best responds to the
belief that all others are level $`k-1`$ ([Stahl and Wilson,
1995](#bib.bib170)); and a cognitive-hierarchy agent best responds to a
distribution over lower levels, usually Poisson ([Camerer et al.,
2004](#bib.bib30)). The reinforcement learning counterpart is exact and
is implemented whenever an agent conditions its policy on a learned
model of the opponent that is itself trained against a lower-depth
model, which is the structure of recursive reasoning methods ([Wen
et al., 2019](#bib.bib191)) and of the finitely nested interactive
partially observable Markov decision process of
[Section 12](#S12 "12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").

###### Example 5.6 (Depth of reasoning in the guessing game).

Every agent picks a number in $`[0,100]`$ and whoever is closest to two
thirds of the average wins. A level-$`0`$ agent picks uniformly, so its
expected choice is $`50`$. A level-$`1`$ agent best responds by picking
$`\tfrac{2}{3}\cdot 50=33.33`$. A level-$`2`$ agent picks
$`\tfrac{2}{3}\cdot 33.33=22.22`$, a level-$`3`$ agent $`14.81`$, a
level-$`4`$ agent $`9.88`$. The unique Nash equilibrium, obtained by
iterated elimination of dominated strategies carried to the limit, is
that everybody picks $`0`$. The sequence makes the point that
equilibrium is the $`k\to\infty`$ limit of a recursion whose
intermediate terms are all different and all individually rational given
the corresponding belief. For multi-agent reinforcement learning the
practical reading is that the depth of an opponent model is a
hyperparameter with a visible effect on behaviour, that training against
a frozen opponent produces a level-$`1`$ policy by construction, that
training against a policy that was itself trained against a frozen
opponent produces a level-$`2`$ policy, and that self-play with a replay
buffer of past opponents produces something close to a cognitive
hierarchy whose depth distribution is set by the sampling scheme over
the buffer. Reporting the depth is therefore reporting a property of the
algorithm, not of the game. $`\lozenge`$

The third family replaces exact maximisation by a smooth approximation,
and here the correspondence with reinforcement learning is an identity
rather than an analogy.

###### Definition 5.7 (Quantal response equilibrium; [McKelvey and Palfrey, 1995](#bib.bib115)).

Fix a precision $`\lambda\geq 0`$. A profile $`\pi^{\lambda}`$ is a
logit quantal response equilibrium when for every agent $`i`$ and every
$`a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}`$,

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |     |     |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\pi^{\lambda}_{i}(a_{i})\;=\;\frac{\exp\!\big(\lambda\,u_{i}(a_{i},\pi^{\lambda}_{-i})\big)}{\sum_{a_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\exp\!\big(\lambda\,u_{i}(a_{i}^{\prime},\pi^{\lambda}_{-i})\big)}.`$ |     | (8) |

At $`\lambda=0`$ the unique solution is uniform play, and as
$`\lambda\to\infty`$ every limit point of $`\pi^{\lambda}`$ is a Nash
equilibrium.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. Replace $`\lambda`$ by $`1/\tau`$
and
[Equation 8](#S5.E8 "In Definition 5.7 (Quantal response equilibrium; , ). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the line of code that turns a $`Q`$-vector into a Boltzmann policy,
$`\pi_{i}(a_{i})\propto\exp(Q_{i}(a_{i})/\tau)`$, with
$`Q_{i}(a_{i})=u_{i}(a_{i},\pi_{-i})`$ the value of action $`a_{i}`$
against the current opponents. The only thing
[Definition 5.7](#S5.Thmtheorem7 "Definition 5.7 (Quantal response equilibrium; , ). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
adds to that line is the word “equilibrium”: it requires the
$`Q`$-vector that produced the softmax to be the one the softmax itself
induces. So a quantal response equilibrium is the fixed point of a
self-play loop in which every agent uses a softmax policy at a fixed
temperature and nobody ever anneals, which is the loop that a great many
multi-agent implementations actually run. The precision $`\lambda`$ is
the inverse temperature, $`\lambda=0`$ is uniform exploration, and
$`\lambda\to\infty`$ is the greedy limit;
[Proposition 5.9](#S5.Thmtheorem9 "Proposition 5.9 (Quantal response is the multi-agent soft Bellman equation). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
shows that at one agent the whole construction collapses to the soft
Bellman operator of maximum-entropy reinforcement learning. The
practical corollary is blunt: a run at a fixed temperature is not
converging to a Nash equilibrium of the game, it is converging to a Nash
equilibrium of a different game whose payoffs carry an entropy bonus,
and
[Example 5.11](#S5.Thmtheorem11 "Example 5.11 (How much the softmax costs, in value). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
prices that bonus.

###### Example 5.8 (One logit response, computed).

Before any fixed point, the response alone. In matching pennies the row
agent’s payoffs are $`+1`$ for matching and $`-1`$ otherwise. Suppose
the column agent plays Heads with probability $`0.7`$. Then
$`u_{1}(H,\pi_{2})=0.7(1)+0.3(-1)=0.4`$ and
$`u_{1}(T,\pi_{2})=0.7(-1)+0.3(1)=-0.4`$, so the $`Q`$-vector is
$`(0.4,-0.4)`$ and the logit response puts weight

|     |                                                                                                                       |     |
| --- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\pi_{1}(H)\;=\;\frac{e^{0.4\lambda}}{e^{0.4\lambda}+e^{-0.4\lambda}}\;=\;\frac{1}{1+e^{-0.8\lambda}}`$ |     |

on Heads. At $`\lambda=0`$ this is $`0.5`$, at $`\lambda=1`$ it is
$`0.690`$, at $`\lambda=5`$ it is $`0.982`$, and in the limit it is
$`1`$, the greedy action. Against a uniform column agent both payoffs
are $`0`$ and the response is uniform at every precision, which is why
the equilibrium of this particular game is unaffected by the temperature
while the path to it, computed in
[Example 5.10](#S5.Thmtheorem10 "Example 5.10 (Tracking the quantal response path to the equilibrium). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
for an asymmetric variant, is not. The arithmetic is the arithmetic of a
softmax layer over two logits, and that is the whole of the
correspondence. $`\lozenge`$

###### Proposition 5.9 (Quantal response is the multi-agent soft Bellman equation).

Let $`n=1`$ and write $`\tau=1/\lambda`$. Then
[Equation 8](#S5.E8 "In Definition 5.7 (Quantal response equilibrium; , ). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
reduces to the softmax policy $`\pi(a)\propto\exp(u_{1}(a)/\tau)`$,
which is the maximiser of the entropy-regularised objective
$`\max_{\pi\in\Delta(\mathcal{A})}\;\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi}[u_{1}(a)]+\tau\,H(\pi)`$,
with optimal value the log-sum-exp
$`\tau\log\sum_{a}\exp(u_{1}(a)/\tau)`$. Consequently the sequential
version of
[Equation 8](#S5.E8 "In Definition 5.7 (Quantal response equilibrium; , ). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
obtained by applying it to the stage game of
[Definition 3.6](#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Definition 3.6 (Induced stage game). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
degenerates at $`n=1`$ to the soft Bellman operator
$`(\mathbb{T}_{\tau}V)(s)=\tau\log\sum_{a}\exp\big(\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}}[\mathcal{R}(s,a,s^{\prime})+\gamma V(s^{\prime})]/\tau\big)`$,
which is the operator of maximum-entropy reinforcement learning
([Haarnoja et al., 2017](#bib.bib72)) and the regularised Markov
decision process of [Geist et al. (2019)](#bib.bib65).

###### Proof.

With one agent the right-hand side of
[Equation 8](#S5.E8 "In Definition 5.7 (Quantal response equilibrium; , ). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
does not depend on $`\pi_{-i}`$, so the fixed-point condition is an
explicit formula. The variational identity
$`\max_{\pi\in\Delta(\mathcal{A})}\{\sum_{a}\pi(a)u(a)-\tau\sum_{a}\pi(a)\log\pi(a)\}=\tau\log\sum_{a}e^{u(a)/\tau}`$
with maximiser $`\pi(a)\propto e^{u(a)/\tau}`$ is the Donsker-Varadhan
variational formula for the log-partition function, obtained by setting
the gradient of the Lagrangian to zero. Substituting
$`u(a)=u^{\,s}_{1}(a)`$ from
[Equation 4](#S3.E4 "In Definition 3.6 (Induced stage game). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
gives the stated operator. ∎

[Proposition 5.9](#S5.Thmtheorem9 "Proposition 5.9 (Quantal response is the multi-agent soft Bellman equation). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
explains why entropy regularisation stabilises multi-agent training for
a reason that has nothing to do with exploration. The quantal response
operator is a contraction for $`\lambda`$ small enough and has a unique
fixed point, whereas the Nash operator is neither, so adding entropy is
a convexification of an ill-posed fixed-point problem. This is the
mechanism behind regularised Nash dynamics ([Perolat et al.,
2021](#bib.bib139)) and behind the unified treatment of [Sokota et al.
(2023)](#bib.bib166), and
[Section 20](#S20 "20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
exhibits the same term as the mutation component of the
replicator-mutator dynamics.

###### Example 5.10 (Tracking the quantal response path to the equilibrium).

Take the zero-sum game with the row agent’s payoff matrix
$`\big(\begin{smallmatrix}2&-1\\
-1&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)`$. Its unique Nash equilibrium has both
agents playing their first action with probability $`0.4`$ and has value
$`0.2`$: the row agent is indifferent when $`3q-1=1-2q`$, that is
$`q=0.4`$, and symmetrically for the column agent. Solving
[Equation 8](#S5.E8 "In Definition 5.7 (Quantal response equilibrium; , ). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
numerically along a path in $`\lambda`$ gives the first action
probabilities $`(0.500,0.500)`$ at $`\lambda=0`$, $`(0.489,0.391)`$ at
$`\lambda=1`$, $`(0.418,0.387)`$ at $`\lambda=5`$, $`(0.404,0.396)`$ at
$`\lambda=20`$ and $`(0.401,0.399)`$ at $`\lambda=100`$, converging to
$`(0.4,0.4)`$. The path is the exact analogue of the regularisation path
of an entropy-regularised policy optimiser as the temperature is
annealed, and the fact that it is a path rather than a point is what
makes annealing work: at each $`\lambda`$ the solution is unique and
locally stable, so a continuation method tracks it, whereas solving at
$`\lambda=\infty`$ directly means solving the original fixed-point
problem with none of its difficulties removed. $`\lozenge`$

###### Example 5.11 (How much the softmax costs, in value).

A state offers three actions with $`Q`$-values $`(1,\,0.5,\,0)`$. The
hard maximum is $`1`$. The soft maximum
$`\tau\log\sum_{a}e^{Q(a)/\tau}`$ is $`1.680`$ at $`\tau=1`$, with the
best action taken with probability $`0.506`$, and $`1.0007`$ at
$`\tau=0.1`$, with the best action taken with probability $`0.993`$. The
soft value always exceeds the hard value, by at most
$`\tau\log\left\lvert\mathcal{A}\right\rvert`$, which is
$`\tau\log 3=1.0986\tau`$ here. A practitioner who anneals $`\tau`$ from
$`1`$ to $`0.1`$ therefore watches a reported value drop by roughly
$`0.68`$ without any deterioration in the policy, because the drop is
the removal of the entropy bonus rather than a loss of return. In the
multi-agent setting the same bonus is what separates the quantal
response equilibrium from the Nash equilibrium, so the same accounting
applies to exploitability measurements, which should be computed against
the unregularised game. $`\lozenge`$

The refinements go in the opposite direction. A Nash equilibrium may
rely on an agent choosing an action that is optimal only because an
event of probability zero is assumed never to occur, and the refinements
rule this out by requiring optimality to survive small perturbations.

###### Definition 5.12 (Trembling-hand perfection and properness; [Selten, 1975](#bib.bib157); [Myerson, 1978](#bib.bib124)).

A profile $`\pi^{\star}`$ is trembling-hand perfect when there is a
sequence of completely mixed profiles $`\pi^{m}\to\pi^{\star}`$ such
that $`\pi_{i}^{\star}`$ is a best response to $`\pi^{m}_{-i}`$ for
every $`i`$ and every $`m`$. It is proper when in addition the
perturbations can be taken so that a costlier mistake receives a
probability at most $`\varepsilon`$ times that of a cheaper one.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. The sequence $`\pi^{m}`$ is an
exploration schedule being annealed to zero, and trembling-hand
perfection is the requirement that the profile survive the whole
schedule rather than only its limit. An $`\varepsilon`$-greedy learner,
or a policy deployed on hardware that occasionally executes the wrong
action, is living at a strictly positive $`m`$ and will therefore be
pushed off any equilibrium that this refinement rejects. Properness is
the same requirement with the trembles weighted by how costly the
mistake is, which is what a Boltzmann schedule does and what uniform
$`\varepsilon`$-greedy exploration does not, so the two standard
exploration mechanisms target two different refinements of the same
solution concept.
[Example 5.13](#S5.Thmtheorem13 "Example 5.13 (An equilibrium that a single tremble destroys). ‣ 5 Dominance, iterated reasoning, and bounded rationality ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
exhibits an equilibrium that a tremble of $`0.01`$ destroys.

###### Example 5.13 (An equilibrium that a single tremble destroys).

Consider the common-payoff game

|     |                                  |     |
| --- | -------------------------------- | --- | --- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r | c   | c   | }\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil c\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil d\hfil\lx@intercol\\ |

\cline{2-3}\cr a&1,1&0,0\\
\cline{2-3}\cr b&1,1&1,1\\
\cline{2-3}\cr\end{array}.`$ | |

The game has three pure Nash equilibria, $`(a,c)`$, $`(b,c)`$ and
$`(b,d)`$, all with payoff $`1`$, and only one of them survives
perturbation. Take $`(a,c)`$ first. If the column agent plays $`d`$ with
any probability $`\varepsilon>0`$, the row agent’s payoff from $`a`$ is
$`1-\varepsilon`$ while its payoff from $`b`$ is $`1`$, so $`a`$ is no
longer a best response and $`(a,c)`$ is not trembling-hand perfect; at
$`\varepsilon=0.01`$ the two values are $`0.99`$ and $`1.00`$. Now take
$`(b,d)`$. If the row agent plays $`a`$ with probability
$`\varepsilon`$, the column agent’s payoff from $`d`$ is
$`1-\varepsilon`$ while its payoff from $`c`$ is $`1`$, so $`d`$ is no
longer a best response either, by the mirror-image argument and with the
mirror-image numbers. The profile $`(b,c)`$ survives every tremble,
because $`b`$ pays $`1`$ whatever the column agent does and $`c`$ pays
$`1`$ whatever the row agent does. The reinforcement learning reading is
direct: $`a`$ is weakly dominated for the row agent and $`d`$ is weakly
dominated for the column agent, and an agent trained with any
exploration noise at all, or deployed on hardware with a non-zero
probability of executing the wrong action, will be pushed off both and
onto the undominated pair. Trembling-hand perfection is thus the
equilibrium concept that an $`\varepsilon`$-greedy learner implicitly
targets, and the refinement is not a mathematical nicety but a
description of what noisy execution does to a policy. Properness refines
this further by making the probability of a tremble decrease in the cost
of the mistake, which is what a Boltzmann exploration schedule does and
what uniform $`\varepsilon`$-greedy exploration does not. $`\lozenge`$

The last item in this section is the one that a practitioner uses
without naming. An $`\varepsilon`$-Nash equilibrium, in which no agent
can gain more than $`\varepsilon`$ by deviating, is the stopping
criterion of every approximate method in the area, and its single-agent
degeneracy is the $`\varepsilon`$-optimal policy returned by approximate
policy iteration. The relation between $`\varepsilon`$-equilibria and
exact ones is however badly behaved in a way that has no single-agent
analogue: an $`\varepsilon`$-equilibrium may be far in strategy space
from every exact equilibrium, and its payoff may differ from every
equilibrium payoff by much more than $`\varepsilon`$. This is why
[Table 39](#S22.T39 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
asks for exploitability rather than for a distance in policy space, and
why the complexity statements of
[Section 21](#S21 "21 The complexity landscape ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
distinguish an approximation in the deviation gain from an approximation
in the strategy.

## 6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process

A repeated game is a normal-form game $`G`$ played at every step
$`t=0,1,2,\dots`$, with each agent observing some function of the
realised joint action before the next round. The monitoring structure is
what determines the difficulty. Under perfect monitoring each agent
observes the full joint action $`a^{t}`$; under imperfect public
monitoring the agents observe a common signal $`y^{t}`$ drawn from a
distribution depending on $`a^{t}`$; under private monitoring each agent
observes its own signal. A strategy of agent $`i`$ is a map
$`\pi_{i}:\mathcal{H}_{i}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})`$ from the agent’s
observation history to distributions over its actions, and the payoff is
the discounted sum
$`u_{i}(\pi)=(1-\gamma)\mathbb{E}[\sum_{t}\gamma^{t}u_{i}(a^{t})]`$, the
normalisation being conventional so that payoffs live in the convex hull
of the stage payoffs.

The two readings are instructive because they attach the same object to
different hyperparameters. A game theorist reads the discount factor as
patience and the strategy space as the set of all history-contingent
plans, and asks which payoff vectors can be sustained. A reinforcement
learning researcher reads the discount factor as a parameter of the
return and the strategy space as whatever the architecture can
represent, and asks what the algorithm converges to. The folk theorem is
the statement that these two questions have the same answer and that the
answer is almost everything, so that in a repeated game the architecture
and the discount factor determine the outcome at least as much as the
learning rule does. We give the theorem and then insist on this reading,
because it is the one with practical consequences for benchmark design.

| Game theory                                                    | Reinforcement learning                                  | How to read the pair                    |
| -------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------- |
| Stage game $`G`$                                               | One-step environment; inner reward                      | The game played every period            |
| History $`h^{t}`$                                              | Trajectory prefix; recurrent hidden state               | Everything observed so far              |
| Strategy $`\pi_{i}:\mathcal{H}_{i}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})`$ | History-dependent policy; recurrent policy              | A plan contingent on the past           |
| Discount factor $`\gamma`$                                     | Discount factor $`\gamma`$                              | Patience; also the effective horizon    |
| Perfect monitoring                                             | Full observability of the joint action                  | You see what everybody did              |
| Imperfect public monitoring                                    | Shared noisy observation of the joint action            | You see a common signal, not the action |
