| Private monitoring                                             | Per-agent noisy observation                             | Each agent sees a different signal      |
| Minmax value $`\underline{u}_{i}`$                             | Worst-case return under adversarial partners            | The punishment that others can impose   |
| Individually rational payoff                                   | Return above the worst-case guarantee                   | Better than being punished forever      |
| Grim trigger, tit-for-tat                                      | Finite-state policy on the opponent’s last action       | A small recurrent controller            |
| Folk theorem                                                   | Expressiveness of the policy class sets the outcome set | Memory creates equilibria               |
| Sequential social dilemma                                      | Temporally extended mixed-motive environment            | The dilemma spread over time            |

Table 9: Repeated-game vocabulary read from both sides.

The translation into reinforcement learning vocabulary is immediate and
exact.

###### Proposition 6.1 (Repeated game as a stochastic game on histories).

A repeated game with perfect monitoring is a stochastic game whose state
at time $`t`$ is the history $`h^{t}=(a^{0},\dots,a^{t-1})`$, whose
transition is the deterministic append $`h^{t+1}=h^{t}a^{t}`$, and whose
reward is $`\mathcal{R}_{i}(h^{t},a^{t})=u_{i}(a^{t})`$. Under imperfect
public monitoring the state is the public history
$`(y^{0},\dots,y^{t-1})`$ and the game is a partially observable
stochastic game in which the hidden component of the state is empty but
the agents’ private action records differ. With $`n=1`$ the former is a
Markov decision process on the history tree whose optimal policy is the
constant policy $`a^{t}\equiv\argmax_{a}u_{1}(a)`$.

The statement is a tautology, and its value lies in what it exposes. The
state space of the induced stochastic game grows exponentially in $`t`$,
so the correspondence does not by itself yield an algorithm; what it
yields is the correct diagnosis of why repeated games are not solved by
stationary policies. In the induced stochastic game, a stationary policy
is one that ignores the history, and the set of Markov perfect
equilibria in stationary policies is exactly the set of profiles that
play a Nash equilibrium of the stage game at every step. Conditioning on
history enlarges the equilibrium set enormously, which is the folk
theorem.

###### Theorem 6.2 (Folk theorem, discounted case; [Fudenberg and Maskin, 1986](#bib.bib62)).

Let $`G`$ be a finite normal-form game with perfect monitoring and a
public randomisation device, let $`\underline{u}_{i}`$ be the minmax
value of agent $`i`$ in independent mixed strategies of the opponents,
and let
$`F^{\star}=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:v\in\mathrm{conv}\,u(\mathcal{A}),\;v_{i}>\underline{u}_{i}\ \forall i\}`$
be the set of strictly individually rational feasible payoffs. If
$`F^{\star}`$ has dimension $`n`$, then for every $`v\in F^{\star}`$
there exists $`\bar{\gamma}<1`$ such that for all
$`\gamma\in(\bar{\gamma},1)`$ the infinitely repeated game has a subgame
perfect equilibrium with payoff profile $`v`$. Without public
randomisation the conclusion weakens to a payoff within an arbitrarily
small $`\varepsilon`$ of $`v`$.

 

The reinforcement learning
reading. [Theorem 6.2](#S6.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 6.2 (Folk theorem, discounted case; , ). ‣ 6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is a statement about the policy class, and it is the reason two runs of
the same algorithm on the same social dilemma can report opposite
conclusions. The three ingredients map onto three lines of a
configuration file. The policy’s memory decides whether
history-contingent strategies are representable at all, so a feedforward
policy can only sustain the stage equilibrium while one bit of recurrent
state can sustain cooperation. The discount factor decides whether the
punishment is worth fearing, and
[Example 6.4](#S6.Thmtheorem4 "Example 6.4 (The discount threshold for grim trigger, computed). ‣ 6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
computes the threshold exactly. The public randomisation device is a
shared random seed, the same object that
[Definition 4.5](#S4.Thmtheorem5 "Definition 4.5 (Correlated and coarse correlated equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
requires. Change any of the three and the set of self-consistent
outcomes changes, with the learning rule untouched. The methodological
consequence is that a reported cooperation rate in an iterated social
dilemma measures the triple (architecture, discount, seed availability)
at least as much as it measures the algorithm, and a comparison that
varies the algorithm while holding the triple implicit is not a
controlled comparison.

###### Example 6.3 (The feasible set of the iterated prisoner’s dilemma).

With the stage payoffs of
[Example 3.4](#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Example 3.4 (The four games that this monograph keeps returning to). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
the minmax value of each agent is $`1`$, attained when the opponent
defects, which a linear program over the stage game confirms. The
feasible set is the convex hull of $`(3,3)`$, $`(0,5)`$, $`(5,0)`$ and
$`(1,1)`$, and $`F^{\star}`$ is the part of it strictly above $`1`$ in
both coordinates.
[Theorem 6.2](#S6.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 6.2 (Folk theorem, discounted case; , ). ‣ 6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
therefore asserts that for a patient enough pair of agents every payoff
vector in that two-dimensional region, including $`(3,3)`$, including
$`(4.3,1.01)`$ and including $`(1.01,4.3)`$, is the payoff of some
subgame perfect equilibrium. The asymmetric pairs are near the boundary:
a linear program over the hull gives $`4.327`$ as the largest payoff the
first agent can receive while the second receives $`1.01`$, so
$`(4.9,1.01)`$ would lie outside the feasible set and is not available
to any profile, equilibrium or not. The statement “the agents reached an
equilibrium of the iterated prisoner’s dilemma” therefore conveys almost
nothing: it excludes only payoffs below the minmax and outside the hull.
Any empirical claim about cooperation in this environment is a claim
about which equilibrium the particular learning rule selected, and is
therefore a claim about the algorithm and the architecture rather than
about the game. $`\lozenge`$

###### Example 6.4 (The discount threshold for grim trigger, computed).

Grim trigger cooperates until the opponent defects and defects forever
after. Suppose both agents play it. On the equilibrium path each earns
$`R/(1-\gamma)=3/(1-\gamma)`$. A one-shot deviation earns $`T=5`$ now
and then $`P=1`$ forever, for a total of $`5+\gamma/(1-\gamma)`$.
Cooperation is sustainable when

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |     |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\frac{3}{1-\gamma}\;\geq\;5+\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad 3\;\geq\;5(1-\gamma)+\gamma\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\gamma\;\geq\;\frac{T-R}{T-P}\;=\;\frac{2}{4}\;=\;\frac{1}{2}.`$ |     |

At $`\gamma=0.4`$ cooperating is worth $`5.0`$ and deviating is worth
$`5.667`$, so defection wins by $`0.667`$; at $`\gamma=0.5`$ the two are
both worth $`6.0`$; at $`\gamma=0.6`$ cooperating is worth $`7.5`$ and
deviating $`6.5`$, so cooperation wins by $`1.0`$. The practical
consequence is blunt and is routinely overlooked: whether cooperation is
an equilibrium of a sequential social dilemma is decided by the discount
factor in the configuration file, and an experiment that reports
defection at $`\gamma=0.4`$ and cooperation at $`\gamma=0.6`$ has
measured the threshold $`1/2`$ rather than a property of the learning
algorithm. $`\lozenge`$

###### Example 6.5 (Tit-for-tat needs more patience than grim trigger).

Tit-for-tat plays what the opponent played last. Against a tit-for-tat
opponent three deviations must be checked, and only the third binds.
Permanent defection earns $`5+\gamma/(1-\gamma)`$, which loses to
$`3/(1-\gamma)`$ exactly when $`\gamma\geq 1/2`$, as for grim trigger. A
single defection followed by a return earns less than that. Alternating
defection and cooperation, however, earns $`T`$ in odd periods and
$`S=0`$ in even ones, for a total of
$`(T+\gamma S)/(1-\gamma^{2})=5/(1-\gamma^{2})`$, and this beats
$`3/(1-\gamma)`$ exactly when $`5/(1+\gamma)>3`$, that is when
$`\gamma<2/3`$. At $`\gamma=0.6`$ the three values are $`7.5`$ for
always cooperating, $`6.5`$ for always defecting and $`7.8125`$ for
alternating, so tit-for-tat is not a best response to itself; at
$`\gamma=0.8`$ they are $`15.0`$, $`9.0`$ and $`13.889`$, and it is. The
threshold is $`(T-R)/(R-S)=2/3`$, strictly above the grim-trigger
threshold of $`1/2`$, because tit-for-tat punishes for exactly one
period and therefore punishes less. The lesson for a practitioner is
that the strength of the punishment encoded in a policy determines the
range of discount factors over which cooperation is self-enforcing, so
the recurrent architecture and the discount factor must be chosen
together. $`\lozenge`$

For a reinforcement learning audience the folk theorem is best read as a
statement about the policy class. The set of attainable equilibrium
payoffs is determined by how much of the history the policy is allowed
to condition on, and making the policy more expressive, by giving the
agent a recurrent state or a longer context window, does not merely
improve the attainable return but changes the set of self-consistent
outcomes. A stationary policy in the iterated prisoner’s dilemma can
only sustain mutual defection; a policy with one bit of memory can
sustain cooperation through a grim trigger or tit-for-tat. The practical
corollary is that reported cooperation rates in iterated social dilemmas
are properties of the joint specification of algorithm, memory
architecture and discount factor, and that comparing an algorithm with a
recurrent policy against one with a feedforward policy on such a
benchmark compares policy classes rather than learning rules.

The second reason the folk theorem matters here is that it is the
cleanest instance of a phenomenon that recurs throughout the monograph:
in multi-agent problems, equilibrium multiplicity is the rule, and
therefore a solution concept alone never determines an outcome. The
single-agent case has a unique optimal value and so a learning algorithm
can be judged by how close it comes to it. In a general-sum repeated
game,
[Theorem 6.2](#S6.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 6.2 (Folk theorem, discounted case; , ). ‣ 6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
says that essentially every individually rational payoff is an
equilibrium payoff, so the statement that an algorithm converged to an
equilibrium carries almost no information. What carries information is
which equilibrium, and the criteria for preferring one, welfare,
fairness, robustness to exploitation, are imported from outside the
equilibrium concept. This is the formal content behind the objection of
[Shoham et al. (2007)](#bib.bib161) that the multi-agent learning
literature frequently leaves its question unstated.

A third connection is worth drawing because it is productive rather than
cautionary. A repeated game in which each agent’s strategy is
implemented by a finite automaton with $`k`$ states is a finite
stochastic game whose state is the tuple of automaton states, and the
best response to a fixed finite-automaton opponent is computable by
ordinary dynamic programming on a state space of size $`k^{n-1}`$. This
is the exact sense in which opponent modelling reduces multi-agent
learning to single-agent learning: if agent $`i`$ maintains a model of
the others as finite-state machines, then agent $`i`$ faces a Markov
decision process over the product of the physical state and the inferred
machine states, and if the model is uncertain, a partially observable
one, which is the subject of
[Section 12](#S12 "12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The reduction is exact when the model class contains the truth and the
others are not learning, and it degrades gracefully in the first respect
and catastrophically in the second.

###### Example 6.6 (Best responding to tit-for-tat is a two-state Markov decision problem).

Fix the opponent to tit-for-tat and let the state be what the opponent
will play next, which under tit-for-tat is what we played last. The
state space is $`\{C,D\}`$, the action space is $`\{C,D\}`$, the
transition is deterministic and sends the state to the action just
taken, and the rewards are the stage payoffs of
[Example 3.4](#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Example 3.4 (The four games that this monograph keeps returning to). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
With $`\gamma=0.6`$, value iteration on this two-state Markov decision
process returns $`V(C)=7.8125`$ and $`V(D)=4.6875`$ with the greedy
action being $`D`$ at the state $`C`$ and $`C`$ at the state $`D`$,
which is exactly the alternating deviation of
[Example 6.5](#S6.Thmtheorem5 "Example 6.5 (Tit-for-tat needs more patience than grim trigger). ‣ 6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The computation takes four numbers and reproduces a conclusion that the
repeated-game argument reaches with an infinite-horizon comparison,
which is the point: once the opponent is a fixed finite automaton,
nothing multi-agent remains and the entire single-agent toolbox applies
verbatim. The hypothesis that fails in a real training run is the
fixity, and
[Section 23](#S23 "23 Correspondences that fail, and the theorems that forbid them ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is devoted to its consequences. $`\lozenge`$

Imperfect monitoring is where repeated games become genuinely harder and
where the reinforcement learning reading changes. Under perfect
monitoring a deviation is detected with certainty, so punishment can be
triggered without error. Under imperfect public monitoring the agents
observe only a noisy signal of the joint action, so a punishment rule
must sometimes punish an innocent partner, and sustaining cooperation
requires balancing the deterrent effect of punishment against its cost
on the equilibrium path. The recursive characterisation of the
equilibrium payoff set by [Abreu et al. (1990)](#bib.bib2) is the
dynamic-programming formulation of exactly that balance: the set of
equilibrium payoffs is the largest self-generating set, which is a fixed
point of a set-valued operator and therefore the set analogue of
[Equation 1](#S2.E1 "In 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The reinforcement learning counterpart is the observation that a value
function over sets of payoff vectors, rather than over scalars, is what
a general-sum sequential problem requires, which is also the shape taken
by the occupancy-state reduction of
[Section 9](#S9 "9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and by the distributional view of multi-objective control.

Social dilemmas repeated in time, rather than in a matrix, are the form
in which this material reaches most practitioners. The empirical study
of cooperation in the iterated prisoner’s dilemma begins with [Axelrod
and Hamilton (1981)](#bib.bib12), whose tournaments established that
simple reciprocating strategies outperform both unconditional
cooperation and unconditional defection against a varied population. A
sequential social dilemma ([Leibo et al., 2017](#bib.bib105)) is a
Markov game in which the temporally extended policies play the roles of
cooperation and defection, so that the dilemma is a property of the
induced empirical game over policies rather than of any single state.
The methodological consequence is that the analysis belongs to
[Section 20](#S20 "20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"):
one estimates the empirical payoff matrix over a finite set of trained
policies and examines whether it has the structure of
[Example 3.4](#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Example 3.4 (The four games that this monograph keeps returning to). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
which is what the evaluation suites in this area do ([Leibo et al.,
2021](#bib.bib106)). The dilemma is thus not assumed but measured, and
the measurement is a normal-form game extracted from a Markov game.

One further construction belongs here because it shows that the repeated
setting admits strategies with no single-agent analogue at all. In the
iterated prisoner’s dilemma a memory-one strategy can enforce a linear
relation between its own long-run payoff and the opponent’s, regardless
of what the opponent does, and in particular can enforce a fixed
extortionate ratio of surpluses ([Press and Dyson, 2012](#bib.bib143)).
The construction works by choosing the four conditional cooperation
probabilities so that a certain determinant vanishes, which makes the
stationary distribution of the induced Markov chain satisfy the desired
linear constraint identically. The reinforcement learning reading is
that an opponent which adapts to maximise its own return will, against
such a strategy, be steered to a point chosen by the extorter, so
opponent shaping in the sense of
[Section 14](#S14 "14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is available even to a memoryless policy with four parameters. This is
the sharpest available counterexample to the idea that a small policy
class implies a weak agent, and it belongs in the same discussion as the
folk theorem because both are statements that memory, however little,
changes the strategic situation qualitatively.

| Monitoring                       | Induced model                        | Policy class needed           | Sustainable outcomes                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| -------------------------------- | ------------------------------------ | ----------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| None (one shot)                  | Normal-form game; bandit if $`n=1`$  | Stateless                     | Stage Nash equilibria                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| Perfect, finite horizon          | Stochastic game on the history tree  | Finite memory                 | Stage Nash by backward induction if unique                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| Perfect, discounted infinite     | Stochastic game on the history tree  | Unbounded or recurrent memory | All of $`F^{\star}`$ ([Theorem 6.2](#S6.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 6.2 (Folk theorem, discounted case; , ). ‣ 6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")) |
| Imperfect public                 | POSG with public state               | Memory over public signals    | Perfect public equilibria; self-generating sets ([Abreu et al., 1990](#bib.bib2))                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| Imperfect private                | POSG with private histories          | Belief over others’ histories | Sequential equilibria; hard                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| Fixed finite-automaton opponents | MDP over product of automaton states | Markov in the product state   | Best response, computable                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| Memory-one, zero determinant     | Four-parameter stationary policy     | One bit of memory             | Enforced linear payoff relation ([Press and Dyson, 2012](#bib.bib143))                                                                                                                                                                                                               |

Table 10: Repeated games and their reinforcement learning reading. The
policy class column indicates what the agent’s policy must condition on;
the equilibrium column indicates what is sustainable.

## 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence

The stochastic game of [Shapley (1953a)](#bib.bib158) is the object from
which almost everything else in this monograph is obtained by
specialisation or by relaxation. It is also the model for which the
correspondence with reinforcement learning is tightest, because the
Markov decision process is literally its one-agent instance.

The two readings coincide so nearly here that the only thing worth
flagging is the vocabulary. What a game theorist calls a stochastic game
a reinforcement learning researcher calls a Markov game, and the two
names denote the same tuple; the first emphasises that the transitions
are random, the second that they are memoryless, and both properties are
in the definition. What a game theorist calls a stationary strategy
profile a reinforcement learning researcher calls a joint policy, and
what the first calls the value of the game at a state the second calls
the value function. The one genuine difference is the object that the
Bellman update applies at each state: a maximum in the single-agent
case, and a solution operator on a payoff tensor in the multi-agent
case. Every algorithmic question in this section is a question about
that operator.

| Game theory                                   | Reinforcement learning                      | How to read the pair                            |
| --------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------- |
| Stochastic game, Markov game                  | Multi-agent Markov decision process         | The same tuple under two names                  |
| State $`s\in\mathcal{S}`$                     | State; observation when fully observed      | What the transition kernel depends on           |
| Stationary strategy $`\pi\_{i}(\cdot\,        | \,s)`$                                      | Policy of agent $`i`$                           | A row of probabilities per state |
| Value of the game at $`s`$                    | $`V_{i}^{\pi}(s)`$                          | Discounted return of agent $`i`$ from $`s`$     |
| Stage game $`G_{s}[V]`$                       | Joint-action critic cross-section at $`s`$  | Reward plus discounted continuation             |
| Solution operator $`\mathrm{sol}_{i}`$        | What replaces $`\max`$ in the backup        | $`\max`$, $`\mathrm{val}`$, or a Nash selection |
| Shapley operator $`\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{mm}}`$ | Minimax Bellman backup                      | Solve a matrix game at every state              |
| Markov perfect equilibrium                    | Stationary joint policy, each one greedy    | Nobody gains by changing its policy             |
| Team Markov game, MMDP                        | Common-payoff fully observed MARL           | One reward, many action components              |
| Adversary choosing the kernel                 | Robust MDP uncertainty set                  | Nature as a second player                       |
| Interchangeability of equilibria              | Safety of mixing trained opponents          | Holds in zero-sum, fails otherwise              |
| Turn-based game                               | Alternating-move environment                | One agent acts per state                        |
| $`\omega`$-regular objective                  | Safety or liveness specification; shielding | A property of the whole trajectory              |

Table 11: Stochastic-game vocabulary read from both sides.

###### Definition 7.1 (Stochastic game, Markov game).

A stochastic game is a tuple

|     |                                                                                                                                                                             |     |
| --- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle G\;=\;\big(\mathcal{N},\;\mathcal{S},\;\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}},\;\mathcal{T},\;\{\mathcal{R}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}},\;\gamma,\;b_{0}\big),`$ |     |

in which $`\mathcal{N}`$, $`\mathcal{S}`$ and $`b_{0}`$ are as before,
$`\mathcal{A}_{i}`$ is the action set of agent $`i`$ with
$`\mathcal{A}=\prod_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}`$ the joint action set,
$`\mathcal{T}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\Delta(\mathcal{S})`$ is
the transition kernel, and
$`\mathcal{R}_{i}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}`$
is the reward function of agent $`i`$. Agents act simultaneously and all
observe the state. A stationary policy of agent $`i`$ is
$`\pi_{i}:\mathcal{S}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})`$ and the value of a
profile is
$`V_{i}^{\pi}(s)=\mathbb{E}[\sum_{t}\gamma^{t}\mathcal{R}_{i}(s_{t},a_{t},s_{t+1})\,|\,s_{0}=s]`$.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. Compare
[Definition 7.1](#S7.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 7.1 (Stochastic game, Markov game). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
with
[Definition 2.1](#S2.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 2.1 (Markov decision process). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
symbol by symbol. The state space, the transition kernel, the discount
and the initial distribution are unchanged. Two things are replaced. The
single action set $`\mathcal{A}`$ becomes a product
$`\prod_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}`$, so the environment’s step function now
takes a tuple and the policy is split across $`n`$ heads, each of which
controls one coordinate and sees none of the others at the moment of
choosing. The single reward $`\mathcal{R}`$ becomes a vector
$`(\mathcal{R}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{R}_{n})`$, so the environment returns
one number per head. That is the entire difference, and everything hard
about multi-agent reinforcement learning follows from those two
substitutions. The value $`V_{i}^{\pi}`$ is the ordinary discounted
return of head $`i`$ under the joint policy $`\pi`$, and the superscript
matters: there is one value function per agent and all of them depend on
all of the policies, so no single scalar is being optimised and the
machinery of
[Section 19](#S19 "19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
replaces the machinery of optimisation. A practitioner who writes a
joint-action critic $`Q_{i}(s,a_{1},\dots,a_{n})`$ has written down
exactly this object.

###### Example 7.2 (Evaluating a fixed joint policy, and why it is not the value of the game).

Let $`\mathcal{S}=\{0,1\}`$, let each agent have two actions, let every
joint action move the state to the other one, and let $`\gamma=0.9`$.
The row agent’s stage payoff is matching pennies at state $`0`$ and
$`\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&3\\
2&-1\end{smallmatrix}\big)`$ at state $`1`$, and the column agent’s is
the negative of it. Fix the stationary profile in which the row agent
plays its first action with probability $`0.8`$ at both states and the
column agent plays its first action with probability $`0.25`$ at both.

Policy evaluation is two steps. First collapse each stage game to a
scalar by averaging over the profile, which is
[Example 3.2](#S3.Thmtheorem2 "Example 3.2 (Evaluating a joint policy, term by term). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
applied twice: at state $`0`$ the expected stage payoff is
$`0.8(0.25-0.75)+0.2(-0.25+0.75)=-0.3`$, and at state $`1`$ it is
$`0.8(0.75\cdot 3)+0.2(0.25\cdot 2-0.75)=1.75`$. Second solve the two
linear equations $`V(0)=-0.3+0.9\,V(1)`$ and $`V(1)=1.75+0.9\,V(0)`$,
which give $`V(0)=6.711`$ and $`V(1)=7.789`$.

Now compare with
[Example 7.7](#S7.Thmtheorem7 "Example 7.7 (Minimax value iteration on a two-state game, by hand). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
which computes the value of the same game to be $`4.737`$ at state
$`0`$. The fixed profile earns the row agent $`1.974`$ more than the
value of the game. There is no contradiction: the value is what the row
agent can guarantee against a best-responding opponent, and this column
agent is not best responding. The lesson is the one that
[Table 39](#S22.T39 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
turns into a measurement rule: $`V^{\pi}`$ reports how a pair of
policies did against each other and says nothing about either of them
separately, so a return curve from self-play is a property of the pair.
The quantity that is a property of the row agent alone is the value it
obtains against a best response, and computing it requires solving a
Markov decision process, not reading a log. $`\lozenge`$

###### Proposition 7.3 (Degeneracies of the stochastic game).

Let $`G`$ be a stochastic game. Then the following hold. First, if
$`n=1`$ then $`G`$ is a Markov decision process in the sense of
[Definition 2.1](#S2.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 2.1 (Markov decision process). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
with the same state space, action space, kernel, reward and discount.
Second, if $`\left\lvert\mathcal{S}\right\rvert=1`$ then $`G`$ is a
repeated normal-form game in the sense of
[Section 6](#S6 "6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
restricted to stationary strategies. Third, if
$`\mathcal{R}_{i}=\mathcal{R}_{j}`$ for all $`i,j`$ then the centralised
control problem over the joint action space, namely the Markov decision
process
$`(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{R},\gamma,b_{0})`$, has
optimal value equal to the maximal value attainable by any joint policy
of $`G`$, and a deterministic optimal stationary joint policy exists.

The third clause is the multi-agent Markov decision process of
[Boutilier (1996)](#bib.bib23), and it is the statement that cooperative
multi-agent planning under full observability is a single-agent problem.
It is also the statement that is most frequently over-read. The
reduction preserves the optimal value and the set of optimal joint
policies, and it says nothing about whether $`n`$ independently acting
agents will jointly realise one of them. If the optimal joint action at
a state is not unique, and each agent independently selects a component
of some optimal joint action, the resulting joint action need not be
optimal, and in a game such as the penalty game of [Claus and Boutilier
(1998)](#bib.bib34) it can be arbitrarily bad. We state this precisely
because the gap is the entire subject of equilibrium selection in
cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning.

###### Remark 7.4 (The reduction preserves value but not decentralisability).

Let $`G`$ be a common-payoff stochastic game and $`M`$ the induced
joint-action Markov decision process. The map $`\pi\mapsto\pi`$ from
joint policies of $`G`$ to policies of $`M`$ is value-preserving and
surjective onto the product policies of $`M`$ only if the policy of
$`M`$ factorises. An optimal policy of $`M`$ chosen arbitrarily need not
factorise as $`\pi(a\,|\,s)=\prod_{i}\pi_{i}(a_{i}\,|\,s)`$, and even
when an optimal factorised policy exists, there is in general no
decentralised rule by which each agent can select its component without
a shared tie-breaking convention or a shared random seed. The reduction
is therefore exact for the planning problem under centralised execution,
and it is a structural analogy only for decentralised execution.

###### Example 7.5 (A penalty game in which greedy learners select the worst equilibrium).

Two agents share the payoff

|     |                                  |     |
| --- | -------------------------------- | --- | --- | --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
|     | $`\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r | c   | c   | c   | }\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil a*{2}=0\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil a*{2}=1\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil a\_{2}=2\hfil\lx@intercol\\ |

\cline{2-4}\cr a*{1}=0&10&-20&0\\
\cline{2-4}\cr a*{1}=1&-20&6&0\\
\cline{2-4}\cr a\_{1}=2&0&0&4\\
\cline{2-4}\cr\end{array},`$ | |

a single number per cell because the game is common-payoff. The
joint-action Markov decision process of
[Proposition 7.3](#S7.Thmtheorem3 "Proposition 7.3 (Degeneracies of the stochastic game). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
has optimal value $`10`$, attained at $`(0,0)`$, and a centralised
controller that issues joint actions reaches it immediately. Independent
learners do not. The game has three pure equilibria, $`(0,0)`$,
$`(1,1)`$ and $`(2,2)`$, worth $`10`$, $`6`$ and $`4`$. Consider an
agent whose partner is still exploring uniformly. Its expected payoffs
are $`\tfrac{1}{3}(10-20+0)=-3.33`$ for action $`0`$,
$`\tfrac{1}{3}(-20+6+0)=-4.67`$ for action $`1`$, and
$`\tfrac{1}{3}(0+0+4)=1.33`$ for action $`2`$. The greedy action under
an exploring partner is therefore the one that leads to the worst
equilibrium, and a pair of independent $`Q`$-learners that bootstrap
from such estimates converges to $`(2,2)`$ and collects $`4`$ instead of
$`10`$, a shortfall of $`6`$ on a problem whose centralised solution is
trivial.

The mechanism is visible in the spreads. Action $`0`$ has outcomes
ranging over $`30`$ units and action $`2`$ over $`4`$, so averaging over
partner uncertainty penalises the action that attains the optimum far
more than the cautious one. This is relative overgeneralisation, and it
is the reason the algorithmic responses of [Lauer and Riedmiller
(2000)](#bib.bib102), [Matignon et al. (2007)](#bib.bib113) and [Panait
et al. (2006)](#bib.bib135) all amount to replacing the average over
partner behaviour by something closer to a maximum: optimistic
initialisation, hysteretic learning rates that raise estimates faster
than they lower them, and leniency that discards a proportion of the
worst observed outcomes. Each is an equilibrium-selection device aimed
at this computation, and each is justified by it rather than by any
convergence theorem. $`\lozenge`$

When $`n=2`$ and $`\mathcal{R}_{2}=-\mathcal{R}_{1}`$, the solution
operator of
[Definition 3.6](#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Definition 3.6 (Induced stage game). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the minimax value of a matrix game, and everything works.

###### Theorem 7.6 ([Shapley, 1953a](#bib.bib158)).

Let $`G`$ be a two-player zero-sum discounted stochastic game and define
$`\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{mm}}:\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{S}}`$
by

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |                                                                          |     |
| --- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle(\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{mm}}V)(s)\;=\;\max_{\pi*{1}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}*{1})}\;\min*{\pi*{2}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{2})}\;\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi*{1}\times\pi*{2}}\mathbb{E}\_{s^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{T}(\cdot\, | \,s,a)}\Big[\mathcal{R}_{1}(s,a,s^{\prime})+\gamma V(s^{\prime})\Big].`$ |     | (9) |

Then $`\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{mm}}`$ is a $`\gamma`$-contraction in the
supremum norm, its unique fixed point $`V^{\star}`$ is the value of
$`G`$, both agents possess optimal stationary mixed policies obtained by
solving the matrix game in
[Equation 9](#S7.E9 "In Theorem 7.6 ( , ). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
at $`V^{\star}`$, and value iteration
$`V^{k+1}=\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{mm}}V^{k}`$ converges geometrically.

###### Example 7.7 (Minimax value iteration on a two-state game, by hand).

Let $`\mathcal{S}=\{0,1\}`$, let each agent have two actions, let every
joint action move the state to the other one, and let $`\gamma=0.9`$.
The stage payoff to the row agent is matching pennies at state $`0`$ and
$`\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&3\\
2&-1\end{smallmatrix}\big)`$ at state $`1`$. Because the transition does
not depend on the action,
[Equation 9](#S7.E9 "In Theorem 7.6 ( , ). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
separates into $`V(s)=\mathrm{val}(A_{s})+\gamma V(1-s)`$, where
$`\mathrm{val}`$ is the value of the stage matrix. The matching-pennies
matrix has value $`0`$, and the second matrix has value $`1`$, obtained
by equating $`2-2p`$ and $`4p-1`$ at $`p=1/2`$. Substituting,
$`V(0)=0+0.9V(1)`$ and $`V(1)=1+0.9V(0)`$, so $`V(0)=0.9(1+0.9V(0))`$,
giving $`V(0)=0.9/0.19=4.7368`$ and $`V(1)=5.2632`$. Running
$`\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{mm}}`$ numerically from $`V=0`$ reproduces these
to machine precision. The equilibrium policies are
$`(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})`$ for both agents at state $`0`$, and at
state $`1`$ the row agent plays $`(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})`$ while
the column agent plays $`(\tfrac{2}{3},\tfrac{1}{3})`$. Two things are
worth noticing. First, the policies are mixed at every state, so a
tabular $`Q`$-learner with greedy action selection cannot represent the
solution, which is
[Example 3.8](#S3.Thmtheorem8 "Example 3.8 (Why matching pennies has no deterministic solution). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
lifted to the sequential case. Second, the backup at each state is a
linear program rather than a maximum, and this is the only change to the
single-agent algorithm; the contraction, the convergence rate and the
stopping criterion are unchanged. That is the whole content of
minimax-Q. $`\lozenge`$

The proof rests on the fact that the value functional of a matrix game
is non-expansive,
$`\left\lvert\mathrm{val}(A)-\mathrm{val}(B)\right\rvert\leq\left\lVert A-B\right\rVert_{\infty}`$,
which lifts the contraction property of the single-agent operator
verbatim. The reinforcement learning algorithm obtained by replacing the
expectation in
[Equation 9](#S7.E9 "In Theorem 7.6 ( , ). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
by a sample is minimax-Q ([Littman, 1994](#bib.bib108)); its convergence
under the usual Robbins-Monro conditions follows from the contraction
property through the general stochastic-approximation analysis of
[Szepesvári and Littman (1999)](#bib.bib175), and is not established in
the paper that introduces the algorithm. Comparison with
[Equation 3](#S2.E3 "In 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
gives the following identity, which we state because it is the clearest
case in this work of two literatures writing down the same operator.

###### Proposition 7.8 (Robust control is a zero-sum stochastic game; [Iyengar, 2005](#bib.bib84); [Nilim and El Ghaoui, 2005](#bib.bib130); [Wiesemann et al., 2013](#bib.bib192)).

Let $`M_{\mathrm{rob}}`$ be a robust Markov decision process with
$`(s,a)`$-rectangular uncertainty set $`\mathcal{P}`$, each
$`\mathcal{P}(s,a)`$ convex and compact. Then $`M_{\mathrm{rob}}`$ is
the two-player zero-sum stochastic game in which agent $`1`$ chooses
$`a\in\mathcal{A}`$, agent $`2`$ observes $`a`$ and chooses
$`p\in\mathcal{P}(s,a)`$, the next state is drawn from $`p`$, and
$`\mathcal{R}_{2}=-\mathcal{R}_{1}`$; the operators
[Equation 3](#S2.E3 "In 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and
[Equation 9](#S7.E9 "In Theorem 7.6 ( , ). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
coincide and the optimal robust policy is the equilibrium policy of
agent $`1`$. If instead $`\mathcal{P}`$ is only $`s`$-rectangular, so
that the adversary must commit to a kernel before observing $`a`$, the
induced game is simultaneous-move, the value still exists by the minimax
theorem, and the optimal robust policy is in general stochastic
([Wiesemann et al., 2013](#bib.bib192)).

The second clause has a concrete consequence: robust reinforcement
learning under $`s`$-rectangular uncertainty requires randomised
policies while the $`(s,a)`$-rectangular case does not, and the reason
is the non-existence of pure equilibria in matching pennies, imported
into control theory. Adversarial reinforcement learning methods that
train a protagonist against a learned disturbance agent ([Pinto et al.,
2017](#bib.bib142); [Morimoto and Doya, 2005](#bib.bib123)) are
instantiating this game, and whether their fixed point is meaningful
depends on whether the adversary’s action set corresponds to a
rectangular uncertainty set.

###### Example 7.9 (Who moves first in the uncertainty set).

Suppose a state offers two actions and the adversary may set the
probability of a good outcome within $`[0.6,0.9]`$ for action $`a`$ and
within $`[0.7,0.7]`$ for action $`b`$, the good outcome being worth
$`10`$. Under $`(s,a)`$-rectangularity the adversary sees the action and
picks $`0.6`$ for $`a`$, so the protagonist compares $`6.0`$ with
$`7.0`$ and plays $`b`$ deterministically; this is
[Example 2.6](#S2.Thmtheorem6 "Example 2.6 (The robust and the nominal choice disagree). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
again, read as a game. Under $`s`$-rectangularity the adversary must
commit to one kernel before seeing the action, and the protagonist may
randomise to make the commitment less effective. The modelling question
that decides which applies is whether the disturbance can react to the
control, which in a learned adversarial setup is decided by whether the
adversary network receives the protagonist’s action as an input. That
single wiring choice determines whether the trained protagonist should
be expected to be deterministic or stochastic, and it is the kind of
design detail that the game-theoretic reading makes visible and that an
unstructured reading does not. $`\lozenge`$

For general-sum discounted stochastic games, [Fink (1964)](#bib.bib55)
and [Takahashi (1964)](#bib.bib176) establish that a stationary Markov
perfect equilibrium exists, by a fixed-point argument on the product of
stationary policy spaces. Existence is all that transfers. The natural
algorithmic analogue of
[Theorem 7.6](#S7.Thmtheorem6 "Theorem 7.6 ( , ). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is to define an operator that replaces $`\mathrm{val}`$ by a Nash value
of the stage game $`G_{s}[V]`$, which is the Nash-Q algorithm of [Hu and
Wellman (2003)](#bib.bib83), and it fails for three distinct reasons
that are worth separating. First, the stage game may have several
equilibria with different payoffs, so the operator is set-valued and
requires an exogenous selection rule on which all agents must agree.
Second, even with a selection rule the resulting operator is not
non-expansive, so the contraction argument is unavailable; the
convergence theorem for Nash-Q assumes that either every stage game
encountered during learning has a global optimum, or every stage game
encountered has a saddle point, with all agents consistently selecting
equilibria of that one type; the disjunction is over the whole run, it
cannot be verified in advance, and it effectively restricts the result
to the cooperative and zero-sum cases. Third, and most fundamentally,
[Zinkevich et al. (2005)](#bib.bib200) prove that no algorithm which
extracts a stationary policy from $`Q`$-values alone can compute
stationary Nash equilibria of general-sum stochastic games, by
exhibiting pairs of games with identical $`Q`$-functions but different
unique stationary equilibria, so that no policy-extraction rule can be
correct in both. The friend-or-foe algorithm of [Littman
(2001)](#bib.bib109) responds by requiring the designer to declare each
opponent cooperative or adversarial, thereby forcing the stage game into
one of the two solvable classes, and the correlated-Q family of
[Greenwald and Hall (2003)](#bib.bib69) responds by replacing the Nash
operator with a correlated-equilibrium operator, which restores
computability of the stage solution by linear programming while leaving
the selection and non-expansiveness problems in place.

###### Example 7.10 (The stage operator is the only thing that changes).

Take the stage game of
[Example 3.7](#S3.Thmtheorem7 "Example 3.7 (A stage game built from bootstrapped values). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
whose payoff tensor at the state in question is
$`\big(\begin{smallmatrix}(9,0)&(0,9)\\
(0,9)&(0,9)\end{smallmatrix}\big)`$. A single-agent backup would report
$`\max=9`$ for agent $`1`$. A minimax backup is undefined because the
game is not zero-sum. A Nash backup must choose among the equilibria,
and enumeration gives exactly two, $`(0,1)`$ and $`(1,1)`$, each worth
$`0`$ to agent $`1`$; the profile $`(0,0)`$, which gives agent $`1`$ its
maximum of $`9`$, is not an equilibrium because agent $`2`$ gains by
deviating, and $`(1,0)`$ is not one because agent $`1`$ does. A
correlated-equilibrium backup is unconstrained by welfare, since every
cell of this tensor has welfare $`9`$, so the selection must come from
somewhere else. So three different backups return different numbers for
the same tensor, and the choice is not determined by the data. This is
the content of the sentence that there is no canonical multi-agent
Bellman operator, and it is why
[Table 12](#S7.T12 "In 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
has a column for the stage operator rather than taking it for granted.
$`\lozenge`$

The most widely used reduction in practice is to ignore the game
structure and run a single-agent algorithm per agent. The following
proposition states exactly when that is sound.

###### Proposition 7.11 (Fixed others induce a Markov decision process).

Let $`G`$ be a stochastic game and fix stationary policies $`\pi_{j}`$
for all $`j\neq i`$. Then agent $`i`$ faces the Markov decision process
$`M_{i}[\pi_{-i}]=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{T}_{i},\mathcal{R}_{i}^{\,\pi_{-i}},\gamma,b_{0})`$
with

|     |                                              |                                                                  |                                |
| --- | -------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | ---- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\_{i}(s^{\prime}\, | \,s,a*{i})=\sum*{a*{-i}}\textstyle\prod*{j\neq i}\pi*{j}(a*{j}\, | \,s)\,\mathcal{T}(s^{\prime}\, | \,s,(a*{i},a*{-i})),\quad\mathcal{R}_{i}^{\,\pi_{-i}}(s,a*{i})=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{R}*{i}(s,(a*{i},a*{-i}),s^{\prime})\big],`$ |     | (10) |

and the optimal policy of $`M_{i}[\pi_{-i}]`$ is exactly a best response
$`\pi_{i}\in\mathrm{BR}_{i}(\pi_{-i})`$. If the $`\pi_{j}`$ are
history-dependent with finite internal memory states $`m_{j}`$, the same
holds with state space
$`\mathcal{S}\times\prod_{j\neq i}\mathcal{M}_{j}`$, and if the
$`m_{j}`$ are not observed by agent $`i`$ the induced model is a
partially observable Markov decision process with hidden component
$`(m_{j})_{j\neq i}`$.

[Proposition 7.11](#S7.Thmtheorem11 "Proposition 7.11 (Fixed others induce a Markov decision process). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is exact, and its hypothesis is violated in every simultaneous learning
run. When the $`\pi_{j}`$ change between episodes, the kernel and reward
of
[Equation 10](#S7.E10 "In Proposition 7.11 (Fixed others induce a Markov decision process). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
are time-varying, agent $`i`$’s problem is a non-stationary Markov
decision process, the Bellman operator it is implicitly iterating has a
moving fixed point, and the convergence guarantee of Q-learning does not
apply. This is the non-stationarity problem, and it is a failure of the
modelling assumption rather than a difficulty of implementation. Three
families of responses exist and are catalogued in
[Section 22](#S22 "22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"):
centralise the critic so that it conditions on the joint action and is
therefore stationary with respect to a fixed joint policy, which is the
centralised-training-decentralised-execution idea; model the others
explicitly and treat their parameters as part of the state, which
converts the problem into the partially observable one of
[Section 12](#S12 "12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning");
or abandon the fixed-point target and use a dynamics whose guarantee is
a regret bound rather than a convergence statement, which is the
no-regret route to coarse correlated equilibria.

A final specialisation deserves mention because it connects this
material to formal methods and to safe reinforcement learning. In a
turn-based stochastic game only one agent acts at each state, which
removes the need for mixing and makes backward induction applicable; the
objective may then be an $`\omega`$-regular property of the trajectory,
such as always avoiding a set of states or visiting a set infinitely
often, rather than a discounted sum. Games of this form are the standard
model of reactive synthesis, and the winning strategy of the protagonist
in a safety game is exactly the shield that filters a learned policy’s
actions in safe reinforcement learning ([Alshiekh et al.,
2018](#bib.bib5)). The correspondence is that the safety specification
is the adversary’s payoff, the environment is the adversary, and the
shield is the protagonist’s winning region computed by backward
induction on the game graph. The complexity of these problems is
collected with the others in
[Section 21](#S21 "21 The complexity landscape ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
where the turn-based and the simultaneous-move cases are seen to
separate sharply.

| Structure                      | Sound reduction                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | Stage operator                | Contraction            | Algorithm               |
| ------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------- | ---------------------- | ----------------------- |
| $`n=1`$                        | Identity; it is an MDP                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | $`\max`$                      | Yes                    | Q-learning              |
| Common payoff, centralised     | Joint-action MDP                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | $`\max`$ over $`\mathcal{A}`$ | Yes                    | Joint-action Q-learning |
| Common payoff, decentralised   | None exact; see [Remark 7.4](#S7.Thmtheorem4 "Remark 7.4 (The reduction preserves value but not decentralisability). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")                                                              | $`\max`$ plus tie-breaking    | Given a selection rule | Distributed Q, FMQ      |
| Two-player zero-sum            | Minimax-Bellman operator; a robust MDP when the adversary is rectangular ([Proposition 7.8](#S7.Thmtheorem8 "Proposition 7.8 (Robust control is a zero-sum stochastic game; , ). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")) | $`\mathrm{val}`$ (minimax)    | Yes                    | Minimax-Q               |
| Two-player constant-sum        | Affine reparametrisation of zero-sum                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | $`\mathrm{val}`$              | Yes                    | Minimax-Q               |
| General sum                    | None; existence only                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | Nash selection                | No                     | Nash-Q, CE-Q            |
| General sum, potential         | Potential MDP; see [Section 16](#S16 "16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")                                                                                                                     | $`\max`$ of $`\Phi`$          | Yes                    | Independent PG          |
| Fixed opponents                | Induced MDP ([Proposition 7.11](#S7.Thmtheorem11 "Proposition 7.11 (Fixed others induce a Markov decision process). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"))                                                              | $`\max`$                      | Yes                    | Any single-agent method |
| Turn-based, $`\omega`$-regular | Game graph; backward induction                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | Attractor computation         | Exact, finite          | Synthesis; shielding    |

Table 12: Stochastic games by reward structure: the sound single-agent
reduction, the solution operator entering the Bellman update, and
whether that operator is a contraction. The last column names the
canonical tabular algorithm.

## 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models

Removing the assumption that every agent observes the state generates
the richest and the most terminologically confused region of the map.
The confusion is avoidable, because the models differ along exactly two
axes, the reward structure and the observability structure, and
[Figure 1](#S1.F1 "In 1 Introduction ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the resulting two-dimensional grid. We give the definitions along
that grid and then state the inclusions as a proposition.

The two readings part company here more sharply than anywhere else, and
the split is terminological rather than mathematical. The planning
literature, which grew out of operations research, names its models by
what the agents cannot see and by the fact that they share a reward,
producing the decentralised partially observable Markov decision process
and its relatives; its question is to compute an optimal joint policy,
and its solution concept is optimality. The game theory literature names
the same tuple by the fact that the agents may want different things,
producing the partially observable stochastic game; its question is to
characterise equilibria, and its solution concept is equilibrium. A
common-payoff partially observable stochastic game and a decentralised
partially observable Markov decision process are the same tuple. The two
literatures ask different questions about it, and
[Proposition 8.6](#S8.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 8.6 (Optimality and equilibrium in common-payoff games). ‣ 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
relates the answers: every optimum is an equilibrium and most equilibria
are not optima.

| Game theory                          | Reinforcement learning                     | How to read the pair                           |
| ------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------- |
| Partially observable stochastic game | General-sum partially observable MARL      | Many agents, private observations, own rewards |
| Common-payoff POSG                   | Dec-POMDP                                  | The same tuple; one shared reward              |
| Jointly observable                   | Dec-MDP                                    | Together the agents see the state              |
| Private information $`p^{i}_{t}`$    | Agent $`i`$’s action-observation history   | What only agent $`i`$ knows                    |
| Common information $`c_{t}`$         | What is public; broadcast channel contents | What everybody knows everybody knows           |
| Interactive belief hierarchy         | Nested opponent models (I-POMDP)           | Beliefs about beliefs about beliefs            |
| Optimal joint policy                 | Team-optimal decentralised controller      | The planner’s answer                           |
| Nash equilibrium of the POSG         | Self-play fixed point                      | The learner’s answer                           |
| Equilibrium selection                | Convention, tie-breaking, shared seed      | Which of several good answers                  |
| Risk dominance                       | Outcome favoured under partner uncertainty | Safe when the partner may err                  |
| Payoff dominance                     | Pareto-best equilibrium                    | Best if coordination succeeds                  |
| Decentralised execution              | No communication at deployment             | Each policy sees only its own input            |

Table 13: Decentralised-model vocabulary read from both sides.

###### Definition 8.1 (Partially observable stochastic game).

A partially observable stochastic game is a tuple
$`G=(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{S},\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\},\{\mathcal{O}_{i}\},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z},\{\mathcal{R}_{i}\},\gamma,b_{0})`$
in which
$`(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{S},\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\},\mathcal{T},\{\mathcal{R}_{i}\},\gamma,b_{0})`$
is a stochastic game, $`\mathcal{O}_{i}`$ is the observation set of
agent $`i`$ with $`\mathcal{O}=\prod_{i}\mathcal{O}_{i}`$, and
$`\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\Delta(\mathcal{O})`$ is a
joint observation kernel, with $`\mathcal{Z}(o\,|\,s^{\prime},a)`$ the
probability that the agents jointly receive $`o=(o_{1},\dots,o_{n})`$
after joint action $`a`$ leads to state $`s^{\prime}`$. Agent $`i`$
observes only $`o_{i}`$ and its own actions, so its policy is a map
$`\pi_{i}:\mathcal{H}_{i}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})`$ on its own
action-observation histories
$`h_{i}^{t}=(o_{i}^{0},a_{i}^{0},\dots,a_{i}^{t-1},o_{i}^{t})`$.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. Take
[Definition 7.1](#S7.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 7.1 (Stochastic game, Markov game). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and give each head its own observation stream. The kernel
$`\mathcal{Z}`$ is the environment’s observation function, $`o_{i}`$ is
the tensor that arrives at head $`i`$, and $`h_{i}^{t}`$ is whatever
that head has accumulated, which in an implementation is a recurrent
hidden state or a stacked context window. The policy signature changes
from $`\pi_{i}:\mathcal{S}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})`$ to
$`\pi_{i}:\mathcal{H}_{i}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})`$, and that single
change is the difference between a feedforward and a recurrent
architecture. One consequence is worth stating before any theorem. In
[Definition 2.3](#S2.Thmtheorem3 "Definition 2.3 (Partially observable Markov decision process). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
a single agent could replace its history by a belief over the state,
because its own history carried all the information in the system about
that state. Here it cannot, because what agent $`i`$ needs in order to
predict the future includes what agent $`j`$ will do, which depends on
$`h_{j}^{t}`$, which agent $`i`$ has never seen. The hidden variable is
no longer the world state; it is the world state together with every
other agent’s history, and
[Proposition 8.4](#S8.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition 8.4 (Inclusions and degeneracies). ‣ 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
records what that costs.

The joint observation kernel is deliberately not assumed to factorise,
because correlated observation noise is common and because the
non-factorised form is what makes the model closed under the operations
we perform on it.

###### Definition 8.2 (Decentralised POMDP and decentralised MDP).

A decentralised partially observable Markov decision process is a
partially observable stochastic game with
$`\mathcal{R}_{i}=\mathcal{R}`$ for all $`i`$. It is a decentralised
Markov decision process when it is in addition jointly fully observable,
meaning that there exists a map $`\varphi:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{S}`$
with $`\mathbb{P}(s_{t}=\varphi(o^{t}))=1`$ for every reachable
$`(s_{t},o^{t})`$, so that the joint observation determines the state
even though no individual observation need do so.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. Setting
$`\mathcal{R}_{i}=\mathcal{R}`$ is one line of the environment: return
the same scalar to every head. That line changes the question being
asked, not the tuple. With distinct rewards there is no best joint
policy, only equilibria; with one shared reward there is a best joint
policy, and the whole apparatus of optimality returns. Joint full
observability is a second, independent line: it holds when concatenating
everyone’s observation would reconstruct the state, which is the
situation of a sensor network whose nodes together see everything and
individually see a slice. The two conditions are what separate the four
cooperative models of
[Figure 1](#S1.F1 "In 1 Introduction ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
and a practitioner can read off which one applies by answering two
yes-or-no questions about the simulator.
[Example 8.3](#S8.Thmtheorem3 "Example 8.3 (One problem, written out as the tuple). ‣ 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
answers them for a concrete problem.

###### Example 8.3 (One problem, written out as the tuple).

Instantiate
[Definition 8.1](#S8.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 8.1 (Partially observable stochastic game). ‣ 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
on the two-rescuer problem that
[Example 8.5](#S8.Thmtheorem5 "Example 8.5 (The two-rescuer problem). ‣ 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
will then solve. The agent set is $`\mathcal{N}=\{1,2\}`$. The state set
is $`\mathcal{S}=\{\mathrm{left},\mathrm{right}\}`$, recording which
door hides the victim, with $`b_{0}`$ uniform. Each agent’s action set
is $`\mathcal{A}_{i}=\{\mathrm{enter\ left},\mathrm{enter\ right}\}`$
and each observation set is
$`\mathcal{O}_{i}=\{\mathrm{signal\ left},\mathrm{signal\ right}\}`$.
The joint observation kernel factorises here,
$`\mathcal{Z}(o\,|\,s)=\prod_{i}\mathcal{Z}_{i}(o_{i}\,|\,s)`$ with each
signal correct with probability $`0.8`$, so it is an array of
$`2\times 4=8`$ numbers. The rewards are equal,
$`\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{R}_{2}`$, with value $`10`$ when both agents
enter the right door, $`-10`$ when both enter the wrong one and a
penalty when they split.

Now answer the two questions. Are the rewards equal? Yes, so by
[Definition 8.2](#S8.Thmtheorem2 "Definition 8.2 (Decentralised POMDP and decentralised MDP). ‣ 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
this is a decentralised partially observable Markov decision process and
the optimal joint policy is a well-posed object. Does the joint
observation determine the state? No: both signals are wrong
simultaneously with probability $`0.2\times 0.2=0.04`$, and after a pair
of left signals the posterior on the left door is
$`0.8^{2}/(0.8^{2}+0.2^{2})=0.941`$, not $`1`$. So it is not a
decentralised Markov decision process, and the model sits in the
Dec-POMDP row of
[Table 14](#S8.T14 "In 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
rather than the Dec-MDP row. Raise the sensor accuracy to $`1`$ and the
second answer flips, the posterior becomes degenerate, and the problem
drops a complexity row. That is the entire content of the distinction,
and it is decided by one number in the observation kernel. $`\lozenge`$

###### Proposition 8.4 (Inclusions and degeneracies).

The following hold and are exhibited in
[Figure 1](#S1.F1 "In 1 Introduction ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
A partially observable stochastic game with $`n=1`$ is a partially
observable Markov decision process. A decentralised partially observable
Markov decision process with $`n=1`$ is a partially observable Markov
decision process. A partially observable stochastic game in which
$`\mathcal{O}_{i}=\mathcal{S}`$ and
$`\mathcal{Z}(o\,|\,s^{\prime},a)=\mathbf{1}[o_{i}=s^{\prime}\ \forall i]`$
is a stochastic game. A decentralised Markov decision process with
$`n=1`$ is a Markov decision process. The class inclusions
$`\mathrm{MDP}\subset\mathrm{MMDP}\subset\mathrm{SG}\subset\mathrm{POSG}`$
and
$`\mathrm{MDP}\subset\mathrm{Dec\text{-}MDP}\subset\mathrm{Dec\text{-}POMDP}\subset\mathrm{POSG}`$
hold, and $`\mathrm{Dec\text{-}POMDP}`$ and $`\mathrm{SG}`$ are
incomparable, their intersection being $`\mathrm{MMDP}`$.

Two consequences of
[Definition 8.1](#S8.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 8.1 (Partially observable stochastic game). ‣ 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
govern everything that follows. The first is that there is no belief
state. In a single-agent partially observable Markov decision process
the agent’s belief $`\mathbb{P}(s_{t}\,|\,h_{t})`$ is a sufficient
statistic because the agent’s own history is all the information in the
system that bears on the hidden state. In a partially observable
stochastic game, what agent $`i`$ needs in order to predict the future
is a belief over the state and over the other agents’ histories jointly,
because the others’ future actions depend on their histories; and the
others’ beliefs about agent $`i`$’s history matter in turn, generating
the infinite hierarchy of interactive beliefs formalised by
[Gmytrasiewicz and Doshi (2005)](#bib.bib67) in the interactive
partially observable Markov decision process. There is no finite
sufficient statistic in general, and this is the structural reason why
decentralised models are harder than their centralised counterparts by
an entire complexity class rather than by a constant factor. The lower
bounds in the last column of
[Table 14](#S8.T14 "In 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
for the two general-sum rows are inherited from the common-payoff
restriction and therefore concern the team-optimal joint policy; the
equilibrium problems of a partially observable stochastic game are not
optimisation problems and are classified separately in
[Section 21](#S21 "21 The complexity landscape ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The finitely nested interactive partially observable Markov decision
process is PSPACE-hard at a finite horizon and undecidable at an
infinite one, exactly as in the single-agent case ([Gmytrasiewicz and
Doshi, 2005](#bib.bib67)).

The smallest problem on which all of this is visible has one time step,
two agents and two states, and we use it repeatedly.

###### Example 8.5 (The two-rescuer problem).

A victim is behind the left or the right door with equal probability.
Two rescuers each receive a private signal that is correct with
probability $`0.8`$. Each must enter a door. If both enter the door with
the victim they score $`10`$, if both enter the empty door they score
$`-10`$, and if they split up they score a penalty $`p`$. The reward is
shared, so this is a one-step decentralised partially observable Markov
decision process in the sense of
[Definition 8.2](#S8.Thmtheorem2 "Definition 8.2 (Decentralised POMDP and decentralised MDP). ‣ 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
and each agent’s type is its own signal, which is
[Proposition 9.9](#S9.Thmtheorem9 "Proposition 9.9 (Stage-wise Bayesian game reduction; , ). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
in its smallest instance.

First compute what a centralised planner that sees both signals would
do. The posterior that the victim is on the left given two left signals
is $`0.8^{2}/(0.8^{2}+0.2^{2})=0.9412`$; given one signal of each kind
it is exactly $`0.5`$. Following the posterior majority, and breaking
the tie arbitrarily, gives an expected score of $`6.0`$.

Now take $`p=-20`$. There are four decision rules per agent, from the
constant rules to following or inverting the signal. Enumerating the
sixteen pairs, the best achievable value is $`0`$, attained by both
agents ignoring their signals and always entering the same fixed door;
following the signal gives $`-0.4`$, which is worse than ignoring it.
The entire informational advantage of the private signals is destroyed
by the risk of splitting up, and the gap to the centralised planner is
the full $`6.0`$.

Now take $`p=-2`$. The best pair of decision rules is for both agents to
follow their signals, which yields $`5.36`$, against $`0`$ for ignoring
them, and the gap to the planner falls to $`0.64`$. The qualitative
conclusion is that whether private information is worth using at all is
decided by the cost of miscoordination, not by the quality of the
information: the signal accuracy was $`0.8`$ in both cases. A
practitioner reading a decentralised benchmark should therefore ask what
the miscoordination penalty is before asking whether the agents learned
to use their observations, because in the first regime the optimal
decentralised policy ignores them and a learner that discovers this is
behaving correctly. $`\lozenge`$

The second consequence is that the optimality criterion must be
re-examined. In a decentralised partially observable Markov decision
process the objective is unambiguous, since all agents share a reward,
and the planning problem is to find a joint policy
$`\pi=(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{n})`$ maximising the common value. This is a
well-posed optimisation problem rather than an equilibrium problem,
which is why the decentralised partially observable Markov decision
process literature speaks of optimal joint policies while the partially
observable stochastic game literature speaks of equilibria. The relation
between the two is that every optimal joint policy of a decentralised
partially observable Markov decision process is a Nash equilibrium of
the corresponding partially observable stochastic game, and the converse
fails badly: the set of Nash equilibria of a common-payoff game includes
every profile from which no agent can unilaterally improve, and in a
coordination problem that set contains arbitrarily poor profiles. This
single inclusion, optimum implies equilibrium but not conversely, is the
precise statement of why independent learners in cooperative tasks
converge to suboptimal conventions.

###### Proposition 8.6 (Optimality and equilibrium in common-payoff games).

Let $`G`$ be a decentralised partially observable Markov decision
process and $`\pi^{\star}`$ an optimal joint policy. Then
$`\pi^{\star}`$ is a Nash equilibrium of $`G`$ viewed as a partially
observable stochastic game. The converse is false: there exist
