common-payoff games with Nash equilibria whose value is strictly smaller
than the optimum, and the gap can be made arbitrarily large.

###### Proof.

If some agent $`i`$ had a profitable unilateral deviation
$`\pi_{i}^{\prime}`$ from $`\pi^{\star}`$, then because all rewards
coincide the joint policy $`(\pi_{i}^{\prime},\pi^{\star}_{-i})`$ would
have strictly larger common value, contradicting optimality. For the
converse, take the two-agent single-state game with
$`\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathcal{A}_{2}=\{0,1\}`$, $`u(0,0)=0`$, $`u(1,1)=K`$
and $`u(a)=-P`$ otherwise. The profile $`(0,0)`$ is a Nash equilibrium
for $`P>0`$, since a unilateral move to $`1`$ yields $`-P<0`$, and its
value is $`0`$ while the optimum is $`K`$. ∎

The game in the proof is a two-action instance of the penalty structure
studied by [Claus and Boutilier (1998)](#bib.bib34), and it isolates the
phenomenon in its minimal form: the suboptimal equilibrium is sustained
by the risk an agent incurs when it unilaterally departs from a
convention, with no conflict of interest anywhere in the game. Risk
dominance and payoff dominance are the game-theoretic names for the two
criteria that conflict here ([Harsanyi and Selten, 1988](#bib.bib74)),
and independent learners with exploration converge to the risk-dominant
equilibrium under a wide range of conditions, which is exactly the
behaviour reported empirically under the name of relative
overgeneralisation ([Wei and Luke, 2016](#bib.bib189)). The algorithmic
responses, optimistic initialisation, lenient learning, hysteretic
learning and distributed Q-learning ([Lauer and Riedmiller,
2000](#bib.bib102); [Matignon et al., 2007](#bib.bib113); [Panait
et al., 2006](#bib.bib135)), are all attempts to bias the dynamics
toward the payoff-dominant equilibrium, and they are best understood as
equilibrium selection devices rather than as learning improvements.

###### Example 8.7 (Why an arbitrary convention counts as an equilibrium).

Suppose two agents must each send one of two symbols, and they score
$`1`$ if the first agent’s symbol equals the second agent’s and $`0`$
otherwise. Both “always send $`0`$” and “always send $`1`$” are optimal
joint policies, and so is any pair that agrees. Now suppose two
independent training runs produce the first convention and the second
respectively. Each run reports a return of $`1`$ and each has found an
optimal joint policy. Pairing one agent from the first run with one from
the second returns $`0`$. No agent is at fault, no run failed, and the
mean return across runs is uninformative about the result. This is the
reason
[Table 39](#S22.T39 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
asks for cross-play, the return obtained when agents from independently
trained runs are paired, as the diagnostic for cooperative decentralised
problems, and it is the failure that [Hu et al. (2020)](#bib.bib81) is
designed to prevent by breaking the symmetry that makes the two
conventions interchangeable. In the vocabulary of this section, the
pathology is that the game has a symmetry group acting on its optimal
policies, self-play selects one orbit element arbitrarily, and nothing
in the objective records which. $`\lozenge`$

| Model             | Rewards      | $`o_{i}\to s`$ | $`o\to s`$ | $`n=1`$ | Finite-horizon complexity                                                                  |
| ----------------- | ------------ | -------------- | ---------- | ------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| MDP               | single agent | yes            | yes        | MDP     | P-complete                                                                                 |
| MMDP              | common       | yes            | yes        | MDP     | P in $`\left\lvert\mathcal{S}\right\rvert\prod_{i}\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\rvert`$ |
| SG (Markov game)  | general      | yes            | yes        | MDP     | P if zero-sum; PPAD-complete if general-sum                                                |
| POMDP             | single agent | no             | no         | POMDP   | PSPACE-complete                                                                            |
| Dec-MDP           | common       | no             | yes        | MDP     | NEXP-complete (horizon $`<\left\lvert\mathcal{S}\right\rvert`$)                            |
| Dec-POMDP         | common       | no             | no         | POMDP   | NEXP-complete (horizon $`<\left\lvert\mathcal{S}\right\rvert`$)                            |
| Jointly obs. POSG | general      | no             | yes        | MDP     | NEXP-hard for the team-optimal problem                                                     |
| POSG              | general      | no             | no         | POMDP   | NEXP-hard for the team-optimal problem                                                     |
| I-POMDP           | general      | no             | no         | POMDP   | PSPACE-hard, finitely nested                                                               |

Table 14: The partially observable lattice. The columns record whether
the individual observation determines the state, whether the joint
observation does, and what the model degenerates to when $`n=1`$. The
final column gives the finite-horizon planning complexity with the model
given explicitly; the infinite-horizon and stationary-equilibrium
classifications, which differ, are collected in
[Table 36](#S21.T36 "In 21 The complexity landscape ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").

## 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones

The results in this section are the technical heart of the
correspondence. Each one takes a model in which several agents act on
private information and produces a single-agent sequential decision
problem whose solution is in bijection with the solution of the
original. They are the multi-agent analogues of
[Theorem 2.5](#S2.Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 2.5 (Belief-MDP reduction; belief sufficiency and the functional equation, , ; piecewise linearity and convexity, , ). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
they share its template, and they share its price: the reduced problem
is a Markov decision process over a space of distributions, so it is
amenable to dynamic programming in principle and not in practice without
further approximation. Collecting them in one place makes visible that
the modern deep algorithms for cooperative and for zero-sum
imperfect-information play are implementations of the same two theorems.

The two readings are, for once, the same reading arrived at twice. The
control theorist constructs a fictitious coordinator because
decentralised control problems are not amenable to dynamic programming
in their own variables; the game theorist constructs a public belief
state because imperfect-information games have no proper subgames in
their own variables. Both are performing the same operation, namely
conditioning on common knowledge and lifting the action space from
actions to functions of private information. Recognising this is the
single most useful item in this monograph, and the table below is its
dictionary.

| Game theory                           | Reinforcement learning                          | How to read the pair                             |
| ------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------ |
| Common knowledge $`c_{t}`$            | Public features; broadcast observations         | What conditioning is legitimate on               |
| Prescription $`\gamma_{t}^{i}`$       | Decision rule mapping private history to action | A function, not an action                        |
| Coordinator                           | Centralised controller issuing prescriptions    | A fictitious agent who sees only the public part |
| Common-information belief $`\Pi_{t}`$ | Posterior over state and private histories      | The coordinator’s belief state                   |
| Occupancy state $`\xi_{t}`$           | Distribution over state and joint history       | The sufficient statistic of a Dec-POMDP          |
| Public belief state $`\beta_{t}`$     | Public state plus per-agent range beliefs       | The zero-sum counterpart of $`\xi_{t}`$          |
| Behavioural prescription              | Policy head conditioned on the public state     | What a network outputs in BAD or ReBeL           |
| Piecewise linear convex value         | $`\alpha`$-vector representation                | Why point-based solvers transfer                 |
| Deterministic transition on $`\xi`$   | Noise absorbed into the state                   | Search over sequences of decision rules          |
| Type in a stage Bayesian game         | Action-observation history at time $`t`$        | The private information you condition on         |
| Subgame rooted at $`\beta_{t}`$       | Depth-limited search subtree                    | Where search can legitimately restart            |

Table 15: Reduction vocabulary read from both sides.

The unifying idea is the common-information decomposition. Partition
what the system knows at time $`t`$ into the common information
$`c_{t}`$, defined as the intersection of the agents’ information, and
the private information $`p_{t}^{i}`$ retained by each agent. A
fictitious coordinator who observes only $`c_{t}`$ cannot choose
actions, because actions must depend on private information, but it can
choose prescriptions, that is, functions
$`\gamma_{t}^{i}:\mathcal{P}_{t}^{i}\to\mathcal{A}_{i}`$ mapping each
agent’s private information to an action. The coordinator’s choice of
$`\gamma_{t}=(\gamma_{t}^{1},\dots,\gamma_{t}^{n})`$ together with the
agents’ mechanical application of their prescriptions reproduces any
decentralised strategy profile.

###### Theorem 9.1 (Common-information reduction; [Nayyar et al., 2013](#bib.bib128)).

Consider a decentralised stochastic control problem with partial history
sharing, in which the common information $`c_{t}`$ is non-decreasing and
each agent $`i`$ knows $`c_{t}`$ and its private information
$`p_{t}^{i}`$. Define the coordinator’s problem as the single-agent
decision problem with observation $`c_{t}`$, action $`\gamma_{t}`$
ranging over the finite set of prescription profiles, and reward the
expected team reward. Then the coordinator’s problem is a partially
observable Markov decision process whose hidden state is
$`(s_{t},p_{t}^{1},\dots,p_{t}^{n})`$, its belief-MDP state is the
common-information belief
$`\Pi_{t}(s,p)=\mathbb{P}(s_{t}=s,\,p_{t}=p\,|\,c_{t})`$, and there are
cost-preserving maps in both directions between coordination strategies
and decentralised profiles, so the two problems have the same achievable
expected total reward. Consequently an optimal coordinator strategy
yields an optimal decentralised profile, and dynamic programming over
$`\Pi_{t}`$ is valid.

[Theorem 9.1](#S9.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 9.1 (Common-information reduction; , ). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
converts a decentralised problem into a centralised one by enlarging the
action space from actions to functions of private information. The
exponential blow-up,
$`\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\rvert^{\left\lvert\mathcal{P}_{t}^{i}\right\rvert}`$
prescriptions per agent, is where the complexity goes, and it is not an
artefact: by
[Section 21](#S21 "21 The complexity landscape ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
the original problem is NEXP-hard, so no polynomial-time reduction
producing a polynomially sized partially observable Markov decision
process can exist unless PSPACE, EXP and NEXP all coincide.

###### Example 9.2 (Counting prescriptions).

In the two-rescuer problem of
[Example 8.5](#S8.Thmtheorem5 "Example 8.5 (The two-rescuer problem). ‣ 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
each agent has two possible private observations and two actions, so
there are $`2^{2}=4`$ prescriptions per agent and $`4^{2}=16`$
prescription profiles, which is why the enumeration in that example was
feasible by hand. Scale the problem a little. With two agents, four
private observations and three actions there are $`3^{4}=81`$
prescriptions each and $`6561`$ profiles. With three agents, eight
private observations and four actions there are $`4^{8}=65536`$
prescriptions each and $`65536^{3}\approx 2.8\times 10^{14}`$ profiles,
which is a single step of the coordinator’s problem. For comparison, the
number of deterministic joint policies of a two-agent decentralised
problem with two observations per step over a horizon of three is
$`(2^{7})^{2}=16384`$, and it grows doubly exponentially in the horizon.
These numbers are the content of the NEXP-hardness in
[Table 36](#S21.T36 "In 21 The complexity landscape ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning");
the reduction is exact and its cost is exactly this counting. The
practical reading is that occupancy-state and coordinator methods are
usable when the private information is small, which in practice means
when the problem has been engineered so that most of what agents observe
is public. $`\lozenge`$

The decentralised partially observable Markov decision process with no
communication is the special case in which the common information is
empty, and the common-information belief then becomes a distribution
over the state and over the joint action-observation history. This
object has its own name and its own theorem.

###### Definition 9.3 (Occupancy state).

Let $`G`$ be a decentralised partially observable Markov decision
process and let $`\mathcal{H}_{t}=\prod_{i}\mathcal{H}_{t}^{i}`$ be the
set of joint action-observation histories of length $`t`$. Given the
initial belief $`b_{0}`$ and the sequence of joint decision rules
$`\delta_{0:t-1}`$ with
$`\delta_{k}=(\delta_{k}^{i}:\mathcal{H}_{k}^{i}\to\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i}`$,
the occupancy state is
$`\xi_{t}\in\Delta(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{H}_{t})`$ defined by
$`\xi_{t}(s,h)=\mathbb{P}(s_{t}=s,\,h_{t}=h\,|\,b_{0},\delta_{0:t-1})`$.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. The occupancy state is the
replacement for the belief state of
[Theorem 2.5](#S2.Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 2.5 (Belief-MDP reduction; belief sufficiency and the functional equation, , ; piecewise linearity and convexity, , ). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
when there is more than one agent, and it is a bigger object for a
precise reason: it is a distribution not over the world state but over
the world state paired with everybody’s recurrent hidden state. A
practitioner who wanted to maintain it would have to track, jointly,
where the world is and what every teammate has seen, because a
teammate’s future action depends on its own history and on nothing the
agent can observe. The decision rule $`\delta_{t}`$ is not an action but
a function from a history to an action, that is, an entire policy
network evaluated at one time step, so the coordinator’s action space is
a space of networks.
[Example 9.4](#S9.Thmtheorem4 "Example 9.4 (An occupancy state written out). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
writes one out in full for an eight-entry instance, and
[Example 9.2](#S9.Thmtheorem2 "Example 9.2 (Counting prescriptions). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
counts how fast it grows.

###### Example 9.4 (An occupancy state written out).

In the two-rescuer problem, after the signals have been drawn and before
any action is taken, the occupancy state is a distribution over the
eight pairs consisting of a door and a pair of signals. With signal
accuracy $`0.8`$ its entries are
$`\xi(\mathrm{left};L,L)=0.5\times 0.8\times 0.8=0.32`$,
$`\xi(\mathrm{left};L,R)=\xi(\mathrm{left};R,L)=0.5\times 0.8\times 0.2=0.08`$,
$`\xi(\mathrm{left};R,R)=0.5\times 0.2\times 0.2=0.02`$, and the four
mirror entries for the right door, summing to one. Three features of
this object are worth naming because they recur at every scale. It is a
single vector, not a pair of beliefs, because no factorisation into
per-agent beliefs is valid; it lives in a simplex of dimension seven
here and of dimension
$`\left\lvert\mathcal{S}\right\rvert\prod_{i}\left\lvert\mathcal{H}^{i}_{t}\right\rvert-1`$
in general, which is where the blow-up lives; and the map from it and a
joint decision rule to the next occupancy state is deterministic,
because all the randomness of the problem has already been absorbed into
the distribution. That determinism is what
[Theorem 9.5](#S9.Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 9.5 (Occupancy-MDP reduction; , ). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
exploits. $`\lozenge`$

###### Theorem 9.5 (Occupancy-MDP reduction; [Dibangoye et al., 2016](#bib.bib45)).

Let $`G`$ be a finite-horizon decentralised partially observable Markov
decision process. The occupancy state $`\xi_{t}`$ is a sufficient
statistic for the history of joint decision rules, the map
$`(\xi_{t},\delta_{t})\mapsto\xi_{t+1}`$ is deterministic, and the
occupancy Markov decision process
$`(\Delta(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{H}),\{\delta\},\text{deterministic transition},\tilde{\mathcal{R}})`$
with
$`\tilde{\mathcal{R}}(\xi_{t},\delta_{t})=\sum_{s,h}\xi_{t}(s,h)\,\mathcal{R}(s,\delta_{t}(h))`$
is a continuous-state, deterministic-transition Markov decision process
whose optimal value equals the optimal value of $`G`$ and whose optimal
policy induces an optimal joint policy of $`G`$. Moreover the optimal
value function
$`V_{t}^{\star}:\Delta(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{H}_{t})\to\mathbb{R}`$
is piecewise linear and convex.

The piecewise linearity and convexity is the same property that
[Theorem 2.5](#S2.Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 2.5 (Belief-MDP reduction; belief sufficiency and the functional equation, , ; piecewise linearity and convexity, , ). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
gives for the single-agent case, and it is the property that makes
point-based approximation methods applicable, which is how the heuristic
search value iteration family was transplanted from partially observable
Markov decision processes to decentralised ones. The determinism of the
transition is worth emphasising, because it is the formal statement that
all the stochasticity of a decentralised partially observable Markov
decision process has been absorbed into the state; the residual problem
is a search over sequences of decision rules.

The zero-sum counterpart replaces the occupancy state by the public
belief state, and the fact that the construction works there too, with
an entirely different solution concept, shows that the reduction is a
property of the information structure rather than of the reward
structure.

###### Definition 9.6 (Public belief state).

In a two-player zero-sum game with imperfect information and public
actions, let the public state $`s^{\mathrm{pub}}_{t}`$ be the common
knowledge produced by the observed public history, and let
$`\mathcal{S}_{i}`$ be the set of agent $`i`$’s information states
consistent with it. The public belief state is
$`\beta_{t}=(s^{\mathrm{pub}}_{t},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})`$ with
$`\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{S}_{i})`$ the distribution over agent
$`i`$’s information states induced by the agents’ strategies and Bayes’
rule.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. A value network for an
imperfect-information game has to take something as input, and
[Definition 9.6](#S9.Thmtheorem6 "Definition 9.6 (Public belief state). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
says what. Not the world state, because the opponent cannot condition on
it and a position’s value depends on what the opponent believes. Not the
public history alone, because two positions with the same betting
sequence are worth different amounts depending on how each agent’s
strategy filtered the hands into it. The right input is the public
history together with one probability vector per agent over that agent’s
possible private states, which is what $`\beta_{t}`$ is. Those vectors
are computed by Bayes’ rule from the strategies, so they exist only once
the strategies are fixed, which is why the construction is a
self-consistency condition and not a preprocessing step. Once the input
is $`\beta_{t}`$ the game has proper subgames again, depth-limited
search becomes legitimate, and the AlphaZero recipe transfers.
[Example 9.8](#S9.Thmtheorem8 "Example 9.8 (The public belief state of a poker hand). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
computes a $`\beta_{t}`$ with three numbers.

###### Remark 9.7 (Public belief state reduction).

Assume the rules of the game and the agents’ policies are common
knowledge. Then a two-player zero-sum game with imperfect information
and public observations induces a continuous-state perfect-information
game whose states are public belief states and whose actions are
behavioural prescriptions $`\mathcal{S}_{i}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})`$.
The two representations are strategically identical, so the value of the
induced game at $`\beta_{0}`$ equals the value of the original and an
equilibrium of the induced game induces an equilibrium of the original.
The construction is the specialisation of
[Theorem 9.1](#S9.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 9.1 (Common-information reduction; , ). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
to the zero-sum case and is stated informally in this form by [Brown
et al. (2020)](#bib.bib27), who credit [Nayyar et al.
(2013)](#bib.bib128) and [Dibangoye et al. (2016)](#bib.bib45).

###### Example 9.8 (The public belief state of a poker hand).

In Kuhn poker, treated in full in
[Example 11.7](#S11.Thmtheorem7 "Example 11.7 (Kuhn poker, counted and solved). ‣ 11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
each agent holds one of three cards and the betting is public. After the
first agent checks, the public state is the single word “check” and the
public belief state consists of that word together with two
distributions over the three cards, one per agent, computed by Bayes’
rule from the agents’ known strategies. If the first agent’s strategy is
to check with probability one on the jack and the queen and with
probability $`2/3`$ on the king, then after a check the second agent’s
belief about the first agent’s card is proportional to $`(1,1,2/3)`$,
that is $`(0.375,0.375,0.25)`$. That triple, not the actual card, is the
state on which a depth-limited search may legitimately restart, which is
the content of
[Remark 9.7](#S9.Thmtheorem7 "Remark 9.7 (Public belief state reduction). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The reinforcement learning reading is exact and practical: a value
network for an imperfect-information game must take the belief pair as
input, because a network that takes only the public history is
evaluating a position whose value is not well defined, and a network
that takes the private card is evaluating a position the opponent cannot
condition on. Every design error in this area is one of those two.
$`\lozenge`$

[Remark 9.7](#S9.Thmtheorem7 "Remark 9.7 (Public belief state reduction). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is
[Theorem 9.1](#S9.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 9.1 (Common-information reduction; , ). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
with the team objective replaced by a zero-sum objective and the single
coordinator replaced by two coordinators playing a perfect-information
game, and the reason the public belief state is well defined is the same
reason the common-information belief is: both are the posterior
conditional on the common knowledge. The practical consequence is that
the techniques of perfect-information search, specifically the
combination of a learned value function with depth-limited search that
underlies AlphaZero ([Silver et al., 2018](#bib.bib162)), become
applicable to imperfect-information games once the state is taken to be
a public belief state rather than a world state, which is the design of
ReBeL ([Brown et al., 2020](#bib.bib27)) and of Student of Games
([Schmid et al., 2023](#bib.bib155)). On the cooperative side the
identical construction, with prescriptions computed by a neural network
conditioned on the public belief, is the Bayesian action decoder
([Foerster et al., 2019](#bib.bib59)), and the observation that these
two lines of work implement the same theorem under different reward
structures is, in our view, the most useful single item in this
monograph. The same construction, applied to the subsets of agents for
whom a fact is common knowledge, is the multi-agent common-knowledge
method of [Schroeder de Witt et al. (2019)](#bib.bib156), which chooses
dynamically how large a group to coordinate as a function of how much
those agents commonly know.

A third reduction of a different flavour converts a decentralised
planning problem into a sequence of one-shot games, which is the bridge
between
[Section 8](#S8 "8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and the Bayesian games of
[Section 12](#S12 "12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").

###### Proposition 9.9 (Stage-wise Bayesian game reduction; [Emery-Montemerlo et al., 2004](#bib.bib50); [Oliehoek et al., 2008](#bib.bib131)).

Let $`G`$ be a finite-horizon decentralised partially observable Markov
decision process and fix the joint decision rules $`\delta_{0:t-1}`$,
which determine $`\xi_{t}`$. Then the choice of $`\delta_{t}`$ that
maximises the remaining expected return is a Bayes-Nash equilibrium,
indeed an optimal profile, of the common-payoff Bayesian game
$`\mathcal{BG}_{t}`$ whose agent set is $`\mathcal{N}`$, whose type
space for agent $`i`$ is $`\mathcal{H}_{t}^{i}`$, whose type
distribution is the marginal of $`\xi_{t}`$ on $`\mathcal{H}_{t}`$, and
whose payoff is the immediate reward plus the optimal continuation
value. Solving $`G`$ exactly amounts to solving the sequence
$`\mathcal{BG}_{0},\mathcal{BG}_{1},\dots`$ with exact continuation
values, and replacing the continuation value by a heuristic yields the
approximate methods of [Emery-Montemerlo et al. (2004)](#bib.bib50).

The statement requires the past joint decision rules to be the optimal
ones, and $`Q^{\star}`$ to be the action-value under the optimal joint
policy rather than a quantity defined by a maximisation, which is how
[Oliehoek et al. (2008)](#bib.bib131) state it.

[Proposition 9.9](#S9.Thmtheorem9 "Proposition 9.9 (Stage-wise Bayesian game reduction; , ). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the precise sense in which an agent’s action-observation history
plays the role of a Harsanyi type. The type of an agent is the private
information that determines its beliefs about the others, and in a
decentralised partially observable Markov decision process at time $`t`$
that is exactly $`h_{t}^{i}`$. This identification is used again in
[Section 11](#S11 "11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
where the information set of an extensive-form game is shown to be the
same object once more, and in
[Section 12](#S12 "12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
where it drives the correspondence between the Harsanyi transformation
and the Bayes-adaptive Markov decision process.

| Source model                      | Reduced state                          | Reduced action                     | Target             | Implemented by                                         |
| --------------------------------- | -------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------- | ------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------ |
| POMDP                             | Belief $`b_{t}\in\Delta(\mathcal{S})`$ | Action $`a_{t}`$                   | MDP                | Recurrent and belief-conditioned policies              |
| Partial history sharing           | Common-information belief $`\Pi_{t}`$  | Prescription $`\gamma_{t}`$        | POMDP              | Coordinator architectures                              |
| Dec-POMDP                         | Occupancy state $`\xi_{t}`$            | Joint decision rule $`\delta_{t}`$ | Deterministic MDP  | Point-based oMDP solvers; BAD                          |
| 2p zero-sum imperfect info        | Public belief state $`\beta_{t}`$      | Policy prescription                | Perfect-info game  | ReBeL; Student of Games                                |
| Dec-POMDP, group common knowledge | Common knowledge of a subset           | Prescription for that subset       | Hierarchical POMDP | MACKRL ([Schroeder de Witt et al., 2019](#bib.bib156)) |
| Dec-POMDP at stage $`t`$          | Type distribution from $`\xi_{t}`$     | Joint type-action map              | Bayesian game      | Bayesian-game approximation                            |
| Latent MDP                        | Belief over $`(s,\theta)`$             | Action $`a_{t}`$                   | BAMDP              | Meta-RL with context encoders                          |
| Fixed-opponent SG                 | State $`s_{t}`$                        | Action $`a_{i}`$                   | MDP                | Any single-agent method                                |
| Finite-automaton opponents        | $`(s_{t},m_{-i})`$                     | Action $`a_{i}`$                   | MDP                | Opponent modelling                                     |

Table 16: Exact reductions of multi-agent models to single-agent ones.
Each reduction preserves optimal value and maps solutions bijectively;
each enlarges the state space to a space of distributions and the action
space to a space of functions. The last column names the deep
reinforcement learning methods that implement the reduction
approximately.

## 10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor

The models of the previous sections are specified by a payoff tensor
with one entry per joint action, so their description length is
exponential in the number of agents. Every practical multi-agent system
avoids this, because real interactions are local: a vehicle is affected
by its neighbours and not by traffic on another continent, a unit in a
battle is affected by the units in its range, a machine in a plant is
affected by its immediate upstream and downstream machines. Game theory
formalises locality by placing the agents on a graph and requiring each
agent’s payoff to depend only on its neighbours, which produces
graphical and polymatrix games; reinforcement learning formalises the
same locality as a factored Markov decision process and as a
coordination graph. The two constructions are the same, and the
correspondence is the one that converts an exponential maximisation into
a message-passing computation.

The two readings differ in what the graph is for. A game theorist draws
the graph to make a representation succinct and then asks whether the
succinctness survives into the solution problem, which is the question
whose answer is in
[Section 21](#S21 "21 The complexity landscape ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
A reinforcement learning researcher draws the graph to make an argmax
tractable and then asks whether the restriction loses the optimum, which
is the question that
[Section 16](#S16 "16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
answers through the Individual-Global-Max condition. Both are asking
what structure buys, and the answers differ: succinct representation
does not imply easy equilibrium, and tractable argmax does imply
restricted expressiveness.

| Game theory                            | Reinforcement learning                   | How to read the pair                           |
| -------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------- |
| Graphical game                         | Coordination graph; factored MARL        | Payoffs depend on neighbours only              |
| Neighbourhood $`N(i)`$                 | Scope of a local payoff factor           | Who can affect agent $`i`$ directly            |
| Local payoff $`u_{i}(a_{i},a_{N(i)})`$ | Local utility term $`f_{e}(a_{e})`$      | One summand of the joint value                 |
| Polymatrix game                        | Pairwise-decomposable joint reward       | Sum of two-agent games on edges                |
| Treewidth of the interaction graph     | Treewidth of the coordination graph      | What makes elimination cheap                   |
| Variable elimination                   | Max-plus, message passing, DCOP          | Compute the argmax without enumerating         |
| Congestion game                        | Resource-sharing MARL environment        | Cost depends on how many choose it             |
| Rosenthal potential                    | Potential of a potential game            | A scalar whose local differences match payoffs |
| Wardrop equilibrium                    | Mean-field equilibrium of a routing game | Nonatomic agents equalise costs                |
| Price of anarchy                       | Gap from selfish learners to a planner   | What locality plus selfishness costs           |
| Anonymous game                         | Permutation-invariant policy; mean-field | Only the count of each action matters          |

Table 17: Structured-game vocabulary read from both sides.

###### Definition 10.1 (Graphical games, [Kearns et al., 2001](#bib.bib92); polymatrix games).

A graphical game is a normal-form game together with an undirected graph
on $`\mathcal{N}`$ such that $`u_{i}(a)`$ depends only on $`a_{i}`$ and
on $`a_{N(i)}`$, where $`N(i)`$ is the set of neighbours of $`i`$. It is
a polymatrix game when in addition the payoff decomposes as a sum over
edges, $`u_{i}(a)=\sum_{j\in N(i)}u_{ij}(a_{i},a_{j})`$, so that the
game is determined by one two-agent matrix per edge. The graphical class
is the one introduced by [Kearns et al. (2001)](#bib.bib92); the
polymatrix class is older and is not theirs.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. A graphical game is a factored
reward model and its reinforcement learning name is a coordination
graph. The neighbourhood $`N(i)`$ is the scope of the factors that touch
agent $`i`$, so an implementation stores one small tensor per hyperedge
instead of one enormous tensor over $`\mathcal{A}`$. Two savings follow
and they are independent. The first is memory: the model shrinks from
$`\left\lvert\mathcal{A}\right\rvert^{n}`$ entries per agent to
$`\left\lvert\mathcal{A}\right\rvert^{d+1}`$, where $`d`$ is the maximum
degree. The second is compute: the joint $`\argmax`$ that a greedy
policy needs no longer requires enumerating $`\prod_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}`$
and becomes message passing, which is
[Proposition 10.2](#S10.Thmtheorem2 "Proposition 10.2 (Coordination graphs and variable elimination). ‣ 10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The polymatrix case, in which every factor has exactly two agents in
scope, is the one in which a pairwise value decomposition is exactly
right rather than an approximation, and
[Example 10.3](#S10.Thmtheorem3 "Example 10.3 (What the graph saves, in arithmetic). ‣ 10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and
[Example 10.4](#S10.Thmtheorem4 "Example 10.4 (Zero-sum on every edge, not zero-sum overall). ‣ 10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
quantify both savings.

The description length of a graphical game is
$`n\cdot\left\lvert\mathcal{A}\right\rvert^{d+1}`$ where $`d`$ is the
maximum degree, against $`n\cdot\left\lvert\mathcal{A}\right\rvert^{n}`$
for the general case, and for a polymatrix game it is
$`\left\lvert E\right\rvert\cdot\left\lvert\mathcal{A}\right\rvert^{2}`$.
This is the first of the two savings. The second is computational and is
the one a reinforcement learning practitioner uses every time a factored
argmax is required.

###### Proposition 10.2 (Coordination graphs and variable elimination).

Let $`Q_{\mathrm{tot}}(s,a)=\sum_{e\in E}f_{e}(s,a_{e})`$ be a joint
action-value that decomposes over the hyperedges of a graph with scopes
$`a_{e}`$. Then $`\max_{a}Q_{\mathrm{tot}}(s,a)`$ and a maximising joint
action are computable by variable elimination in time exponential in the
induced width of the elimination ordering and linear in $`n`$, rather
than exponential in $`n`$. When the graph is a tree the induced width is
one and the computation is two sweeps of message passing. This is the
construction of [Guestrin et al. (2001)](#bib.bib71). The max-plus
algorithm of [Kok and Vlassis (2006)](#bib.bib93) is the loopy
counterpart: it is exact on trees, where it coincides with the above,
and on a graph with cycles it returns an approximate maximiser rather
than the exact one, which is the trade it makes for being anytime and
fully distributed. Its deep instance is the coordination graph network
of [Boehmer et al. (2020)](#bib.bib21).

###### Example 10.3 (What the graph saves, in arithmetic).

Take $`n`$ agents with five actions each, arranged in a chain so that
agent $`i`$ interacts only with agents $`i-1`$ and $`i+1`$. The joint
action space has $`5^{n}`$ elements: $`625`$ for four agents,
$`390{,}625`$ for eight, and about $`9.5\times 10^{13}`$ for twenty.
Variable elimination along the chain passes one message per edge, each
of which is a table of $`5\times 5=25`$ entries, for a total of
$`(n-1)\cdot 25`$ operations: $`75`$, $`175`$ and $`475`$ respectively.
The saving is from exponential to linear in $`n`$ and it is exact, not
approximate, provided the value really does decompose over the chain.
The qualifier is the whole of the difficulty: a value function trained
end to end does not decompose unless the architecture forces it to, and
forcing it restricts the hypothesis class, which is the trade-off that
[Proposition 16.8](#S16.Thmtheorem8 "Proposition 16.8 (Monotone mixing implies IGM). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
quantifies. A chain is the easy case; for a general graph the cost of
elimination is exponential in the induced width, so a densely connected
set of agents recovers the original difficulty, correctly, because a
densely connected set of agents really is a hard coordination problem.
$`\lozenge`$

###### Example 10.4 (Zero-sum on every edge, not zero-sum overall).

Place three agents on a triangle and put rock-paper-scissors on each
edge, so that $`u_{i}(a)=\sum_{j\neq i}u_{\mathrm{RPS}}(a_{i},a_{j})`$.
Each pairwise game has value $`0`$ and the unique pairwise equilibrium
is uniform. Every edge of this game is itself zero-sum, so it is a
pairwise constant-sum polymatrix game, which is the easy subclass; the
payoffs also sum to zero at every joint action, but here that is a
consequence of the edges rather than an independent property. The
substance of [Cai and Daskalakis (2011)](#bib.bib29) is that the two
coincide: a game whose payoffs sum to a constant globally, without any
pair being in a constant-sum relation in isolation, is payoff-equivalent
to a pairwise constant-sum polymatrix game, so the easy subclass is the
whole class. Its representation needs three matrices of nine entries,
that is $`27`$ numbers, against the $`27\times 3=81`$ numbers of the
explicit three-agent tensor. The structural point, which is the content
of [Cai and Daskalakis (2011)](#bib.bib29), is that zero-sum polymatrix
games retain the good properties of two-player zero-sum games: the
minmax theorem generalises to them, their equilibria form a convex set,
those equilibria are computable in polynomial time, and no-regret
learning converges to them. The same source notes that unrestricted
three-player zero-sum games are already PPAD-complete, so the
pairwise-separable structure is essentially the furthest that
tractability can be pushed in the many-agent constant-sum setting.
Recognising that an application falls in this class is worth more than
any algorithmic refinement. $`\lozenge`$

Congestion games are the most important structured class in
applications, because resource sharing is the commonest form of
multi-agent interaction in engineering.

###### Definition 10.5 (Congestion game; [Rosenthal, 1973](#bib.bib148)).

Let $`\mathcal{E}`$ be a finite set of resources, let each agent $`i`$
choose a subset $`a_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{E}`$ from an allowed family,
and let $`c_{e}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}`$ be a cost function for
resource $`e`$. The cost to agent $`i`$ is
$`\sum_{e\in a_{i}}c_{e}(n_{e}(a))`$, where $`n_{e}(a)`$ is the number
of agents using $`e`$. Every congestion game is an exact potential game
in the sense of
[Definition 16.1](#S16.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 16.1 (Potential game; , ). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
with Rosenthal’s potential
$`\Phi(a)=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{e}(a)}c_{e}(k)`$.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. A congestion game is the formal
model of every multi-agent environment in which agents compete for a
shared resource whose quality degrades with use: bandwidth, road
capacity, a charging station, a processing queue, a piece of map. The
defining restriction is that an agent’s cost depends on how many others
chose each resource and not on which others did, so the reward function
is anonymous in the sense used at the end of this section. The payoff of
the restriction is large and is stated by
[Definition 10.5](#S10.Thmtheorem5 "Definition 10.5 (Congestion game; , ). ‣ 10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
itself: every such game has an exact potential, so independent learners
that only ever take improving steps are hill-climbing a single scalar
and must stop. That is the strongest convergence guarantee available
anywhere in this monograph to agents that do not coordinate. What it
does not guarantee is that the resting point is good, and
[Example 10.6](#S10.Thmtheorem6 "Example 10.6 (Pigou’s network and a price of anarchy of four thirds). ‣ 10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
measures the shortfall.

The potential guarantees that better-reply dynamics terminate at a pure
equilibrium, which is the strongest convergence statement available to
independent learners and is developed in
[Section 16](#S16 "16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
What the potential does not guarantee is that the equilibrium is good,
and the gap between the equilibrium and the social optimum is the price
of anarchy, treated in full in
[Section 15](#S15 "15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The two smallest instances are worth having explicitly because they are
the examples every practitioner should be able to reconstruct.

###### Example 10.6 (Pigou’s network and a price of anarchy of four thirds).

One unit of traffic travels from a source to a sink along two parallel
links. The upper link has constant cost $`1`$ regardless of load; the
lower link has cost equal to the fraction $`x`$ of traffic using it.
Selfish traffic all moves to the lower link, because for any $`x<1`$ the
lower link is cheaper, so the equilibrium flow is $`x=1`$ and every unit
pays $`1`$, for a total cost of $`1`$. A planner minimising the total
cost $`x\cdot x+(1-x)\cdot 1`$ differentiates to get $`2x-1=0`$, so
$`x=1/2`$ and the total cost is $`\tfrac{1}{4}+\tfrac{1}{2}=0.75`$. The
price of anarchy is $`1/0.75=4/3`$. The reinforcement learning reading
is that a population of independently trained agents sharing a resource
will overload it by exactly this factor, that no amount of better
optimisation fixes it because the equilibrium is exactly what each agent
wants, and that the two remedies are to change the reward, which is the
tolling construction of
[Section 15](#S15 "15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
or to centralise the decision, which is the planner’s solution.
$`\lozenge`$

###### Example 10.7 (Braess’s paradox ([Braess et al., 2005](#bib.bib25)): adding capacity makes everybody worse off).

A unit of traffic travels from $`s`$ to $`t`$. There are two routes,
$`s\to A\to t`$ with costs $`x`$ then $`1`$, and $`s\to B\to t`$ with
costs $`1`$ then $`x`$, where $`x`$ is the flow on the edge in question.
By symmetry the equilibrium splits the traffic in half and every unit
pays $`\tfrac{1}{2}+1=1.5`$. Now add a free link from $`A`$ to $`B`$.
The route $`s\to A\to B\to t`$ costs $`x_{sA}+0+x_{Bt}`$, and taking it
is a dominant choice for every unit, so all traffic uses it and every
unit pays $`1+0+1=2`$. Adding capacity raised the equilibrium cost by a
factor $`4/3`$ while the social optimum is unchanged. The lesson
transfers verbatim to multi-agent reinforcement learning: enlarging the
action space of selfish learners can lower the equilibrium welfare, so
an ablation showing that a richer action space hurts is not necessarily
an optimisation failure and may be the correct equilibrium of a
different game. Diagnosing which requires comparing against a
centralised planner on the same model, which is the tenth row of
[Table 39](#S22.T39 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
$`\lozenge`$

The last structured class worth naming here is the anonymous game, in
which each agent’s payoff depends on its own action and on the number of
other agents playing each action, but not on their identities. Anonymity
is the finite-$`n`$ version of the exchangeability assumption that
produces the mean-field limit of
[Section 18](#S18 "18 The population limit: mean-field games and mean-field control ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
and in reinforcement learning it is the structural property that
licenses a permutation-invariant architecture, in which the other agents
are summarised by a pooled embedding rather than concatenated. The
correspondence is exact: a permutation-invariant critic is well
specified precisely when the underlying game is anonymous, and using one
when the game is not anonymous introduces a representational error of
the same kind as using a monotone mixing network on a game that violates
the Individual-Global-Max condition.

| Class               | Structure assumed               | RL counterpart                | What it buys                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| ------------------- | ------------------------------- | ----------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| General normal form | None                            | Joint-action critic           | Nothing; $`\left\lvert\mathcal{A}\right\rvert^{n}`$ entries                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| Graphical game      | Payoffs depend on $`N(i)`$      | Scoped critic factors         | Representation: $`\left\lvert\mathcal{A}\right\rvert^{d+1}`$ per agent                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Polymatrix game     | Payoffs sum over edges          | Pairwise value decomposition  | Representation; if zero-sum, a minmax theorem and polynomial-time equilibria ([Cai and Daskalakis, 2011](#bib.bib29))                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| Coordination graph  | Value sums over hyperedges      | Max-plus, DCG                 | Argmax by elimination ([Proposition 10.2](#S10.Thmtheorem2 "Proposition 10.2 (Coordination graphs and variable elimination). ‣ 10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")) |
| Congestion game     | Cost depends on load only       | Shared-resource environment   | Pure equilibrium exists; potential ([Rosenthal, 1973](#bib.bib148))                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| Anonymous game      | Payoff depends on action counts | Permutation-invariant critic  | Representation linear in $`n`$                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Mean-field limit    | Exchangeable, $`n\to\infty`$    | Representative-agent training | Cost independent of $`n`$ ([Section 18](#S18 "18 The population limit: mean-field games and mean-field control ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"))                                                                                             |

Table 18: Structured game classes, the reinforcement learning
architecture that exploits the structure, and what the structure buys.
The last column records whether the saving is in representation, in
computation of the argmax, or in the solution concept itself.

## 11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories

The extensive form represents a game as a tree in which nodes are
decision points, edges are actions, and the points that a player cannot
distinguish are grouped into information sets. It is the representation
in which the entire literature on imperfect-information game solving is
written, including counterfactual regret minimisation and the superhuman
poker systems, and it is the representation that reinforcement learning
practitioners most often find alien. The purpose of this section is to
show that it is the familiar partially observable sequential decision
problem in different coordinates.

The two readings are the two ways of writing down the same trajectory
distribution. A game theorist writes the tree and groups its nodes into
information sets, so that a strategy is a labelling of information sets
by distributions over actions, and the quantity to be computed is a
realisation probability for each terminal node. A reinforcement learning
researcher writes a state, an observation kernel and a policy, so that a
policy is a map from histories to distributions over actions, and the
quantity to be computed is a visitation measure. Information set and
action-observation history are the same equivalence class of play,
behavioural strategy and policy are the same object, and realisation
plan and occupancy measure are the same vector. The one real difference
is that the tree makes the recursive structure of regret explicit while
the state makes bootstrapping explicit, and the two algorithm families
follow the representation rather than the problem.

| Game theory                           | Reinforcement learning                     | How to read the pair                           |
| ------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------ |
| Node $`h`$ of the tree                | Full history; state-trajectory prefix      | A position including hidden information        |
| Information set $`I`$                 | Action-observation history $`h^{i}_{t}`$   | What the acting agent can distinguish          |
| Chance node                           | Transition or observation kernel           | Nature’s move with a known distribution        |
| Behavioural strategy $`\pi_{i}(I)`$   | Policy $`\pi\_{i}(\cdot\,                  | \,h^{i}\_{t})`$                                | Local randomisation at each decision |
| Mixed strategy                        | Distribution over deterministic policies   | Randomise once, over whole plans               |
| Perfect recall                        | The policy sees the full own history       | You never forget what you did                  |
| Realisation plan                      | Occupancy measure; visitation distribution | Probability of reaching each sequence          |
| Sequence form                         | Bellman flow polytope                      | Linear constraints instead of a simplex        |
| Counterfactual reach $`\pi^{-i}(h)`$  | Discounted visitation of others            | How often the rest of the world sends you here |
| Counterfactual value                  | Advantage weighted by reach                | Local contribution to global regret            |
| Subgame                               | Sub-MDP rooted at a state                  | A part solvable on its own                     |
| Sequential equilibrium                | On-policy belief filter plus greedy play   | Beliefs consistent with the policy             |
| Extensive-form correlated equilibrium | Shared seed revealed as play unfolds       | A mediator who recommends step by step         |

Table 19: Extensive-form vocabulary read from both sides.

###### Definition 11.1 (Extensive-form game).

An extensive-form game consists of a finite rooted tree with node set
$`\mathcal{V}`$, terminal nodes $`Z\subseteq\mathcal{V}`$, a player
function assigning each non-terminal node to an agent in
$`\mathcal{N}\cup\{c\}`$ where $`c`$ is chance, a probability
distribution over the edges at each chance node, payoff functions
$`u_{i}:Z\to\mathbb{R}`$, and for each agent $`i`$ a partition
$`\mathcal{I}_{i}`$ of the nodes assigned to $`i`$ into information
sets, such that all nodes in an information set $`I`$ have the same set
of available actions $`\mathcal{A}(I)`$. A behavioural strategy of agent
$`i`$ is a map $`\pi_{i}:\mathcal{I}_{i}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A}(\cdot))`$
assigning a distribution over actions to each of its information sets. A
mixed strategy is a distribution over pure strategies, a pure strategy
being a deterministic choice at every information set of $`i`$.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. Unroll a partially observable
environment into the tree of all its possible trajectories and
[Definition 11.1](#S11.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 11.1 (Extensive-form game). ‣ 11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is what you have written down. A node is a full history including the
hidden state; a chance node is one call to the transition or observation
kernel; an information set is the set of nodes the acting agent cannot
tell apart, which is exactly the set of nodes sharing an
action-observation history, so an information set is a recurrent hidden
state. A behavioural strategy is a policy: one distribution over actions
per information set, which is one forward pass per observed history. A
mixed strategy is something a policy gradient method never manipulates
and a population-based method always does, namely a distribution over
whole deterministic policies.
[Proposition 11.6](#S11.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 11.6 (Information sets are action-observation histories). ‣ 11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
states the correspondence formally and
[Theorem 11.4](#S11.Thmtheorem4 "Theorem 11.4 ( , ). ‣ 11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
says when the two strategy notions are interchangeable.

###### Example 11.2 (The smallest tree, with and without an observation).

Chance flips a fair coin; the agent then picks $`L`$ or $`R`$; it scores
$`1`$ if its pick matches the coin and $`0`$ otherwise. The tree has one
chance node at the root, two decision nodes below it and four terminal
nodes.

Write it as
[Definition 11.1](#S11.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 11.1 (Extensive-form game). ‣ 11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
twice. In the blind version the agent does not see the coin, so both
decision nodes lie in one information set, the agent has a single
behavioural strategy parameter $`p=\pi(L)`$, and its expected payoff is
$`0.5p+0.5(1-p)=0.5`$ for every $`p`$. The whole strategy space is worth
the same, so there is nothing to learn. In the sighted version the agent
sees the coin, the two nodes lie in two singleton information sets, the
agent has two parameters, and matching the coin at each gives an
expected payoff of $`1`$.

The difference, $`1-0.5=0.5`$, is the value of information, and it is
the same $`0.5`$ that
[Example 13.2](#S13.Thmtheorem2 "Example 13.2 (The whole of emergent communication in four numbers). ‣ 13 Communication: cheap talk, signalling, persuasion and emergent protocols ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
obtains by letting one agent tell another what the state is. That is not
a coincidence: a communication channel is a device for splitting an
information set, and splitting information sets is the only thing that
raises the value of a one-agent tree. Read as reinforcement learning,
the blind version is a feedforward policy on an unobservable state and
the sighted version is the same policy with the state appended to its
input, and the $`0.5`$ is the return gap that no optimiser can close on
the first. $`\lozenge`$

###### Definition 11.3 (Perfect recall).

Agent $`i`$ has perfect recall when, for every information set
$`I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}`$, all nodes in $`I`$ induce the same sequence of
$`i`$’s own past information sets and actions. A game has perfect recall
when every agent does.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. Perfect recall is a property of the
architecture, not of the task. A policy that conditions on its full
action-observation history has it by construction, so any recurrent
network with enough capacity, or any transformer whose context window
covers the episode, is a perfect-recall agent. A feedforward policy on
the current observation does not have it, because two nodes that differ
only in what the agent itself did earlier land in the same information
set. A truncated context window does not have it either, for the same
reason, and the truncation point is exactly where recall is lost.
[Theorem 11.4](#S11.Thmtheorem4 "Theorem 11.4 ( , ). ‣ 11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
says what is at stake: under perfect recall, optimising a policy loses
nothing relative to optimising a distribution over policies, and without
it the policy class is strictly weaker, so local randomisation becomes
necessary.
[Example 11.5](#S11.Thmtheorem5 "Example 11.5 (Perfect recall is a property of the architecture, not the task). ‣ 11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
exhibits the failure on a four-cell corridor.

###### Theorem 11.4 ([Kuhn, 1953](#bib.bib98)).

In an extensive-form game in which agent $`i`$ has perfect recall, for
every mixed strategy of agent $`i`$ there is a behavioural strategy that
induces the same distribution over terminal nodes against every profile
of the other agents, and conversely. Without perfect recall the
equivalence fails in one direction only: a behavioural strategy always
admits an outcome-equivalent mixed strategy, while a mixed strategy need
not admit an outcome-equivalent behavioural one, and perfect recall is a
sufficient condition for it to do so. The one-directional statement
presumes that no information set is met twice along a play, that is,
that the agent is not absent-minded.

[Theorem 11.4](#S11.Thmtheorem4 "Theorem 11.4 ( , ). ‣ 11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the reason reinforcement learning can be applied to extensive-form
games at all. A behavioural strategy is precisely a policy, a map from
what the agent currently knows to a distribution over actions, and a
mixed strategy is a distribution over deterministic policies, which is
the object a population-based or evolutionary method manipulates.
Perfect recall is the condition under which optimising a policy is
without loss relative to optimising a distribution over policies, and it
is assumed, usually silently, whenever a recurrent policy is trained on
a partially observable task. The failure mode is concrete: a feedforward
policy on a partially observable task is an imperfect-recall agent, the
equivalence of
[Theorem 11.4](#S11.Thmtheorem4 "Theorem 11.4 ( , ). ‣ 11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
does not apply to it, and the optimal memoryless policy may require
randomisation and may be strictly worse than the optimal
history-dependent one ([Singh et al., 1994](#bib.bib164)).

###### Example 11.5 (Perfect recall is a property of the architecture, not the task).

Consider a corridor of four cells in which the agent observes only
whether a wall is immediately to its left and to its right, so that the
two middle cells produce the same observation. A policy with memory
distinguishes them by counting steps and has perfect recall; a
feedforward policy on the current observation does not, because the two
middle cells lie in one information set even though the agent’s own past
actions differ between them. For the memoryless agent the optimal policy
may require randomisation, since moving right deterministically in the
ambiguous observation can trap it and moving left deterministically can
too, and [Singh et al. (1994)](#bib.bib164) show exactly this. The
game-theoretic statement and the reinforcement learning statement are
the same statement: dropping perfect recall breaks
[Theorem 11.4](#S11.Thmtheorem4 "Theorem 11.4 ( , ). ‣ 11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
so the behavioural policy class is strictly weaker than the mixed one,
and the gap is filled by local randomisation. The practical corollary is
that the choice between a feedforward and a recurrent policy is a choice
of game, not of function approximator. $`\lozenge`$

The correspondence with partial observability is now immediate, and we
state it in the direction that is useful.

###### Proposition 11.6 (Information sets are action-observation histories).

Let $`P`$ be a finite-horizon partially observable Markov decision
process. Unrolling $`P`$ into its tree of state-action-observation
trajectories, with chance nodes implementing $`b_{0}`$, $`\mathcal{T}`$
and $`\mathcal{Z}`$, yields a one-player extensive-form game with
perfect recall in which two nodes lie in the same information set if and
only if they share the same action-observation history $`h_{t}`$.
Conversely, let $`E`$ be a one-player extensive-form game with perfect
recall; then $`E`$ is a finite-horizon partially observable Markov
decision process whose states are the nodes of the tree, whose
observations are the information sets, and whose transition kernel is
given by the chance distributions. Under this correspondence,
behavioural strategies are policies
$`\pi:\mathcal{H}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A})`$, and the expected payoff of a
behavioural strategy equals the expected return of the corresponding
policy.

For $`n\geq 2`$ the same unrolling relates extensive-form games to
partially observable stochastic games, with one caveat that is a genuine
difference and not a notational one. A partially observable stochastic
game as defined in
[Definition 8.1](#S8.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 8.1 (Partially observable stochastic game). ‣ 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
has simultaneous moves at every step, whereas an extensive-form game has
a single acting agent at each node. Each representation can simulate the
other. Simultaneous moves are encoded in the extensive form by letting
the agents move in an arbitrary fixed order while placing all nodes that
differ only in the earlier agents’ unobserved choices into a single
information set; turn taking is encoded in a partially observable
stochastic game by including the identity of the agent to move in the
state and giving the others a no-op action. The encodings are
value-preserving, so the model classes are equivalent in expressive
power, and they differ in which quantities are natural to compute. The
extensive form makes the tree structure and therefore regret
decomposition explicit; the partially observable stochastic game makes
the state and therefore bootstrapping explicit. Algorithms follow the
representation: counterfactual regret minimisation traverses the tree,
temporal-difference methods bootstrap on the state.

The smallest poker game is the right place to make all of this concrete,
and we work it out completely because every quantity in it can be
checked by hand or by a short program.

###### Example 11.7 (Kuhn poker, counted and solved).

Three cards, a jack, a queen and a king, are shuffled and one is dealt
to each of two agents; the third is unseen. Each agent antes one chip.
The first agent checks or bets one chip. If it checks, the second agent
checks, ending the hand with a showdown for the antes, or bets, after
which the first agent folds, conceding the antes, or calls, producing a
showdown for two chips each. If the first agent bets, the second folds
or calls.

Count the objects. The first agent has three information sets at the
root, one per card, and three more after a check followed by a bet, so
six in all; the second agent has three after a check and three after a
bet, so six; twelve in total. A pure strategy of either agent picks one
of two actions at each of its six information sets, so each has
$`2^{6}=64`$ pure strategies and the normal form is a $`64\times 64`$
matrix. The sequence form instead lists the realisation weight of each
sequence of own actions: the empty sequence plus four per card for each
agent, so thirteen variables per agent. The reduction from $`64`$ to
$`13`$ is the whole point of the sequence form, and the same reduction
in a real poker variant is the difference between an impossible and a
routine linear program.

Solve it. The sequence-form linear program returns a value of
$`-1/18\approx-0.0556`$ chips per hand to the first agent, so the game
favours the second agent slightly. Equilibrium play is not unique: there
is a one-parameter family in which the first agent bluffs with the jack
at a rate that is tied, by the indifference conditions, to the rate at
which it bets the king.

Read it as reinforcement learning. The state is the pair of cards plus
the betting history, the observation is the agent’s own card plus the
public betting history, the information set is exactly the
action-observation history, and a behavioural strategy is a policy
conditioned on that history. The public belief state of
[Definition 9.6](#S9.Thmtheorem6 "Definition 9.6 (Public belief state). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the betting history together with the two posterior distributions
over cards, which is
[Example 9.8](#S9.Thmtheorem8 "Example 9.8 (The public belief state of a poker hand). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The value $`-1/18`$ is the number against which exploitability is
measured: a trained agent is evaluated not by its return against its
training partner but by the gap between $`-1/18`$ and its return against
a best response, which is computable exactly here by solving a
one-player Markov decision process against the frozen policy.
$`\lozenge`$

Three further correspondences complete the picture. The first concerns
representation size. Every extensive-form game has an equivalent normal
form whose action sets are the sets of pure strategies, and these are
exponentially large in the number of information sets, so the normal
form is not a usable computational representation. [Koller and Megiddo
(1992)](#bib.bib94) show that the equilibrium of a two-player zero-sum
extensive-form game with perfect recall is computable in time polynomial
in the tree, by a linear program with exponentially many constraints
solved through the ellipsoid method. The sequence form, arrived at
independently by several authors and developed for computation by [von
Stengel (1996)](#bib.bib184), replaces the simplex of mixed strategies
by the polytope of realisation plans, whose dimension is linear in the
number of sequences, and thereby reduces the same problem to a linear
program whose size is linear in the tree; [Koller et al.
(1996)](#bib.bib95) extend the device to the general-sum case, where the
equilibrium conditions become a linear complementarity problem solved by
Lemke’s algorithm rather than a linear program. The reinforcement
learning reading is that in the one-player case the realisation plan is
literally the occupancy measure, the state-action visitation
distribution whose use converts policy optimisation into a linear
program over the polytope of admissible occupancies, and the Bellman
flow constraints are the sequence-form constraints. With two players the
construction becomes a saddle point over a pair of such polytopes, so
the correspondence is structural rather than an identity.

The second concerns the solution concepts. Nash equilibrium applied to
the normal form of an extensive-form game permits incredible threats,
and the refinement that forbids them is subgame perfection, which under
perfect information is computed by backward induction and is therefore
literally dynamic programming. Under imperfect information proper
subgames are rare, since a subgame must begin at a node whose
information set is a singleton and must contain entire information sets,
and the refinements that replace subgame perfection are sequential
equilibrium ([Kreps and Wilson, 1982](#bib.bib97)) and the perfect
Bayesian equilibrium, both of which require a belief system assigning a
distribution over nodes within each information set together with a
consistency requirement linking beliefs to strategies. The reinforcement
learning counterpart of a belief system is the belief state of
[Theorem 2.5](#S2.Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 2.5 (Belief-MDP reduction; belief sufficiency and the functional equation, , ; piecewise linearity and convexity, , ). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
and the consistency requirement is the statement that the belief is the
Bayes posterior under the policy being evaluated, which is exactly what
an on-policy belief filter computes and what an off-policy one does not.
This is the formal reason why belief-conditioned policies trained
off-policy can be inconsistent in a way that has no single-agent
analogue, and it is the problem that off-belief learning ([Hu et al.,
2021](#bib.bib82)) is designed to remove by fixing the belief to one
induced by a reference policy.

Correlation in the sequential setting needs its own concept, because a
mediator who must reveal recommendations as play unfolds is weaker than
one who reveals everything at the start. The extensive-form correlated
equilibrium is defined by requiring that no agent gain by deviating at
any information set, given the recommendation received there and the
posterior it induces, and the set it defines sits between the Nash and
the normal-form correlated equilibria in a way that is specific to the
tree. For reinforcement learning the relevant reading is that a shared
random seed sampled once before an episode implements a normal-form
correlated equilibrium, while a shared signal broadcast at each step
implements the extensive-form notion, and these are different objects
with different attainability properties; a system whose agents share a
seed at reset and nothing thereafter is in the first category.

The third concerns regret. Counterfactual regret minimisation
([Zinkevich et al., 2007](#bib.bib201)) decomposes the overall external
regret of a behavioural strategy into a sum of local regrets at
information sets, weighted by counterfactual reach probabilities, and
minimising each local regret with a no-regret rule such as regret
matching drives the overall regret to zero. In a two-player zero-sum
game the average strategy therefore converges to a Nash equilibrium at
rate $`O(1/\sqrt{T})`$. The decomposition theorem is the game-theoretic
analogue of the performance difference lemma of single-agent
reinforcement learning ([Kakade and Langford, 2002](#bib.bib89)), which
likewise expresses a global value difference as a discounted sum of
local advantages weighted by visitation probabilities, and the
counterfactual reach probability plays the role of the discounted state
visitation measure. Recognising this makes the deep variants legible:
Deep CFR ([Brown et al., 2019](#bib.bib26)) is approximate
counterfactual regret minimisation with function approximation of the
regret table, neural fictitious self-play ([Heinrich and Silver,
2016](#bib.bib78)) is approximate fictitious play with a supervised
average-policy network, and regularised Nash dynamics ([Perolat et al.,
2021](#bib.bib139); [Perolat et al., 2022a](#bib.bib140)) replaces the
regret machinery with a continuous-time dynamics whose convergence comes
from an entropy regulariser, which is the same device that makes mirror
descent converge in single-agent policy optimisation.

###### Example 11.8 (Regret matching, three rounds by hand).

Regret matching plays each action with probability proportional to its
accumulated positive regret, and uniformly when all regrets are
non-positive. Take rock-paper-scissors against an opponent who always
plays Rock, with payoffs $`+1`$ for a win, $`-1`$ for a loss and $`0`$
for a draw.

Round one. All regrets are zero, so the agent plays uniformly. The
payoffs of the three actions against Rock are $`(0,1,-1)`$ and the
realised expected payoff is $`\tfrac{1}{3}(0+1-1)=0`$, so the regrets
become $`(0,1,-1)`$.

Round two. The positive part of the regret vector is $`(0,1,0)`$, so the
