agent plays Paper with probability one. Its payoff is $`1`$, and the
regrets update by $`(0-1,\,1-1,\,-1-1)=(-1,0,-2)`$, giving cumulative
regrets $`(-1,1,-3)`$.

Round three. The positive part is again $`(0,1,0)`$, so the agent plays
Paper. After a thousand rounds the average strategy places weight
$`0.9993`$ on Paper, which is the best response, and the external regret
per round has gone to zero.

Now the same rule in self play, both agents running regret matching from
a slightly perturbed start. The iterates cycle, as
[Section 20](#S20 "20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
predicts, and the time averages converge: after twenty thousand rounds
the average strategy is $`(0.334,0.329,0.337)`$, close to uniform, and
its exploitability is $`0.014`$ and falling at the $`O(1/\sqrt{T})`$
rate. The two halves of this example are the two halves of the practical
advice: against a fixed opponent the iterate is what you want, and in
self play the average is, and reporting the wrong one is the
methodological error recorded in
[Section 23](#S23 "23 Correspondences that fail, and the theorems that forbid them ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
$`\lozenge`$

| Extensive form                            | Reinforcement learning                                       | Status                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| ----------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Node of the tree                          | State-history pair; trajectory prefix                        | Definitional                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| Information set $`I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}`$   | Action-observation history $`h_{t}^{i}`$                     | Exact under perfect recall                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Chance node                               | Stochastic transition or observation                         | Definitional                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| Behavioural strategy                      | Policy $`\pi_{i}:\mathcal{H}_{i}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})`$ | Definitional                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| Mixed strategy                            | Distribution over deterministic policies                     | Equivalent by [Theorem 11.4](#S11.Thmtheorem4 "Theorem 11.4 ( , ). ‣ 11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning") |
| Perfect recall                            | Policy conditions on full own history                        | Definitional                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| Imperfect recall                          | Memoryless or truncated-context policy                       | Definitional                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| Realisation plan; sequence form           | Occupancy measure; Bellman flow polytope                     | Exact for one player                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| Subgame                                   | Sub-MDP rooted at a state                                    | Definitional                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| Subgame perfection                        | Bellman principle of optimality                              | Definitional                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| Backward induction                        | Value iteration on a tree                                    | Definitional                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| Belief system of a sequential equilibrium | On-policy belief filter                                      | Exact                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| Extensive-form correlated equilibrium     | Signal broadcast at each step                                | Definitional                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| Normal-form correlated equilibrium        | Shared seed sampled once at reset                            | Definitional                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| Counterfactual reach probability          | Discounted state visitation measure                          | Exact                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| CFR regret decomposition                  | Performance difference lemma                                 | Structural                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Average strategy of CFR                   | Policy averaging; target network of NFSP                     | Exact in zero-sum                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |

Table 20: Extensive-form vocabulary and its reinforcement learning
translation.

## 12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning

Partial observability concerns ignorance about the state. Incomplete
information concerns ignorance about the game: who the other agents are,
what they want, and what they know. Game theory handles it by the device
of [Harsanyi (1967)](#bib.bib73), which converts ignorance about the
game into ignorance about a state variable, and reinforcement learning
handles it by the device of Bayesian reinforcement learning, which
converts ignorance about the model into ignorance about a state
variable. They are the same device.

The two readings are almost interchangeable once the device is applied,
and the only thing that differs is what the unknown is taken to be. A
game theorist calls the unknown a type and attaches it to an agent, so
that uncertainty is about preferences and information. A reinforcement
learning researcher calls the unknown a task parameter and attaches it
to the environment, so that uncertainty is about dynamics and rewards.
Both then put a prior on it, fold it into the state, and solve the
enlarged problem. The one asymmetry is that with several agents a type
is something an agent may wish to conceal, and concealment has no
single-agent counterpart at all, which is where signalling begins.

| Game theory               | Reinforcement learning                        | How to read the pair                        |
| ------------------------- | --------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- |
| Type $`\theta_{i}`$       | Latent task parameter; opponent identity      | Private information determining preferences |
| Type space $`\Theta_{i}`$ | Task family; model class                      | The range of what the unknown can be        |
| Common prior $`p`$        | Task distribution; meta-training distribution | The shared belief before anything is seen   |
| Harsanyi transformation   | State augmentation with the latent parameter  | Make the unknown part of the state          |
| Interim payoff            | Value conditional on the posterior            | After you learn your own type               |
| Ex-ante payoff            | Expected return over the task distribution    | Before anything is drawn                    |
| Bayes-Nash equilibrium    | Fixed point over type-conditioned policies    | Best response at every type                 |
| Bayes-optimal policy      | Solution of the BAMDP                         | The $`n=1`$ case of the above               |
| Value of information      | Exploration bonus; information gain           | Why a costly action can be worth taking     |
| Signalling                | No single-agent counterpart                   | Acting to change what others believe        |
| Belief hierarchy          | Nested opponent models                        | Beliefs about beliefs, without end          |
| Common knowledge          | The meet of the agents’ partitions            | What conditioning everyone can agree on     |
| Zero-shot coordination    | Generalisation to an unseen partner           | Ex-ante payoff over a partner prior         |

Table 21: Incomplete-information vocabulary read from both sides.

###### Definition 12.1 (Bayesian game).

A Bayesian game is a tuple
$`(\mathcal{N},\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\},\{\Theta_{i}\},p,\{u_{i}\})`$ in
which $`\Theta_{i}`$ is the finite type set of agent $`i`$,
$`p\in\Delta(\prod_{i}\Theta_{i})`$ is a common prior over type
profiles, and $`u_{i}:\mathcal{A}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}`$ is the
payoff of agent $`i`$. Each agent observes its own type $`\theta_{i}`$
and nothing else, so a strategy is a map
$`\pi_{i}:\Theta_{i}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})`$.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. A Bayesian game is a task
distribution. The type profile $`\theta`$ is the task parameter drawn at
reset, the common prior $`p`$ is the sampler that draws it, and
$`u_{i}(a,\theta)`$ is a reward function that depends on that parameter.
The one feature with no single-agent counterpart is that the draw is
revealed asymmetrically: agent $`i`$ is told $`\theta_{i}`$ and nothing
else, so the strategy signature
$`\pi_{i}:\Theta_{i}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})`$ is a policy conditioned
on a private context vector. In an implementation $`\theta_{i}`$ is
whatever is in head $`i`$’s observation at reset and not in anybody
else’s: its own capability, its own goal, its own system prompt. Reading
a multi-agent problem as a Bayesian game is therefore the act of asking
which parts of the reset state are private, and the answer determines
whether an agent can be trained against a fixed partner or must be
trained against a distribution of them.

###### Definition 12.2 (Bayes-Nash equilibrium).

A profile $`\pi^{\star}`$ is a Bayes-Nash equilibrium when for every
agent $`i`$ and every type $`\theta_{i}`$ with $`p(\theta_{i})>0`$,

|     |                                         |                                                                                                    |                                                                |
| --- | --------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | ---- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\pi^{\star}\_{i}(\cdot\, | \,\theta*{i})\in\argmax*{\pi*{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}*{i})}\;\mathbb{E}_{\theta_{-i}\sim p(\cdot\, | \,\theta*{i})}\;\mathbb{E}*{a*{-i}\sim\pi^{\star}*{-i}(\cdot\, | \,\theta*{-i})}\;\mathbb{E}*{a*{i}\sim\pi*{i}}\big[u_{i}(a,\theta)\big].`$ |     | (11) |

 

The reinforcement learning reading. Compare
[Equation 11](#S12.E11 "In Definition 12.2 (Bayes-Nash equilibrium). ‣ 12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
with
[Definition 4.1](#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 4.1 (Nash equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The fixed-point condition is the same, with one extra layer of
conditioning: the policy of head $`i`$ must be greedy not once but once
per value of its private context, against a posterior over the others’
contexts obtained by conditioning the prior on its own. So a Bayes-Nash
equilibrium is what a context-conditioned joint policy converges to when
every head is trained to best respond at every context simultaneously,
and the inner expectation
$`\mathbb{E}_{\theta_{-i}\sim p(\cdot\,|\,\theta_{i})}`$ is the implicit
belief that a policy acquires about its partners when it is trained
across a population of them. The $`n=1`$ case is the Bayes-optimal
policy of
[Remark 12.5](#S12.Thmtheorem5 "Remark 12.5 (Harsanyi transformation and Bayesian reinforcement learning coincide). ‣ 12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
which is the meta-reinforcement learning target.
[Example 12.3](#S12.Thmtheorem3 "Example 12.3 (A two-type entry game solved by interim best response). ‣ 12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
solves a two-type instance by enumeration.

The Harsanyi transformation reads
[Equation 11](#S12.E11 "In Definition 12.2 (Bayes-Nash equilibrium). ‣ 12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
as a Nash equilibrium of an enlarged game with an initial chance move
that draws $`\theta\sim p`$ and reveals $`\theta_{i}`$ to agent $`i`$.
Formally, a Bayesian game is the extensive-form game whose root is a
chance node over $`\Theta`$ and whose information sets for agent $`i`$
are indexed by $`\theta_{i}`$; a Bayes-Nash equilibrium of the former is
a Nash equilibrium of the latter and conversely. The construction places
incomplete information inside the framework of
[Section 11](#S11 "11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and therefore inside the framework of partial observability.

###### Example 12.3 (A two-type entry game solved by interim best response).

An entrant decides whether to enter a market; an incumbent decides
whether to fight or accommodate, without observing whether the entrant
is strong or weak. The entrant is strong with probability $`\mu=0.6`$.
Payoffs are listed as (entrant, incumbent). A strong entrant that enters
earns $`1`$ if fought and $`3`$ if accommodated, and the incumbent earns
$`-1`$ and $`1`$ respectively. A weak entrant that enters earns $`-2`$
if fought and $`2`$ if accommodated, and the incumbent earns $`2`$ and
$`1`$. If the entrant stays out, both earn $`0`$ and $`4`$.

Solve it by checking all eight pure profiles. Entry is a best response
for the strong type against both incumbent actions, since $`1>0`$ and
$`3>0`$, and for the weak type only against accommodation, since
$`-2<0<2`$. Given that both types enter, the incumbent compares
fighting, worth $`0.6(-1)+0.4(2)=0.2`$, with accommodating, worth
$`0.6(1)+0.4(1)=1.0`$, and accommodates. The profile in which both types
enter and the incumbent accommodates is therefore the unique pure
Bayes-Nash equilibrium: interim payoffs $`3`$ for a strong entrant and
$`2`$ for a weak one, ex-ante payoff $`0.6\times 3+0.4\times 2=2.6`$,
and $`1.0`$ for the incumbent.

The threshold matters more than the solution. The incumbent fights when
$`\mu(-1)+(1-\mu)(2)>\mu(1)+(1-\mu)(1)`$, that is when $`\mu<1/3`$. So
the same game with the same payoffs has a different equilibrium if the
prior changes, and a weak entrant benefits from the presence of strong
entrants in the population, which is the pooling effect that makes
reputation work. For multi-agent reinforcement learning the exact
counterpart is that an agent trained against a population of partners
behaves according to the population mixture rather than to any
individual partner, so changing the training distribution changes the
learned policy even when every individual partner is unchanged. That is
a statement about the prior and not about the algorithm, and it is the
reason
[Section 22](#S22 "22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
asks what the partner distribution at deployment will be. $`\lozenge`$

###### Definition 12.4 (Family of Markov decision processes with an unobserved index).

A latent Markov decision process is a family
$`\{M_{\theta}\}_{\theta\in\Theta}`$ of Markov decision processes
sharing state and action spaces, together with a prior
$`p\in\Delta(\Theta)`$. At the start of an episode $`\theta\sim p`$ is
drawn and held fixed; the agent does not observe $`\theta`$.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. This is the standard
meta-reinforcement learning setup written as a tuple: a family of
environments, a sampler over them, one draw per episode, and no
indicator telling the agent which draw it got. Everything a practitioner
does with it follows from
[Theorem 2.5](#S2.Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 2.5 (Belief-MDP reduction; belief sufficiency and the functional equation, , ; piecewise linearity and convexity, , ). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
applied to the hidden pair $`(s,\theta)`$. A recurrent policy trained
across the family is approximating the posterior over $`\theta`$ in its
hidden state; a context encoder is approximating it explicitly; and the
exploration an agent performs early in an episode is not a heuristic
addition but the optimal action of the enlarged problem, because the
belief over $`\theta`$ is part of the state and acting to sharpen it has
value.
[Example 12.6](#S12.Thmtheorem6 "Example 12.6 (The value of information, computed exactly). ‣ 12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
prices that value at $`1/6`$ on a three-pull bandit.

###### Remark 12.5 (Harsanyi transformation and Bayesian reinforcement learning coincide).

Let $`\{M_{\theta}\},p`$ be a latent Markov decision process. Define the
partially observable Markov decision process $`P`$ with hidden state
$`(s,\theta)`$, transition
$`\mathcal{T}_{P}((s^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\,|\,(s,\theta),a)=\mathcal{T}_{\theta}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a)\,\mathbf{1}[\theta^{\prime}=\theta]`$,
observation $`o=s`$, reward $`\mathcal{R}_{\theta}(s,a,s^{\prime})`$ and
initial belief $`b_{0}\otimes p`$. Then $`P`$ is the Harsanyi
transformation of $`\{M_{\theta}\}`$ with $`n=1`$, the belief Markov
decision process of $`P`$ obtained from
[Theorem 2.5](#S2.Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 2.5 (Belief-MDP reduction; belief sufficiency and the functional equation, , ; piecewise linearity and convexity, , ). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the Bayes-adaptive Markov decision process of [Duff
(2002)](#bib.bib48), its state $`(s_{t},b_{t})`$ with
$`b_{t}\in\Delta(\Theta)`$ is the posterior over the unknown model, and
its optimal policy is the Bayes-optimal policy, which is the $`n=1`$
degeneracy of
[Definition 12.2](#S12.Thmtheorem2 "Definition 12.2 (Bayes-Nash equilibrium). ‣ 12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The identification is definitional rather than a theorem, and the scope
differs in one respect worth recording: Duff takes the information state
to be a posterior over the transition parameters themselves, of which
the finite latent index used here is a special case.

This degeneracy is worth dwelling on because it is the one that an
engineer uses most often without naming it. The Bayes-optimal policy is
the solution of a planning problem in which the exploration-exploitation
trade-off is not a heuristic addition but a consequence of the belief
dynamics: an action is valuable partly for its reward and partly for the
information its outcome carries about $`\theta`$, and the latter appears
automatically in the Bellman equation of the Bayes-adaptive Markov
decision process because the belief is part of the state. The
game-theoretic reading is that exploration is what remains of incomplete
information when there is only one agent. With several agents, the same
structure produces the value of concealing one’s own type, which has no
single-agent counterpart at all: in a Bayesian game an agent may
rationally forgo immediate payoff to avoid revealing $`\theta_{i}`$, and
signalling and screening are the names for the resulting phenomena.
There is no analogue of signalling in single-agent reinforcement
learning because there is nobody to signal to, and conversely the
information-gathering value of an action survives in both settings. We
therefore place exploration and signalling as the two descendants of a
single structure, which is the clean way to see why multi-agent
exploration is not merely harder single-agent exploration.

###### Example 12.6 (The value of information, computed exactly).

Two arms, three pulls. The first arm is known to pay $`1`$ with
probability $`0.5`$. The second pays $`1`$ with an unknown probability
given a uniform prior, that is $`\mathrm{Beta}(1,1)`$. The
Bayes-adaptive state is the pair of Beta counts, and dynamic programming
over it is exact.

With one pull left the agent takes whichever arm has the higher
posterior mean, so $`V_{1}(a,b)=\max(0.5,\,a/(a+b))`$; with the prior
counts this is $`0.5`$. With two pulls left, pulling the known arm first
is worth $`0.5+V_{1}(1,1)=1.0`$, while pulling the unknown arm first is
worth
$`0.5(1+V_{1}(2,1))+0.5\,V_{1}(1,2)=0.5(1+\tfrac{2}{3})+0.5(0.5)=1.0833`$,
so $`V_{2}(1,1)=1.0833`$. With three pulls left the same comparison
gives $`0.5+V_{2}(1,1)=1.5833`$ for the known arm against
$`0.5(1+V_{2}(2,1))+0.5\,V_{2}(1,2)=0.5(1+1.3333)+0.5(1.0)=1.6667`$ for
the unknown one, so the Bayes-optimal first action is to explore and
$`V_{3}(1,1)=1.6667`$.

An agent that always pulls the known arm collects $`1.5`$. The
difference, $`1/6\approx 0.1667`$, is the value of information, and it
appears in the Bellman equation of the enlarged problem with no
exploration bonus added by hand. Two consequences are worth recording.
First, the exploration is not a heuristic but the optimal action of a
well-posed planning problem, which is what makes the Bayes-adaptive
formulation the correct reference point for any exploration method.
Second, the same quantity in a multi-agent setting splits into two: the
information an action reveals to oneself, which is this bonus, and the
information it reveals to others, which is a cost rather than a benefit
whenever the others can use it, and which is the content of
[Section 13](#S13 "13 Communication: cheap talk, signalling, persuasion and emergent protocols ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
$`\lozenge`$

The practical instantiations follow. Meta-reinforcement learning trains
a policy on a distribution over tasks and evaluates it on a held-out
task, which is exactly the problem of approximating the Bayes-optimal
policy of the corresponding Bayes-adaptive Markov decision process;
recurrent meta-learners such as $`\mathrm{RL}^{2}`$ ([Duan et al.,
2016](#bib.bib46)) learn to carry an implicit posterior in their hidden
state, and variational methods such as VariBAD ([Zintgraf et al.,
2020](#bib.bib202)) learn an explicit approximate posterior over
$`\theta`$ and condition the policy on it, which is the belief-MDP
reduction made executable. Agent modelling is the multi-agent instance:
an agent uncertain about the policies of its partners holds a posterior
over a type space of candidate policies, and acting optimally with
respect to that posterior is the same computation ([Albrecht and Stone,
2018](#bib.bib3)). The interactive partially observable Markov decision
process of [Gmytrasiewicz and Doshi (2005)](#bib.bib67) is the version
in which the type space consists of other agents’ models, including
their own models of oneself. Because the beliefs may be nested
arbitrarily deeply, [Gmytrasiewicz and Doshi (2005)](#bib.bib67)
describe the optimal solutions as only asymptotically computable, with
finitely nested approximations computable at a finite horizon. The
universal type space of [Mertens and Zamir (1985)](#bib.bib117) is the
construction that makes the infinite hierarchy a well-defined object,
and its reinforcement learning counterpart is the observation that any
finite recursive opponent model truncates a hierarchy whose completion
exists but is not representable.

Common knowledge is the concept that organises this hierarchy, and it
deserves an explicit treatment because it is what the reductions of
[Section 9](#S9 "9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
condition on.

###### Example 12.7 (Posteriors that differ without disagreement being common knowledge).

Four states, equally likely, and an event $`E=\{1,2\}`$ whose prior
probability is $`0.5`$. Ann’s information partition is
$`\{\{1,2\},\{3,4\}\}`$ and Bob’s is $`\{\{1\},\{2,3\},\{4\}\}`$. At
state $`1`$ Ann’s posterior for $`E`$ is $`1`$, since her cell is
contained in $`E`$, and Bob’s is also $`1`$, since his cell is
$`\{1\}`$. At state $`2`$ Ann’s posterior is still $`1`$ while Bob’s is
$`1/2`$, because his cell $`\{2,3\}`$ meets $`E`$ in one of its two
states. The two disagree.

Aumann’s theorem ([Aumann, 1976](#bib.bib11)) says that if the
posteriors were common knowledge they would have to be equal, and the
example does not contradict it, because nothing non-trivial is common
knowledge here: the meet of the two partitions, which is the partition
into events that both agents know that both agents know, is the single
cell $`\{1,2,3,4\}`$, since $`\{1,2\}`$ and $`\{2,3\}`$ overlap and
chain the four states together. The reinforcement learning reading is
exactly the obstruction of
[Section 8](#S8 "8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"):
two agents may hold persistently different beliefs about the world, and
a protocol that merely exchanges point estimates will not reconcile
them, because reconciliation requires the exchange to be common
knowledge. The coordinator constructions of
[Section 9](#S9 "9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
buy exactly this, by conditioning only on the meet, and the method of
[Schroeder de Witt et al. (2019)](#bib.bib156) chooses dynamically how
large a group to treat as sharing common knowledge. $`\lozenge`$

A final correspondence belongs here because it is becoming the dominant
formulation of cooperative evaluation. Zero-shot coordination asks for a
policy that performs well with an unseen partner drawn from a
distribution over reasonable partners rather than with a partner it was
trained alongside. In the vocabulary of this section the partner’s
identity is a type, the training distribution is the prior, and the
objective is the ex-ante Bayesian payoff, which makes zero-shot
coordination the Bayesian-game formulation of cooperative play.
Other-play ([Hu et al., 2020](#bib.bib81)) achieves it by optimising
against partners whose policies have been relabelled by a random
symmetry of the game, which removes the reliance on the arbitrary
labelling that self-play would otherwise exploit, and it is best read as
an equilibrium-selection refinement in the sense of
[Section 8](#S8 "8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
rather than as a learning algorithm.

| Game theory                                                   | Reinforcement learning                         | Status and caveat                     |
| ------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------- |
| Type $`\theta_{i}`$                                           | Latent task parameter; opponent model index    | Definitional                          |
| Common prior $`p`$                                            | Task distribution; meta-training distribution  | Definitional                          |
| Harsanyi transformation                                       | Augmenting the state with the latent parameter | Exact                                 |
| Bayes-Nash equilibrium                                        | Bayes-optimal policy when $`n=1`$              | Exact at $`n=1`$                      |
| Interim payoff                                                | Value conditional on the posterior             | Definitional                          |
| Ex-ante payoff                                                | Expected return over the task distribution     | Definitional                          |
| Value of information                                          | Exploration bonus in a BAMDP                   | Exact                                 |
| Signalling and screening                                      | No single-agent counterpart                    | Multi-agent only                      |
| Belief hierarchy                                              | I-POMDP nested models                          | Exact; only asymptotically computable |
| Universal type space ([Mertens and Zamir, 1985](#bib.bib117)) | Completion of nested opponent models           | Exact; not representable              |
| Common knowledge; the meet                                    | Public features legitimate to condition on     | Exact                                 |
| Agreement theorem ([Aumann, 1976](#bib.bib11))                | Belief exchange must itself be public          | Exact                                 |
| Purification of mixed strategies                              | Deterministic policy with latent noise         | Exact                                 |
| Equilibrium selection under uncertainty                       | Zero-shot coordination; other-play             | Structural                            |

Table 22: Incomplete information and its reinforcement learning
counterparts.

## 13 Communication: cheap talk, signalling, persuasion and emergent protocols

Every model so far has fixed what each agent observes. Communication is
the operation of letting agents choose what others observe, and it is
the single largest body of multi-agent reinforcement learning work with
no corresponding chapter in a reinforcement learning textbook, because
with one agent there is nobody to speak to. Game theory has studied the
operation for four decades under three headings that differ in who bears
the cost of the message and in who controls the channel. Cheap talk is
costless and uncommitted; signalling is costly and therefore credible;
information design, or Bayesian persuasion, is the case in which one
party commits in advance to an information policy. All three appear in
multi-agent reinforcement learning, usually unnamed, and all three come
with results that settle questions practitioners otherwise answer
empirically.

The two readings differ in what is taken to be given. A game theorist
takes the message space as given and asks which mappings from
information to messages are equilibria, so that the central question is
whether informative communication is self-enforcing. A reinforcement
learning researcher takes the channel as a differentiable component and
asks whether gradient descent will learn to use it, so that the central
question is whether a useful protocol emerges from training. The results
below answer the first question and thereby constrain the second: an
informative protocol that is not an equilibrium of the induced game will
not survive training, whatever the architecture.

| Game theory                 | Reinforcement learning                       | How to read the pair                             |
| --------------------------- | -------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------ |
| Message $`m\in\mathcal{M}`$ | Communication action; emitted token          | An action whose only effect is on others’ inputs |
| Cheap talk                  | Costless differentiable channel              | Messages do not enter the payoff directly        |
| Babbling equilibrium        | A channel the agents ignore                  | Messages uncorrelated with anything              |
| Informative equilibrium     | A learned protocol that is used              | Messages correlated with private state           |
| Signalling game             | Costly communication action                  | The message has a price that differs by type     |
| Separating equilibrium      | Protocol that reveals the type               | Different types send different messages          |
| Pooling equilibrium         | Protocol that hides the type                 | All types send the same message                  |
| Bayesian persuasion         | Information design; teacher curriculum       | Commit to a signal before seeing the state       |
| Communication equilibrium   | Mediated play with private channels          | A mediator who listens and advises               |
| Sender bias $`b`$           | Misalignment of the two reward functions     | How far apart the objectives are                 |
| Partition equilibrium       | Coarse, quantised protocol                   | Only intervals can be communicated               |
| Positive signalling         | Mutual information between state and message | The speaker is saying something                  |
| Positive listening          | Causal effect of messages on actions         | The listener is acting on it                     |

Table 23: Communication vocabulary read from both sides.

###### Definition 13.1 (Cheap talk extension).

Let $`G`$ be a Bayesian game and let $`\mathcal{M}_{i}`$ be a finite
message set for each agent. The cheap talk extension of $`G`$ is the
game in which each agent first observes its type, then simultaneously
sends a message $`m_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{i}`$ which is observed by all,
and then plays an action. Payoffs are those of $`G`$ and do not depend
on the messages.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. Add to each head a second output
that no reward term reads, and route it into every other head’s
observation at the next step. That is a cheap talk extension, and it is
what a differentiable communication channel is. The message set
$`\mathcal{M}_{i}`$ is the vocabulary or the dimension of the emitted
vector, and the fact that payoffs do not depend on messages is what
makes the channel cheap: no gradient reaches the speaker except through
the listener’s behaviour. The immediate consequence, and the one that
explains most empirical results in this area, is that every equilibrium
of the game without the channel survives with it, realised by messages
that carry no information and listeners that ignore them. A training run
that ends with an unused channel has therefore converged, correctly, to
one of the equilibria of the game it was given, and the remedy is an
equilibrium selection device rather than a larger channel.

The device and its equilibrium consequences are surveyed by [Farrell and
Rabin (1996)](#bib.bib53). Because the payoffs do not depend on
messages, every equilibrium of $`G`$ extends to an equilibrium of its
cheap talk extension in which messages are uncorrelated with types and
ignored, which is called babbling. The question is whether anything else
is an equilibrium, and the answer depends entirely on how aligned the
agents’ objectives are.

###### Example 13.2 (The whole of emergent communication in four numbers).

A sender privately observes whether the world is in state $`A`$ or
$`B`$, each with probability one half, and a receiver must take an
action matching the state. Both earn $`1`$ for a match and $`0`$
otherwise. Without communication the receiver guesses and both earn
$`0.5`$. With a two-symbol channel, the profile in which the sender maps
$`A`$ to the first symbol and $`B`$ to the second, and the receiver maps
symbols to the matching action, earns $`1`$. It is an equilibrium:
neither agent gains by deviating, since the sender’s payoff is the
receiver’s and the receiver is already best responding. The gain from
communication is exactly $`0.5`$.

Three facts about this trivial example generalise. First, the relabelled
protocol in which $`A`$ maps to the second symbol is an equally good
equilibrium, so the protocol is not identified and two independent
training runs will generally produce incompatible languages, which is
the cross-play failure of
[Example 8.7](#S8.Thmtheorem7 "Example 8.7 (Why an arbitrary convention counts as an equilibrium). ‣ 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
in its purest form. Second, babbling remains an equilibrium, so a
training run that converges to a useless channel has not failed to
converge; it has converged to the wrong equilibrium, and the remedy is
an equilibrium selection device rather than a better optimiser. Third,
the entire gain is available only because the two payoffs coincide. The
next example shows what happens when they do not. $`\lozenge`$

###### Theorem 13.3 (Cheap talk under misaligned objectives; [Crawford and Sobel, 1982](#bib.bib38)).

Let the state be uniform on $`[0,1]`$, let the receiver’s loss be
$`(y-\theta)^{2}`$ and the sender’s be $`(y-\theta-b)^{2}`$ for a bias
$`b>0`$, where $`y`$ is the receiver’s action. Then every equilibrium of
the cheap talk game is an interval partition equilibrium, in which the
sender reports only which element of a finite partition of $`[0,1]`$
contains $`\theta`$, and the number of intervals is bounded: the largest
attainable number $`N(b)`$ is the largest integer satisfying
$`2bN(N-1)<1`$. As $`b\to 0`$ the attainable partitions become
arbitrarily fine, and for $`b`$ large enough only the babbling
equilibrium survives.

###### Example 13.4 (How much can be said, as a function of how much is shared).

Applying the bound of
[Theorem 13.3](#S13.Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 13.3 (Cheap talk under misaligned objectives; , ). ‣ 13 Communication: cheap talk, signalling, persuasion and emergent protocols ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
a bias of $`b=0.05`$ permits at most three intervals, since
$`0.1\cdot 3\cdot 2=0.6<1`$ while $`0.1\cdot 4\cdot 3=1.2>1`$; a bias of
$`b=0.1`$ permits two; and a bias of $`b=0.25`$ or more permits only
one, which is babbling. The content for multi-agent reinforcement
learning is a hard limit that no architecture can exceed. Giving two
agents with partly conflicting rewards a wide communication channel does
not produce fine-grained communication, because fine-grained
communication is not an equilibrium of the game they are playing; what
emerges is a coarse quantisation whose resolution is set by the
misalignment. Reported channel capacities in such settings are therefore
measuring the reward misalignment, and the way to obtain a richer
protocol is to align the rewards, to make the messages costly, or to
give one party commitment power, which are precisely the three devices
of this section. $`\lozenge`$

Costly messages are the second device. In a signalling game ([Spence,
1973](#bib.bib169)) the sender’s message enters its own payoff, with a
cost that depends on its type, and a separating equilibrium exists when
the cost differential is large enough that a low type does not wish to
imitate a high one. The reinforcement learning counterpart is any
communication channel whose use consumes a resource that different
agents value differently, such as bandwidth under a budget, an action
slot that could have been used for a physical action, or a delay. The
structural lesson is that costless channels support both informative and
babbling equilibria while appropriately costly ones can make
informativeness the unique outcome, so adding a small cost to
communication can be an equilibrium selection device rather than a
handicap.

The third device is commitment, and it is the one with the sharpest
results.

###### Definition 13.5 (Bayesian persuasion; [Kamenica and Gentzkow, 2011](#bib.bib91)).

A sender commits, before observing the state, to a signal, that is a map
from states to distributions over messages. A receiver observes the
message, forms a Bayes posterior, and acts to maximise its own payoff.
The sender’s problem is to choose the signal maximising its own expected
payoff, subject to the constraint that the distribution of posteriors it
induces must average to the prior.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. The sender is a leader in the sense
of
[Section 14](#S14 "14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
whose committed object is an information policy rather than an action,
so the construction is the bilevel problem of
[Equation 12](#S14.E12 "In Definition 14.1 (Stackelberg equilibrium). ‣ 14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
with the inner agent’s input, not its reward, under the outer agent’s
control. In reinforcement learning this is the teacher of a
teacher-student or curriculum setup: the teacher commits to a
distribution over tasks or over what the student is shown, the student
best responds by learning, and the teacher’s objective is the student’s
eventual behaviour. The constraint that the induced posteriors average
to the prior is the formal statement that a curriculum can redistribute
what the student believes but cannot shift the average, which is why a
curriculum changes what is learned without changing the underlying task
distribution.
[Example 13.6](#S13.Thmtheorem6 "Example 13.6 (The prosecutor who convicts sixty per cent of defendants). ‣ 13 Communication: cheap talk, signalling, persuasion and emergent protocols ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
shows that the optimal commitment is deliberately noisy, and that full
disclosure is strictly worse for the sender than a calibrated lie.

The constraint named at the end is the whole theory: a signal is
feasible exactly when the induced distribution over posteriors has mean
equal to the prior, so the sender’s problem is to choose a
mean-preserving spread of the prior and the solution is the
concavification of the sender’s value as a function of the posterior.

###### Example 13.6 (The prosecutor who convicts sixty per cent of defendants).

A judge convicts when the posterior probability of guilt is at least
$`1/2`$ and acquits otherwise. The prior probability of guilt is
$`0.3`$, so with no information the judge acquits always and the
prosecutor, who wants convictions, obtains nothing. With full
information the judge convicts exactly the guilty and the prosecutor
obtains $`0.3`$.

Now let the prosecutor commit to an investigation that always reports
“convict” when the defendant is guilty and reports “convict” with
probability $`x`$ when innocent. The posterior after a “convict” report
is $`0.3/(0.3+0.7x)`$, which equals $`1/2`$ when
$`x=3/7\approx 0.4286`$. At that $`x`$ the judge is just willing to
convict, and the probability of a conviction is
$`0.3+0.7\cdot\tfrac{3}{7}=0.6`$. The prosecutor therefore obtains
$`0.6`$, double what full disclosure yields and infinitely more than
silence. Committing to a noisy investigation beats committing to an
accurate one.

The reinforcement learning readings are two and both are useful. In
teacher-student and curriculum settings the teacher is a sender who
commits to a distribution over tasks and the student is a receiver who
best responds; the example says the teacher’s optimal curriculum is in
general not the most informative one, which is the formal counterpart of
the empirical finding that intermediate-difficulty tasks beat maximally
informative ones. In explanation and alignment settings the same
computation says that a system optimised to produce a particular
downstream decision will, if it can commit to a reporting policy, prefer
a calibrated-but-noisy report to an honest one, which is a statement
about incentives rather than about any particular training objective.
$`\lozenge`$

The fourth object belongs to this section because it completes the
hierarchy of
[Section 4](#S4 "4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
A communication equilibrium ([Forges, 1986](#bib.bib60)) is the outcome
of a mediator who receives private reports from the agents and sends
private recommendations back, subject to the agents being willing both
to report truthfully and to obey. It generalises the correlated
equilibrium of
[Definition 4.5](#S4.Thmtheorem5 "Definition 4.5 (Correlated and coarse correlated equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
which is the special case in which the mediator receives nothing and
only sends, and in a game of incomplete information the set of
communication equilibria is strictly larger than the set of correlated
equilibria. For multi-agent reinforcement learning the correspondence is
that a learned communication protocol with a central module that both
receives and broadcasts implements a communication equilibrium, so its
attainable outcomes exceed those of a shared random seed, and the price
is that truthful reporting must itself be incentive compatible, which is
a constraint that a differentiable channel does not enforce
automatically.

###### Example 13.7 (What mediation is worth in the game of chicken).

Collecting the numbers computed in
[Example 4.6](#S4.Thmtheorem6 "Example 4.6 (Correlation that beats every equilibrium of chicken). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
the best pure Nash equilibrium of chicken has welfare $`9`$, the mixed
equilibrium has welfare $`9.333`$, the best correlated equilibrium has
welfare $`10.5`$, and the cooperative optimum, which no equilibrium
attains, has welfare $`12`$. A shared random seed therefore recovers
$`10.5-9.333=1.167`$ of the $`2.667`$ that separates the mixed
equilibrium from the cooperative optimum, and nothing short of changing
the game recovers the rest. This is the precise accounting of what
communication buys in a mixed-motive problem, and it bounds what any
emergent protocol can achieve in such a problem without a change to the
rewards. $`\lozenge`$

The reinforcement learning literature on this topic is large and is best
read through the three headings above. Differentiable inter-agent
learning ([Foerster et al., 2016](#bib.bib56)) and the communication
architectures of [Sukhbaatar et al. (2016)](#bib.bib172) are cheap talk
extensions with a differentiable channel, and they work well in
common-payoff settings, which
[Example 13.2](#S13.Thmtheorem2 "Example 13.2 (The whole of emergent communication in four numbers). ‣ 13 Communication: cheap talk, signalling, persuasion and emergent protocols ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
explains, and degrade as the rewards diverge, which
[Theorem 13.3](#S13.Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 13.3 (Cheap talk under misaligned objectives; , ). ‣ 13 Communication: cheap talk, signalling, persuasion and emergent protocols ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
explains. Referential games of the kind studied by [Lazaridou et al.
(2017)](#bib.bib104) are common-payoff cheap talk games with a
particular message space, so the emergence of an informative protocol is
expected and the arbitrariness of the protocol is also expected. The
diagnostic question of whether a protocol has emerged at all requires
measuring both that messages depend on private information and that
actions depend on messages, which are the positive signalling and
positive listening criteria of [Lowe et al. (2019)](#bib.bib111);
reporting a return improvement alone does not distinguish an informative
equilibrium from a babbling one that happens to coordinate by other
means. Finally, influence-based intrinsic rewards ([Jaques et al.,
2019](#bib.bib85)) change the game by paying agents for the causal
effect of their messages, and report improved coordination empirically;
the claim is not that babbling ceases to be an equilibrium, and indeed
against a listener that ignores messages every message has zero causal
influence and the speaker remains indifferent. The device biases the
dynamics away from the babbling equilibrium rather than removing it, so
it is an equilibrium selection device and should be reported as one,
since the game solved is not the game posed.

| Structure                 | RL counterpart                                         | Equilibrium consequence                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | What decides                            |
| ------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------- |
| No channel                | Independent policies                                   | Stage equilibria only                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | Nothing                                 |
| Cheap talk, common payoff | Differentiable channel, shared reward                  | Fully informative equilibria exist; so does babbling                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | Equilibrium selection                   |
| Cheap talk, biased        | Channel with misaligned rewards                        | Only coarse partitions ([Theorem 13.3](#S13.Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 13.3 (Cheap talk under misaligned objectives; , ). ‣ 13 Communication: cheap talk, signalling, persuasion and emergent protocols ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")) | The bias $`b`$                          |
| Costly signalling         | Bandwidth budget; action slot                          | Separating equilibria can be unique                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | Cost differential by type               |
| Bayesian persuasion       | Teacher, curriculum, information design                | Sender prefers noisy disclosure                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | Commitment power                        |
| Public correlation device | Shared random seed at reset                            | Correlated equilibrium ([Definition 4.5](#S4.Thmtheorem5 "Definition 4.5 (Correlated and coarse correlated equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"))         | Availability of a seed                  |
| Mediator with reports     | Central module that listens and advises                | Communication equilibrium ([Forges, 1986](#bib.bib60))                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | Truthful reporting must be incentivised |
| Common knowledge groups   | MACKRL ([Schroeder de Witt et al., 2019](#bib.bib156)) | Coordination within a commonly informed group                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | Size of the meet                        |

Table 24: Communication structures, their equilibrium consequences and
the multi-agent reinforcement learning constructions that instantiate
them. The last column names what determines whether informative
communication survives.

## 14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping

Every solution concept so far has treated the agents symmetrically in
time. The Stackelberg model breaks that symmetry by letting one agent
commit to a strategy that the others observe before choosing their own,
and the resulting asymmetry is the game-theoretic content of a
surprisingly large part of modern machine learning, including
adversarial training, reward design, curriculum generation and the
fine-tuning of language models from human feedback.

The two readings are a definition and an algorithm for the same object.
A game theorist writes a Stackelberg equilibrium as a maximisation over
the leader’s strategies of the leader’s payoff evaluated at the
follower’s best response, and asks what commitment is worth. A
reinforcement learning researcher writes the same expression as a
bilevel optimisation problem and asks how to differentiate through the
inner solution. The gradient that results has two terms, and the second
term, the one that accounts for how the leader’s move changes what the
follower will do, is exactly what distinguishes the leader from a
simultaneous mover. Every opponent-shaping method in the literature is
an approximation to that second term.

| Game theory                                      | Reinforcement learning                    | How to read the pair                     |
| ------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------- |
| Leader, first mover                              | Outer loop; slow timescale parameters     | Commits before the other chooses         |
| Follower                                         | Inner loop; fast timescale parameters     | Best responds to what it observes        |
| Best-response correspondence $`\mathrm{BR}_{2}`$ | Inner argmax; converged inner solve       | The follower’s reaction                  |
| Strong Stackelberg equilibrium                   | Bilevel optimum with favourable tie-break | Leader resolves follower indifference    |
| Value of commitment                              | Gain from timescale separation            | What acting first is worth               |
| Implicit function theorem                        | Differentiating through the inner optimum | How the leader’s gradient is computed    |
| Mechanism design                                 | Reward design; environment design         | The leader chooses the game, not a move  |
| Revelation principle                             | Restrict to truthful direct protocols     | Without loss, agents report honestly     |
| Incentive compatibility                          | Reward hacking does not pay               | Honest or intended behaviour is optimal  |
| Principal-agent problem                          | RLHF; delegation to a learned policy      | One party’s objective, another’s actions |
| Credible commitment                              | Frozen weights; immutable reward model    | A promise the follower believes          |

Table 25: Commitment vocabulary read from both sides.

###### Definition 14.1 (Stackelberg equilibrium).

In a two-agent game, let agent $`1`$ be the leader and agent $`2`$ the
follower. The follower’s best-response correspondence is
$`\mathrm{BR}_{2}(\pi_{1})`$. A strong Stackelberg equilibrium is a pair
$`(\pi_{1}^{\star},\pi_{2}^{\star})`$ with

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                 |     |      |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | ---- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{\star}\in\argmax_{\pi_{1}}\;\max_{\pi_{2}\in\mathrm{BR}_{2}(\pi_{1})}u_{1}(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}),\qquad\pi_{2}^{\star}\in\mathrm{BR}_{2}(\pi_{1}^{\star}),`$ |     | (12) |

with the inner maximisation replaced by a minimisation in the weak
version. The leader’s Stackelberg value is the value of
[Equation 12](#S14.E12 "In Definition 14.1 (Stackelberg equilibrium). ‣ 14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").

 

The reinforcement learning
reading. [Equation 12](#S14.E12 "In Definition 14.1 (Stackelberg equilibrium). ‣ 14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is a nested training loop, and the nesting is the whole of the concept.
The outer loop updates the leader’s parameters $`\psi`$; the inner loop
trains the follower to convergence against the current $`\psi`$; the
leader’s objective is evaluated at the converged follower. Every setup
with a slow outer optimiser and a fast inner one is an instance:
adversarial training with the adversary trained to convergence between
protagonist updates, curriculum generation with the student trained on
each proposed task, and reward-model tuning with the policy optimised
against each candidate reward. The contrast is with running both loops
at the same rate, which is the simultaneous game and has a different
solution. Timescale separation is therefore a modelling decision rather
than an implementation detail, and
[Equation 13](#S14.E13 "In 14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is what the leader’s gradient becomes once it is made. The strong
tie-break, which resolves the follower’s indifference in the leader’s
favour, is the convention that makes the maximum attained;
[Example 14.2](#S14.Thmtheorem2 "Example 14.2 (Commitment to a mixture, worth one and a half). ‣ 14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
shows that the optimum sits exactly on that tie.

The structure of
[Equation 12](#S14.E12 "In Definition 14.1 (Stackelberg equilibrium). ‣ 14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is bilevel optimisation, and this is the first correspondence: a
Stackelberg game is a bilevel program, and conversely. The second is the
value of commitment. In a two-player game in which the leader may commit
to a mixed strategy, the leader’s Stackelberg value is at least its
payoff in any Nash equilibrium of the simultaneous-move game ([von
Stengel and Zamir, 2010](#bib.bib185)), so commitment is weakly
beneficial, which is why so many learning setups are deliberately
arranged to give one party the leader role. In zero-sum games the
Stackelberg value coincides with the maximin value and the asymmetry is
immaterial, which is another instance of the general pattern that the
zero-sum case collapses distinctions that matter elsewhere.

###### Example 14.2 (Commitment to a mixture, worth one and a half).

Take the game in which the leader’s payoffs are
$`\big(\begin{smallmatrix}2&4\\
1&3\end{smallmatrix}\big)`$ and the follower’s are
$`\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)`$. The leader’s first action strictly
dominates its second, so in the simultaneous-move game it plays the
first, the follower best responds with its first action, and the leader
earns $`2`$. That is the unique Nash equilibrium.

Now let the leader commit to playing its first action with probability
$`p`$. The follower compares $`p`$, the payoff of its first action, with
$`1-p`$, the payoff of its second, and prefers the second whenever
$`p<1/2`$. The leader’s payoff when the follower takes its second action
is $`4p+3(1-p)=3+p`$, which the leader wants as large as possible, so it
sets $`p`$ as close to $`1/2`$ as it can. At $`p=1/2`$ exactly the
follower is indifferent and the strong Stackelberg convention resolves
the tie in the leader’s favour, giving $`3.5`$. Commitment is therefore
worth $`1.5`$, or seventy-five per cent of the equilibrium payoff, and
the committed strategy is a strictly mixed one even though the leader
has a dominant pure action.

Two reinforcement learning consequences follow. The first is that the
gain comes from committing to a randomisation, so a leader restricted to
deterministic policies captures none of it; this is why security-game
solvers and unsupervised environment designers randomise. The second is
that the entire construction relies on the follower observing the
commitment, so a leader whose policy the follower cannot observe is not
a leader, which is the credibility question that
[Section 24](#S24 "24 Open problems at the interface ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
raises for learned systems. $`\lozenge`$

The reinforcement learning counterpart is the total derivative. Suppose
the follower is itself a learner whose parameters $`\phi`$ converge to a
stationary point of its own objective given the leader’s parameters
$`\psi`$, so that $`\nabla_{\phi}u_{2}(\psi,\phi^{\star}(\psi))=0`$
defines $`\phi^{\star}`$ implicitly. The leader’s gradient is then

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |     |      |
| --- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | ---- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\psi}\,u_{1}\big(\psi,\phi^{\star}(\psi)\big)\;=\;\nabla_{\psi}u_{1}\;+\;\Big(\frac{d\phi^{\star}}{d\psi}\Big)^{\!\top}\nabla_{\phi}u_{1},\qquad\frac{d\phi^{\star}}{d\psi}=-\big(\nabla^{2}_{\phi\phi}u_{2}\big)^{-1}\nabla^{2}_{\phi\psi}u_{2},`$ |     | (13) |

the second expression following from implicit differentiation of the
follower’s stationarity condition, assuming
$`\nabla^{2}_{\phi\phi}u_{2}`$ invertible. The first term of
[Equation 13](#S14.E13 "In 14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is what naive simultaneous gradient ascent uses, and the second term is
what distinguishes a leader from a simultaneous mover. Learning with
opponent-learning awareness ([Foerster et al., 2018a](#bib.bib57))
replaces $`\phi^{\star}(\psi)`$ by one step of the follower’s gradient
ascent and differentiates through it, which is the first-order
truncation of
[Equation 13](#S14.E13 "In 14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning");
Stackelberg actor-critic methods ([Fiez et al., 2020](#bib.bib54))
compute the implicit term directly. The reason opponent shaping produces
cooperation in the iterated prisoner’s dilemma, where naive learners
converge to mutual defection, is visible in
[Equation 13](#S14.E13 "In 14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"):
the second term credits the leader for changing what the follower will
learn, which is precisely the mechanism that sustains reciprocity.

###### Example 14.3 (The shaping term, on the smallest saddle).

Take $`u_{1}(x,y)=xy`$ and $`u_{2}(x,y)=-xy`$ with scalar parameters,
start at $`x=y=0.5`$ and use a step size of $`0.1`$. Naive simultaneous
ascent applies $`x\leftarrow x+\eta y`$ and $`y\leftarrow y-\eta x`$,
which is a rotation scaled by $`\sqrt{1+\eta^{2}}`$, so the iterate
spirals outward: the distance from the origin grows from $`0.707`$ to
$`0.907`$ in fifty steps, and the spectral radius of the update matrix
is $`\sqrt{1+\eta^{2}}=1.005`$.

A leader that anticipates one gradient step of the follower optimises
$`x\,(y-\eta x)`$ instead, whose derivative is $`y-2\eta x`$. The extra
term $`-2\eta x`$ is the shaping correction, and with it the distance
from the origin falls from $`0.707`$ to $`0.502`$ over the same fifty
steps. The update matrix is $`\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1-2\eta^{2}&\eta\\
-\eta&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)`$, whose determinant is $`1-\eta^{2}`$ and
whose eigenvalues are complex, so their modulus is
$`\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}}=0.99499`$. Two scalars, fifty steps and one extra
term turn divergence into convergence.

The example is the smallest possible instance of what
[Section 19](#S19 "19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
treats in general, and it makes the mechanism legible: the naive game
dynamics has a purely rotational Jacobian, the shaping term adds a
negative-definite component, and the sum contracts. Opponent shaping,
extragradient and optimistic updates are three ways of adding that
component, and the first is distinguished from the other two by being
asymmetric, since only the leader applies it. $`\lozenge`$

Three applications deserve naming because in each case the Stackelberg
reading explains a design choice that otherwise looks arbitrary.
Adversarial training of a protagonist against a disturbance agent is a
Stackelberg game whenever the inner adversary is optimised to
convergence before each outer update, and a simultaneous-move zero-sum
game whenever the two are updated at the same rate; the two have
different solutions, and the difference between
$`\max_{\psi}\min_{\phi}`$ and the simultaneous saddle point is the
reason that timescale separation is not an implementation detail.
Environment and curriculum design, in which a teacher selects tasks and
a student learns to solve them, is a Stackelberg game with the teacher
as leader, and the unsupervised environment design framework of [Dennis
et al. (2020)](#bib.bib43) makes the regret-based objective of
[Equation 12](#S14.E12 "In Definition 14.1 (Stackelberg equilibrium). ‣ 14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
explicit. Reinforcement learning from human feedback is a Stackelberg
game in which the reward model designer is the leader and the policy
optimiser the follower, and the known pathologies of reward hacking are
the known pathologies of a follower exploiting a misspecified leader
commitment, which is the subject matter of mechanism design.

###### Example 14.4 (Learning from human feedback as a two-level game).

Label the stages. A set of annotators supplies comparisons; a reward
model $`r_{\psi}`$ is fitted to them; a policy $`\pi_{\phi}`$ is
optimised against $`r_{\psi}`$ with a penalty for departing from a
reference policy ([Christiano et al., 2017](#bib.bib32); [Ouyang et al.,
2022](#bib.bib134)). In the vocabulary of this section the designer who
selects the reward model and the penalty coefficient is the leader, the
policy optimiser is the follower, and the follower’s best response is
$`\argmax_{\phi}\mathbb{E}[r_{\psi}]-\beta\,\KL(\pi_{\phi}\|\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})`$.
Reward hacking is then not an anomaly but the follower doing its job on
a misspecified commitment, exactly as a contractor maximising a badly
written contract. The penalty $`\beta`$ is a commitment device that
limits how far the follower may travel from the reference, which is a
trust region in optimisation language and a limited-liability clause in
contracting language. The aggregation of several annotators into one
reward model is a social choice problem, which
[Section 15](#S15 "15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
shows cannot be solved without giving something up, and the question of
which equilibrium of the induced game is reached is the
equilibrium-selection question of
[Section 8](#S8 "8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
Nothing here requires a new theory; it requires recognising which of the
existing ones applies to which stage. $`\lozenge`$

Mechanism design itself is the limiting case in which the leader chooses
not a strategy but the game. Given a desired social choice function, the
designer selects payoff rules so that the agents’ equilibrium behaviour
implements it, and the revelation principle states that attention may be
restricted without loss to direct mechanisms in which agents report
their types truthfully. The reinforcement learning counterpart is reward
design, and the correspondence is exact in the following sense: a reward
function assigned to a learning agent is a commitment by the designer,
the agent’s optimal policy is the follower’s best response, and the
designer’s problem of choosing rewards so that the induced optimal
policy is the desired one is the mechanism design problem with a single
agent. Potential-based reward shaping of [Ng et al. (1999)](#bib.bib129)
is then the characterisation of the reward modifications that leave the
follower’s best response unchanged, which is the invariance condition
for this mechanism, and
[Section 15](#S15 "15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
develops the multi-agent version in full while
[Section 16](#S16 "16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
develops the shaping side.

## 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy

The sections so far have taken the game as given and asked what the
agents will do. Mechanism design inverts the question: the game is the
unknown and the behaviour is the specification. A designer who controls
the payoff rules chooses them so that the agents, each pursuing its own
objective, produce the outcome the designer wants. This is reward design
seen from the other side, and the inversion is exact: in reinforcement
learning the designer writes a reward function and the agent’s optimal
policy is the induced behaviour, which is a one-agent mechanism design
problem; with several agents the induced behaviour is an equilibrium
rather than an optimum, and the extra difficulty is precisely the
subject of this section.

The two readings differ in what is held fixed. A mechanism designer
holds the desired outcome fixed and solves for the rules, taking
equilibrium behaviour for granted. A reward engineer holds the rules
fixed and solves for the behaviour, taking the reward for granted. The
dictionary between them identifies the reward function with the payment
rule, the induced optimal policy with the equilibrium, the specification
with the social choice function, and the failure mode known as reward
hacking with the failure of incentive compatibility. Everything the
mechanism design literature knows about the limits of what rules can
achieve is therefore a statement about the limits of reward engineering,
and two of those limits are impossibility theorems.

| Game theory                      | Reinforcement learning                   | How to read the pair                       |
| -------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------ |
| Mechanism $`(f,t)`$              | Reward function plus environment rules   | The game the designer writes               |
| Social choice function           | Specification; desired behaviour         | What the designer wants to happen          |
| Type report $`\hat{\theta}_{i}`$ | Self-reported capability or preference   | What an agent tells the designer           |
| Incentive compatibility          | Reward hacking does not pay              | Honest behaviour is optimal                |
| Dominant-strategy IC             | Optimal regardless of the others         | Robust to any opponent behaviour           |
| Individual rationality           | Participation is better than opting out  | Nobody is worse off for taking part        |
| Transfer $`t_{i}`$               | Shaped reward term; payment              | A scalar the designer may add              |
| Externality                      | Effect of one agent on others’ return    | What VCG charges you for                   |
| Revelation principle             | Restrict to direct truthful protocols    | Without loss of generality                 |
| Price of anarchy                 | Equilibrium welfare over planner welfare | What selfish learning costs                |
| Price of stability               | Best equilibrium over planner welfare    | What the best convention costs             |
| Smooth game                      | Robust welfare bound                     | PoA bounds that extend to $`\mathrm{CCE}`$ |
| Toll, Pigouvian tax              | Reward shaping that changes equilibria   | A transfer that moves the fixed point      |

Table 26: Mechanism-design vocabulary read from both sides.

###### Definition 15.1 (Mechanism, incentive compatibility, individual rationality).

A direct mechanism for a Bayesian game with type profiles
$`\theta\in\Theta`$ consists of an allocation rule
$`f:\Theta\to\mathcal{O}`$ mapping reported types to outcomes and a
transfer rule $`t:\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{n}`$. Agent $`i`$ with type
$`\theta_{i}`$ reporting $`\hat{\theta}_{i}`$ obtains
$`u_{i}(f(\hat{\theta}_{i},\hat{\theta}_{-i}),\theta_{i})-t_{i}(\hat{\theta})`$.
The mechanism is dominant-strategy incentive compatible when truthful
reporting is optimal for every agent, every type and every profile of
other reports, and individually rational when truthful participation
yields at least the payoff of abstaining.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. Read $`f`$ as the environment’s
outcome logic and $`t`$ as the reward function, and
