[Definition 15.1](#S15.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 15.1 (Mechanism, incentive compatibility, individual rationality). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is a specification of an environment written backwards from the
behaviour it is meant to induce. The report $`\hat{\theta}_{i}`$ is
whatever an agent communicates about itself: a declared capability, a
bid, a self-assessed confidence, a claimed preference. Incentive
compatibility is the property that the agent gains nothing by
misreporting, which is reward hacking failing to pay. Individual
rationality is the property that participating beats opting out, which
in a deployed system is the condition under which an agent with an
outside option stays in the pool. A reward engineer who writes a reward
and then discovers that agents game it has written a mechanism that is
not incentive compatible, and the rest of this section is the catalogue
of rules for which that cannot happen.

The revelation principle states that any outcome implementable by any
mechanism in which agents play an equilibrium is also implementable by a
direct mechanism in which they report truthfully, obtained by folding
the equilibrium strategies into the mechanism itself. The reinforcement
learning reading is that there is no loss of generality in assuming the
agents behave as specified and putting the whole burden on the rules,
which is what a reward engineer does implicitly.

###### Example 15.2 (Why the second-price auction is honest, by cases).

A single item is sold to the highest bidder, who pays the second-highest
bid. Suppose an agent values the item at $`10`$ and the highest
competing bid is $`7`$. Bidding truthfully wins the item and pays $`7`$,
for a surplus of $`3`$. Bidding $`12`$ also wins and also pays $`7`$,
for the same surplus of $`3`$, so overbidding never helps. Bidding $`5`$
loses the item, for a surplus of $`0`$, so underbidding can only hurt.
Now suppose the agent values the item at $`5`$ against the same
competing bid of $`7`$. Bidding truthfully loses, for a surplus of
$`0`$. Bidding $`8`$ wins and pays $`7`$, for a surplus of $`-2`$, so
overbidding can hurt. The four cases exhaust the possibilities and
establish dominant-strategy incentive compatibility without any
assumption about the other bidders’ behaviour or beliefs.

The structure of the argument is the one worth carrying away. The
agent’s bid determines only whether it wins, never what it pays, so the
bid can be set to make the win-or-lose decision correct, and the correct
decision is to win exactly when the price is below the value, which is
what a truthful bid achieves. This decoupling of what you influence from
what you pay is the mechanism designer’s central device, and its
reinforcement learning counterpart is a reward term that an agent cannot
game because the agent’s action does not enter it, which is also the
defining property of the baselines of
[Section 16](#S16 "16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
$`\lozenge`$

###### Definition 15.3 (Vickrey-Clarke-Groves mechanism; [Vickrey, 1961](#bib.bib182); [Clarke, 1971](#bib.bib33); [Groves, 1973](#bib.bib70)).

Let the allocation rule maximise reported welfare,
$`f(\hat{\theta})\in\argmax_{o}\sum_{i}u_{i}(o,\hat{\theta}_{i})`$, and
let the transfer charge each agent the externality it imposes,

|     |                                                                                                                                                              |     |      |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --- | ---- |
|     | $`\displaystyle t_{i}(\hat{\theta})\;=\;\max_{o}\sum_{j\neq i}u_{j}(o,\hat{\theta}_{j})\;-\;\sum_{j\neq i}u_{j}\big(f(\hat{\theta}),\hat{\theta}_{j}\big).`$ |     | (14) |

Then truthful reporting is a dominant strategy and the allocation is
welfare maximising.

 

The reinforcement learning
reading. [Equation 14](#S15.E14 "In Definition 15.3 (Vickrey-Clarke-Groves mechanism; , ). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is a counterfactual baseline. The first term is the team’s value with
agent $`i`$ removed and the rest free to reorganise; the second is the
team’s value with agent $`i`$ present but its own contribution excluded.
The difference is the harm agent $`i`$ does to everybody else, and
charging it makes the agent’s own objective coincide with the team’s.
That is precisely the construction of
[Proposition 16.3](#S16.Thmtheorem3 "Proposition 16.3 (Difference rewards preserve the potential structure). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
with absence as the default action, so the VCG transfer and the
difference reward of cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning are
the same formula serving two purposes: here to make truth-telling
optimal, there to reduce the variance of a policy gradient. The property
that delivers both is that the subtracted term does not depend on what
agent $`i`$ does.
[Example 15.4](#S15.Thmtheorem4 "Example 15.4 (VCG on two items and three bidders). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
computes it on two items and three bidders.

###### Example 15.4 (VCG on two items and three bidders).

Two items, $`A`$ and $`B`$, and three bidders. The first values $`A`$ at
$`6`$ and has no use for $`B`$; the second values $`B`$ at $`5`$ and has
no use for $`A`$; the third values the pair at $`9`$ and either item
alone at nothing. The welfare-maximising allocation gives $`A`$ to the
first and $`B`$ to the second, for a welfare of $`11`$, beating the
$`9`$ of giving the pair to the third.

Apply
[Equation 14](#S15.E14 "In Definition 15.3 (Vickrey-Clarke-Groves mechanism; , ). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
Removing the first bidder, the best the others could do is $`9`$, by
giving the pair to the third; in the chosen allocation the others obtain
$`5`$. The first bidder therefore pays $`9-5=4`$ and keeps a surplus of
$`6-4=2`$. Removing the second bidder, the others could obtain $`9`$ and
in the chosen allocation obtain $`6`$, so the second pays $`3`$ and
keeps $`2`$. Removing the third bidder changes nothing, since the chosen
allocation does not involve it and the others still obtain $`11`$, so
the third pays $`0`$. Total revenue is $`7`$.

The first bidder pays $`4`$ for an item it values at $`6`$, and the
$`4`$ is not a price in any market sense: it is the harm the first
bidder’s presence does to the others, namely that the third bidder loses
the chance to take both items. Read as reward design, the VCG transfer
is the difference reward of
[Proposition 16.3](#S16.Thmtheorem3 "Proposition 16.3 (Difference rewards preserve the potential structure). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
with the default action being absence, and the agent’s shaped reward is
its marginal contribution to the team. The property that makes VCG
truthful is the same property that makes the difference reward an
unbiased baseline: the subtracted term does not depend on the agent’s
own report. The connection runs both ways, and
[Section 17](#S17 "17 Coalitions, stability of credit, and bargaining ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
completes it by showing that averaging the same marginal contribution
over orders rather than evaluating it once gives the Shapley value.
$`\lozenge`$

###### Example 15.5 (Two auction formats, one revenue).

Two bidders have values drawn independently and uniformly on $`[0,1]`$.
In the second-price auction each bids its value, by
[Example 15.2](#S15.Thmtheorem2 "Example 15.2 (Why the second-price auction is honest, by cases). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
and the seller receives the second-highest value, whose expectation for
two uniform draws is $`1/3`$. In the first-price auction bidding one’s
value yields zero surplus, so bidders shade; the symmetric Bayes-Nash
equilibrium bid is $`b(v)=\tfrac{n-1}{n}v`$, which for $`n=2`$ is
$`v/2`$. The seller then receives half the highest value, whose
expectation is $`\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\tfrac{2}{3}=1/3`$. The two formats
raise the same revenue. A Monte Carlo check over five hundred thousand
draws returns $`0.3338`$ and $`0.3335`$ respectively, confirming the
closed forms.

Revenue equivalence is the general statement behind this coincidence,
and the revenue-maximising mechanism is not either of these two but one
that sometimes refuses to sell: [Myerson (1981)](#bib.bib125) shows that
for uniform values the optimal reserve price is $`1/2`$ regardless of
the number of bidders, so the seller should walk away from a sale whose
highest bid lies below half the support, trading efficiency for revenue.
Read as reward design, the reserve is a threshold below which the
designer prefers no activity to activity, which is the same device as an
action mask or a minimum-quality gate in a deployed system, and the
lesson is that revenue maximisation and welfare maximisation select
different rules. $`\lozenge`$

Two impossibility results bound what the designer can do, and both have
direct consequences for systems that aggregate preferences.

###### Theorem 15.6 (Impossibility in social choice; [Arrow, 1950](#bib.bib8); [Gibbard, 1973](#bib.bib66); [Satterthwaite, 1975](#bib.bib153)).

With at least three alternatives and at least two agents, no rule
aggregating individual rankings into a social ranking satisfies
unrestricted domain, positive association of social and individual
values, independence of irrelevant alternatives, citizens’ sovereignty
and non-dictatorship simultaneously; these are the conditions of [Arrow
(1950)](#bib.bib8), and the familiar restatement in which the second and
fourth are replaced by unanimity belongs to the second edition of the
accompanying monograph rather than to the paper cited here. With at
least three alternatives in the range, no deterministic voting rule
without transfers is simultaneously onto, non-dictatorial and
strategy-proof.

###### Example 15.7 (The aggregation step of preference-based training).

A reward model fitted to comparisons from several annotators is a
preference aggregation rule.
[Theorem 15.6](#S15.Thmtheorem6 "Theorem 15.6 (Impossibility in social choice; , ). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
says that no such rule can satisfy the four classical desiderata at
once, so every choice of aggregation gives one of them up, and which one
is given up is a design decision that is usually left implicit. Fitting
a single scalar reward by maximum likelihood over a Bradley-Terry model,
which is the standard practice, abandons independence of irrelevant
alternatives, because the fitted ranking of two responses depends on the
other comparisons in the dataset. Taking a majority vote per comparison
abandons strategy-proofness, because an annotator who knows the
aggregation can misreport to shift the outcome. Neither failure is a bug
to be fixed; they are the two sides of an impossibility. The practical
consequence is that the right question about an aggregation scheme is
which axiom it drops, and the right report is to state it. $`\lozenge`$

The second body of results in this section quantifies what selfish
behaviour costs when the designer does not intervene, which is the
question a reinforcement learning practitioner faces whenever
independent agents are deployed without a shared objective.

###### Definition 15.8 (Price of anarchy, [Koutsoupias and Papadimitriou, 1999](#bib.bib96); price of stability, [Anshelevich et al., 2008](#bib.bib7)).

Let $`\mathrm{opt}`$ be the optimal social welfare and let
$`\mathcal{E}`$ be a set of equilibria. The price of anarchy is
$`\mathrm{opt}/\min_{\pi\in\mathcal{E}}\mathrm{welfare}(\pi)`$ for a
welfare to be maximised, or the reciprocal for a cost to be minimised,
and the price of stability replaces the minimum by the maximum.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. The price of anarchy is the number a
practitioner should compute before blaming an optimiser. It is the ratio
between what a centralised planner achieves on the model and what
independently trained agents achieve at equilibrium, so it measures the
cost of decentralisation itself rather than any shortfall in training. A
gap equal to the price of anarchy is the system working as specified; a
gap larger than it is a learning failure. The price of stability is the
same ratio at the best equilibrium rather than the worst, so the
difference between the two is exactly what an equilibrium selection
device could recover, and a designer who cannot change the rewards is
choosing among the equilibria in that interval.
[Example 10.6](#S10.Thmtheorem6 "Example 10.6 (Pigou’s network and a price of anarchy of four thirds). ‣ 10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
computes both for the smallest congestion instance, where the answer is
$`4/3`$, and
[Theorem 15.9](#S15.Thmtheorem9 "Theorem 15.9 (Smoothness and robust price of anarchy; , ). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
says when the bound survives the move from equilibria to whatever
no-regret learning actually reaches.

###### Theorem 15.9 (Smoothness and robust price of anarchy; [Roughgarden, 2015](#bib.bib149)).

A cost-minimisation game is $`(\lambda,\mu)`$-smooth when for every pair
of profiles $`a`$ and $`a^{\star}`$,
$`\sum_{i}\mathrm{cost}_{i}(a^{\star}_{i},a_{-i})\leq\lambda\,\mathrm{Cost}(a^{\star})+\mu\,\mathrm{Cost}(a)`$
with $`\lambda>0`$ and $`\mu<1`$. Any such bound implies a price of
anarchy of at most $`\lambda/(1-\mu)`$ not only for pure Nash equilibria
but, with no quantitative degradation, for mixed Nash equilibria, for
correlated equilibria, and for the average objective value of every
outcome sequence generated by no-regret learners, the last clause being
the coarse correlated case.

[Theorem 15.9](#S15.Thmtheorem9 "Theorem 15.9 (Smoothness and robust price of anarchy; , ). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the result in this monograph with the most immediate practical value,
and the reason is the last clause.
[Section 4](#S4 "4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
established that no-regret self-play converges to the coarse correlated
equilibria of the empirical game and not to anything stronger, which
looks like a weak guarantee. Smoothness converts it into a welfare
guarantee: whenever the underlying game is smooth, the welfare bound
that holds at pure equilibria holds automatically at the limit of
independent no-regret learning, with no assumption that the learners
coordinate or converge. For a practitioner the recipe is to check
smoothness of the deployment game once, offline, and then to deploy
independent learners with the welfare bound in hand.

###### Example 15.10 (The selfish-routing bound, and what a toll buys).

In Pigou’s network of
[Example 10.6](#S10.Thmtheorem6 "Example 10.6 (Pigou’s network and a price of anarchy of four thirds). ‣ 10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
the equilibrium cost is $`1`$ and the optimum is $`0.75`$, so the price
of anarchy is $`4/3`$, and the same bound holds for all of nonatomic
selfish routing with affine latencies ([Roughgarden and Tardos,
2002](#bib.bib150)). Because the bound is proved by a smoothness
argument,
[Theorem 15.9](#S15.Thmtheorem9 "Theorem 15.9 (Smoothness and robust price of anarchy; , ). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
extends it to the empirical distribution of play of independent
no-regret learners, so a fleet of selfish routing agents trained by any
no-regret method overloads the network by at most this factor.

Now add a toll. Charge the lower link a price equal to the marginal
congestion it causes, that is $`x`$, so that a unit using it perceives
$`x+x=2x`$. Selfish traffic now equalises $`2x`$ against $`1`$ and sends
$`x=1/2`$ along the lower link, which is the planner’s split, and the
resulting physical cost is $`0.75`$. The toll is a Pigouvian tax; in the
vocabulary of
[Section 16](#S16 "16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
it is a reward modification that changes the equilibrium rather than
preserving it, and in the vocabulary of
[Section 14](#S14 "14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
it is the leader’s commitment in a Stackelberg game whose followers are
the routing agents. The three vocabularies describe one intervention and
the arithmetic is the same in all three. $`\lozenge`$

The remainder of the correspondence is a list of problems that
practitioners meet under other names. Automated mechanism design
computes the rules by optimisation rather than by closed form: by search
and mixed-integer programming in [Conitzer and Sandholm
(2004)](#bib.bib36), and by supervised deep learning over a
parameterised class of mechanisms in [Dütting et al.
(2024)](#bib.bib49). Neither uses reinforcement learning, and in a
multi-agent system whose designer may set rewards this is nevertheless
the formal version of reward search. The principal-agent problem, in
which one party chooses an incentive scheme and another chooses an
effort level that the first observes only through a noisy outcome, is
the model of delegation to a learned policy, and the standard result
that the optimal contract trades off insurance against incentives is the
formal version of the trade-off between a dense shaped reward and a
sparse outcome reward. Two-sided matching ([Gale and Shapley,
1962](#bib.bib64)) is the model of task assignment in multi-robot
systems, and the deferred-acceptance algorithm produces a stable
assignment in which no pair prefers each other to their assigned
partners, a stability condition that is the matching analogue of the
core of
[Section 17](#S17 "17 Coalitions, stability of credit, and bargaining ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The economy-level version, in which a learned planner sets taxes and
learned agents respond, is a Stackelberg problem solved by nested
reinforcement learning ([Zheng et al., 2022](#bib.bib199)), and its
difficulties are the difficulties of
[Equation 13](#S14.E13 "In 14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
at scale.

| Mechanism                                                                                 | Guarantee                         | RL counterpart                             | Price paid                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Second price                                                                              | Dominant-strategy truthful        | Reward independent of own report           | Revenue below first price in some settings                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| VCG ([Vickrey, 1961](#bib.bib182))                                                        | Truthful and welfare maximising   | Difference reward; counterfactual baseline | Not budget balanced                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| Myerson optimal                                                                           | Revenue maximising in expectation | Reward shaped by a reserve threshold       | Efficiency is sacrificed                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| Majority voting                                                                           | Simple and anonymous              | Ensemble reward by vote                    | Not strategy-proof ([Theorem 15.6](#S15.Thmtheorem6 "Theorem 15.6 (Impossibility in social choice; , ). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")) |
| Scalarised aggregation                                                                    | Single scalar reward              | Bradley-Terry reward model                 | Independence of irrelevant alternatives                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| Pigouvian toll                                                                            | Shifts equilibrium to the optimum | Reward shaping that is not potential-based | Changes the equilibrium set                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| Deferred acceptance ([Gale and Shapley, 1962](#bib.bib64))                                | Stable matching                   | Task assignment                            | Not strategy-proof for both sides                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| Learned mechanism ([Dütting et al., 2024](#bib.bib49); [Zheng et al., 2022](#bib.bib199)) | Approximate, data-driven          | Bilevel RL                                 | No exact guarantee                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |

Table 27: Mechanism design read as reward design. The third column names
the reinforcement learning construction and the fourth records what the
mechanism guarantees and at what cost.

## 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit

Two distinct notions of potential appear in this field and they are
regularly confused, so we separate them before using either. The first
is the potential of a potential game, a function on joint actions whose
differences reproduce every agent’s unilateral payoff differences. The
second is the potential of potential-based reward shaping, a function on
states whose discounted differences are added to the reward without
changing the optimal policy. They are different objects serving
different purposes, and both have a role here.

The two readings of this section’s material are a structural condition
and an architectural constraint. A game theorist asks which games admit
a potential, because in those games unilateral improvement is a
hill-climb on a single scalar and therefore terminates, which is the
strongest convergence property available to uncoupled learners. A
reinforcement learning researcher asks which architectures force a joint
value function to have the property that decentralised greedy action
selection is jointly greedy, which is the Individual-Global-Max
condition. The two questions are the same question asked about the game
and about the hypothesis class respectively, and the correspondence is
that a value factorisation is a commitment to solving only games with a
particular structure.

| Game theory                    | Reinforcement learning                                 | How to read the pair                                   |
| ------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------ |
| Exact potential $`\Phi`$       | A single scalar all agents hill-climb                  | Unilateral payoff differences match $`\Phi`$           |
| Finite improvement property    | Better-reply dynamics terminate                        | No cycles; a pure equilibrium exists                   |
| Markov potential game          | Class where independent PG converges                   | The sequential version of the above                    |
| Supermodular game              | Monotone best responses                                | More of one agent’s action invites more of the other’s |
| Strategic complements          | Positive feedback between agents                       | Topkis’ lattice structure                              |
| Difference reward $`D_{i}`$    | Counterfactual baseline; COMA advantage                | Team return minus your absence                         |
| Shapley value $`\phi_{i}`$     | Order-averaged credit                                  | The same difference, over all orders                   |
| Efficiency axiom               | Credits sum to the realised return                     | A decomposition that decomposes                        |
| Individual-Global-Max          | Decentralised greedy is jointly greedy                 | Argmax factorises across agents                        |
| Monotone mixing                | QMIX-style architecture                                | Sufficient for IGM, not necessary                      |
| Potential-based shaping        | $`\gamma\Psi(s^{\prime})-\Psi(s)`$ added to the reward | Invisible to the solution concept                      |
| Equilibrium-preserving shaping | Per-agent potential terms                              | Preserves the set, not the selection                   |

Table 28: Potential, factorisation and credit vocabulary read from both
sides.

###### Definition 16.1 (Potential game; [Monderer and Shapley, 1996b](#bib.bib122)).

A normal-form game is an exact potential game when there exists
$`\Phi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}`$ such that for every agent $`i`$, every
$`a_{-i}\in\mathcal{A}_{-i}`$ and every
$`a_{i},a_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}`$,

|     |                                                                                                                        |     |      |
| --- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | ---- |
|     | $`\displaystyle u_{i}(a_{i},a_{-i})-u_{i}(a_{i}^{\prime},a_{-i})\;=\;\Phi(a_{i},a_{-i})-\Phi(a_{i}^{\prime},a_{-i}).`$ |     | (15) |

It is an ordinal potential game when the two sides of
[Equation 15](#S16.E15 "In Definition 16.1 (Potential game; , ). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
merely have the same sign, and a weighted potential game when they are
proportional with an agent-dependent positive weight.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. A potential is a single scalar that
every agent is implicitly hill-climbing even though no agent is
optimising it and no agent can see it. That is the property single-agent
reinforcement learning has by construction, since the return is one
number, and that multi-agent reinforcement learning loses. Recovering it
is worth a great deal: $`\Phi`$ is a Lyapunov function for better-reply
dynamics, so independent learners that only take improving steps cannot
cycle and must stop at a local maximum of $`\Phi`$, which is a pure Nash
equilibrium. This is the strongest convergence statement available in
this monograph to agents that do not coordinate, and it is why the
structural check in
[Section 22](#S22 "22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
asks for potential structure before it asks anything else. The
sequential version, the Markov potential game at the end of this
section, is the class in which independent policy gradient provably
converges.

The structural consequence just invoked deserves its name. Any sequence
of unilateral strictly improving deviations increases $`\Phi`$ and
therefore terminates, which is the finite improvement property, so every
finite ordinal potential game has a pure Nash equilibrium and
better-reply dynamics converge to one. Two special cases cover most of
what arises in practice. A common-payoff game is an exact potential game
with $`\Phi=u`$, so cooperative tasks are potential games by
construction. A game in which each agent’s payoff is its own
contribution to a shared objective minus a term independent of its own
action is also an exact potential game, which is the design principle
behind difference rewards.

###### Example 16.2 (A potential, exhibited and used).

Take the two-agent game with row payoffs $`\big(\begin{smallmatrix}2&4\\
5&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)`$ and column payoffs
$`\big(\begin{smallmatrix}2&4\\
4&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)`$, which are not equal, so the game is not
common-payoff. It is nevertheless an exact potential game with
$`\Phi=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&2\\
3&-1\end{smallmatrix}\big)`$, as one verifies in four lines: for the row
agent, $`2-5=-3=0-3`$ in the first column and $`4-1=3=2-(-1)`$ in the
second; for the column agent, $`2-4=-2=0-2`$ in the first row and
$`4-0=4=3-(-1)`$ in the second.

The potential does three things at once. It proves a pure equilibrium
exists, since a finite function has a maximum and the maximiser is
unlocked by no unilateral deviation. It identifies the equilibria as the
local maxima of $`\Phi`$: here $`(0,1)`$ with $`\Phi=2`$ and $`(1,0)`$
with $`\Phi=3`$ are both equilibria while $`(0,0)`$ and $`(1,1)`$ are
not. And it orders them, with the global maximiser $`(1,0)`$
distinguished, which is the natural selection rule and is the one that
stochastic stability picks out in
[Example 19.6](#S19.Thmtheorem6 "Example 19.6 (Which equilibrium of the stag hunt survives rare mistakes). ‣ 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
Two independent learners that each improve greedily will stop at one of
the two local maxima and which one depends on the initial condition,
which is the general shape of equilibrium selection in this class:
convergence is guaranteed, the destination is not. $`\lozenge`$

###### Proposition 16.3 (Difference rewards preserve the potential structure).

Let $`u_{i}(a)=u(a)`$ be a common payoff and define the difference
reward $`D_{i}(a)=u(a)-u(c_{i},a_{-i})`$ for an arbitrary fixed default
action $`c_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}`$. Then the game with payoffs
$`\{D_{i}\}`$ is an exact potential game with potential $`u`$, and it
has the same pure Nash equilibria and the same better-reply graph as the
original common-payoff game.

###### Proof.

For fixed $`a_{-i}`$ the subtracted term $`u(c_{i},a_{-i})`$ does not
depend on $`a_{i}`$, so
$`D_{i}(a_{i},a_{-i})-D_{i}(a_{i}^{\prime},a_{-i})=u(a_{i},a_{-i})-u(a_{i}^{\prime},a_{-i})`$,
which is
[Equation 15](#S16.E15 "In Definition 16.1 (Potential game; , ). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
with $`\Phi=u`$. Identity of the better-reply graphs is immediate from
the same display. ∎

[Proposition 16.3](#S16.Thmtheorem3 "Proposition 16.3 (Difference rewards preserve the potential structure). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the formal content of the difference-reward construction of [Wolpert
and Tumer (2001)](#bib.bib193): subtracting an agent-independent
baseline changes the variance of the learning signal and changes nothing
about the equilibria. The counterfactual baseline of COMA ([Foerster
et al., 2018b](#bib.bib58)) is the same operation applied to the
advantage inside a policy gradient, where it is additionally unbiased
because the baseline does not depend on the agent’s own action, so the
correspondence is that an exact-potential-preserving reward
transformation in game theory is an unbiased baseline in policy-gradient
estimation. The Shapley value supplies the principled version of the
same idea.

###### Definition 16.4 (Shapley value; [Shapley, 1953b](#bib.bib159)).

Let $`v:2^{\mathcal{N}}\to\mathbb{R}`$ be a characteristic function with
$`v(\emptyset)=0`$. The Shapley value of agent $`i`$ is

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                 |     |      |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | ---- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\phi_{i}(v)\;=\;\sum_{C\subseteq\mathcal{N}\setminus\{i\}}\frac{\left\lvert C\right\rvert!\,(n-\left\lvert C\right\rvert-1)!}{n!}\Big[v(C\cup\{i\})-v(C)\Big].`$ |     | (16) |

It is the unique map satisfying efficiency
$`\sum_{i}\phi_{i}(v)=v(\mathcal{N})`$, symmetry, the null-player axiom
and linearity.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. Read $`v(C)`$ as the return the team
obtains when the agents outside $`C`$ are replaced by a default policy,
which is a quantity a simulator can measure by ablation. Then
$`\phi_{i}`$ is a credit signal for agent $`i`$, and the four axioms are
four properties a practitioner would want from one. Efficiency says the
credits sum to the realised team return, so nothing is invented or lost.
The null-player axiom says an agent whose presence never changes the
return receives nothing. Symmetry says credit tracks contribution rather
than agent index. Linearity says credit for a sum of tasks is the sum of
the credits. The simplest instance fits in one line: with two agents,
$`v(\{1\})=v(\{2\})=0`$ and $`v(\{1,2\})=10`$,
[Equation 16](#S16.E16 "In Definition 16.4 (Shapley value; , ). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
gives $`\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}=5`$, since each agent adds $`0`$ when it
arrives first and $`10`$ when it arrives second.
[Example 16.6](#S16.Thmtheorem6 "Example 16.6 (Where the two credit schemes disagree, in numbers). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
works a three-agent case in which the cheap alternative over-attributes
by exactly the synergy.

###### Proposition 16.5 (Difference rewards are a single Shapley term).

Fix a joint action $`a`$ and define the characteristic function
$`v_{a}(C)=u(a_{C},c_{-C})-u(c)`$, the gain over the all-default profile
when the agents in $`C`$ play their components of $`a`$ and the rest
play their default actions, normalised so that $`v_{a}(\emptyset)=0`$ as
[Definition 16.4](#S16.Thmtheorem4 "Definition 16.4 (Shapley value; , ). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
requires. Then the difference reward $`D_{i}(a)`$ of
[Proposition 16.3](#S16.Thmtheorem3 "Proposition 16.3 (Difference rewards preserve the potential structure). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
equals the marginal contribution $`v_{a}(C\cup\{i\})-v_{a}(C)`$
evaluated at the single coalition $`C=\mathcal{N}\setminus\{i\}`$, which
is the term of weight $`1/n`$ in
[Equation 16](#S16.E16 "In Definition 16.4 (Shapley value; , ). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The Shapley value averages this marginal contribution over all
$`2^{n-1}`$ coalitions with the stated weights, and coincides with the
difference reward for every $`a`$ when $`u`$ is additively separable.

###### Example 16.6 (Where the two credit schemes disagree, in numbers).

Three agents share a task. Working alone the first two each produce
$`4`$ and the third produces $`1`$. Any pair produces a bonus of $`3`$
on top of their individual contributions and all three produce a further
$`2`$, so the characteristic function is $`v(\{1\})=v(\{2\})=4`$,
$`v(\{3\})=1`$, $`v(\{1,2\})=11`$, $`v(\{1,3\})=v(\{2,3\})=8`$ and
$`v(\{1,2,3\})=14`$.

The Shapley value of
[Equation 16](#S16.E16 "In Definition 16.4 (Shapley value; , ). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is $`(5.667,\,5.667,\,2.667)`$, which sums to $`14`$ exactly, as
efficiency demands. The difference reward with absence as the default is
$`D_{i}=v(\mathcal{N})-v(\mathcal{N}\setminus\{i\})`$, giving
$`(6,\,6,\,3)`$, which sums to $`15`$.

The shortfall of $`1`$ is the synergy that no single agent can claim
alone, and the fact that the difference rewards over-attribute by
exactly that amount is the general phenomenon: marginal contributions
evaluated at the full coalition double count complementarities. In a
cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning system this is the
difference between a credit signal that sums to the team return and one
that does not, and it matters whenever the credits are used as learning
targets rather than merely as baselines, because the surplus is
distributed arbitrarily across agents. The cost of the principled
version is $`2^{n-1}`$ evaluations instead of one, which is why
Shapley-based multi-agent methods ([Wang et al., 2020](#bib.bib187);
[Wang et al., 2022](#bib.bib188)) estimate
[Equation 16](#S16.E16 "In Definition 16.4 (Shapley value; , ). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
by sampling permutations. $`\lozenge`$

The reinforcement learning reading is that difference rewards and
Shapley-value credit assignment differ in how many counterfactuals they
average over, that the former is cheap and order-dependent while the
latter is expensive and order-free, and that the sampling-based
estimators used by Shapley-value multi-agent methods ([Wang et al.,
2020](#bib.bib187); [Wang et al., 2022](#bib.bib188)) are Monte Carlo
approximations of
[Equation 16](#S16.E16 "In Definition 16.4 (Shapley value; , ). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The axioms are what justify the extra cost: efficiency says the credits
sum to the realised team return, which is the property that makes a
decomposition a decomposition, and it is exactly the property that
value-factorisation architectures impose by construction.

###### Definition 16.7 (Individual-Global-Max).

A joint action-value $`Q_{\mathrm{tot}}(s,a)`$ and per-agent utilities
$`\{Q_{i}(s,a_{i})\}`$ satisfy the Individual-Global-Max condition at
$`s`$ when

|     |                                                                                                                                                  |     |      |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --- | ---- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\argmax_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Q_{\mathrm{tot}}(s,a)\;=\;\Big(\argmax_{a_{1}}Q_{1}(s,a_{1}),\dots,\argmax_{a_{n}}Q_{n}(s,a_{n})\Big).`$ |     | (17) |

 

The reinforcement learning
reading. [Equation 17](#S16.E17 "In Definition 16.7 (Individual-Global-Max). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the condition under which decentralised execution is free. The
left-hand side is what a centralised controller would do, namely
maximise the joint value over $`\prod_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}`$; the
right-hand side is what $`n`$ independent heads do, namely each maximise
its own small utility over its own actions. When the two agree, the
agents need no communication, no shared seed and no tie-breaking
convention at run time, because greedy local action selection lands on
the global optimum by construction, and the coordination problem of
[Remark 7.4](#S7.Thmtheorem4 "Remark 7.4 (The reduction preserves value but not decentralisability). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
does not arise. The cost is that not every joint value function admits
such a decomposition, so imposing
[Equation 17](#S16.E17 "In Definition 16.7 (Individual-Global-Max). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
through an architecture restricts the hypothesis class, and
[Example 16.9](#S16.Thmtheorem9 "Example 16.9 (A two-by-two matrix that no monotone mixing network represents). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
exhibits a two-by-two matrix outside it.

###### Proposition 16.8 (Monotone mixing implies IGM).

If $`Q_{\mathrm{tot}}(s,a)=f_{s}(Q_{1}(s,a_{1}),\dots,Q_{n}(s,a_{n}))`$
with $`\partial f_{s}/\partial Q_{i}\geq 0`$ for every $`i`$, then
[Equation 17](#S16.E17 "In Definition 16.7 (Individual-Global-Max). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
holds. In particular the additive factorisation
$`Q_{\mathrm{tot}}=\sum_{i}Q_{i}`$ of [Sunehag et al.
(2018)](#bib.bib173) and the monotone mixing network of [Rashid et al.
(2018)](#bib.bib145) satisfy IGM. The converse fails: there exist
common-payoff stage games whose action-value admits no monotone
factorisation, the canonical example being the two-agent three-action
matrix game of [Son et al. (2019)](#bib.bib167), so the representable
class is strictly smaller than the class satisfying IGM.

###### Example 16.9 (A two-by-two matrix that no monotone mixing network represents).

Let the common payoff at a state be
$`Q_{\mathrm{tot}}=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}8&-12\\
-12&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)`$. Suppose a monotone $`f`$ and per-agent
utilities reproduced it. Monotonicity in the second argument means that
the sign of $`Q_{\mathrm{tot}}(i,0)-Q_{\mathrm{tot}}(i,1)`$ equals the
sign of $`Q_{2}(0)-Q_{2}(1)`$ for every row $`i`$. The first row gives
$`8-(-12)=20>0`$ and the second gives $`-12-0=-12<0`$, so the required
sign is both positive and negative. No monotone $`f`$ exists.

The Individual-Global-Max condition nevertheless holds for this matrix,
since its argmax is the corner $`(0,0)`$, which is the product of the
per-agent argmaxes of the utilities $`Q_{1}=(1,0)`$ and $`Q_{2}=(1,0)`$.
So the matrix is in the IGM class and outside the monotone class, which
is
[Proposition 16.8](#S16.Thmtheorem8 "Proposition 16.8 (Monotone mixing implies IGM). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")’s
converse failing in the smallest possible instance. The best additive
surrogate $`Q_{1}(i)+Q_{2}(j)`$ misfits by $`8`$ in the supremum norm,
because additive functions satisfy $`M_{00}+M_{11}=M_{01}+M_{10}`$ while
this matrix has $`8+0=8`$ against $`-12-12=-24`$, a discrepancy of
$`32`$ that is best split four ways. A value-decomposition network
trained on this stage game converges to a function that is wrong by
$`8`$ at every cell and no quantity of data corrects it. Detecting the
failure requires comparing against a centralised planner on the same
model, which is the fifth row of
[Table 39](#S22.T39 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
$`\lozenge`$

The game-theoretic content of
[Equation 17](#S16.E17 "In Definition 16.7 (Individual-Global-Max). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is that the stage game induced at $`s`$ by $`Q_{\mathrm{tot}}`$ is one
in which greedy independent action selection lands on a global optimum,
so the coordination problem of
[Remark 7.4](#S7.Thmtheorem4 "Remark 7.4 (The reduction preserves value but not decentralisability). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
disappears by construction. Factorisation therefore operates as an
equilibrium-selection device: it restricts the hypothesis class to games
in which decentralised greedy play is optimal, and it pays for that
restriction with representational capacity, which is what the
counterexamples motivating QTRAN ([Son et al., 2019](#bib.bib167)) and
QPLEX ([Wang et al., 2021](#bib.bib186)) exhibit. Reading the
factorisation literature through
[Definition 16.7](#S16.Thmtheorem7 "Definition 16.7 (Individual-Global-Max). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
makes the trade-off legible: every proposal in that family is a choice
of how large a class of stage games to admit while retaining
[Equation 17](#S16.E17 "In Definition 16.7 (Individual-Global-Max). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").

A second structural class deserves a place beside potential games
because it yields convergence of best-response dynamics for an entirely
different reason.

###### Definition 16.10 (Supermodular game and strategic complements; [Topkis, 1979](#bib.bib178); [Milgrom and Roberts, 1990](#bib.bib120)).

Order each $`\mathcal{A}_{i}`$ as a lattice. A game is supermodular when
each $`u_{i}`$ is supermodular in $`a_{i}`$ and has increasing
differences in $`(a_{i},a_{-i})`$, meaning that the gain from raising
$`a_{i}`$ is non-decreasing in $`a_{-i}`$. In such a game the
best-response correspondence is non-decreasing and the set of Nash
equilibria has a least and a greatest element ([Topkis,
1979](#bib.bib178)); iterated elimination of dominated strategies
converges to the interval between them, so that the rationalisable set
and the equilibrium set have the same extreme points ([Milgrom and
Roberts, 1990](#bib.bib120)). That the equilibrium set is in addition a
complete lattice is a later result and is not what [Topkis
(1979)](#bib.bib178) proves.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. Supermodularity says that the more
one agent does of something, the more the others want to do of it:
effort is contagious. Coverage, investment, adoption and most tasks in
which agents reinforce each other’s progress have this shape. The payoff
for a practitioner is a convergence guarantee obtained without any
step-size condition. Start every head at its smallest action and iterate
best responses: the sequence is monotone increasing and converges to the
least equilibrium. Start every head at its largest and it converges to
the greatest. The two limits bracket every equilibrium of the game, so
two cheap runs return an interval that is guaranteed to contain the
answer, which is a diagnostic no other class in this monograph offers.

The reinforcement learning reading is practical. In a supermodular game
the best-response map is monotone, so starting all agents at the
smallest action profile and repeatedly best responding produces an
increasing sequence that converges to the least equilibrium, and
starting at the largest produces a decreasing sequence converging to the
greatest. Independent learners therefore converge from either extreme
without any step-size condition, and the two limits bracket every
equilibrium. Problems in which more effort by one agent raises the
return to effort by another, which covers most investment, adoption and
coverage tasks, are supermodular, and recognising this gives the same
kind of guarantee that potential structure gives, by a different route.

The second notion of potential closes the section. Potential-based
reward shaping adds $`F(s,a,s^{\prime})=\gamma\Psi(s^{\prime})-\Psi(s)`$
to the reward for an arbitrary $`\Psi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}`$, and
[Ng et al. (1999)](#bib.bib129) prove that this is the only form of
additive shaping that leaves the set of optimal policies unchanged for
all transition kernels and rewards. The multi-agent extension of [Devlin
and Kudenko (2011)](#bib.bib44) shows that adding an agent-specific
potential-based term $`F_{i}(s,s^{\prime})`$ to agent $`i`$’s reward in
a stochastic game leaves the set of Nash equilibria unchanged, which is
the right generalisation: in the single-agent case shaping preserves
optimality, in the multi-agent case it preserves the equilibrium set. It
does not preserve which equilibrium the learning dynamics selects, a
caveat [Devlin and Kudenko (2011)](#bib.bib44) state themselves, so
shaping remains an equilibrium-selection device even when it is
solution-preserving. The two results together say that potential-based
shaping is the class of reward modifications that are invisible to the
solution concept, which is precisely what a designer wants from a tool
meant to accelerate learning rather than to change the problem. The
connection to
[Definition 16.1](#S16.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 16.1 (Potential game; , ). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is that both results identify a potential function whose differences
cancel in the quantity the solution concept compares, over time in the
shaping case and across unilateral deviations in the potential-game
case.

###### Example 16.11 (Shaping that preserves and shaping that does not).

Add $`F(s,a,s^{\prime})=\gamma\Psi(s^{\prime})-\Psi(s)`$ with $`\Psi`$
equal to the negative distance to a goal. Along any trajectory the added
terms telescope to $`\gamma^{T}\Psi(s_{T})-\Psi(s_{0})`$, so the return
of every policy shifts by a constant depending only on the endpoints,
and the ranking of policies from a fixed start is unchanged. This is the
single-agent statement.

Now give two agents the shaped rewards
$`\mathcal{R}_{1}+\gamma\Psi_{1}(s^{\prime})-\Psi_{1}(s)`$ and
$`\mathcal{R}_{2}+\gamma\Psi_{2}(s^{\prime})-\Psi_{2}(s)`$ with each
$`\Psi_{i}`$ a function of the state alone. Each agent’s value shifts by
a constant for every joint policy, so no unilateral deviation changes
sign and the equilibrium set is unchanged. That is the multi-agent
statement, and the hypothesis that each $`\Psi_{i}`$ depends on the
state and not on the other agents’ actions is essential. Add instead a
term that rewards agent $`1`$ when agent $`2`$ does well, as
cooperation-inducing shaping schemes do, and the payoff tensor changes:
the prisoner’s dilemma with each agent’s reward replaced by the sum of
both rewards is a common-payoff game with $`(C,C)`$ as the unique
equilibrium, which is a different game with a different answer. That may
well be the right engineering decision, and the point is that it must be
reported as a change to the problem rather than as an acceleration of
its solution. $`\lozenge`$

Finally, the sequential generalisation of
[Definition 16.1](#S16.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 16.1 (Potential game; , ). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the Markov potential game, in which there exists $`\Phi_{\pi}`$ such
that
$`V_{i}^{(\pi_{i},\pi_{-i})}(s)-V_{i}^{(\pi_{i}^{\prime},\pi_{-i})}(s)=\Phi_{(\pi_{i},\pi_{-i})}(s)-\Phi_{(\pi_{i}^{\prime},\pi_{-i})}(s)`$
for every state, agent and unilateral deviation ([Valcarcel Macua
et al., 2018](#bib.bib181); [Leonardos et al., 2022](#bib.bib107)). In
this class independent policy gradient converges to a Nash equilibrium
at a rate polynomial in the relevant quantities, which is the largest
general-sum stochastic game class we are aware of for which a
convergence guarantee of that strength is available. Identifying whether
an application lies in it is therefore the single most valuable
structural check a practitioner can perform before choosing an
algorithm, and the two sufficient conditions that cover most cases are a
common reward and a reward of the form
$`\mathcal{R}_{i}(s,a)=\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{shared}}(s,a)+g_{i}(s,a_{i})`$
with the second term depending only on the agent’s own action.

## 17 Coalitions, stability of credit, and bargaining

Everything so far has been non-cooperative: agents choose actions and
the analysis predicts what they choose. Cooperative game theory
abstracts away from actions entirely and asks a different question.
Given that groups of agents can achieve certain values together, how
should the value of the whole group be divided, and which divisions are
stable against a subgroup walking away? The multi-agent reinforcement
learning counterpart is credit assignment, and the abstraction is
exactly right for it: a credit scheme is a division rule, the value of a
coalition is the return achievable when the rest are replaced by a
default, and stability is the property that no subset of agents would
rather train on its own.

The two readings differ in what the numbers mean. A cooperative game
theorist treats $`v(C)`$ as a binding agreement about what the coalition
$`C`$ can guarantee, and the solution concepts are normative: they say
how a surplus ought to be divided. A reinforcement learning researcher
treats $`v(C)`$ as a counterfactual return measured by freezing the
agents outside $`C`$, and the solution concepts are operational: they
say what number to put in the loss. The normative reading supplies
axioms that the operational one inherits, and the axioms are not
decorative, since efficiency is what makes a decomposition sum to the
team return and stability is what makes a decomposition robust to agents
leaving.

| Game theory                                           | Reinforcement learning                      | How to read the pair                      |
| ----------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------- |
| Characteristic function $`v(C)`$                      | Return with the agents outside $`C`$ frozen | What a subgroup achieves on its own       |
| Grand coalition $`v(\mathcal{N})`$                    | Team return of the full joint policy        | The number to be divided                  |
| Imputation $`x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}`$                     | Per-agent credit vector                     | What each agent is told it earned         |
| Efficiency                                            | Credits sum to the realised return          | No surplus invented or lost               |
| Null-player axiom                                     | Zero credit for a no-op agent               | Doing nothing earns nothing               |
| Symmetry                                              | Interchangeable agents get equal credit     | Credit depends on contribution, not label |
| Core                                                  | No subgroup would rather defect             | Stability of a credit scheme              |
| Nucleolus                                             | Lexicographic min-max of complaints         | The least objectionable division          |
| Banzhaf index ([Dubey and Shapley, 1979](#bib.bib47)) | Unweighted marginal contribution            | A cheaper, non-efficient alternative      |
| Superadditivity                                       | Merging coalitions never hurts              | Cooperation is weakly beneficial          |
| Convexity                                             | Increasing marginal contributions           | Late joiners add more; core is large      |
| Bargaining set, frontier                              | Pareto frontier of achievable returns       | What is jointly attainable                |
| Disagreement point $`d`$                              | Return if cooperation fails                 | Each agent’s fallback                     |
| Nash bargaining solution                              | Maximiser of $`\prod_{i}(u_{i}-d_{i})`$     | Product of advantages over fallback       |

Table 29: Cooperative-game vocabulary read from both sides.

###### Definition 17.1 (Transferable-utility game, core and nucleolus; [Shapley, 1971](#bib.bib160); [Schmeidler, 1969](#bib.bib154)).

A transferable-utility game is a pair $`(\mathcal{N},v)`$ with
$`v:2^{\mathcal{N}}\to\mathbb{R}`$ and $`v(\emptyset)=0`$. It is
superadditive when $`v(C\cup D)\geq v(C)+v(D)`$ for disjoint $`C`$ and
$`D`$, and convex when $`v(C\cup\{i\})-v(C)`$ is non-decreasing in
$`C`$. The core is

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                    |     |      |
| --- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | ---- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\mathrm{core}(v)\;=\;\Big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\;:\;\textstyle\sum_{i}x_{i}=v(\mathcal{N}),\;\;\sum_{i\in C}x_{i}\geq v(C)\ \ \forall\,C\subseteq\mathcal{N}\Big\},`$ |     | (18) |

the set of efficient divisions that no coalition can improve upon. The
excess of $`C`$ at $`x`$ is $`e(C,x)=v(C)-\sum_{i\in C}x_{i}`$, and the
nucleolus is the unique efficient $`x`$ lexicographically minimising the
vector of excesses sorted in decreasing order. In a convex game the core
is non-empty and contains the Shapley value; the nucleolus always lies
in the core when the core is non-empty.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. The core answers a question the
Shapley value does not: not what each agent contributed, but what each
agent could insist on. An allocation is in the core when no subset of
agents could do better by leaving and operating on its own, so
membership is the stability condition for a federation of independently
owned agents. The inequalities are one per subset, and checking them is
a linear program. The excess $`e(C,x)`$ is the complaint of coalition
$`C`$ at the allocation $`x`$, and the nucleolus is the allocation that
minimises the loudest complaint, then the second loudest, and so on; it
is the fallback when the core is empty, which
[Example 17.3](#S17.Thmtheorem3 "Example 17.3 (Majority voting: no stable division exists). ‣ 17 Coalitions, stability of credit, and bargaining ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
shows is a real possibility. For a system whose agents are owned by one
party the core is a diagnostic rather than a constraint, and the Shapley
value remains the better learning signal because it always exists and is
continuous in $`v`$.

The core is defined by $`2^{n}`$ linear inequalities, so membership is a
linear program, and emptiness is a real possibility. The two smallest
examples exhibit both outcomes and both are worth having by heart.

###### Example 17.2 (The glove market: scarcity takes everything).

Three agents. The first two each hold a left glove and the third holds a
right glove, and a matched pair is worth $`1`$ while unmatched gloves
are worth nothing, so
$`v(C)=\min(\left\lvert C\cap\{1,2\}\right\rvert,\,\left\lvert C\cap\{3\}\right\rvert)`$.
The grand coalition is worth $`1`$.

The core is the single point $`(0,0,1)`$. To see it, note that any core
allocation must satisfy $`x_{1}+x_{3}\geq v(\{1,3\})=1`$ and
$`x_{2}+x_{3}\geq 1`$, and efficiency gives $`x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}=1`$.
Adding the first two inequalities gives $`x_{1}+x_{2}+2x_{3}\geq 2`$,
and subtracting efficiency gives $`x_{3}\geq 1`$; with non-negativity
this forces $`x_{3}=1`$ and $`x_{1}=x_{2}=0`$. The nucleolus, being in
the core, is the same point.

The Shapley value is $`(\tfrac{1}{6},\tfrac{1}{6},\tfrac{2}{3})`$, which
is not in the core. It gives the left-glove holders something because in
two of the six orderings a left-glove holder arrives after the
right-glove holder and adds the whole unit of value.

Both answers are correct and they answer different questions. The
Shapley value asks what each agent contributes on average and the core
asks what each agent can insist on. For a multi-agent system the
practical consequence is sharp: a credit scheme based on average
marginal contribution will pay agents whose participation is not
actually necessary, and a credit scheme based on bargaining power will
pay nothing to agents who are substitutes for one another. Which is
wanted depends on whether the credits are being used as learning
signals, where the Shapley answer is better behaved because it is
continuous in $`v`$, or as a stability analysis of a federation of
independently owned agents, where the core answer is the relevant one.
$`\lozenge`$

###### Example 17.3 (Majority voting: no stable division exists).

Three agents, any two of whom can secure a unit of value and no single
one of whom can secure anything, so $`v(C)=1`$ when
$`\left\lvert C\right\rvert\geq 2`$ and $`0`$ otherwise. Efficiency
gives $`x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}=1`$, and the three pair constraints give
$`x_{1}+x_{2}\geq 1`$, $`x_{1}+x_{3}\geq 1`$ and $`x_{2}+x_{3}\geq 1`$.
Adding the three gives $`2(x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3})\geq 3`$, that is
$`1\geq 1.5`$, a contradiction. The core is empty.

The Shapley value is $`(\tfrac{1}{3},\tfrac{1}{3},\tfrac{1}{3})`$ by
symmetry and remains well defined, which is the sense in which it is
more robust than the core: it always exists, is unique and is
continuous. The emptiness of the core is not a pathology of the example
but the formal content of an unstable alliance, and it occurs in any
multi-agent system in which coalitions overlap and compete. A
practitioner whose agents are independently owned should expect no
division to be immune from renegotiation in such a system, and the
nucleolus, which minimises the largest complaint even when no complaint
can be eliminated, is the natural fallback. $`\lozenge`$

###### Example 17.4 (The warehouse team, with all three answers).

Return to the three agents of
[Example 4.9](#S4.Thmtheorem9 "Example 4.9 (Credit for a shared return, exactly). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
whose characteristic function was $`v(\{1\})=v(\{2\})=10`$,
$`v(\{3\})=4`$, $`v(\{1,2\})=26`$, $`v(\{1,3\})=v(\{2,3\})=16`$ and
$`v(\mathcal{N})=36`$. The Shapley value is $`(14.67,14.67,6.67)`$. The
core is non-empty, and the Shapley value lies in it, because every pair
constraint is satisfied: $`14.67+14.67=29.33\geq 26`$ and
$`14.67+6.67=21.33\geq 16`$. The nucleolus is $`(14.5,14.5,7)`$, which
differs from the Shapley value by a sixth of a unit for each of the
first two agents and by a third in the other direction for the third,
because it equalises the worst complaint rather than averaging
contributions.

Three schemes, three answers, all efficient and all defensible. The
difference between $`14.67`$ and $`14.5`$ is small here and is not
always small; what matters for a reinforcement learning system is that
the choice of scheme is a modelling decision with consequences for the
gradient each agent receives, and reporting which scheme was used is as
necessary as reporting the learning rate. $`\lozenge`$

The bargaining problem is the non-transferable counterpart, in which the
agents cannot simply move utility between themselves and must agree on a
point of a feasible set.

###### Definition 17.5 (Bargaining problem and its solutions; [Nash, 1950a](#bib.bib126); [Kalai and Smorodinsky, 1975](#bib.bib90); [Rubinstein, 1982](#bib.bib151)).

A bargaining problem is a pair $`(\mathcal{U},d)`$ with
$`\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}`$ compact and convex and
$`d\in\mathcal{U}`$ the disagreement point. The Nash bargaining solution
is the maximiser of $`\prod_{i}(u_{i}-d_{i})`$ over
$`\{u\in\mathcal{U}:u\geq d\}`$, and is the unique solution satisfying
Pareto efficiency, symmetry, invariance to affine rescaling of each
agent’s utility and independence of irrelevant alternatives. The
Kalai-Smorodinsky solution replaces the last axiom by monotonicity and
selects the point on the Pareto frontier along the ray from $`d`$ to the
ideal point $`b`$, where
$`b_{i}=\max\{u_{i}:u\in\mathcal{U},u\geq d\}`$. The egalitarian
solution maximises $`\min_{i}(u_{i}-d_{i})`$.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. A bargaining problem is a
multi-objective optimisation problem with a reference point, and the
three solutions are three scalarisations of it. The feasible set
$`\mathcal{U}`$ is the Pareto frontier of achievable return vectors,
which is the object a multi-objective reinforcement learning method
approximates, and the disagreement point $`d`$ is the return each agent
obtains if cooperation fails, which is the per-agent fallback a designer
must specify and usually leaves implicit. The Nash solution maximises
the product of advantages over the fallback, the Kalai-Smorodinsky
solution equalises advantages as fractions of the best each agent could
possibly obtain, and the egalitarian solution maximises the smallest
advantage. Choosing among them is choosing an axiom, not an optimiser,
and
[Example 17.6](#S17.Thmtheorem6 "Example 17.6 (Risk aversion is punished by one axiom and protected by another). ‣ 17 Coalitions, stability of credit, and bargaining ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
shows that the choice moves the answer by a visible margin as soon as
the agents differ in risk attitude.

###### Example 17.6 (Risk aversion is punished by one axiom and protected by another).

A unit pie is to be divided. The first agent is risk neutral, so its
utility is its share $`x`$. The second is risk averse, with utility
$`\sqrt{y}`$ where $`y=1-x`$. The disagreement point is $`(0,0)`$ and
the ideal point is $`(1,1)`$, since either agent could in principle
receive the whole pie.

The Nash solution maximises $`x\sqrt{1-x}`$. Differentiating,
$`\sqrt{1-x}-x/(2\sqrt{1-x})=0`$, that is $`2(1-x)=x`$, so $`x=2/3`$.
The risk-neutral agent receives two thirds of the pie, the utilities are
$`(0.667,\,0.577)`$ and the Nash product is $`0.385`$.

The Kalai-Smorodinsky solution picks the frontier point with equal
proportional gains, which here means $`x=\sqrt{1-x}`$, that is
$`x^{2}+x-1=0`$ and $`x=(\sqrt{5}-1)/2=0.618`$. Both agents obtain
utility $`0.618`$ and the risk-neutral agent receives a smaller share
than under Nash. The egalitarian solution coincides with
Kalai-Smorodinsky here because the ideal point is symmetric.

The phenomenon is general and is the reason the two solutions are both
in use. The Nash solution is invariant to each agent’s utility scale,
which means it cannot see how risk averse anyone is, and the consequence
is that risk aversion is rewarded with a smaller share because a
risk-averse agent gains more utility per unit of pie near zero.
Kalai-Smorodinsky gives up that invariance for monotonicity and
equalises relative gains instead. For multi-objective and multi-agent
reinforcement learning the choice between maximising a product of
advantages and equalising normalised advantages is exactly this choice,
and it is a choice of axiom rather than of optimiser. $`\lozenge`$

###### Example 17.7 (Alternating offers makes patience into bargaining power).

Two agents alternate proposing a split of a unit pie, and each rejection
costs both a factor $`\delta`$ of discounting. In the unique subgame
perfect equilibrium the proposer offers the responder exactly its
continuation value and the responder accepts, which yields the proposer
a share $`1/(1+\delta)`$. With $`\delta=0.5`$ the proposer takes
$`2/3`$; with $`\delta=0.9`$ it takes $`0.526`$; with $`\delta=0.99`$ it
takes $`0.5025`$. As the agents become patient the first-mover advantage
disappears and the split approaches the symmetric Nash bargaining
solution, which is the sense in which the non-cooperative model
implements the cooperative one.

The derivation is one line of self-reference. Let $`\sigma`$ be the
proposer’s equilibrium share. The responder, by rejecting, becomes the
proposer one period later and obtains $`\delta\sigma`$, so the proposer
must offer it exactly that, keeping $`\sigma=1-\delta\sigma`$, hence
$`\sigma=1/(1+\delta)`$. The reinforcement learning reading is that in a
negotiation environment the discount factor is not a tuning parameter of
the algorithm but a determinant of the equilibrium outcome, exactly as
in
[Example 6.4](#S6.Thmtheorem4 "Example 6.4 (The discount threshold for grim trigger, computed). ‣ 6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
and that the agent who moves first in the protocol has a structural
advantage that no amount of training removes. Reporting a negotiation
result without reporting the protocol and the discount factor reports
almost nothing. $`\lozenge`$

The correspondences this section buys are collected in
[Table 30](#S17.T30 "In 17 Coalitions, stability of credit, and bargaining ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
Two of them carry practical weight beyond credit assignment. The first
is ad hoc teamwork ([Stone et al., 2010](#bib.bib171)), in which an
agent must join an unfamiliar team and contribute without
pre-coordination; in the vocabulary used here the agent is computing its
marginal contribution to a coalition whose composition it does not
control, and the zero-shot coordination formulation of
[Section 12](#S12 "12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is its Bayesian version. The second is federation: when several
independently trained and independently owned agents must decide whether
to pool their efforts, the core is the exact criterion for whether a
sharing rule will hold, and
[Example 17.3](#S17.Thmtheorem3 "Example 17.3 (Majority voting: no stable division exists). ‣ 17 Coalitions, stability of credit, and bargaining ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
shows that in some structures no rule will.

| Concept             | Definition in one line               | RL use                            | Property for learning                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| ------------------- | ------------------------------------ | --------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Difference reward   | Return minus return without you      | COMA-style baseline               | Cheap; does not sum to the return                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| Shapley value       | Order-averaged marginal contribution | Per-agent credit                  | Efficient, unique, continuous; $`2^{n-1}`$ terms                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| Banzhaf index       | Unweighted marginal contribution     | Cheaper credit proxy              | Not efficient                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Core                | No coalition can improve             | Stability of a federation         | May be empty ([Example 17.3](#S17.Thmtheorem3 "Example 17.3 (Majority voting: no stable division exists). ‣ 17 Coalitions, stability of credit, and bargaining ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")) |
| Nucleolus           | Lexicographic min-max excess         | Least-objectionable division      | Always exists; LP sequence                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Nash bargaining     | Maximise $`\prod_{i}(u_{i}-d_{i})`$  | Welfare objective across agents   | Scale invariant; ignores risk attitude                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| Kalai-Smorodinsky   | Equal proportional gains             | Normalised multi-objective target | Monotone; not scale invariant                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Egalitarian         | Maximise $`\min_{i}(u_{i}-d_{i})`$   | Fairness objective                | Non-smooth                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Rubinstein protocol | Alternating offers with discounting  | Negotiation environment           | Outcome set by $`\delta`$ and move order                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |

Table 30: Cooperative solution concepts and their multi-agent
reinforcement learning uses. The last column records the property that
makes the concept suitable or unsuitable as a learning signal.

## 18 The population limit: mean-field games and mean-field control

Everything so far has been exponential in the number of agents: the
joint action space is $`\prod_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}`$, the occupancy state
of
[Definition 9.3](#S9.Thmtheorem3 "Definition 9.3 (Occupancy state). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
ranges over joint histories, and the complexity results of
[Section 21](#S21 "21 The complexity landscape ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
are stated for a fixed small $`n`$. The mean-field limit removes that
dependence by letting $`n\to\infty`$ under a symmetry assumption, and
the resulting models are the only ones in this monograph whose cost is
