independent of the population size.

The two readings are a limit theorem and a training loop. A game
theorist takes the limit to obtain a fixed-point condition between a
representative agent’s optimal policy and the distribution it generates,
and then asks when the fixed point exists, is unique and approximates
the finite game. A reinforcement learning researcher sees an outer loop
that alternates a single-agent solver with a population rollout, and
asks when the alternation converges. The correspondence is exact and the
two questions are the same question: the alternation converges precisely
when the composed map is a contraction or satisfies a monotonicity
condition, and the devices that rescue it are the same devices that
rescue fictitious play in
[Section 19](#S19 "19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").

| Game theory                         | Reinforcement learning                    | How to read the pair                       |
| ----------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------ |
| Representative agent                | The single policy being trained           | One agent stands for all of them           |
| Population distribution $`\mu_{t}`$ | Empirical state distribution of the fleet | What the others look like in aggregate     |
| Exchangeability                     | Permutation-invariant architecture        | Identities do not matter, counts do        |
| Mean-field game equilibrium         | Fixed point of solve-then-rollout         | Policy optimal against the flow it creates |
| Mean-field control                  | Markov decision process on distributions  | One planner for the whole population       |
| Monotonicity condition              | Congestion-like interaction               | Crowds repel, so the fixed point is unique |
| Damped population update            | Averaging the outer loop                  | Fictitious play on the distribution        |
| $`\varepsilon_{n}`$-Nash guarantee  | Finite-fleet approximation error          | How wrong the limit is at size $`n`$       |
| Price of anarchy in the limit       | Selfish fleet against a planner           | What the fixed point costs                 |
| Mean-action approximation           | Neighbour actions replaced by their mean  | A modelling choice, not a limit            |

Table 31: Mean-field vocabulary read from both sides.

The assumption is exchangeability: all agents share state and action
spaces, and each agent’s reward and transition depend on the others only
through the empirical distribution
$`\mu_{t}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j}\delta_{s_{t}^{j}}`$ of their states, so
that $`\mathcal{R}_{i}(s_{t}^{i},a_{t}^{i},\mu_{t})`$ and
$`\mathcal{T}(s_{t+1}^{i}\,|\,s_{t}^{i},a_{t}^{i},\mu_{t})`$. Under this
assumption a representative agent’s problem, given a fixed flow
$`(\mu_{t})_{t\geq 0}`$, is an ordinary Markov decision process with a
time-varying kernel, and the equilibrium condition is a fixed point.

###### Definition 18.1 (Mean-field game equilibrium).

A pair $`(\pi^{\star},\mu^{\star})`$ is a mean-field game equilibrium
when $`\pi^{\star}`$ is optimal for the Markov decision process induced
by the fixed flow $`\mu^{\star}`$, and $`\mu^{\star}`$ is the state
distribution flow generated by the whole population playing
$`\pi^{\star}`$ from the given initial distribution.

 

The reinforcement learning
reading. [Definition 18.1](#S18.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 18.1 (Mean-field game equilibrium). ‣ 18 The population limit: mean-field games and mean-field control ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is a training loop with two alternating steps. Fix a flow $`\mu`$, which
in an implementation is a histogram of where the fleet is at each time
step, and train one policy against an environment whose dynamics and
rewards depend on that histogram; this is ordinary single-agent
reinforcement learning with a time-varying but known perturbation. Then
roll the whole population out under the trained policy and recompute the
histogram; this is ordinary on-policy state-distribution estimation. A
mean-field equilibrium is a fixed point of the composition, so the
training loop is solve, roll out, repeat. The appeal is that neither
step costs anything in the number of agents: one policy is trained, not
$`n`$, and the histogram has the size of the state space rather than of
the joint state space. The catch is that the composition need not be a
contraction, so the alternation need not converge, which is why the
outer step is damped in practice for the same reason that fictitious
play averages.

Among the equilibrium concepts in this work, the mean-field equilibrium
is the one reached by an unmodified single-agent algorithm wrapped in an
outer loop, which is why it has become the standard route to very large
populations. The smallest instance is one in which all three readings of
the same arithmetic are available at once.

###### Example 18.2 (Pigou’s network as a mean-field game).

A unit mass of agents chooses between two roads once. Road $`0`$ costs
$`m`$, the fraction of the mass that uses it, and road $`1`$ costs $`1`$
regardless of load. Given a flow in which a fraction $`m`$ uses road
$`0`$, the representative agent’s best response is to take road $`0`$
when $`m<1`$, so the only fixed point is $`m^{\star}=1`$, every agent
pays $`1`$ and the social cost is $`1`$.

The mean-field control problem replaces the fixed point by an
optimisation: a planner chooses $`m`$ to minimise
$`m\cdot m+(1-m)\cdot 1`$, differentiates to get $`2m-1=0`$, and obtains
$`m=1/2`$ with social cost $`0.75`$. The ratio $`1/0.75=4/3`$ is the
price of anarchy, the same number as in
[Example 10.6](#S10.Thmtheorem6 "Example 10.6 (Pigou’s network and a price of anarchy of four thirds). ‣ 10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
which it must be, since this is that example with the finite population
replaced by a continuum.

Three readings are available for the same arithmetic and each is used in
practice. As a mean-field game it is a fixed point reached by
alternating a solver with a rollout. As mean-field control it is a
Markov decision process on the one-dimensional space of distributions.
As a finite congestion game it is
[Definition 10.5](#S10.Thmtheorem5 "Definition 10.5 (Congestion game; , ). ‣ 10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
with an affine cost. The gap between the first and the second is the gap
between what selfish learners reach and what a planner would choose, and
it exists at every population size. $`\lozenge`$

The outer loop requires care, because naive alternation, which is
fictitious play with best responses computed exactly, converges only
under a contraction or monotonicity condition on the composed map. The
monotonicity condition of [Lasry and Lions (2007)](#bib.bib101), which
holds when the interaction is congestion-like so that agents prefer to
avoid crowded states, guarantees uniqueness of the equilibrium and
convergence of suitably damped iterations. Without it the composition
can cycle, and the remedies are the ones familiar from
[Section 20](#S20 "20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"):
average the iterates, as in fictitious play, or regularise, as in online
mirror descent ([Perolat et al., 2022b](#bib.bib141); [Laurière et al.,
2022](#bib.bib103)). The parallel is exact: damping the population
update in a mean-field game plays the role that policy averaging plays
in fictitious play and that the entropy regulariser plays in mirror
descent, and all three are devices for turning a non-contractive fixed
point iteration into a convergent one.

A distinction that is frequently elided separates mean-field games from
mean-field control. In a mean-field game each agent maximises its own
objective and the solution concept is the Nash fixed point of
[Definition 18.1](#S18.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 18.1 (Mean-field game equilibrium). ‣ 18 The population limit: mean-field games and mean-field control ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
In mean-field control a single planner chooses one policy for the whole
population to maximise the population average, which is a Markov
decision process on the space of distributions, with state $`\mu_{t}`$
and action a policy, and whose solution is the social optimum. The two
coincide only in degenerate cases, and the gap between them is the price
of anarchy. In the vocabulary of this monograph, mean-field control is
the $`n\to\infty`$ limit of the multi-agent Markov decision process of
[Proposition 7.3](#S7.Thmtheorem3 "Proposition 7.3 (Degeneracies of the stochastic game). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and mean-field games the limit of the general-sum stochastic game, so
the distinction is the population-scale version of the common-payoff
versus general-sum distinction that has organised the whole of this
work.

The finite-population guarantee is what makes the limit useful rather
than merely elegant. Under Lipschitz continuity of the rewards and
transitions in $`\mu`$, a mean-field equilibrium policy played by each
of $`n`$ agents in the corresponding finite game is an
$`\varepsilon_{n}`$-Nash equilibrium with
$`\varepsilon_{n}=O(1/\sqrt{n})`$, the rate coming from the
concentration of the empirical measure; [Yardim et al.
(2023)](#bib.bib196) establish it for $`n`$ agents running independent
policy mirror ascent, with the Nash property holding for the regularised
game. The practical consequence is that mean-field methods are justified
for large populations and give no guarantee for the small $`n`$ of most
multi-agent reinforcement learning benchmarks, and the intermediate
regime is handled by the mean-field approximation of [Yang et al.
(2018)](#bib.bib194), which replaces the joint action of the neighbours
by their mean action in the action-value function. That approximation is
a modelling choice rather than a limit theorem, and we classify it as a
structural analogy.

###### Example 18.3 (What the rate means at benchmark scale).

The $`O(1/\sqrt{n})`$ bound evaluates to $`0.316`$ at ten agents,
$`0.100`$ at one hundred, $`0.032`$ at one thousand and $`0.010`$ at ten
thousand, before the problem-dependent constant is applied. Multi-agent
reinforcement learning benchmarks typically use between two and
twenty-five agents, where the bound delivers an $`\varepsilon`$ of the
same order as the payoff range, which is to say no guarantee at all.
Fleet-scale applications, traffic systems, electricity markets and large
recommendation populations, sit at $`10^{3}`$ or more, where the same
bound is informative. Reporting mean-field methods on small benchmarks
is therefore reporting a heuristic, and the correct statement is that
the method is justified asymptotically and used here outside its regime,
which is honest and is also how the field uses many of its tools.
$`\lozenge`$

## 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to

Game theory has studied learning rules since the 1950s, and the
classical results answer, for each rule and each class of games, the
question that a multi-agent reinforcement learning practitioner actually
has: if I run this update in self-play, what will happen. The answers
are sharper than the ones usually available in the deep learning
literature, because the rules are simpler and the games are smaller, and
they transfer, because the failure modes are properties of the game
rather than of the function approximator. This section collects them.

The two readings are a dynamical system and a training loop. A game
theorist writes a learning rule as a map on the product of the strategy
simplices and asks about its rest points, their stability and the
asymptotic behaviour of the trajectory. A reinforcement learning
researcher writes the same map as an optimiser applied to a loss that
depends on the other agents’ parameters, and asks whether the loss goes
down. The two differ in one decisive respect. A single-agent optimiser
has a Lyapunov function by construction, since the objective itself
decreases; a multi-agent update has none in general, because there is no
scalar that all agents are jointly optimising. Potential games are
exactly the class where there is one, which is why they are the class
with guarantees.

| Game theory               | Reinforcement learning                           | How to read the pair                            |
| ------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------- |
| Fictitious play           | Best response to the empirical opponent          | Average what you have seen, then be greedy      |
| Best-response dynamics    | Greedy policy iteration in self-play             | Jump straight to the argmax                     |
| Better-reply dynamics     | Any improving step                               | Only go uphill, not necessarily to the top      |
| Multiplicative weights    | Softmax over cumulative payoffs; MWU             | Exponentiate the running total                  |
| No-regret property        | Sublinear regret of the learner                  | Asymptotically as good as the best fixed action |
| Blackwell approachability | Vector-valued target set                         | Steer an average vector into a set              |
| Stochastic stability      | Small-noise limit of a learning chain            | Which equilibrium survives rare mistakes        |
| Log-linear learning       | Boltzmann revision with low temperature          | Selects the potential maximiser                 |
| Monotone game, DSC        | Convex-concave structure; variational inequality | The condition under which gradients behave      |
| Extragradient, optimism   | Look-ahead or past-gradient correction           | Cancel the rotational part of the field         |
| Last-iterate convergence  | The final checkpoint is the answer               | No averaging required                           |
| Time-average convergence  | The averaged policy is the answer                | The checkpoint is not the output                |

Table 32: Learning-dynamics vocabulary read from both sides.

Fictitious play is the oldest rule and the one whose convergence class
is best mapped. Each agent maintains the empirical frequency of each
opponent’s past actions and plays a best response to it.

###### Theorem 19.1 (Convergence classes of fictitious play; [Robinson, 1951](#bib.bib146); [Monderer and Shapley, 1996a](#bib.bib121)).

In a two-player zero-sum game the empirical frequencies of fictitious
play converge to the set of Nash equilibria. In a finite game with
identical interests, and more generally in a finite weighted potential
game, fictitious play has the fictitious play property, meaning that the
beliefs converge to the set of Nash equilibria. Neither statement
extends to general-sum games, in which fictitious play may cycle without
the empirical frequencies converging.

###### Example 19.2 (Fictitious play on matching pennies: the iterate never settles).

Start the two agents with the belief counts $`(1,0)`$ and $`(0,1)`$
respectively, so that the row agent believes the column agent plays
Tails and the column agent believes the row agent plays Heads. The row
agent best responds with Tails, the column agent with Tails; the counts
update; each agent then best responds to the new empirical frequency.
The pure action played at each step keeps switching, and it switches in
runs of increasing length, so the sequence of actions never settles.
After twenty thousand steps, however, the empirical frequency of Heads
is $`0.5025`$ for the first agent and $`0.5025`$ for the second, which
is within $`0.0025`$ of the equilibrium $`(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})`$
and converging.

Both halves matter. The iterate is a pure action at every step, so the
policy at any checkpoint has an exploitability of $`1`$, the maximum
possible; the average has exploitability going to zero. Reporting the
last checkpoint of a self-play run in a zero-sum game therefore reports
the worst object the run produced, and this is not a quirk of fictitious
play but the generic situation, as
[Section 23](#S23 "23 Correspondences that fail, and the theorems that forbid them ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
records. Neural fictitious self-play ([Heinrich and Silver,
2016](#bib.bib78)) is this algorithm with the empirical frequency
replaced by a supervised network that is trained to imitate the agent’s
own past play, and the averaging network is not an implementation
convenience but the carrier of the entire guarantee. $`\lozenge`$

###### Example 19.3 (The same rule on a coordination game converges outright).

Take the common-payoff game with payoff $`3`$ on the first diagonal
entry, $`1`$ on the second and $`0`$ off the diagonal, which is a
potential game with potential equal to the payoff. The row agent prefers
its first action whenever its belief puts weight above $`1/4`$ on the
opponent’s first action, since $`3q>1-q`$ exactly there, so the
threshold is low and the basin of the payoff-dominant equilibrium is
large. Starting from beliefs that place slightly more mass on the second
action, at $`q=0.49`$, both agents are already past the threshold and
play the first action from the first step onward; after twenty thousand
steps the empirical frequency of the first action is $`0.99997`$ for
both, the residual being the fractional prior counts. The contrast with
[Example 19.2](#S19.Thmtheorem2 "Example 19.2 (Fictitious play on matching pennies: the iterate never settles). ‣ 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the whole of
[Theorem 19.1](#S19.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 19.1 (Convergence classes of fictitious play; , ). ‣ 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"):
the same rule converges in one class and cycles in the other, and the
discriminating property is the existence of a potential. $`\lozenge`$

The second family is the no-regret family, whose guarantees are the ones
that transfer most directly to the general-sum case. Multiplicative
weights maintains cumulative payoffs and plays a softmax of them; its
regret after $`T`$ rounds is
$`O(\sqrt{T\log\left\lvert\mathcal{A}\right\rvert})`$ ([Freund and
Schapire, 1999](#bib.bib61)), so the time-averaged play converges to the
coarse correlated equilibria by
[Definition 4.5](#S4.Thmtheorem5 "Definition 4.5 (Correlated and coarse correlated equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
and in a two-player zero-sum game the time average converges to the
value. What does not happen is convergence of the iterate.

###### Example 19.4 (Multiplicative weights spirals away from the equilibrium).

Run multiplicative weights with step size $`0.1`$ in self-play on
rock-paper-scissors, starting from the perturbed strategies
$`(0.4,0.35,0.25)`$ and $`(0.35,0.4,0.25)`$. Measure the distance from
the equilibrium by the sum of the two Kullback-Leibler divergences to
the uniform distribution, which is $`0.0362`$ at the start. After two
thousand steps it is $`1.202`$, and the first agent’s probability of
Rock has drifted from $`0.4`$ to $`0.138`$. The iterate is moving away
from the equilibrium and towards the boundary of the simplex, which is
the discrete-time statement of the result that the continuous-time
dynamics conserves the divergence and cycles while the discrete-time one
increases it ([Mertikopoulos et al., 2018](#bib.bib118); [Bailey and
Piliouras, 2018](#bib.bib13)).

The time average still converges, and so does the exploitability of the
average. A practitioner who logs the current policy’s win rate against
the current opponent sees a flat curve, because the two policies are
rotating together; a practitioner who logs the exploitability of the
current policy sees it rise. The second measurement is the informative
one, and it costs a best-response computation, which is the price
[Table 39](#S22.T39 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
asks to be paid. $`\lozenge`$

Blackwell approachability is the result that organises this family and
deserves a statement because it explains where regret matching comes
from.

###### Theorem 19.5 (Approachability; [Blackwell, 1956](#bib.bib17)).

Consider a repeated game with vector payoffs
$`m(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ and a closed convex target set
$`S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}`$. Agent $`1`$ can force the average payoff
vector to converge to $`S`$ regardless of agent $`2`$’s play if and only
if for every halfspace $`H`$ containing $`S`$ there is a mixed strategy
of agent $`1`$ guaranteeing that the expected payoff vector lies in
$`H`$.

Taking $`d=\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{1}\right\rvert`$, the vector payoff
to be the vector of regrets for each alternative action and $`S`$ to be
the non-positive orthant, approachability of $`S`$ is exactly the
no-regret property, and the halfspace-response strategy that the theorem
supplies is regret matching. [Abernethy et al. (2011)](#bib.bib1) show
the converse reduction as well, so approachability and no-regret
learning are equivalent problems. For a reinforcement learning audience
the useful content is that the construction is vector-valued, so the
same machinery applies when the target is a set of constraints rather
than a single scalar, which is the structure of constrained and
multi-objective reinforcement learning. A constrained policy optimiser
that drives a vector of constraint violations into the negative orthant
is running an approachability algorithm whether or not it is described
as one.

The third family is explicitly stochastic and is the one that answers
the equilibrium-selection question left open throughout this monograph.
In log-linear learning ([Blume, 1993](#bib.bib20)) one agent at a time
revises, drawing its action from a Boltzmann distribution over its own
payoffs at the current profile; in adaptive play ([Young,
1993](#bib.bib197)) agents best respond to a random sample of recent
history with a small probability of error. Both define an irreducible
Markov chain on the profile space whose stationary distribution, as the
noise vanishes, concentrates on a subset of the equilibria called the
stochastically stable set.

###### Example 19.6 (Which equilibrium of the stag hunt survives rare mistakes).

Take the symmetric stag hunt of
[Example 3.4](#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Example 3.4 (The four games that this monograph keeps returning to). ‣ 3 Normal-form games as the stateless limit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
in which hunting the stag together pays $`4`$ each, hunting hare pays
$`3`$ each, and hunting the stag alone pays $`1`$ against the hare
hunter’s $`3`$. The stag equilibrium is payoff dominant and the hare
equilibrium is risk dominant: the Harsanyi-Selten comparison is
$`(4-3)^{2}=1`$ for stag against $`(3-1)^{2}=4`$ for hare, so hare wins.
The game is an exact potential game with
$`\Phi=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}-1&-2\\
-2&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)`$, whose maximiser is the hare profile.

Run log-linear learning and compute the stationary distribution of the
induced four-state chain exactly. At inverse temperature $`\beta=0.5`$
the chain spends $`0.259`$ of its time at the stag profile and $`0.427`$
at the hare profile; at $`\beta=2`$ the figures are $`0.115`$ and
$`0.853`$; at $`\beta=10`$ they are $`0.0000454`$ and $`0.99995`$. As
the noise vanishes the chain concentrates entirely on the hare profile,
which is simultaneously the potential maximiser and the risk-dominant
equilibrium.

The conclusion is the one a cooperative multi-agent practitioner needs
and rarely sees stated. Independent learners with persistent exploration
do not select the best equilibrium; they select the one that is most
robust to the partner’s mistakes, and in this game that is the one worth
$`3`$ rather than the one worth $`4`$. The gap is not an optimisation
failure and will not close with more samples, a larger network or a
better learning rate. It closes only with an equilibrium selection
device, which is what optimistic initialisation, leniency and hysteresis
are, and the device works by biasing the very comparison that this
stationary distribution computes. $`\lozenge`$

The fourth family is the modern one and is where the correspondence with
contemporary deep learning is tightest. Write the simultaneous gradient
of a smooth game as the vector field
$`F(\theta)=(\nabla_{\theta_{1}}u_{1},\dots,\nabla_{\theta_{n}}u_{n})`$
and note that a Nash equilibrium in the interior is a zero of $`F`$.
Unlike a gradient of a scalar objective, $`F`$ need not be a gradient
field, so its Jacobian need not be symmetric, and the asymmetric part is
a rotation. Everything that distinguishes multi-agent from single-agent
optimisation is in that rotation.

###### Definition 19.7 (Monotone games and diagonal strict concavity; [Rosen, 1965](#bib.bib147)).

A game with convex compact strategy sets and payoffs concave in the own
strategy satisfies diagonal strict concavity when
$`(\theta-\theta^{\prime})^{\top}(F(\theta^{\prime})-F(\theta))>0`$ for
all $`\theta\neq\theta^{\prime}`$, equivalently when $`-F`$ is strictly
monotone. Under this condition the Nash equilibrium is unique and
gradient play with a suitable step size converges to it.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. Diagonal strict concavity is the
multi-agent replacement for strong concavity of a single objective, and
it is the condition that decides whether the usual intuition about
gradient methods survives. In a single-agent problem the gradient is the
derivative of one scalar, so its Jacobian is a Hessian and is therefore
symmetric, and gradient ascent on a strongly concave objective
contracts. In a multi-agent problem $`F`$ stacks the gradients of $`n`$
different objectives, so nothing forces its Jacobian to be symmetric,
and the asymmetric part is a rotation that no step size removes.
[Definition 19.7](#S19.Thmtheorem7 "Definition 19.7 (Monotone games and diagonal strict concavity; , ). ‣ 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is exactly the condition that the rotation is dominated by the
contraction. When it holds, independent policy gradient converges to the
unique equilibrium and the usual tuning intuition applies; when it
fails, as in the bilinear case of
[Example 19.8](#S19.Thmtheorem8 "Example 19.8 (Why extragradient fixes what gradient ascent-descent breaks). ‣ 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
the iterate spirals outward however small the step size, and the remedy
is a look-ahead correction rather than a smaller learning rate.

Diagonal strict concavity is the multi-agent analogue of strong
concavity of a single objective, and when it fails the field can rotate
without contracting, which is exactly the bilinear case.

###### Example 19.8 (Why extragradient fixes what gradient ascent-descent breaks).

Take $`u_{1}=xy`$ and $`u_{2}=-xy`$ with scalars, which is matching
pennies in continuous form and is the bilinear core of a generative
adversarial objective ([Goodfellow et al., 2014](#bib.bib68)). The
simultaneous update with step $`\eta=0.1`$ is the linear map
$`\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\eta\\
-\eta&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)`$, whose eigenvalues have modulus
$`\sqrt{1+\eta^{2}}=1.00499`$, so every trajectory spirals out: starting
at $`(1,1)`$ the norm grows to $`3.825`$ after two hundred steps.

The extragradient update takes a trial step, evaluates the field there,
and applies that field at the original point. Its linear map is
$`\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1-\eta^{2}&\eta\\
-\eta&1-\eta^{2}\end{smallmatrix}\big)`$, whose eigenvalues have modulus
$`\sqrt{(1-\eta^{2})^{2}+\eta^{2}}=0.99504`$, so trajectories spiral in:
from the same start the norm falls to $`0.523`$. One look-ahead
evaluation converts divergence at rate $`1.005^{T}`$ into convergence at
rate $`0.995^{T}`$, and the optimistic variant, which reuses the
previous gradient instead of computing a new one, achieves the same
effect for the price of one memory slot ([Rakhlin and Sridharan,
2013](#bib.bib144); [Daskalakis et al., 2017](#bib.bib40)).

Comparison with
[Example 14.3](#S14.Thmtheorem3 "Example 14.3 (The shaping term, on the smallest saddle). ‣ 14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is instructive. Opponent shaping subtracts $`2\eta^{2}`$ from one
diagonal entry of the Jacobian and obtains a modulus of
$`\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}}=0.99499`$; extragradient subtracts $`\eta^{2}`$ from
both and obtains $`\sqrt{(1-\eta^{2})^{2}+\eta^{2}}=0.99504`$. The two
numbers are close without being equal, and the near-coincidence is the
point: both methods add a negative-definite component to a rotational
field, one asymmetrically and one symmetrically, and either suffices to
turn divergence into convergence. The practical difference is that
extragradient is symmetric and therefore appropriate when neither agent
is a leader, while opponent shaping is asymmetric and encodes a
commitment. $`\lozenge`$

The upshot of this section is a decision table, and we state it as such.
If the game is zero-sum, no-regret dynamics with averaging converges,
the iterate does not, and extragradient or regularisation buys
last-iterate convergence. If the game is a potential or supermodular
game, better-reply and fictitious play converge and the limit is the
potential maximiser under vanishing noise. If the game is monotone in
the sense of
[Definition 19.7](#S19.Thmtheorem7 "Definition 19.7 (Monotone games and diagonal strict concavity; , ). ‣ 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
gradient play converges to the unique equilibrium. Otherwise nothing
converges in general, by
[Theorem 23.1](#S23.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 23.1 (Impossibility of uncoupled convergence; , ). ‣ 23 Correspondences that fail, and the theorems that forbid them ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
the attainable target is a coarse correlated equilibrium of the
empirical game, and the question to ask is not how to make the dynamics
converge but which equilibrium one wants and what device will select it.

| Dynamics                                                  | Converges in                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | Limit                                        | RL counterpart                       |
| --------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------ |
| Best-reply dynamics                                       | Potential, supermodular games                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | Pure equilibrium, iterate                    | Greedy policy iteration in self-play |
| Fictitious play                                           | Zero-sum; potential ([Robinson, 1951](#bib.bib146); [Monderer and Shapley, 1996a](#bib.bib121))                                                                                                                                                                                                        | Nash, average                                | Neural fictitious self-play          |
| Multiplicative weights                                    | All games, in the sense of regret                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | $`\mathrm{CCE}`$, average; value in zero-sum | Softmax policy gradient              |
| Regret matching ([Hart and Mas-Colell, 2000](#bib.bib75)) | All games                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | $`\mathrm{CE}`$, average                     | CFR at an information set            |
| Approachability ([Blackwell, 1956](#bib.bib17))           | All games, vector targets                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | Average vector in the target set             | Constrained policy optimisation      |
| Log-linear learning ([Blume, 1993](#bib.bib20))           | Potential games                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | Potential maximiser; risk dominant           | Boltzmann exploration with annealing |
| Adaptive play ([Young, 1993](#bib.bib197))                | Weakly acyclic games                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Stochastically stable set                    | Sampled-history opponent models      |
| Gradient play                                             | Monotone games ([Definition 19.7](#S19.Thmtheorem7 "Definition 19.7 (Monotone games and diagonal strict concavity; , ). ‣ 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")) | Unique Nash, iterate                         | Independent policy gradient          |
| Extragradient, optimism                                   | Zero-sum and monotone                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | Nash, iterate                                | Extragradient and optimistic Adam    |
| Opponent shaping                                          | No general guarantee                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Shaped non-equilibrium point                 | LOLA; Stackelberg actor-critic       |
| Entropy regularisation                                    | All games, to the regularised solution                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | $`\mathrm{QRE}`$; Nash as $`\tau\to 0`$      | Soft actor-critic; R-NaD             |

Table 33: Learning dynamics, their guarantees and the reinforcement
learning construction that implements each. The Limit column states what
converges when anything does; iterate means the current policy and
average means the time-averaged policy.

## 20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning

The second continuum limit is taken in the learning rate rather than in
the population. Evolutionary game theory studies the differential
equations describing how the composition of a population of strategies
changes over time, and the central object is the replicator dynamics

|     |                                                                                                                  |     |      |
| --- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | ---- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\dot{\pi}_{i}(a_{i})\;=\;\pi_{i}(a_{i})\Big[u_{i}(a_{i},\pi_{-i})-u_{i}(\pi_{i},\pi_{-i})\Big],`$ |     | (19) |

in which the growth rate of a strategy’s share is its excess payoff over
the population average. The correspondence with reinforcement learning
is that
[Equation 19](#S20.E19 "In 20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the continuous-time limit of standard learning rules, which makes the
qualitative theory of dynamical systems, rest points, basins of
attraction, limit cycles and chaos, directly applicable to the analysis
of multi-agent training. The mathematical theory of these dynamics is
surveyed by [Hofbauer and Sigmund (2003)](#bib.bib80).

The two readings are a biological metaphor and an exact limit, and only
the second is used here. A biologist reads
[Equation 19](#S20.E19 "In 20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
as differential reproduction in a population of types. A reinforcement
learning researcher reads it as the vanishing-step-size limit of a
policy update, which it literally is for the rules named in the theorems
below. The consequence is that the qualitative dictionary of dynamical
systems becomes a dictionary about training runs: a rest point is a
converged run, an unstable rest point is a run that leaves a plateau, a
limit cycle is a run whose return curve is flat while its policy
rotates, and a strange attractor is a run that never repeats and never
settles.

| Game theory                         | Reinforcement learning                     | How to read the pair                        |
| ----------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------- |
| Population share $`\pi_{i}(a_{i})`$ | Policy probability of action $`a_{i}`$     | The same number in both readings            |
| Replicator dynamics                 | Vanishing-step-size limit of the update    | Grow what is above average                  |
| Replicator-mutator                  | Softmax update with entropy                | Selection plus diffusion                    |
| Fitness $`u_{i}(a_{i},\pi_{-i})`$   | Action value $`Q_{i}(a_{i})`$              | Payoff against the current population       |
| Rest point                          | Converged training run                     | No component is growing                     |
| Evolutionarily stable strategy      | Locally attracting, invasion-proof policy  | Resists a small population of mutants       |
| Limit cycle                         | Flat return curve with rotating policies   | Looks converged, is not                     |
| Basin of attraction                 | Set of initialisations with the same limit | Which equilibrium the seed selects          |
| Quantal response equilibrium        | Rest point of the regularised dynamics     | What a fixed temperature converges to       |
| Empirical game (EGTA)               | Payoff matrix over trained policies        | A normal-form game made of checkpoints      |
| $`\alpha`$-rank                     | Ranking over a policy population           | Stationary distribution of a mutation chain |
| Exploitability                      | Gap to a best response                     | The diagnostic a return curve hides         |

Table 34: Evolutionary vocabulary read from both sides.

###### Theorem 20.1 (Cross-learning and the replicator dynamics; [Börgers and Sarin, 1997](#bib.bib22)).

Consider agents using the cross-learning rule, which after playing
$`a_{i}`$ and receiving payoff $`u\in[0,1]`$ updates
$`\pi_{i}(a_{i})\leftarrow\pi_{i}(a_{i})+\alpha\,u\,(1-\pi_{i}(a_{i}))`$
and
$`\pi_{i}(a_{i}^{\prime})\leftarrow\pi_{i}(a_{i}^{\prime})-\alpha\,u\,\pi_{i}(a_{i}^{\prime})`$
for $`a_{i}^{\prime}\neq a_{i}`$. As the step size $`\alpha\to 0`$ with
time rescaled by $`1/\alpha`$, the stochastic process converges, over
any fixed finite time horizon, to the solution of
[Equation 19](#S20.E19 "In 20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").

###### Theorem 20.2 (Boltzmann Q-learning and the replicator-mutator dynamics; [Tuyls et al., 2003](#bib.bib179); [Tuyls et al., 2006](#bib.bib180); frequency adjustment, [Kaisers and Tuyls, 2010](#bib.bib88); survey, [Bloembergen et al., 2015](#bib.bib18)).

For agents running frequency-adjusted Q-learning, which updates all
action values simultaneously, with a Boltzmann policy at a constant
temperature $`\tau`$, the continuous-time limit of the induced policy
dynamics is

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |     |      |
| --- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | ---- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\dot{\pi}_{i}(a_{i})\;=\;\frac{\pi_{i}(a_{i})}{\tau}\Big[u_{i}(a_{i},\pi_{-i})-u_{i}(\pi_{i},\pi_{-i})\Big]\;-\;\pi_{i}(a_{i})\Big[\log\pi_{i}(a_{i})-\sum_{a_{i}^{\prime}}\pi_{i}(a_{i}^{\prime})\log\pi_{i}(a_{i}^{\prime})\Big],`$ |     | (20) |

the replicator term scaled by the inverse temperature plus an entropy
term that acts as mutation and pushes the trajectory toward the uniform
distribution. For plain Q-learning, which updates only the value of the
action taken, the same expression is an approximation rather than an
identity.

[Equation 20](#S20.E20 "In Theorem 20.2 (Boltzmann Q-learning and the replicator-mutator dynamics; , ; frequency adjustment, , ; survey, , ). ‣ 20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the cleanest available statement of what exploration does to a
multi-agent learning system. The first term is selection and the second
is diffusion; the temperature sets their ratio; rest points of the
combined dynamics are quantal response equilibria rather than Nash
equilibria, converging to the latter as $`\tau\to 0`$. The same
structure appears in single-agent policy optimisation, where the
entropy-regularised objective produces a softmax optimal policy and the
regularisation path approaches the greedy policy as the coefficient
vanishes, and recognising the two as the same term explains why entropy
regularisation stabilises multi-agent training for a reason that goes
beyond exploration: it convexifies the fixed-point problem, and in the
limit of large regularisation the equilibrium becomes unique and the
dynamics contractive. The regularised Nash dynamics of [Perolat et al.
(2021)](#bib.bib139) makes this explicit by adding a regulariser toward
a slowly moving anchor policy, obtaining convergence to the Nash
equilibrium of the unregularised game by annealing the anchor, and this
is the mechanism behind DeepNash ([Perolat et al., 2022a](#bib.bib140)).

Evolutionary stability is the refinement that the dynamics justifies,
and it deserves a definition because it is the one concept in this area
that is a statement about robustness to a perturbation of the population
rather than about a single agent’s incentives.

###### Definition 20.3 (Evolutionarily stable strategy; [Maynard Smith and Price, 1973](#bib.bib114); [Taylor and Jonker, 1978](#bib.bib177)).

In a symmetric two-player game with payoff $`u`$, a strategy
$`\pi^{\star}`$ is evolutionarily stable when for every
$`\pi\neq\pi^{\star}`$ either
$`u(\pi^{\star},\pi^{\star})>u(\pi,\pi^{\star})`$, or
$`u(\pi^{\star},\pi^{\star})=u(\pi,\pi^{\star})`$ and
$`u(\pi^{\star},\pi)>u(\pi,\pi)`$. Every evolutionarily stable strategy
is a Nash equilibrium, and in the replicator dynamics it is
asymptotically stable.

 

The reinforcement learning reading. Evolutionary stability is a
statement about a population of policies rather than about a single one,
which is the setting of league play and population-based training. Read
$`\pi^{\star}`$ as the incumbent policy pool and $`\pi`$ as a newly
trained challenger injected at low frequency. The first clause says the
incumbent beats the challenger outright; the second says that when they
tie against the incumbent, the incumbent beats the challenger in the
rare encounters between challengers. Together they say the challenger
cannot gain a foothold, so an evolutionarily stable pool is one that no
single new policy can invade, which is exactly the property a league is
maintained to achieve. The relation to
[Definition 4.1](#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 4.1 (Nash equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is one-directional and worth remembering: every evolutionarily stable
strategy is a Nash equilibrium, and many Nash equilibria are not
evolutionarily stable, so stability is the stronger and more
operationally meaningful requirement for a population.
[Example 20.4](#S20.Thmtheorem4 "Example 20.4 (Hawk and dove: the stable mixture is computable in one line). ‣ 20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
computes one in a line.

###### Example 20.4 (Hawk and dove: the stable mixture is computable in one line).

Two animals contest a resource worth $`V=2`$. Two hawks fight and share
the expected value minus an injury cost $`C=4`$, so each receives
$`(V-C)/2=-1`$. A hawk against a dove takes the whole resource, $`V=2`$,
leaving the dove nothing. Two doves split it, $`V/2=1`$. The payoff
matrix to the row animal is

|     |                                  |     |
| --- | -------------------------------- | --- | --- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r | c   | c   | }\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil\mathrm{Hawk}\hfil\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil\mathrm{Dove}\hfil\lx@intercol\\ |

\cline{2-3}\cr\mathrm{Hawk}&-1&2\\
\cline{2-3}\cr\mathrm{Dove}&0&1\\
\cline{2-3}\cr\end{array}.`$ | |

Neither pure strategy is stable, since a population of doves is invaded
by a hawk earning $`2`$ against the doves’ $`1`$, and a population of
hawks is invaded by a dove earning $`0`$ against the hawks’ $`-1`$. The
stable mixture makes the two equal: with a fraction $`p`$ of hawks, a
hawk earns $`-p+2(1-p)=2-3p`$ and a dove earns $`1-p`$, equal when
$`2-3p=1-p`$, that is $`p=1/2=V/C`$. At that mixture both earn $`0.5`$
and the population average is $`0.5`$, which is half of what a
population of pure doves would earn.

The last sentence is the content for multi-agent reinforcement learning.
The evolutionarily stable outcome is strictly worse for the population
than a cooperative one, it is reached by a dynamics that no agent
controls, and the fraction of aggressive behaviour it sustains is set by
the ratio of the prize to the cost of conflict. A training population of
self-interested agents in a contested-resource environment will settle
at this ratio, and changing it requires changing $`V/C`$, which is an
environment design decision, not an algorithmic one. $`\lozenge`$

###### Example 20.5 (Rock-paper-scissors: a conserved quantity instead of convergence).

Simulate
[Equation 19](#S20.E19 "In 20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
for the single-population rock-paper-scissors game from
$`(0.4,0.35,0.25)`$. The product $`\pi(R)\pi(P)\pi(S)`$ is a conserved
quantity of the continuous-time dynamics, and the numerical integration
confirms it: starting from a product of $`0.0350`$, after fifty thousand
steps of size $`4\times 10^{-4}`$ the product is $`0.034995`$, a
relative drift of $`1.5\times 10^{-4}`$ attributable to the
discretisation. The trajectory is a closed orbit around the uniform
equilibrium, and the probability of Rock is $`0.419`$ at the end of the
run, back near where it started.

A training run of this system would produce a return curve pinned at
zero, since the game is zero-sum and symmetric, with a policy that is
rotating steadily. No scalar logged by a standard training harness
distinguishes this from convergence. The quantity that does distinguish
it is the conserved product, or equivalently the Kullback-Leibler
divergence to the equilibrium, and in general the exploitability. This
example is the reason
[Table 39](#S22.T39 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
exists. $`\lozenge`$

Gradient-based learning admits the same treatment. Infinitesimal
gradient ascent in a two-agent two-action game ([Singh et al.,
2000](#bib.bib163)) yields a linear system whose trajectories either
converge to a Nash equilibrium or orbit it on closed elliptical curves,
the latter occurring exactly when the game has a unique mixed
equilibrium. The average payoff still converges to the equilibrium
payoff in the orbiting case, which foreshadows the general phenomenon
that time averages of no-regret dynamics behave better than the dynamics
themselves. The win-or-learn-fast modification of [Bowling and Veloso
(2002)](#bib.bib24) varies the step size according to whether the agent
is performing better or worse than a reference, and turns the closed
orbits into inward spirals, which is a control-theoretic fix to a
dynamical-systems problem and has no single-agent analogue, since in a
single-agent problem gradient ascent on a smooth objective cannot cycle.

The broader lesson of this section is methodological. Single-agent
reinforcement learning is an optimisation problem and is analysed with
the tools of optimisation: descent lemmas, contraction, convergence
rates to a fixed target. Multi-agent reinforcement learning is a
dynamical system and must be analysed with the tools of dynamical
systems: stability of rest points, Lyapunov functions, bifurcations as
parameters such as the temperature or the step size cross thresholds,
and the possibility of chaos, which does occur in simple games ([Sato
et al., 2002](#bib.bib152)). The empirical practice of reporting a
learning curve and declaring convergence when it flattens is adequate
for the first setting and misleading for the second, because a flat
curve is consistent with a limit cycle whose period exceeds the plotting
window. The appropriate diagnostics are exploitability, measured as the
gap to a best response, and the distance of the empirical joint play to
the coarse correlated equilibrium polytope, both of which are computable
in the games where this matters and neither of which is a return.

The empirical game-theoretic analysis methodology of [Wellman
(2006)](#bib.bib190) is the operational form of this advice. One takes a
finite set of trained policies, estimates the payoff matrix of the game
they define by simulation, and analyses that empirical normal-form game
with the tools of
[Sections 4](#S4 "4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and [20](#S20 "20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"):
its equilibria, its replicator flow, its response graph. The outcome is
a characterisation of the strategic landscape that a learning curve
cannot provide, and the $`\alpha`$-rank solution concept of [Omidshafiei
et al. (2019)](#bib.bib132) supplies a ranking of policies that is
computable in time polynomial in the total number of pure strategy
profiles, and is defined for general-sum many-agent games, for which no
polynomial-time Nash computation is available.

###### Example 20.6 (What a ranking of checkpoints can and cannot say).

Build the mutation chain of $`\alpha`$-rank over three strategies with
selection intensity $`\alpha=10`$ and population size $`2`$. On
rock-paper-scissors the stationary distribution is uniform,
$`(\tfrac{1}{3},\tfrac{1}{3},\tfrac{1}{3})`$, with a spread of less than
$`10^{-15}`$, which is the correct answer: the three strategies are
cyclically related and none dominates. On a transitive game in which the
first strategy beats the second by one and the third by two, and the
second beats the third by one, the stationary distribution concentrates
almost entirely on the first, with mass $`0.99999998`$.

The contrast is the diagnostic. A ranking that comes out uniform is not
a failure of the method but a report that the policy population is
intransitive, which is exactly the situation in which average win rate
across a population is meaningless and in which a league or a
population-based method is required. A ranking that concentrates reports
a transitive landscape, in which the best checkpoint is a meaningful
notion. Running $`\alpha`$-rank on the checkpoints of a self-play run
therefore answers, before any further analysis, whether the run’s own
history forms a ladder or a cycle. $`\lozenge`$

## 21 The complexity landscape

A correspondence between two models is only useful if one knows what it
costs to exploit. This section collects the complexity of the solution
problems attached to each model class so that the reader can see, for
any proposed reduction, whether it buys tractability or merely relocates
the difficulty. Unless stated otherwise, the problems are the decision
versions of exact solution with the model given explicitly, so the input
size is polynomial in $`\left\lvert\mathcal{S}\right\rvert`$,
$`\prod_{i}\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\rvert`$ and the horizon, and
sampling-based learning complexity is discussed separately at the end.
Because the horizon and the discounting convention change the answers,
every statement below names them.

The two readings here differ in what is being counted. A complexity
theorist counts operations as a function of the input size, where the
input is the explicit description of the game, and is therefore
interested in whether an exponential blow-up lives in the class or in
the encoding. A reinforcement learning researcher counts environment
interactions as a function of the accuracy, where the model is never
written down at all, and is therefore interested in sample complexity.
The two are related but not the same, and the commonest error in this
area is to read a planning hardness result as a statement about learning
or the reverse. We separate them explicitly, and the rule of thumb is
that planning hardness bounds what any amount of data can buy, while
sample complexity bounds what a given amount of data can buy for a
problem whose planning version is already easy.

| Game theory             | Reinforcement learning                                           | How to read the pair                        |
| ----------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- |
| Input size              | Size of the explicit model                                       | What the exponent is measured against       |
| Planning problem        | Model known; compute a solution                                  | No interaction needed                       |
| Learning problem        | Model unknown; interact and approximate                          | Sample complexity, not time                 |
| P-complete              | Solvable by linear programming                                   | Easy, and not parallelisable in general     |
| PPAD-complete           | Fixed point guaranteed by a parity argument                      | Hard, but not NP-hard                       |
| PLS-complete            | Local search with an improving step                              | Each step is cheap; the path is long        |
| NEXP-complete           | Nondeterministic exponential time                                | No polynomial algorithm exists              |
| Undecidable             | No algorithm at all                                              | Infinite horizon with partial observability |
| Curse of multi-agents   | $`\prod_{i}\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\rvert`$ in the input | Exponential in $`n`$ inside the input size  |
| Succinct representation | Factored or graphical model                                      | Small description, possibly hard solution   |
| Approximate solution    | $`\varepsilon`$-equilibrium; $`\varepsilon`$-optimal policy      | What is actually computed                   |

Table 35: Complexity vocabulary read from both sides.

The single-agent reference points are classical. Deciding whether a
finite flat discounted Markov decision process has value at least a
threshold is P-complete, with membership by linear programming
([Papadimitriou and Tsitsiklis, 1987](#bib.bib137)). The finite-horizon
partially observable Markov decision process problem, for
history-dependent policies, is PSPACE-complete ([Papadimitriou and
Tsitsiklis, 1987](#bib.bib137)), the jump reflecting the exponential
history space that the belief state of
[Theorem 2.5](#S2.Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 2.5 (Belief-MDP reduction; belief sufficiency and the functional equation, , ; piecewise linearity and convexity, , ). ‣ 2 The single-agent baseline ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
summarises but does not shrink. The infinite-horizon partially
observable problem is undecidable, and so is its
$`\varepsilon`$-approximate version under undiscounted criteria, the
average criterion among them ([Madani et al., 2003](#bib.bib112)).

###### Example 21.1 (Where the exponentials actually live).

Two arithmetic exercises make the rest of this section readable. First,
the curse of multi-agents. With five actions per agent the joint action
space has $`25`$ elements for two agents, $`3125`$ for five,
$`9{,}765{,}625`$ for ten and about $`9.5\times 10^{13}`$ for twenty,
while $`\max_{i}\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\rvert`$ stays at $`5`$.
A multi-agent Markov decision process is solvable in time polynomial in
$`\left\lvert\mathcal{S}\right\rvert\prod_{i}\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\rvert`$,
so the algorithm is polynomial in a quantity that is itself exponential
in the parameter of interest, and at ten agents the gap between the
joint and the per-agent count is a factor of about two million. This is
why the sample-complexity results that scale in
$`\max_{i}\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\rvert`$ rather than
$`\prod_{i}\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\rvert`$ ([Jin et al.,
2024](#bib.bib87)) matter so much, and why factorisation, the mean-field
limit and non-stationary correlated targets are the three escapes
catalogued in this monograph.

Second, the history space. With two actions and two observations the
number of action-observation histories of length $`h`$ is $`4^{h}`$,
which is $`64`$ at $`h=3`$, $`1024`$ at $`h=5`$ and $`1{,}048{,}576`$ at
$`h=10`$. The number of deterministic history-dependent policies is
$`2`$ raised to the number of histories of every length up to $`h-1`$,
that is $`2^{(4^{h}-1)/3}`$, whose base-ten logarithm is $`6.3`$ at
$`h=3`$, $`102.7`$ at $`h=5`$ and $`105{,}217.5`$ at $`h=10`$. These are
the numbers behind the PSPACE and NEXP entries of
[Table 36](#S21.T36 "In 21 The complexity landscape ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
and the jump between them is the jump between enumerating histories and
enumerating functions of histories, which is also the jump between a
belief state and a prescription in
[Section 9](#S9 "9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
$`\lozenge`$

The multi-agent reference points are the following. For finite-horizon
decentralised models with at least two agents, both the decentralised
partially observable Markov decision process and the decentralised
Markov decision process problems are NEXP-hard, and they are
NEXP-complete when the horizon is strictly less than the number of
states ([Bernstein et al., 2002](#bib.bib16)). The hardness implies, by
the time hierarchy theorem and the inclusions
$`\textsc{P}\subsetneq\textsc{EXP}\subseteq\textsc{NEXP}`$, that no
polynomial-time algorithm exists, and it implies that no polynomial-time
reduction producing a polynomially sized partially observable Markov
decision process can exist unless PSPACE, EXP and NEXP coincide, which
places the reductions of
[Section 9](#S9 "9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
in context: they are exact and they are necessarily exponential in the
quantity that matters, the prescription space. Structural restrictions
recover lower classes. When transitions and observations are independent
across agents, each agent is locally fully observable, and the coupling
enters only through a joint reward structure, the problem drops to
NP-complete ([Becker et al., 2004](#bib.bib14)), which is the formal
statement that coupling through reward alone is far milder than coupling
through dynamics. The infinite-horizon versions are undecidable,
inheriting the single-agent result ([Bernstein et al.,
2002](#bib.bib16); [Madani et al., 2003](#bib.bib112)).

For the stateless models, two-player zero-sum normal-form games are
solved in polynomial time by linear programming, which is von Neumann’s
minimax theorem made constructive, the constructive part being Dantzig’s
equivalence with linear programming and the polynomial bound being
Khachiyan’s. Finding a Nash equilibrium of a two-player general-sum
normal-form game is PPAD-complete ([Chen et al., 2009](#bib.bib31)), the
three-player and general cases having been settled first ([Daskalakis
et al., 2009](#bib.bib39)); PPAD captures total search problems whose
solutions are guaranteed by a parity argument and is widely believed to
lie strictly between P and NP-hardness. The relaxations are easy, and
for an explicitly given game they are easy for a trivial reason:
correlated and coarse correlated equilibria are defined by polynomially
many linear inequalities in the joint distribution, so a linear program
suffices. The substantive result concerns succinct representations. For
games of polynomial type whose expected utilities are computable in
polynomial time, which covers graphical, anonymous, polymatrix,
congestion, scheduling and local-effect games, the
ellipsoid-against-hope method of [Papadimitriou and Roughgarden
(2008)](#bib.bib136) computes a correlated equilibrium in polynomial
time; the published algorithm returns an approximate equilibrium, and an
exact variant is due to [Jiang and Leyton-Brown (2015)](#bib.bib86). The
contrast between PPAD-completeness for Nash and polynomial time for
correlated equilibrium is the complexity-theoretic justification for the
recommendation of
[Section 4](#S4 "4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
that no-regret dynamics targeting coarse correlated equilibria are the
right default. In potential games the problem is different again: a pure
Nash equilibrium is guaranteed to exist by the finite improvement
property, and finding one is PLS-complete for congestion games in
general while being polynomial for the symmetric network case
([Fabrikant et al., 2004](#bib.bib52)), which means that the
better-reply dynamics may take exponentially many steps even though each
step is cheap.

Zero-sum structure in the many-agent case is the one place where a
positive result survives beyond two agents. Unrestricted three-player
zero-sum games are PPAD-complete, but when the payoffs decompose over
the edges of a graph, so that the game is a zero-sum polymatrix game in
the sense of
[Definition 10.1](#S10.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 10.1 (Graphical games, , ; polymatrix games). ‣ 10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
the minmax theorem generalises, the equilibria form a convex set, they
are computable in polynomial time and no-regret learning converges to
them ([Cai and Daskalakis, 2011](#bib.bib29)). This is essentially the
limit of what can be pushed, and the practical consequence is that a
many-agent adversarial application should be examined for pairwise
separability before any algorithm is chosen.

Stochastic games are where the folklore most needs correcting, because
the turn-based and the simultaneous-move cases separate. Deciding the
winner of a simple stochastic game, which is turn-based, undiscounted
and stopping, lies in NP$`\,\cap\,`$coNP ([Condon, 1992](#bib.bib35)),
and the turn-based discounted, mean-payoff and parity stochastic games
are polynomial-time equivalent to it ([Andersson and Miltersen,
2009](#bib.bib6)); no polynomial algorithm is known for any of them. For
the simultaneous-move discounted game that
[Equation 9](#S7.E9 "In Theorem 7.6 ( , ). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
actually defines, the exact value problem is harder: it lies in PSPACE,
it is square-root-sum hard, and it is not known to lie in NP ([Etessami
and Yannakakis, 2010](#bib.bib51)). What is unproblematic is
approximation at a fixed discount factor. Since
$`\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{mm}}`$ is a $`\gamma`$-contraction by
[Theorem 7.6](#S7.Thmtheorem6 "Theorem 7.6 ( , ). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and each per-state matrix game is solved by a linear program,
$`O\big(\log(1/\varepsilon)/\log(1/\gamma)\big)`$ sweeps deliver an
$`\varepsilon`$-optimal value, so the running time is polynomial in the
model size, in $`\log(1/\varepsilon)`$ and in $`1/(1-\gamma)`$. The last
dependence is why the qualifier matters: with $`\gamma`$ part of the
input the strong approximation problem is known to sit in PPAD, with no
polynomial-time algorithm known ([Etessami and Yannakakis,
2010](#bib.bib51)).

For general-sum stochastic games, computing a stationary Nash
equilibrium is PPAD-hard already by inheritance from the stateless case,
a one-state game being a normal-form game, and [Daskalakis et al.
(2023)](#bib.bib41) strengthen this to two-player turn-based games with
a constant discount factor and a constant approximation, where the
inheritance argument does not apply. The same work removes the hope that
the relaxation which rescues the stateless case rescues the sequential
one: computing a constant-approximate stationary Markov coarse
correlated equilibrium is PPAD-hard under the same restrictions,
unconditionally for the perfect-correlated-equilibrium refinement and,
for the unrefined notion, under the PCP-for-PPAD conjecture. The rescue
comes instead from relaxing stationarity, and here the literature must
be read carefully, because two different objects are on offer.
V-learning and its relatives return a non-stationary, history-dependent
correlated policy, with a sample complexity scaling in
$`\max_{i}\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\rvert`$ rather than
$`\prod_{i}\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\rvert`$, which is what
avoids the curse of multi-agents ([Jin et al., 2024](#bib.bib87); [Song
et al., 2022](#bib.bib168)); those policies are explicitly not Markov.
Non-stationary Markov coarse correlated equilibria, which are Markov in
the state at each stage, are learnable in time and sample complexity
polynomial in all parameters by the decentralised algorithm of
[Daskalakis et al. (2023)](#bib.bib41). Both targets are correlated
objects and both require shared randomness at execution.

For extensive-form games, the equilibrium of a two-player zero-sum game
with perfect recall is computable in time polynomial in the size of the
game tree ([Koller and Megiddo, 1992](#bib.bib94)), which may itself be
exponential in the natural description of the game; the sequence form,
developed for computation by [von Stengel (1996)](#bib.bib184), replaces
the simplex of mixed strategies by the polytope of realisation plans and
brings the linear program down to a size linear in the tree. [Koller
et al. (1996)](#bib.bib95) extend the device to the general-sum case,
where the equilibrium conditions become a linear complementarity problem
solved by Lemke’s algorithm, which is not polynomial. Dropping perfect
recall makes the guaranteed-payoff problem, that of deciding whether a
player can secure a given payoff, NP-hard ([Koller and Megiddo,
1992](#bib.bib94)); dropping the zero-sum assumption makes equilibrium
computation PPAD-hard by inheritance from the normal form. For
Stackelberg equilibria the pattern inverts in an instructive way:
computing an optimal commitment in a two-player normal-form game is
polynomial, by solving one linear program per pure follower strategy,
while the Bayesian version with multiple follower types and the version
with three or more agents are NP-hard ([Conitzer and Sandholm,
2006](#bib.bib37)). Computing the Shapley value of a weighted voting
game is \#P-complete ([Deng and Papadimitriou, 1994](#bib.bib42);
[Michalak et al., 2013](#bib.bib119)), while the same work shows it is
polynomial for induced-subgraph games, so the hardness is a property of
the representation rather than of the solution concept; it is
nevertheless the reason why Shapley-based credit assignment in
multi-agent reinforcement learning is implemented by Monte Carlo
sampling over permutations. For the stability concepts of
[Section 17](#S17 "17 Coalitions, stability of credit, and bargaining ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
the same paper reports that, on its weighted-graph vehicle, the core is
easy to characterise when the game is convex and NP-complete otherwise,
and that similar results hold for the kernel, the nucleolus, the
$`\varepsilon`$-core and the bargaining set, so a federation stability
check is cheap exactly when the coalition value has increasing marginal
returns.
