Table 36: Complexity of exact solution, model given explicitly. The
horizon is finite unless stated. The Target column names the solution
concept whose computation is being classified; PPAD and PLS are total
search classes, so the stated hardness concerns the search problem
rather than a decision problem. Side conditions that the sources require
are given in the Problem column rather than relegated to the text.

Three readings of
[Table 36](#S21.T36 "In 21 The complexity landscape ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
are worth stating because they have direct algorithmic consequences. The
first is that partial observability costs one complexity class and
decentralisation costs another, and the two costs compound; the jump
from PSPACE to NEXP between a partially observable Markov decision
process and a decentralised one is the price of the absence of a common
belief state, and it is the formal justification for every architecture
that manufactures common knowledge by communication, since a
communication channel that makes all observations public collapses the
decentralised problem back to the centralised one.

The second is that relaxing the solution concept helps in the stateless
case and does not help in the sequential case. Correlated equilibrium is
polynomial where Nash is PPAD-complete in normal-form games, and
stationary Markov coarse correlated equilibrium remains PPAD-hard in
Markov games even for two-player turn-based instances with a constant
discount factor. The practical conclusion is that one must also relax
stationarity, and the learning algorithms with the strongest guarantees
in general-sum Markov games return correlated policies that require a
shared random seed at execution, Markov in the state in the case of
[Daskalakis et al. (2023)](#bib.bib41) and history-dependent in the case
of [Jin et al. (2024)](#bib.bib87). Whether either is acceptable is an
engineering question about the deployment, and it is one that the
complexity results force the designer to confront rather than a
limitation of any particular algorithm.

The third is that the exponential dependence on the number of agents is
rarely in the complexity class and almost always in the input size,
which is the arithmetic of
[Example 21.1](#S21.Thmtheorem1 "Example 21.1 (Where the exponentials actually live). ‣ 21 The complexity landscape ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
A multi-agent Markov decision process is solvable in time polynomial in
$`\left\lvert\mathcal{S}\right\rvert`$ and
$`\prod_{i}\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\rvert`$, and the second
factor is exponential in $`n`$, so the algorithm is polynomial in a
quantity that is itself exponential in the parameter of interest. This
is the curse of multi-agents, and the three structural escapes from it
are the three that this monograph has catalogued: factorisation, which
restricts the representable value functions
([Sections 16](#S16 "16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and [10](#S10 "10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"));
the mean-field limit, which removes the dependence on $`n`$ by symmetry
([Section 18](#S18 "18 The population limit: mean-field games and mean-field control ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"));
and the restriction to non-stationary correlated targets, whose sample
complexity scales with
$`\max_{i}\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{i}\right\rvert`$ ([Jin et al.,
2024](#bib.bib87)).

## 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model

The dictionary assembled so far is of practical use only if it narrows
the choice of training procedure, and it does. This section states a
decision procedure, justifies each branch by the results already
established, and collects the recommendations in
[Table 38](#S22.T38 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and
[Table 39](#S22.T39 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").

The two readings converge here, which is the point of the whole
monograph. A game theorist asked to advise on a multi-agent system would
ask what the game is, which solution concept is appropriate and whether
it is attainable. A reinforcement learning engineer would ask what
algorithm to run. The claim of this section is that the second question
has no answer independent of the first, and that the five structural
questions below extract exactly the information needed to answer it.

| Game theory               | Reinforcement learning                     | How to read the pair                                   |
| ------------------------- | ------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------ |
| Reward structure          | Shared, opposed or mixed-motive rewards    | Decides which reduction is sound                       |
| Information structure     | Who observes what, and what is public      | Decides whether a sufficient statistic exists          |
| Execution architecture    | Centralised or decentralised at deployment | Decides whether factorisation is needed                |
| Shared randomisation      | A common seed available at run time        | Decides whether $`\mathrm{CE}`$ targets are executable |
| Opponent class            | Fixed, co-trained, or unknown partners     | Decides whether the problem is single-agent            |
| Population size           | Number of interacting agents               | Decides whether the mean-field limit applies           |
| Solution concept targeted | What the training loop converges to        | Must be stated before choosing a method                |
| Exploitability            | Gap to a best response                     | The zero-sum diagnostic                                |
| NashConv                  | Summed unilateral improvement              | The general-sum diagnostic                             |
| Cross-play                | Pairing agents from different runs         | The cooperative convention diagnostic                  |
| Empirical game            | Payoff matrix over trained checkpoints     | The population diagnostic                              |

Table 37: Deployment vocabulary read from both sides.

Five questions determine the branch, and they should be answered about
the deployment rather than about the simulator. The first is the reward
structure: whether the agents share a reward exactly, oppose each other
exactly, or neither. The second is the observability: whether each agent
sees the state, whether the agents jointly see it, and whether anything
is public. The third is the execution architecture: whether a central
controller issues joint actions at deployment time, whether the agents
act on private information, and whether a shared random seed or a
communication channel is available. The fourth is whether the other
agents are fixed, co-trained, or unknown at training time. The fifth is
the population size, which decides whether the mean-field limit applies.
The reason to ask these about the deployment is that the sound reduction
depends on what is available at execution, not on what is available
during training, which is the entire content of the
centralised-training-decentralised-execution distinction.

If the reward is shared, the state is fully observed and a central
controller issues joint actions,
[Proposition 7.3](#S7.Thmtheorem3 "Proposition 7.3 (Degeneracies of the stochastic game). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
applies and the problem is a Markov decision process over joint actions.
Any single-agent algorithm is sound; the only difficulty is the size of
$`\prod_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}`$, and the remedy is a factored action
representation rather than a multi-agent algorithm. Practitioners who
reach for a multi-agent method in this setting are solving a harder
problem than the one they have.

If the reward is shared and execution is decentralised, the problem is a
decentralised partially observable Markov decision process, the optimum
is well defined, and the obstacle is equilibrium selection rather than
equilibrium computation
([Proposition 8.6](#S8.Thmtheorem6 "Proposition 8.6 (Optimality and equilibrium in common-payoff games). ‣ 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")).
Three routes are sound and they trade off differently. Value
factorisation under the Individual-Global-Max condition of
[Definition 16.7](#S16.Thmtheorem7 "Definition 16.7 (Individual-Global-Max). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
makes decentralised greedy play optimal by construction and is the
cheapest option, at the cost of restricting the representable stage
games; monotone mixing in the manner of [Rashid et al.
(2018)](#bib.bib145) is the standard default and its failure mode is
predictable from
[Proposition 16.8](#S16.Thmtheorem8 "Proposition 16.8 (Monotone mixing implies IGM). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and is not a violation of
[Equation 17](#S16.E17 "In Definition 16.7 (Individual-Global-Max). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"):
it is a joint value function that satisfies the Individual-Global-Max
condition and admits no monotone factorisation, which
[Example 16.9](#S16.Thmtheorem9 "Example 16.9 (A two-by-two matrix that no monotone mixing network represents). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
exhibits in two by two. Centralised critics with decentralised actors,
as in [Lowe et al. (2017)](#bib.bib110) and [Yu et al.
(2022)](#bib.bib198), impose no representational restriction and provide
no selection guarantee, so they must be paired with a selection device.
Occupancy-state and public-belief methods implement
[Theorem 9.5](#S9.Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 9.5 (Occupancy-MDP reduction; , ). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and
[Theorem 9.1](#S9.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 9.1 (Common-information reduction; , ). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
directly, are the only route with an exactness guarantee, and are the
most expensive; the Bayesian action decoder of [Foerster et al.
(2019)](#bib.bib59) is the practical form. Independent learning with an
optimism, leniency or hysteresis bias ([Lauer and Riedmiller,
2000](#bib.bib102); [Matignon et al., 2007](#bib.bib113); [Panait
et al., 2006](#bib.bib135)) is a selection device rather than an
algorithm family, and it is the correct accompaniment to any of the
above in tasks with the penalty structure of
[Example 7.5](#S7.Thmtheorem5 "Example 7.5 (A penalty game in which greedy learners select the worst equilibrium). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
If the interaction is local, a coordination graph in the sense of
[Proposition 10.2](#S10.Thmtheorem2 "Proposition 10.2 (Coordination graphs and variable elimination). ‣ 10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
converts the joint argmax into message passing and should be used before
any approximation is contemplated.

If the game is two-player zero-sum, the machinery is complete and should
be used. Under perfect information, the recipe is self-play with search
and a learned value function ([Silver et al., 2018](#bib.bib162)), which
attains the minimax value in the tabular limit and is validated
empirically rather than by a convergence theorem. Under imperfect
information, the sound targets are the average of a no-regret dynamics
or the solution of the public-belief-state game. Counterfactual regret
minimisation and its sampled variants ([Zinkevich et al.,
2007](#bib.bib201); [Lanctot et al., 2009](#bib.bib99)) drive the
average external regret to zero at rate $`O(1/\sqrt{T})`$, so the
average strategy converges to a Nash equilibrium; the neural variant
([Brown et al., 2019](#bib.bib26)) inherits the scheme with function
approximation and its regret bound carries an additive floor set by the
approximation error, so it has no vanishing-regret guarantee; neural
fictitious self-play ([Heinrich and Silver, 2016](#bib.bib78))
approximates fictitious play with the same averaging logic; regularised
Nash dynamics ([Perolat et al., 2021](#bib.bib139)) obtains last-iterate
convergence by anchoring; and ReBeL and Student of Games ([Brown et al.,
2020](#bib.bib27); [Schmid et al., 2023](#bib.bib155)) combine the
public belief state of
[Remark 9.7](#S9.Thmtheorem7 "Remark 9.7 (Public belief state reduction). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
with search. What is not sound is naive self-play with a single network
and no averaging, because the dynamics in a zero-sum game with a unique
mixed equilibrium orbits rather than converges
([Examples 19.4](#S19.Thmtheorem4 "Example 19.4 (Multiplicative weights spirals away from the equilibrium). ‣ 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and [20.5](#S20.Thmtheorem5 "Example 20.5 (Rock-paper-scissors: a conserved quantity instead of convergence). ‣ 20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"));
the time average converges and the iterate does not, so reporting the
final checkpoint rather than the average is a methodological error with
a theorem behind it. If there are more than two agents but the payoffs
decompose over pairs and sum to zero,
[Table 36](#S21.T36 "In 21 The complexity landscape ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
says the good properties survive and the same machinery applies ([Cai
and Daskalakis, 2011](#bib.bib29)).

If the game is general-sum, no procedure converges to a Nash equilibrium
in general
([Section 23](#S23 "23 Correspondences that fail, and the theorems that forbid them ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")),
and the question becomes which attainable target to aim at. The
defensible answers are a coarse correlated equilibrium through no-regret
dynamics, which is attainable and polynomial in the stateless case and
requires non-stationarity in the sequential case ([Jin et al.,
2024](#bib.bib87)); a Stackelberg equilibrium if the deployment
genuinely grants one party commitment power
([Section 14](#S14 "14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"));
a social welfare optimum if the designer controls the reward and can
impose it, which converts the problem into the common-payoff case; or a
best response to a specified population of opponents, which converts it
into the single-agent case by
[Proposition 7.11](#S7.Thmtheorem11 "Proposition 7.11 (Fixed others induce a Markov decision process). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
Before any of these, the structural check is worth performing: if the
game is a Markov potential game or is supermodular, the convergence
results of
[Sections 16](#S16 "16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and [19](#S19 "19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
apply and independent learning is sound; if the game is smooth in the
sense of
[Theorem 15.9](#S15.Thmtheorem9 "Theorem 15.9 (Smoothness and robust price of anarchy; , ). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
the welfare of whatever no-regret learning reaches is bounded without
any convergence assumption, which is often all that is needed.
Population-based methods, double oracle and policy-space response
oracles ([McMahan et al., 2003](#bib.bib116); [Lanctot et al.,
2017](#bib.bib100)), occupy a useful middle ground: they maintain an
explicit empirical game over a growing set of policies, solve it with a
meta-solver whose choice makes the target explicit, and add a best
response to the meta-solution. Their value is that the target is a
parameter rather than an accident, and the empirical game they maintain
is exactly the object that [Wellman (2006)](#bib.bib190) analyses.

If the agents are many and exchangeable,
[Section 18](#S18 "18 The population limit: mean-field games and mean-field control ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
applies and the training loop reduces to a single-agent solver inside a
population-update outer loop, with damping or regularisation on the
outer loop. If the other agents are fixed and known, the problem is
single-agent by
[Proposition 7.11](#S7.Thmtheorem11 "Proposition 7.11 (Fixed others induce a Markov decision process). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
If they are fixed and unknown, it is Bayesian and the Bayes-adaptive
construction of
[Remark 12.5](#S12.Thmtheorem5 "Remark 12.5 (Harsanyi transformation and Bayesian reinforcement learning coincide). ‣ 12 Incomplete information: Harsanyi types and Bayes-adaptive learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
applies, which in practice means a recurrent or context-conditioned
policy trained across a distribution of partners, that is, the zero-shot
coordination protocol.

The procedure is easier to trust once it has been run on concrete
deployments, so we run it on four.

###### Example 22.1 (Four deployments, four answers).

A fleet of warehouse robots executing a picking plan. The reward is
shared, the state is fully observed by a central scheduler, and the
scheduler issues joint actions at run time. By
[Proposition 7.3](#S7.Thmtheorem3 "Proposition 7.3 (Degeneracies of the stochastic game). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
this is a single-agent Markov decision process over joint actions; the
only obstacle is the size of the joint action space, and the remedy is
the coordination graph of
[Proposition 10.2](#S10.Thmtheorem2 "Proposition 10.2 (Coordination graphs and variable elimination). ‣ 10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
since a robot interacts only with the robots in its aisle. No
multi-agent algorithm is required and using one would be solving a
harder problem.

The same fleet with an unreliable network, so that at run time each
robot sees only its own sensors. The reward is still shared, execution
is now decentralised, and the problem is a decentralised partially
observable Markov decision process. The obstacle is equilibrium
selection, the optimum is still well defined, and the branch above
applies: factorisation if the task is IGM-representable, a centralised
critic plus an explicit selection device if it is not, and cross-play
across independently trained runs as the diagnostic, since
[Example 8.7](#S8.Thmtheorem7 "Example 8.7 (Why an arbitrary convention counts as an equilibrium). ‣ 8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is exactly the failure mode that a mean-return plot hides.

A bidding agent in a repeated advertising auction. The reward is
general-sum, the auction rules are fixed and public, the agent acts
alone, and the other bidders are neither fixed nor controllable. The
relevant questions are whether the auction is truthful, in which case
[Example 15.2](#S15.Thmtheorem2 "Example 15.2 (Why the second-price auction is honest, by cases). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
says the agent’s problem collapses to estimating its own value and the
strategic content disappears, and whether the game is smooth, in which
case
[Theorem 15.9](#S15.Thmtheorem9 "Theorem 15.9 (Smoothness and robust price of anarchy; , ). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
bounds the welfare of whatever no-regret method is run. The target to
state is a coarse correlated equilibrium of the empirical game, and the
diagnostic is the maximum constant-deviation gain under empirical play.

A pair of language-model agents negotiating a contract. The reward is
general-sum, each agent has private information, messages are cheap
talk, and one party may have commitment power through a system prompt
that the other can read. The applicable results are
[Theorem 13.3](#S13.Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 13.3 (Cheap talk under misaligned objectives; , ). ‣ 13 Communication: cheap talk, signalling, persuasion and emergent protocols ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
which bounds how informative the messages can be as a function of the
misalignment;
[Example 17.7](#S17.Thmtheorem7 "Example 17.7 (Alternating offers makes patience into bargaining power). ‣ 17 Coalitions, stability of credit, and bargaining ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
which says the protocol and the discounting determine the split before
any learning happens; and
[Section 14](#S14 "14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
which says that whichever party can credibly commit obtains at least its
equilibrium payoff. The evaluation should be an empirical game over
prompt-defined strategies in the sense of [Wellman (2006)](#bib.bib190),
not an average return, and the open difficulty is that the strategy
space is not characterised, which is the ninth problem in
[Section 24](#S24 "24 Open problems at the interface ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
$`\lozenge`$

Table 38: Training recommendations by model class. The Target column
states what the procedure converges to when it converges, and the
Guarantee column records the strength of that statement: T for a theorem
under stated conditions, A for an asymptotic or average-iterate
statement, H for a heuristic with no convergence guarantee.

A separate word is owed to evaluation, because the correspondence has
sharper consequences there than it has for training. Return against the
training opponents is not a measure of quality in any game that is not
common-payoff, since it is a measure of the pair and not of the policy.
The appropriate measures follow from the solution concept being
targeted. For a zero-sum game the measure is exploitability, the gap
between the value of the policy against a best response and the value of
the game, computable exactly in small games and approximable by training
a best-response agent in large ones; its multi-agent generalisation is
the sum of per-agent improvement from unilateral best response, often
called NashConv. For a general-sum game targeting a coarse correlated
equilibrium the measure is the maximum per-agent gain from a constant
deviation under the empirical joint play, which is the finite-sample
version of
[Definition 4.5](#S4.Thmtheorem5 "Definition 4.5 (Correlated and coarse correlated equilibrium). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
and if the target is a correlated equilibrium the measure is the maximum
swap gain instead, which
[Example 4.7](#S4.Thmtheorem7 "Example 4.7 (A coarse correlated equilibrium that is not correlated). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
shows is a strictly stronger requirement. For cooperative games with
decentralised execution the measure is cross-play, the return obtained
when agents trained in different runs are paired, which detects the
conventions that self-play silently installs and that [Hu et al.
(2020)](#bib.bib81) and [Hu et al. (2021)](#bib.bib82) are designed to
prevent. For populations the measure is the response graph and the
$`\alpha`$-rank distribution of [Omidshafiei et al.
(2019)](#bib.bib132), whose reading is the subject of
[Example 20.6](#S20.Thmtheorem6 "Example 20.6 (What a ranking of checkpoints can and cannot say). ‣ 20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
For a learned communication channel the measures are positive signalling
and positive listening ([Lowe et al., 2019](#bib.bib111)), since a
return improvement alone does not distinguish an informative protocol
from a babbling one. Reporting a learning curve of self-play return is,
for every model class except the common-payoff one with centralised
execution, uninformative about the quantity of interest.

| Target                         | Measure                                              | Failure detected                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| ------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Minimax value                  | Exploitability; gap to best response                 | Cycling; overfitting to the self-play opponent                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| Nash equilibrium, $`n\geq 2`$  | NashConv, the summed unilateral gain                 | Convergence to a non-equilibrium rest point                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| Coarse correlated equilibrium  | Maximum constant-deviation gain under empirical play | Regret accumulating despite flat return                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| Correlated equilibrium         | Maximum swap-deviation gain                          | Perfectly correlated play that no constant deviation beats ([Example 4.7](#S4.Thmtheorem7 "Example 4.7 (A coarse correlated equilibrium that is not correlated). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")) |
| Optimal joint policy           | Gap to a centralised planner on the same model       | Equilibrium selection failure; unrepresentable $`Q_{\mathrm{tot}}`$                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| Decentralised convention       | Cross-play return across independent runs            | Hidden handshakes and arbitrary conventions                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| Robustness to partners         | Return against a held-out partner population         | Overfitting to co-trained partners                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| Population quality             | Response graph; $`\alpha`$-rank; cross-play matrix   | Intransitivity masked by average return                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| Learned protocol               | Positive signalling and positive listening           | A channel that is present but unused                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| Welfare under selfish learning | Measured welfare against a planner solution          | Price of anarchy mistaken for an optimisation failure                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Mean-field equilibrium         | Exploitability of the representative agent           | Non-convergence of the population flow                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |

Table 39: Evaluation measures implied by the target solution concept.
The last column indicates the failure that the measure detects and that
mean return does not.

## 23 Correspondences that fail, and the theorems that forbid them

A dictionary is only trustworthy if it marks the words that have no
translation. This section collects the negative results, each of which
forbids a correspondence that is natural to conjecture and that appears
in the literature in informal form.

The two readings agree here, which is why this section is short on
vocabulary and long on consequences. A game theorist reads each result
as a limit on what rationality can deliver; a reinforcement learning
researcher reads it as a limit on what an algorithm can deliver. The
statements are the same and the consequence for practice is the same: a
system whose specification requires one of these correspondences to hold
is misspecified, and the remedy is to change the specification rather
than the optimiser.

| Game theory                  | Reinforcement learning                  | How to read the pair                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| ---------------------------- | --------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Non-stationary environment   | Others are learning; the kernel moves   | [Proposition 7.11](#S7.Thmtheorem11 "Proposition 7.11 (Fixed others induce a Markov decision process). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning") loses its hypothesis |
| Uncoupled dynamics           | Each agent sees only its own payoff     | The only realistic information structure                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| Cyclic equilibrium           | A cycle of policies, not a point        | What value iteration reaches in general sum                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| Last-iterate divergence      | Final checkpoint is not the output      | Only the average converges                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Representational failure     | Hypothesis class excludes the truth     | More data does not help                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Equilibrium multiplicity     | Many answers, no selection rule         | “Converged” is not a performance claim                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| Risk versus payoff dominance | Noise selects the safe equilibrium      | Not the best one                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| Price of anarchy             | Equilibrium welfare below the planner’s | Not an optimisation failure                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| Aggregation impossibility    | No rule satisfies every axiom           | Some desideratum must be dropped                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| Communication bound          | Misalignment caps informativeness       | A wider channel does not help                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |

Table 40: Failure modes read from both sides.

The first failure is the independent-learner reduction.
[Proposition 7.11](#S7.Thmtheorem11 "Proposition 7.11 (Fixed others induce a Markov decision process). ‣ 7 Stochastic games: the central correspondence ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is exact under the hypothesis that the other agents are stationary, and
in simultaneous learning the hypothesis is false by construction. The
consequences are not confined to a loss of guarantees. The Bellman
operator that agent $`i`$ is implicitly iterating has a fixed point that
moves as the others learn, so convergence of the iterates is not
expected even in the limit of infinite data; experience stored in a
replay buffer was generated under a joint policy that no longer exists,
so off-policy correction is required with respect to the others’
policies and not only one’s own; and the value function being learned is
an average over a non-stationary distribution of opponent behaviour,
which is not the value of any fixed game. The response of centralising
the critic addresses the third issue and not the first two, because a
critic conditioned on the joint action is stationary with respect to a
fixed joint policy and the joint policy is still moving.

The second failure concerns the possibility of a general convergent
learning rule. A learning dynamics is uncoupled when each agent’s update
depends on its own payoff function and on the observed play of the
others, but not on the others’ payoff functions, which is the only
informational setting in which a general-purpose algorithm makes sense.

###### Theorem 23.1 (Impossibility of uncoupled convergence; [Hart and Mas-Colell, 2003](#bib.bib76)).

There is no uncoupled, stationary, continuous-time dynamic
$`\dot{x}^{i}=F^{i}(x;u_{i})`$ that converges to the Nash equilibrium of
every game in a family containing a neighbourhood of a given game, even
when every game in the family has a unique Nash equilibrium and the
dynamic is required to be $`C^{1}`$ with a hyperbolic asymptotically
stable rest point.

[Theorem 23.1](#S23.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 23.1 (Impossibility of uncoupled convergence; , ). ‣ 23 Correspondences that fail, and the theorems that forbid them ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is a genuine impossibility rather than a statement about particular
algorithms, and it fixes the ceiling for a specific class of rules: no
stationary, memoryless, deterministic uncoupled dynamic converges to
Nash equilibria in general. The discrete-time picture is finer and
should not be summarised as the same statement. No uncoupled stationary
rule with one-period recall converges almost surely to pure Nash
equilibria in all games that possess them, and no uncoupled
finite-recall rule makes the behaviour probabilities converge to Nash
equilibria in every game; two-period recall together with randomisation
does, however, suffice for almost sure convergence of play ([Hart and
Mas-Colell, 2006](#bib.bib77)). Relaxing stationarity or memorylessness
therefore does admit convergent uncoupled rules, and the impossibility
should be read as delimiting which relaxations are necessary. It is the
reason the positive results of
[Section 22](#S22 "22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
are all conditional on structure, zero-sum, potential, common-payoff,
mean-field, and the reason that the unconditional results are all about
coarse correlated equilibria, which uncoupled no-regret dynamics do
reach. The contrast is instructive: the obstruction is informational,
since the agents collectively possess enough information to compute an
equilibrium and no agent individually does.

The third failure is specific to the sequential case and removes the
natural dynamic-programming approach.

###### Theorem 23.2 (Cyclic equilibria; [Zinkevich et al., 2005](#bib.bib200)).

There exist general-sum stochastic games, already deterministic
turn-taking ones with two agents and two states, in which the stationary
equilibrium policy is not a function of the $`Q`$-values: two such games
share the same $`Q`$-function and yet have different unique stationary
equilibria with different values. No algorithm that derives a stationary
policy from $`Q`$-values alone can therefore compute a stationary Nash
equilibrium of both. A cyclic equilibrium is a finite cycle of
stationary policies satisfying the correlated-equilibrium condition at
each stage, of which a stationary correlated equilibrium is the case of
cycle length one; value iteration with a utilitarian
correlated-equilibrium selection rule converges to a cyclic equilibrium
on these games.

Together with the PPAD-hardness of stationary coarse correlated
equilibria in Markov games ([Daskalakis et al., 2023](#bib.bib41)),
[Theorem 23.2](#S23.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 23.2 (Cyclic equilibria; , ). ‣ 23 Correspondences that fail, and the theorems that forbid them ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
says that the whole of the dynamic programming apparatus, which is the
foundation of single-agent reinforcement learning, has no general-sum
sequential counterpart. What survives is the apparatus restricted to the
structured classes, and the non-stationary correlated targets, which are
reached by online learning rather than by dynamic programming.

The fourth failure concerns the iterate rather than the average. In a
two-player zero-sum game with an interior equilibrium, the
continuous-time replicator and multiplicative-weights dynamics conserve
a Kullback-Leibler quantity and therefore cycle on invariant curves
rather than converging, and the discrete-time version diverges from the
equilibrium at a rate controlled by the step size ([Mertikopoulos
et al., 2018](#bib.bib118); [Bailey and Piliouras, 2018](#bib.bib13)).
The time average converges, which is why every classical guarantee in
this area is stated for the average strategy. The methodological
consequence has been stated already and bears repeating because it is
routinely violated: in a zero-sum game trained by self-play without
explicit averaging, the final checkpoint is not the output of the
algorithm and reporting it conflates the iterate with the average. The
measured version of this is
[Example 19.4](#S19.Thmtheorem4 "Example 19.4 (Multiplicative weights spirals away from the equilibrium). ‣ 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
in which the divergence to the equilibrium rises from $`0.036`$ to
$`1.202`$ over two thousand steps while the return stays pinned at zero,
and
[Example 19.2](#S19.Thmtheorem2 "Example 19.2 (Fictitious play on matching pennies: the iterate never settles). ‣ 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
in which the iterate has the maximum possible exploitability at every
step while the average converges. The remedy is either explicit
averaging, as in fictitious play and counterfactual regret minimisation,
or a regulariser that makes the dynamics contractive, as in regularised
Nash dynamics and magnetic mirror descent ([Perolat et al.,
2021](#bib.bib139); [Sokota et al., 2023](#bib.bib166)), or a look-ahead
correction, as in extragradient and optimistic updates
([Example 19.8](#S19.Thmtheorem8 "Example 19.8 (Why extragradient fixes what gradient ascent-descent breaks). ‣ 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")).

The fifth failure is representational and belongs to the cooperative
case. Monotone value factorisation satisfies the Individual-Global-Max
condition of
[Definition 16.7](#S16.Thmtheorem7 "Definition 16.7 (Individual-Global-Max). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and the converse fails, so there are common-payoff stage games whose
action-value function no monotone mixing network can represent, the
smallest being the two-by-two matrix of
[Example 16.9](#S16.Thmtheorem9 "Example 16.9 (A two-by-two matrix that no monotone mixing network represents). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning"),
which an additive decomposition misfits by $`8`$ in every cell. The
practical form of the failure is that an algorithm in this family can be
driven to a representable value function that is far from the true one,
with the learned greedy joint action suboptimal, and no amount of data
corrects it because the error is in the hypothesis class. Diagnosing it
requires comparing against a centralised planner on the same model,
which is the fifth row of
[Table 39](#S22.T39 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").

The sixth failure is conceptual and is the one with the largest
practical footprint. Nash equilibrium is a poor learning target in
general-sum games, for reasons that are independent of its computational
hardness. It is not unique, and the folk theorem of
[Theorem 6.2](#S6.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 6.2 (Folk theorem, discounted case; , ). ‣ 6 Repeated games and the history-indexed Markov decision process ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
makes the non-uniqueness extreme in repeated settings; it is not Pareto
optimal, as the prisoner’s dilemma shows; it is not a prediction of
behaviour, since it presupposes coordination on a particular profile
that the dynamics do not supply; and it is not what a designer wants,
which is typically welfare, fairness or robustness. The critique of
[Shoham et al. (2007)](#bib.bib161) remains unanswered, and the sound
response is to state the target explicitly, as
[Table 38](#S22.T38 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
does, and to evaluate against that target rather than against
equilibrium language used decoratively.

The seventh failure is that reward shaping does not transfer its
guarantee across agents without care. Potential-based shaping preserves
optimal policies in a Markov decision process and preserves Nash
equilibria in a stochastic game when each agent’s shaping term is
potential-based in that agent’s own information ([Ng et al.,
1999](#bib.bib129); [Devlin and Kudenko, 2011](#bib.bib44)). Shaping
terms that depend on other agents’ actions do not enjoy the invariance,
and a shaping scheme designed to encourage cooperation generically
changes the equilibrium set, which is sometimes the intent and is rarely
acknowledged. The distinction is between accelerating the search for a
solution of the given game and replacing the game with another one, and
only the first is what shaping means in the single-agent case;
[Example 16.11](#S16.Thmtheorem11 "Example 16.11 (Shaping that preserves and shaping that does not). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
exhibits both.

The eighth failure is that no-external-regret learning does not deliver
a correlated equilibrium, only a coarse one, and the two differ as soon
as some agent has three actions.
[Example 4.7](#S4.Thmtheorem7 "Example 4.7 (A coarse correlated equilibrium that is not correlated). ‣ 4 Solution concepts as fixed points, and what they collapse to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
exhibits a distribution in rock-paper-scissors under which both agents
are perfectly correlated, neither has any external regret, and a
rewiring of each agent’s own action as a function of what it was about
to play would gain $`1/3`$ per round. A practitioner who monitors
external regret alone will not see this, and the diagnostic that does
see it is the maximum swap gain. The common claim that self-play
converges to a correlated equilibrium is therefore false as stated; it
converges to the coarse set unless the rule is specifically a
no-swap-regret one.

The ninth failure is that noisy independent learning does not select the
best equilibrium.
[Example 19.6](#S19.Thmtheorem6 "Example 19.6 (Which equilibrium of the stag hunt survives rare mistakes). ‣ 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
computes the stationary distribution of log-linear learning on the stag
hunt exactly and finds that as the noise vanishes the chain concentrates
with probability $`0.99995`$ on the equilibrium worth $`3`$ rather than
the one worth $`4`$. The selected equilibrium is the risk-dominant one
and equivalently the potential maximiser, and the selection is a
property of the dynamics rather than of the objective. No increase in
sample size, network capacity or training time changes it, and the only
remedies are the equilibrium selection devices of
[Section 8](#S8 "8 Partial observability and the lattice of decentralised models ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").

The tenth failure is that enlarging the agents’ options can lower the
equilibrium welfare.
[Example 10.7](#S10.Thmtheorem7 "Example 10.7 (Braess’s paradox ( , ): adding capacity makes everybody worse off). ‣ 10 Graphical, factored and congestion games: structure in the payoff tensor ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
exhibits a network in which adding a free link raises the equilibrium
cost by a factor of $`4/3`$ while leaving the social optimum unchanged.
An ablation in which a richer action space produces worse outcomes is
therefore not necessarily an optimisation failure, and distinguishing
the two requires the planner comparison of
[Table 39](#S22.T39 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The same caution applies to the price of anarchy generally: a gap
between independent learners and a centralised planner is the expected
equilibrium behaviour of the system, and closing it requires changing
the rewards, as
[Example 15.10](#S15.Thmtheorem10 "Example 15.10 (The selfish-routing bound, and what a toll buys). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
does, rather than improving the optimiser.

The eleventh failure is that a wider communication channel does not buy
more communication.
[Theorem 13.3](#S13.Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 13.3 (Cheap talk under misaligned objectives; , ). ‣ 13 Communication: cheap talk, signalling, persuasion and emergent protocols ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
bounds the number of distinguishable messages that can be sustained in
equilibrium by the misalignment of the objectives, giving at most three
intervals at a bias of $`0.05`$ and only babbling at a bias of $`0.25`$,
irrespective of the channel’s capacity. An architecture that provides a
high-bandwidth differentiable channel to agents with partly conflicting
rewards is therefore providing capacity that equilibrium forbids using,
and the coarse protocol that emerges is the correct answer rather than
an optimisation artefact.

The twelfth failure is that no rule aggregates preferences without cost.
By
[Theorem 15.6](#S15.Thmtheorem6 "Theorem 15.6 (Impossibility in social choice; , ). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
any scheme that combines several agents’ or annotators’ rankings into
one must abandon one of the five conditions listed there, and any
deterministic voting rule without transfers must abandon one of
surjectivity, non-dictatorship and strategy-proofness. Reward models
fitted to multiple annotators are such schemes, so the question is not
whether they have a defect but which one they have, and the honest
report names it.

A final observation ties these together. Almost every failure in this
section is a failure of a reduction that would have made the multi-agent
problem single-agent, and the remainder are failures of the hope that a
multi-agent system can be given everything a designer wants at once.
That is not a coincidence. The reductions of
[Section 9](#S9 "9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
that do succeed all pay the same price, an exponential enlargement of
the action space to a space of functions, and the complexity results of
[Section 21](#S21 "21 The complexity landscape ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
show that the price is necessary. The negative results are the statement
that there is no cheaper currency.

## 24 Open problems at the interface

The correspondences catalogued here delimit what is known, and the
boundary is sharp enough in several places to name concrete problems. We
state thirteen, in decreasing order of how well posed they are.

The first is a learning-time version of
[Theorem 9.5](#S9.Thmtheorem5 "Theorem 9.5 (Occupancy-MDP reduction; , ). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
The occupancy state is computed by propagating the model forward, so the
occupancy Markov decision process is a planning construction. A
model-free surrogate that is provably a sufficient statistic, learned
from interaction alone and with a bound relating the loss in the
surrogate to the loss in value, would convert the single exact reduction
available for cooperative decentralised problems into a deep learning
method with a guarantee. The same question for the public belief state
of
[Remark 9.7](#S9.Thmtheorem7 "Remark 9.7 (Public belief state reduction). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
is the obstacle to extending ReBeL-style search beyond games whose
public information structure is given by construction.

The second is last-iterate convergence outside the zero-sum and
potential classes. Regularised Nash dynamics obtains it in two-player
zero-sum games by anchoring, and the analysis uses the monotonicity of
the zero-sum operator. No general-sum analogue is known, and the
PPAD-hardness of stationary coarse correlated equilibria ([Daskalakis
et al., 2023](#bib.bib41)) suggests that the correct statement involves
a weaker target. Identifying the largest class of general-sum Markov
games admitting a last-iterate-convergent uncoupled dynamics is open,
and the Markov potential class is almost certainly not maximal; the
monotone class of
[Definition 19.7](#S19.Thmtheorem7 "Definition 19.7 (Monotone games and diagonal strict concavity; , ). ‣ 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
and the supermodular class of
[Definition 16.10](#S16.Thmtheorem10 "Definition 16.10 (Supermodular game and strategic complements; , ). ‣ 16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
are the two obvious candidates for an extension, and neither has a
sequential counterpart with the strength of [Leonardos et al.
(2022)](#bib.bib107).

The third is a solution concept for general-sum sequential problems that
is simultaneously attainable by uncoupled learning, executable without
shared randomness and meaningful as a design target. Coarse correlated
equilibrium satisfies the first, fails the second in its non-stationary
form, and satisfies the third only weakly. Stationary product-form
targets fail the first by
[Theorem 23.1](#S23.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 23.1 (Impossibility of uncoupled convergence; , ). ‣ 23 Correspondences that fail, and the theorems that forbid them ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning").
Whether some intermediate object, for instance a correlated equilibrium
implementable by publicly observable history rather than by an external
mediator, threads the three requirements is unresolved and is the single
most consequential open question for applications in which several
independently deployed systems must interoperate.

The fourth concerns the three-player and many-player zero-sum case.
Constant-sum games with more than two agents lose the interchangeability
and payoff-uniqueness of equilibria, so the guarantees underlying
self-play collapse even though the total payoff is still conserved. The
pairwise-separable case is solved by [Cai and Daskalakis
(2011)](#bib.bib29) and the unrestricted case is PPAD-complete, so the
question is what lies between: which structural restrictions short of
polymatrix separability preserve tractability. Practical systems in this
regime rely on heuristics, and a principled target comparable to the
minimax value is missing. The $`\alpha`$-rank concept of [Omidshafiei
et al. (2019)](#bib.bib132) is the most developed candidate and its
relation to the payoffs of a deployed system is not understood.

The fifth is a unified theory of regret decomposition and
temporal-difference bootstrapping.
[Section 11](#S11 "11 Extensive-form games, perfect recall, and information sets as histories ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
observed that counterfactual regret decomposition and the performance
difference lemma express the same factorisation of a global value gap
into local terms weighted by a visitation measure. The two algorithm
families built on them, tree-traversing regret minimisers and
bootstrapping value learners, have disjoint analyses and complementary
strengths, the former with equilibrium guarantees and poor scaling in
depth, the latter with the reverse. A common analysis would be of
immediate practical consequence and the ingredients appear to be
available.

The sixth is finite-population correction for mean-field methods. The
$`O(1/\sqrt{n})`$ bound is pessimistic in practice, and
[Example 18.3](#S18.Thmtheorem3 "Example 18.3 (What the rate means at benchmark scale). ‣ 18 The population limit: mean-field games and mean-field control ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
records that at the ten to twenty-five agents of most benchmarks it
delivers no guarantee at all. The regimes in which mean-field policies
perform well at that scale are not characterised. A refined bound
exploiting the structure of the interaction, rather than only its
Lipschitz constant, would turn a limit theorem into a usable guarantee.

The seventh is partially observable Markov potential games. The
potential structure that yields convergence of independent policy
gradient is defined for fully observed stochastic games, and the
cooperative problems that motivate it are typically partially observed.
Whether the potential property survives under partial observability, and
under what condition on the observation kernel, is not settled, and a
positive answer would supply the first convergence guarantee for
independent learners in decentralised partially observable problems.

The eighth is the status of commitment in learned systems. The
Stackelberg reading of
[Section 14](#S14 "14 Commitment, bilevel structure, and opponent shaping ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
presumes that commitment is credible, and in a deployed learning system
credibility is a property of the training pipeline rather than of the
game: a leader that can be retrained is not committed. The mechanism
design literature addresses credibility through contracts and
reputation, and the translation of those devices into the setting in
which the leader is a learning algorithm is unexplored.

The ninth is the strategic analysis of language-model agents. Systems
that negotiate, delegate and form agreements in natural language
instantiate games with cheap talk, incomplete information about types,
and commitment devices realised through text rather than through a
formal mechanism. The game-theoretic objects are the ones in this
monograph, with types given by system prompts and private context, and
the empirical methodology of
[Section 20](#S20 "20 The learning-rate limit: evolutionary dynamics as the flow of learning ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
applies directly, since one can estimate an empirical payoff matrix over
prompt-defined strategies and analyse it. What is missing is any
characterisation of the strategy space, and therefore any sense in which
an equilibrium computed over a sampled subset is informative about the
whole.

The tenth is a smoothness calculus for sequential games.
[Theorem 15.9](#S15.Thmtheorem9 "Theorem 15.9 (Smoothness and robust price of anarchy; , ). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
converts a welfare bound at pure equilibria into a welfare bound for the
time-averaged play of no-regret learners, and it is stated for one-shot
games. A Markov-game version, in which a per-state smoothness condition
composes into a bound on the discounted welfare of the empirical
occupancy of independent learners, would give the first practically
checkable welfare guarantee for general-sum multi-agent reinforcement
learning. Whether the composition is possible at all, and what it costs
in the constant, is open.

The eleventh is the equilibrium selection problem stated as an
optimisation problem.
[Example 19.6](#S19.Thmtheorem6 "Example 19.6 (Which equilibrium of the stag hunt survives rare mistakes). ‣ 19 Learning dynamics in games and what each of them converges to ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
shows that noisy independent learning selects the risk-dominant
equilibrium and that the selection is computable in closed form for a
two-by-two game. For larger games the stochastically stable set is
defined by a minimum-spanning-tree computation over the profile space,
which is exponential. An approximation that is computable from samples
and that predicts which convention a given learning rule will install
would turn equilibrium selection from a post-hoc observation into a
design variable.

The twelfth is credit assignment with a guarantee.
[Section 17](#S17 "17 Coalitions, stability of credit, and bargaining ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
supplies axiomatic credit schemes and
[Section 16](#S16 "16 Potential and supermodular structure, value factorisation, and credit ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
supplies variance-reduction baselines, and no result relates the two:
there is no theorem saying that using Shapley-value credit instead of a
difference reward improves a sample-complexity or convergence bound.
Since the Shapley value costs $`2^{n-1}`$ evaluations against one, the
absence of such a theorem means the extra cost is currently justified
only by the axioms.

The thirteenth is the interaction between aggregation impossibility and
preference-based training.
[Theorem 15.6](#S15.Thmtheorem6 "Theorem 15.6 (Impossibility in social choice; , ). ‣ 15 Mechanism design, auctions, and the price of anarchy ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
says that every aggregation of several annotators’ preferences abandons
one classical desideratum, and the practice of fitting a single scalar
reward model abandons independence of irrelevant alternatives. Which
axiom one should prefer to abandon, and what the downstream consequence
is for the policy trained against the aggregate, is not studied. This is
a well-posed question with an immediate application and no literature
connecting the two sides.

## 25 Conclusions

The central claim of this monograph is that the relationship between
game theory and reinforcement learning is one of identity over a large
part of its extent, of exact reduction over a further substantial part,
and of analogy only in a small and precisely delimitable remainder.
Stating which of the three applies in each case is the whole of the
contribution, and the cases divide as follows.

The identities are the stochastic game and the Markov decision process,
which coincide at $`n=1`$; the partially observable stochastic game and
the partially observable Markov decision process, which coincide at
$`n=1`$; the common-payoff partially observable stochastic game and the
decentralised partially observable Markov decision process, which
coincide by definition; the information set of an extensive-form game
with perfect recall and the action-observation history of a partially
observable problem; best response and greedy policy improvement; subgame
perfection and the principle of optimality; Markov perfect equilibrium
and the restriction to stationary policies; the behavioural strategy and
the policy; the graphical game and the coordination graph; the strictly
dominated strategy and the action that no mask need preserve; and the
Nash equilibrium and the fixed point of simultaneous policy improvement,
which collapses to the fixed point of the Bellman optimality operator
when there is one agent.

The exact reductions are the belief-state reduction of a partially
observable problem to a continuous-state Markov decision process; the
common-information reduction of a decentralised control problem with
partial history sharing to a coordinator’s partially observable problem
over prescriptions; the occupancy-state reduction of a decentralised
partially observable Markov decision process to a deterministic
continuous-state Markov decision process with a piecewise linear convex
value function; the public-belief-state reduction of a two-player
zero-sum imperfect-information game to a perfect-information game, which
[Remark 9.7](#S9.Thmtheorem7 "Remark 9.7 (Public belief state reduction). ‣ 9 Exact reductions of decentralised models to single-agent ones ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
records as a remark because the source states it informally; the
Harsanyi transformation and its identity with the Bayes-adaptive
construction; the sequence form and the occupancy measure; the minimax
Bellman operator and the robust Bellman operator; the quantal response
equilibrium and the soft Bellman operator of maximum-entropy
reinforcement learning; the difference reward and a single term of the
Shapley value; the VCG transfer and the counterfactual baseline;
Blackwell approachability and no-regret learning; the replicator
dynamics as the continuous-time limit of cross-learning; and the
replicator-mutator dynamics as the limit of frequency-adjusted Boltzmann
Q-learning. Every one of these pays for exactness with an enlargement of
the state space to a space of distributions and of the action space to a
space of functions, and the complexity results of
[Section 21](#S21 "21 The complexity landscape ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
establish that the price is not an artefact of the constructions.

The analogies that fail, and the conditions under which they fail, are
the reduction of a multi-agent problem to a single-agent one by treating
the others as part of the environment, which is exact for fixed others
and invalid under simultaneous learning; the reduction of a
common-payoff game to a joint-action Markov decision process, which is
exact for centralised execution and silent about decentralised
execution; the extension of dynamic programming to general-sum
stochastic games, which is forbidden by the cyclic-equilibrium result;
the identification of what no-external-regret self-play reaches with a
correlated equilibrium, which holds only in the coarse sense and fails
as soon as an agent has three actions; the expectation that noisy
independent learners reach the payoff-dominant equilibrium, which the
stochastic-stability computation contradicts; and the pursuit of Nash
equilibrium as a general learning target, which is forbidden by the
impossibility of uncoupled convergence and is in any case not what a
designer wants.

Three consequences for practice follow and we restate them because they
are actionable. The first is that the choice of training procedure
should be made by answering the five structural questions of
[Section 22](#S22 "22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
about the deployment, not by selecting from a list of algorithms by
reported benchmark performance, because the guarantees in
[Table 38](#S22.T38 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
are conditional on exactly those structural properties and benchmark
performance is not. The second is that evaluation must be matched to the
target solution concept, as
[Table 39](#S22.T39 "In 22 How to train: matching the algorithm to the model ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
sets out, because in every model class except the common-payoff one with
centralised execution the mean return against the training opponents
measures a pair rather than a policy. The third is that before any
algorithm is chosen the deployment game should be checked for the three
structural properties that carry guarantees, namely potential or
supermodular structure, pairwise-separable zero-sum structure, and
smoothness, because each converts an intractable problem into a
tractable one and each is cheap to verify.

The dictionary is not complete, and the open problems of
[Section 24](#S24 "24 Open problems at the interface ‣ When Is a Game a Markov Decision Process?Correspondences Between Game Theoryand Reinforcement Learning")
mark where it stops. The three that matter most, in our assessment, are
a model-free sufficient statistic that would make the occupancy-state
reduction a learning method rather than a planning method, a solution
concept for general-sum sequential problems that is simultaneously
learnable, executable without shared randomness and meaningful as a
design target, and a sequential version of the smoothness calculus that
would give a checkable welfare guarantee for independent learners in
Markov games. Progress on any of them would move a substantial region of
the map from the analogy column into the exact column, which is the
direction in which this field advances.
